# The Security Vocabulary

One thousand nine hundred ninety-six named concepts that engineering uses to
talk about systems under attack, folded from a catalog of 13,764 named,
established entries; the research record beside this file preserves how. The
entries sit in eight families, ordered as a reader moves from what the subject
is to how a system fails without harming anyone: what security is and who you
are up against, how code is broken, the surfaces an application exposes,
cryptography from primitives to deployment, identity and authority, boundaries
and what crosses them, operations and delivery, and failing safely. Read the
family you are standing in, and treat each entry as an option to weigh, so an
omission becomes a decision rather than an accident.

Each entry opens with the names it answers to. The first is the name the field
uses most often, and the bold names after it are the others that reach the same
entry, whether a true synonym, a competing coinage, or a member the entry
folded in. A vocabulary earns its keep by answering to the term someone just
heard, so the names are the product and the glosses only explain them.

Two entries in five end by naming what they cost, and those are the ones a
person applies, a mechanism or a countermeasure or a practice or a protocol
choice. The rest end at the definition, because nobody applies an attack, a
weakness, a property, a model, or a failure mode, and knowing those costs
nothing. That split is the study's first finding, and it falls in a sharper
place than the word "security" suggests. Almost every entry carries a price in
identity and authority, in failing safely, and among boundaries, which are the
families built from switches somebody turns on and then lives with. Almost
nothing carries one in foundations, which holds properties and principles, or
in operations, where the bulk of the vocabulary names what an adversary does
rather than what a defender installs. A reader hunting for decisions should
start where the prices are.

One boundary here is deliberate and one is partial. Security is present in
full, the discipline whose opponent is an adversary rather than entropy. Safety
engineering is not, except for the single slice a software template family can
carry, fail-safe states and fault containment, which is the eighth family. The
certification universe governing safety-critical software is out, and so is
physical security, though a channel a program can observe or exploit is in even
where its physics is physical. Products, tools, and vendors are never entries,
while a named algorithm, format, protocol, or model is one wherever the field
treats the name as a concept.

## What security is and who you are up against

### What security means

- **Confidentiality**: information is disclosed only to parties entitled to see it.
- **Integrity**: data and systems change only in authorized ways, and unauthorized change is detectable.
- **Availability**: a system delivers its service to legitimate users when they need it.
- **CIA triad**, also **AIC triad**: the classical grouping of confidentiality, integrity, and availability as the core goals of information security.
- **Parkerian hexad**: Donn Parker's six-attribute set adding authenticity, possession, which counts holding a copy as a loss in itself, and utility, which counts data made useless to its owner.
- **Authenticity**: a claim about the origin of data or the identity of a party is genuine.
- **Non-repudiation**: an actor cannot credibly deny having performed an action for which evidence was recorded, which for signatures also demands exclusive ownership, since one reinterpretable under a second key and message can be claimed by whoever builds that pair.
- **Accountability**, also **auditability**: actions are traceable to the party responsible, and enough evidence is retained for an independent party to reconstruct what happened.
- **Authentication, authorization, and accounting**, also **AAA**: the framework naming who a party is, what that party may do, and what it did, opening with identification, where a party asserts the identity it claims.
- **Privacy**: individuals retain control over what information about them is collected, used, and disclosed.
- **Anonymity and pseudonymity**: two conditions of acting unnamed, one where an actor cannot be resolved within a set of candidates, the other where an actor holds a persistent identifier not bound to a real identity.
- **Unlinkability, unobservability, and undetectability**: three privacy properties, that two actions cannot be tied to one party, that an action cannot be seen to have happened, and that an item cannot be told apart from nothing.
- **Plausible deniability**: a party can deny authorship of an action or a message without the denial being refutable.
- **Safety, liveness, and hyperproperties**: three shapes of stated property, that nothing bad happens in one execution, that something good eventually happens, and that a claim holds across sets of executions.
- **Negative requirement**: a requirement stating that something must never happen, which testing can never exhaustively confirm.
- **Trustworthiness**: a system deserves the reliance placed on it, on the strength of evidence rather than assumption.
- **Trusted system**: a component whose misbehavior would break the security policy, whether or not it merits that reliance.

### The design-principle canon

- **Economy of mechanism**: the design of a protection mechanism should be kept as small and simple as review can fully cover.
- **Fail-safe defaults**: access decisions should rest on explicit permission, so that the absence of a rule denies rather than allows.
- **Complete mediation**: every access to every object must be checked for authority, with no cached or bypassed path.
- **Open design**: the design should not depend on the ignorance of an attacker, only on the secrecy of keys and passwords.
- **Separation of privilege**, also **distributed privilege**, also **predicate permission**: an operation should require two independent keys or approvals so that no single holder can perform it alone.
- **Least privilege**: each subject should operate with only the authority its task requires, for only as long as it requires it, and should know only what its duty needs.
- **Least common mechanism**: the amount of mechanism shared by and depended on by multiple users should be minimized.
- **Psychological acceptability**: a protection mechanism must fit the user's mental image of the goal, or users will apply it wrongly.
- **Work factor**: the strength of a mechanism is measured by the effort an attacker must expend to defeat it.
- **Compromise recording**: recording reliable evidence that a compromise happened is sometimes worth more than preventing it.
- **Principle of least authority**, also **POLA**, also **designation as authorization**: authority should be delegated as narrow capabilities rather than as broad identity-based rights, so naming a resource and being permitted to use it are one act.
- **Kerckhoffs's principle**, also **Shannon's maxim**: a system should stay secure when everything about it except the key is public, so design on the assumption that the enemy knows the system in full.
- **Security through obscurity**: reliance on the secrecy of design or implementation as the source of protection, worth keeping only as an added delay, at the cost of hiding weaknesses from defenders and reviewers too.
- **Defense in depth**, also **layered defense**: independent overlapping controls are placed so no single failure gives an attacker the objective, at the cost of expense, latency, and operational complexity.
- **Weakest link**: an attacker chooses the cheapest point of failure, so overall strength is bounded by the poorest control.
- **Hiding secrets is hard**: any secret embedded in software or hardware that reaches an adversary should be assumed recoverable.
- **Attack surface reduction**: exposed interfaces, features, privileges, and code paths are removed or disabled to shrink what an attacker can reach, at the cost of lost functionality.
- **Assume breach**: design and operations assume an adversary is already inside, at the cost of controls that pay off only after failure.
- **Secure by design**: security requirements shape architecture from the start rather than being added later, at the cost of slower early design and scarce expertise.
- **Secure by default**: a product ships in its safe configuration with risky features off and limits set low enough that an unconfigured system cannot consume without bound, and gives operators the means to run and patch it, at the cost of out-of-box functionality and support calls.
- **Shift left**, also **build security in**: security becomes a property of the development process and moves earlier, where defects are cheaper to remove, at the cost of developer time and restructured work.
- **Fail securely**, also **secure failure and recovery**: a function that fails, and any recovery action afterward, leaves the system no less protected than before it ran.
- **Compartmentalization**, also **blast radius reduction**, also **assume-breach compartment design**: a system is divided so that entities with different protection needs share nothing implicitly and a compromise of one part does not reach the others, at the cost of design effort, crossing latency, duplicated validation, and awkward legitimate sharing.
- **Fix the class, not the instance**: a flaw is remedied by removing the pattern that produced it rather than the single reported case, at the cost of larger changes.

### Engineering and resiliency principles

- **Modularity and layering**, also **partially ordered dependencies**: function is divided into separable units arranged so each layer depends only on those beneath it, with no cycles in calling, synchronization, or initialization.
- **Minimized sharing**: resources are not shared between subjects unless sharing is required, which removes channels and contention between them.
- **Hierarchical trust**, also **hierarchical protection**: dependence flows only toward components at least as trustworthy, and the more trustworthy a component is, the harder it must be to change.
- **Compositional trustworthiness**, also **secure distributed composition**: the trustworthiness of an assembly follows from its parts and the way they are composed, each part remaining secure when isolated.
- **Commensurate protection**, also **commensurate rigor**, also **commensurate response**: protection strength, engineering effort, and reaction all scale with the value of what is protected and the consequence of losing it.
- **Continuous protection**: protective function is enforced without gaps across startup, operation, failure, maintenance, and shutdown.
- **Cyber resiliency goals and objectives**: the four goals of anticipate, withstand, recover, and adapt, refined into objectives to prevent, prepare, continue, constrain, reconstitute, understand, transform, and re-architect.
- **Cyber resiliency techniques**: the fourteen named techniques of adaptive response, analytic monitoring, contextual awareness, coordinated protection, deception, diversity, dynamic positioning, non-persistence, privilege restriction, realignment, redundancy, segmentation, substantiated integrity, and unpredictability, at the cost of infrastructure and analyst time.
- **Cyber resiliency design principles**: the eighteen strategic and structural principles paired with the techniques, from focusing on common critical assets and reducing attack surfaces through assuming compromised resources, expecting adversaries to evolve, limiting the need for trust, planning diversity, maximizing transience, and making unpredictability transparent to users.
- **Cyber resiliency approach**: the taxonomy's third tier, naming how each technique is implemented, including architectural, design, synthetic, information, path and supplier diversity, non-persistent information, services and connectivity, dynamic segmentation and isolation, provenance tracking, integrity checks, behavior validation, dynamic privileges, protected backup and restore, and temporal and contextual unpredictability.
- **Diversity**: heterogeneous implementations of one function so a single flaw does not reach every instance, drawn from architectural, design, synthetic, information, path, and supplier variants, at the cost of duplicated engineering and harder maintenance.
- **Non-persistence**, also **least persistence**: information, services, and connectivity exist only for the interval the operation requires, so a foothold decays on its own, at the cost of lost state and orchestration machinery.
- **Deception and unpredictability**: assets are hidden or misrepresented through obfuscation, disinformation, misdirection, and tainting, and changes are made unannounced so reconnaissance goes stale, at the cost of defender confusion and debugging difficulty.

### Trust and its boundaries

- **Trust**: reliance placed on a component or party such that its misbehavior would cause harm.
- **Trust assumption**: an unproven belief about a component or party that the security argument depends on.
- **Trust boundary**: a line in a design where data or control passes between parties of different privilege or trustworthiness, violated by mixing trusted and attacker-influenced data in one structure so later code cannot tell them apart.
- **Trust model**: the description of who trusts whom for what within a system, stating each dependence for authentication, authorization, or correctness.
- **Trust domain**: a set of components governed by one authority and trusting one another by default.
- **Transitive trust**: trust extended indirectly, where trusting one party silently means trusting everything that party trusts.
- **Root of trust**: a component trusted unconditionally because nothing beneath it is available to check it, with each later link vouched for by the one before.
- **Trusted computing base**, also **TCB**: the totality of hardware, firmware, and software that enforcement of the security policy depends on.
- **TCB minimization**: deliberately shrinking what must be trusted so review and verification can cover it, at the cost of restructuring and lost convenience.
- **Trusted path**, also **secure attention key**, also **secure attention sequence**: a user-to-component channel that other software cannot spoof or observe, entered by a signal only the platform can intercept, at the cost of platform support, reserved input handling, and interface constraints.
- **Trusted third party**: a party both sides rely on for a security function, and whose compromise breaks the arrangement for both.
- **Security perimeter**, also **perimeter security**: a boundary separating a protected region from an untrusted outside, at the cost of leaving the interior undefended once the boundary is crossed.
- **Zero trust**, also **never trust, always verify**: network position grants no privilege, so a zero trust architecture authenticates and authorizes every request afresh instead of admitting implicit trust zones, at the cost of identity infrastructure, latency, and migration effort.

### What a threat model names

- **Threat**: a potential event or actor with the ability to cause harm to an asset.
- **Threat source**, also **risk source**: the origin of a threat, whether an adversary, an accident, or an environmental event.
- **Threat event**, also **threat scenario**: a specific occurrence in which a threat source acts against an asset, or an ordered set of such occurrences pursuing one objective.
- **Attack**: an attempt to destroy, expose, alter, disable, steal, or gain unauthorized access to an asset.
- **Vulnerability**: a weakness in design, implementation, or operation that a threat can act through.
- **Weakness**: a flaw in software or hardware that may become a vulnerability in a specific product.
- **Exposure**: a configuration or condition that is not itself a flaw but gives an attacker useful advantage.
- **Exploit**: a technique or artifact that turns a vulnerability into actual unauthorized effect, its exploitability being how far conditions and attacker skill allow that in practice.
- **Asset**: something of value to the defender whose loss or misuse is the harm being prevented, the crown jewels being those whose loss would be unrecoverable.
- **Attack surface**: the sum of points at which an adversary can supply input to or extract output from a system, measured by counting entry points and untrusted data items weighted by privilege.
- **Attack vector**: the specific route and means by which an attacker reaches a target.
- **Monoculture**: a population of identical systems in which one flaw is exploitable everywhere at once.

### The adversary

- **Adversary**, also **threat actor**: the hostile party a security argument is made against, and which acts or may act to cause harm.
- **Adversary model**: an explicit statement of what the assumed attacker can observe, do, corrupt, and compute.
- **Capability, intent, and opportunity**: the three conditions all required before a threat actor is an actual threat, joined by the money, people, and time the actor can spend.
- **Active and passive adversary**: two positions on a channel, one injecting, modifying, replaying, or dropping messages, the other only observing traffic or state without altering anything.
- **Honest-but-curious adversary**, also **semi-honest adversary**: a participant that follows the protocol exactly but tries to learn everything it can from what it sees.
- **Malicious adversary**: a participant that may deviate from the protocol in any way, including aborting or sending crafted values, the covert variant deviating only when detection is unlikely.
- **Byzantine adversary**: a corrupted participant that may behave arbitrarily, including appearing correct to some parties and not to others.
- **Adaptive, static, bounded, and unbounded adversaries**: the dimensions of a cryptographic adversary model, whether corruptions are chosen as the protocol unfolds or fixed beforehand, and whether computation is feasible-only or unlimited.
- **Dolev-Yao model**, also **network attacker model**: a network adversary model in which the attacker controls the whole network but cannot break cryptographic primitives, the frame in which a protocol's security claim is stated, the global passive variant only watching every point at once.
- **Advanced persistent threat**, also **APT**: a well-resourced actor that maintains long-term covert access to a chosen target and returns after eviction.
- **Nation-state actor**: an attacker acting for a government, with legal cover, budget, and intelligence support, alongside the cyber mercenary who sells such capability to whoever pays.
- **Cybercriminal**: an attacker whose goal is direct financial gain, working through organized groups that divide labor between initial access brokers, ransomware operators, botnet operators, and launderers.
- **Hacktivist and script kiddie**: two low-budget attacker types, one motivated by a political or social cause rather than money, the other running tools written by others without understanding them.
- **Insider threat**: harm caused by a party who already holds legitimate access, whether deliberately, through carelessness, or because an outsider is operating their credentials.
- **Intimate partner threat**: an attacker with physical device access and knowledge of the victim's secrets, arising from a personal relationship.
- **Tactics, techniques, and procedures**, also **TTPs**: the description of adversary behavior at three levels, from goals down to specific tool usage.

### Eliciting threats

- **Threat model**: a structured description of what is being protected, from whom, and which failures are considered in scope.
- **Threat modeling**: working through a design to ask what is being built, what can go wrong, what to do about it, and whether that was enough, at the cost of skilled analyst time.
- **Data flow diagram**: a threat modeling drawing of processes, stores, flows, external entities, and trust boundaries, at the cost of drift from the real system.
- **STRIDE**: the six-category scheme for eliciting threats, covering spoofing, tampering, repudiation, information disclosure, denial of service, and elevation of privilege, applied per diagram element or per interaction between elements.
- **DREAD**: the five-factor rating scheme scoring damage, reproducibility, exploitability, affected users, and discoverability to rank threats.
- **LINDDUN**: the privacy threat taxonomy covering linkability, identifiability, non-repudiation, detectability, disclosure of information, unawareness, and non-compliance, worked through as card-driven GO sessions or the full per-flow PRO process.
- **OCTAVE**: an asset-driven organizational risk evaluation run by the organization's own staff, published as OCTAVE-S for small teams, Allegro for information assets, and FORTE for enterprise risk alignment, at the cost of extensive facilitated sessions.
- **PASTA**: a seven-stage risk-centric threat modeling process running from business objectives to residual risk analysis, at the cost of heavy documentation and analyst time.
- **Trike, VAST, and CORAS**: model-driven methods, one working from a requirements model of actors and permitted actions, one from paired application and operational models, one from a graphical risk language, at the cost of building and maintaining that model.
- **Hybrid Threat Modeling Method**, also **hTMM**: a combination of persona-based and systematic elicitation aiming for full coverage with no false positives, at the cost of a longer process.
- **Security Cards, Persona non Grata, and the Elevation of Privilege card game**: prompt-driven elicitation aids, one dealing human impact and adversary motivation, one reasoning from archetypal attackers, one dealing concrete threat examples, at the cost of facilitation and unpruned output.
- **Software-centric, attacker-centric, and asset-centric threat modeling**: the three starting points, from the design and its flows, from adversary goals and capabilities, or from what is worth protecting, each blind to what its starting point does not reach.
- **Misuse case, abuse case, and evil user story**: requirements written from the attacker's side, stating interactions the system must not permit, paired with anti-requirements, security use cases, and the SQUARE process for prioritizing them.
- **Privacy by design**: privacy protection is built into a system as the default rather than added as notice and consent, through data minimization and purpose limitation, at the cost of constraining data-hungry features.

### Attack structures and the published taxonomies

- **Attack tree**: a tree whose root is an attacker goal, decomposed through AND nodes needing every child and OR nodes needing one, and extended into an attack-defense tree by adding countermeasure nodes.
- **Attack graph**, also **attack path**: a graph of system states and transitions showing every route from an attacker's initial position to a goal, one concrete route through it being a path.
- **Attack chain**, also **exploit chain**: a series of individually limited steps combined so that each one enables the next.
- **Cyber kill chain**: the Lockheed Martin model of an intrusion as seven ordered phases, reconnaissance, weaponization, delivery, exploitation, installation, command and control, and actions on objectives, lengthened by the Unified Kill Chain and attack lifecycle models.
- **Diamond Model of intrusion analysis**: a model describing every intrusion event as an adversary using a capability over an infrastructure against a victim, where knowing one vertex leads to the others.
- **MITRE ATT&CK**: a knowledge base organizing observed adversary behavior into tactics, techniques, and sub-techniques with documented usage, published as matrices for enterprise, mobile, and industrial control systems.
- **ATT&CK tactics**: the fourteen adversary objectives of reconnaissance, resource development, initial access, execution, persistence, privilege escalation, defense evasion, credential access, discovery, lateral movement, collection, command and control, exfiltration, and impact.
- **CAPEC**: an enumeration of attack patterns organized by the mechanism the attacker uses and by the domain attacked, covering software, hardware, communications, supply chain, social engineering, and physical security.
- **Mechanisms of Attack**: the nine CAPEC pattern categories of deceptive interaction, abuse of existing functionality, manipulation of data structures, manipulation of system resources, injection of unexpected items, probabilistic techniques, timing and state manipulation, information collection, and subversion of access control.
- **CWE**: an enumeration of weakness types as a common language for describing flaws, written at pillar, class, base, and variant abstraction, related as chains where one flaw enables the next and composites needing several at once.
- **Bug versus flaw**: the distinction between an implementation error in code and a design decision that is wrong however well it is coded.
- **Seven pernicious kingdoms**: a taxonomy of coding errors for developers covering input validation and representation, API abuse, security features, time and state, errors, code quality, encapsulation, and environment, alongside the Nineteen deadly sins of software security.
- **Landwehr taxonomy, the RISOS study, and the Protection Analysis study**: early classifications of program security flaws, by genesis and time and place of introduction, by seven categories of integrity violation, and by patterns amenable to automated search, judged against Bishop's criterion of unambiguous classification.

### Policy, controls, and defensive posture

- **Security policy**: a statement of what is and is not permitted in a system, independent of how it is enforced, with mechanisms and named security services supplying enforcement as arranged by the X.800 security architecture.
- **Control**, also **safeguard**, also **countermeasure**: a measure put in place to reduce risk to an asset, at the cost of its own expense, friction, and maintenance.
- **Preventive, detective, corrective, deterrent, compensating, directive, and recovery controls**: the division of controls by effect, from stopping an action before it takes hold, through revealing it, restoring state, discouraging the actor, substituting for an impractical measure, and instructing people, to returning service.
- **Administrative, technical, and physical controls**: the division of controls by whether they act through procedure, through technology, or through the environment.
- **Mitigation**, also **remediation**: a change reducing the likelihood or impact of a threat, or one removing the underlying weakness outright, at the cost of residual exposure in the first case and engineering breakage in the second.
- **Control baseline**: the minimum set of controls required for a class of system, tailored to actual conditions and modified by community overlays, at the cost of justification work and inconsistency between systems.
- **Hardening**: a system's configuration is tightened against an approved baseline and compared against it over time to catch drift, at the cost of testing effort and broken assumptions in dependent software.
- **Security governance**: the arrangement of authority, accountability, and oversight for security decisions, expressed as mandatory standards and procedures, advisory guidelines, and time-limited exceptions, at the cost of management attention and exceptions that quietly become permanent.
- **Due care**, also **due diligence**: the ordinary protective action expected of an organization and the investigation expected before it relies on a system or partner, measured after harm against a standard of care, at the cost of spending no single case justifies.

### Risk and its arithmetic

- **Risk**: the combination of how likely a harmful event is and how much harm it would do, its level being that combination of likelihood and consequence expressed as a magnitude.
- **Inherent risk and residual risk**: the risk present before any control is applied, and the risk that remains once the chosen controls are in place and working.
- **Risk appetite, tolerance, and capacity**: the risk an organization states it will pursue, the deviation from that it accepts in a specific case, and the maximum loss it could absorb without failing.
- **Risk treatment**: the chosen response to an assessed risk, from accepting it, avoiding the activity, mitigating with controls, transferring the financial consequence, or sharing exposure across parties.
- **Risk assessment**: structured estimation of likelihood and impact for identified risks, compared against criteria set beforehand to decide what needs treatment, at the cost of analyst time and dependence on unreliable inputs.
- **Risk management**: the continuing process of framing, identifying, assessing, treating, and monitoring risk, recorded in a register with a named owner for each entry, at the cost of ongoing effort and registers that go stale.
- **Qualitative, quantitative, and semi-quantitative risk assessment**: rating risk on descriptive scales, in monetary and probabilistic terms, or on numeric bands mapped from descriptions, at the cost of unaddable ratings, heavy data demands, or the weaknesses of both.
- **Risk matrix**, also **heat map**: a grid pairing likelihood bands against impact bands, whose range compression collapses wide spans of real probability into one ordinal cell.
- **Annualized loss expectancy**, also **ALE**: expected yearly monetary loss, obtained from asset value and the exposure factor lost per event to give a single loss expectancy, multiplied by the annualized rate of occurrence.
- **Factor Analysis of Information Risk**, also **FAIR**: a quantitative model decomposing loss event frequency into contact frequency, probability of action, and susceptibility, and comparing threat capability against control resistance strength to reach primary and secondary loss.
- **Monte Carlo simulation**: repeated random sampling from input distributions to produce a distribution of outcomes, drawn as a loss exceedance curve, at the cost of modeling effort and reliance on the chosen distributions.
- **Systemic risk**: exposure arising because many parties depend on the same component, provider, or assumption, so individually acceptable risks aggregate and supposedly independent elements fail together in the tail.
- **CVSS**: a scoring system expressing a vulnerability's intrinsic severity from base metrics, adjusted by temporal metrics for exploit availability and environmental metrics for the affected asset, which is severity rather than one organization's risk.
- **EPSS, CWSS, and CWRAF**: three scoring schemes beyond severity, estimating near-term exploitation probability, ranking weaknesses in code before any vulnerability exists, and weighting those ranks by business consequence in a domain.

### Assurance and evaluation

- **Assurance**: grounds for confidence that a system meets its security claims, obtained from evidence rather than assertion.
- **Assurance case**: a structured argument that a system satisfies a stated claim, written as claim, argument, and evidence or in Goal Structuring Notation, at the cost of substantial documentation effort.
- **Evaluation assurance level**, also **EAL**: a graded measure of how much confidence the evidence supports, built from assurance classes, families, and components, selected as packages and augmented above the chosen rung.
- **Target of evaluation**: the specific product, configuration, and version an evaluation applies to, whose security-enforcing portion is its TOE security functionality and whose external boundary is the TSF interface.
- **Protection profile and security target**: a reusable implementation-independent statement of requirements for a class of product, and the vendor-written claim for one product, each resting on a security problem definition, objectives, and a conformance claim.
- **Trusted Computer System Evaluation Criteria**, also **Orange Book**: the historical rating scheme for operating system security, rising through controlled access protection, labeled security protection, structured protection, security domains, and verified design, with ITSEC scoring functionality and assurance on separate scales.
- **Verification and validation**: checking that a system was built to match its specification, and that specification and system meet the actual need, performed by a party separate from the builder, at the cost of expense and schedule.
- **Formal verification**: mathematical proof that a design or implementation satisfies a formal specification, by model checking a finite model, by theorem proving, or by refinement between levels, at the cost of expertise, state explosion, and the assumptions the proof rests on.
- **Certification**, also **accreditation**, also **authorization to operate**: an independent technical judgment that a system meets stated requirements, followed by a management decision to accept its residual risk, kept current by continuous authorization and assurance continuity, at the cost of results tied to one configuration.
- **Red team, blue team, and purple team**: a group emulating an adversary against a live organization, the defenders being exercised, and the arrangement where both work together, running adversary emulation or assumed breach exercises, at the cost of expense, disruption, and lost realism.
- **Absence of evidence problem**: testing can show that flaws exist but cannot show that none remain.

### The economics of security

- **Security economics**: the study of security failures as consequences of misaligned incentives rather than only of technical error.
- **Externality**: a cost imposed on parties other than the one deciding, so a compromised host harms third parties while network effects reward speed to market over care.
- **Information asymmetry**, also **market for lemons**: one side of a transaction can judge a product's security and the other cannot, so quality commands no premium and falls, and the parties most at risk are the keenest to insure.
- **Moral hazard**, also **principal-agent problem**: a party takes more risk because another bears the consequence, the one acting not being the one that loses.
- **Liability**: legal responsibility for harm, which decides who pays and therefore who invests in prevention, dumped onto the party least able to refuse or assigned to the one best placed to prevent it.
- **Interdependent security**: one party's best investment depends on what others invest, so outcomes turning on the sum of efforts, the weakest link, or the best shot leave protection a public good that invites free riding.
- **Attacker economics**: the study of an adversary's costs, revenues, and alternatives, where defense aims to make an operation uneconomic rather than impossible, and an underground economy rents packaged attack capability to unskilled buyers.
- **Gordon-Loeb model**: an economic model showing that optimal spending on protecting an information set is a fraction of the expected loss, since each further increment buys less reduction than the last.
- **Attacker advantage**, also **defender's dilemma**: the attacker needs one working route at one moment while the defender must cover all of them continuously, which time-based security states as protection needing to outlast detection plus response.
- **Security theater**, also **checkbox compliance**: measures that produce a feeling of security or satisfy the letter of a requirement while leaving the underlying exposure intact.

### People and security decisions

- **Usable security**: the study and design of protection that people can operate correctly under real conditions, given the mental models and folk models they hold and the fact that security is never their primary task.
- **Compliance budget**, also **users are not the enemy**: a user spends a finite amount of effort and goodwill on security before disengaging, and unworkable rules rather than malice produce most rule-breaking, with repeated demands wearing the budget down through fatigue and habituation.
- **Active warning and passive warning**: an interruption blocking the task until a security decision is made, or an indicator shown alongside it, traced through the C-HIP model and written by the NEAT and SPRUCE guidance, at the cost of habituation and false alarms the base rate fallacy makes inevitable.
- **Secure interaction design**: interfaces are built so what users intend and what the system authorizes stay aligned, the easiest path being the safe one and authority granted only by deliberate action and visible while in force, at the cost of constrained interface freedom.
- **Optimism bias, present bias, and the availability heuristic**: recurring distortions in how people judge security risk, rating their own exposure below others', preferring present convenience, and estimating from examples that come to mind, joined by anchoring, framing, loss aversion, and risk compensation.

### Practice models and disclosure

- **Software security maturity model**: a graded description of software security practices, descriptive when drawn from observed practice and prescriptive when stating what to adopt, spanning governance, intelligence, touchpoints, and deployment, or the Secure Software Development Framework's four groups.
- **DevSecOps**: security work is integrated into continuous delivery so it runs at the pace of development, through a paved road, automated guardrails, policy as code, and continuous compliance, at the cost of automation investment and tool noise.
- **Coordinated vulnerability disclosure**, also **vulnerability handling**, **multi-party coordinated disclosure**: a flaw is reported to the party able to fix it and published once a fix exists, unlike full disclosure, non-disclosure, or an embargo on a deadline, and harder when many vendors share the flaw and a coordinator must broker it, at the cost of exposure while the fix is built and leak risk rising with each party informed.
- **Zero-day vulnerability**: a flaw with no fix available when it is exploited or disclosed, an N-day once a fix exists but goes unapplied, and a forever-day where the product is unmaintained, the patch gap measuring the delay, the exposure window covering the period of reachability, patch diffing recovering the flaw from the fix, and vulnerability half-life measuring how fast exposure falls.
- **Vulnerabilities Equities Process**, also **NOBUS**, **exploit stockpiling**: a government's procedure for deciding whether to disclose a flaw it has found or retain it for intelligence use, resting on the claim that nobody but us can exploit it, with retention creating a stockpile that leaks.

## How code is broken

### What memory safety is, and what the language leaves undefined

- **Memory safety**, also **spatial safety**, also **temporal safety**: property holding that a program reads and writes only memory it validly owns, spatial safety being the in-bounds half and temporal safety the still-alive half.
- **Type safety**: property holding that every value is manipulated only through operations its type permits, whose initialization companion holds that no storage is read before a write has given it a determinate value.
- **Undefined behavior**: program condition the language standard imposes no requirement on, licensing any result. Its milder neighbours are unspecified behavior, where the standard permits several results, and implementation-defined behavior, where the choice must be documented.
- **Indeterminate value**, also **trap representation**: content of fresh uninitialized storage, either an unspecified value or a bit pattern encoding no value of the object's type.
- **Object lifetime**, also **storage duration**: interval over which storage is reserved for an object and its address stays valid, classified as static, thread, automatic or allocated.
- **Pointer provenance**, also **provenance**: the allocation a pointer derives from, which fixes the region that pointer may lawfully access.
- **Strict aliasing rule**, also **effective type**: language rule that stored data may be accessed only through a pointer whose type is compatible with the type the declaration or last write established.
- **Misaligned access**, also **alignment requirement**: read or write through an address that is not the multiple of the accessed type's required alignment.
- **Abstract machine**, also **as-if rule**: the idealized executor a language standard describes, which an implementation may reach by any transformation preserving observable behavior.
- **Weird machine**: unintended computational device formed by a program's corrupted state or by input its author never modelled, which an exploit payload programs, and whose reachability is what a judgment of exploitability decides.
- **Memory model**: specification of which written values a read may observe under concurrency, whose vocabulary includes sequential consistency, the happens-before relation and data race freedom.
- **Compiler-removed safety code**: weakness where an optimizer deletes a secret-clearing store as a dead store, or deletes a null or bounds test because the path reaching it was already undefined.
- **C family local defect group**: the language's catalog of small coding faults, covering the unsafe macro that evaluates a parameter twice or leaves it unparenthesized, reserved and shadowed identifiers, restrict qualifier violations, casting away const, incompatible and implicit function declarations, unsequenced modification, evaluation order dependence, precedence errors, omitted switch breaks, and calls with wrong arguments.

### Reading and writing outside the object

- **Buffer overflow**, also **buffer overrun**: write carrying data past the end of the destination buffer into adjacent storage, named by region as stack, heap or global overflow, and mirrored by the underwrite that lands before the buffer's start.
- **Out-of-bounds access**, also **buffer over-read**, also **buffer under-read**: any store to or load from an address outside the bounds of the object its pointer was derived from, a read of which returns adjacent memory to the caller.
- **Off-by-one error**, also **fencepost error**: boundary computation wrong by exactly one element, most often from a loop miscount or a forgotten terminator.
- **Single-byte overflow**: overflow writing exactly one byte past a buffer, enough to alter a length field or a pointer's low byte, and in its poison null byte form writing a zero that clears an adjacent block's in-use flag or size byte.
- **Array index out of bounds**: use of a subscript outside an array's valid range, whether from an unchecked external index, a negative signed index, unverified index arithmetic or wraparound to a small value.
- **Pointer arithmetic error**: computation of a pointer that leaves the object it was derived from, including wrong element scaling, dereferencing the one-past-the-end address, sizing from unrelated pointer differences and comparing pointers into different objects.
- **Incorrect calculation of buffer size**: allocation or copy length derived by faulty arithmetic, covering sizeof taken on a pointer, bounding by the source rather than the destination, and length arguments that do not describe the buffer used.
- **Unbounded copy**: transfer of attacker-controlled length into a fixed-size destination with no limit applied.
- **Stack exhaustion**: consumption of the whole stack region by deep recursion or oversized frames, crashing or corrupting adjacent mappings.
- **Stack clash**: growth of the stack across a guard region into another mapping, letting stack writes reach unrelated memory, driven where automatic allocation is sized from untrusted input and jumps the stack pointer by an attacker-chosen amount.
- **Struct member overflow**, also **intra-object overflow**: write that stays inside an allocation but crosses from one field into another, including flexible array member miscounts and indexing with a count meant for a different element type.
- **Null pointer dereference**: read or write through a pointer whose value is null, which faults or corrupts where low addresses are mapped, its untrusted sibling being a dereference of a pointer value that arrived from outside the trust boundary unvalidated.
- **Uncontrolled recursion**, also **infinite loop**: recursion depth or loop exit condition that attacker input controls, consuming stack or thread without bound, whose abrupt cousin is an assertion the same input can drive to failure and so terminate the process on demand.

### Strings and formatted output

- **String termination error**, also **improper null termination**: character array used as a string with no terminating zero present, so a scan for the terminator runs past the array.
- **Unbounded string copy**: copy or append of a source string with no limit derived from the destination's capacity, the defect behind the gets function and behind input conversions given no field width.
- **String truncation**: silent loss of trailing characters when a bounded copy shortens data, including the bounded copy that fills the destination exactly and stores no terminator, and the unchecked return value that hides it.
- **Character count versus byte count confusion**: length arithmetic mixing element counts with byte counts, which splits multibyte characters and mis-sizes narrow to wide conversions.
- **Embedded null byte**: interior zero byte in supplied data that shortens a string for one layer while later layers still read the full buffer.
- **Unstable returned buffer**: function handing back storage it does not own, the address of an automatic array valid only until the frame is discarded, a shared static buffer a later call overwrites, or a string literal whose storage need not be modifiable.
- **Size-bounded string function**: copy and append routine taking the destination's capacity, whether an Annex K bounds-checking interface that calls a handler on violation or the strlcpy and strlcat pair that always terminate, at the cost of uneven platform support and silent truncation.
- **Managed string type**: length-carrying string abstraction that owns and resizes its storage, whether a library type, a standard string class or a length-prefixed representation, at the cost of allocation overhead and incompatibility with terminator-based interfaces.
- **String immutability**: design keeping string values unmodifiable after creation, removing overflow of shared buffers at the cost of copying on every change.
- **Format string vulnerability**, also **uncontrolled format string**: passing attacker-supplied text as the format argument of a formatting or logging function, whose benign cousin is a format specifier mismatch or missing argument that reads adjacent stack and register contents, and which pays out as a format string information leak, an arbitrary write or a format string denial of service.
- **Format directive primitives**: the exploitation toolkit inside a controlled format, covering stack scraping by repeated directives, reads through a string directive, chosen writes through the count-storing directive, short writes, direct parameter access by number and width or precision padding to set the count.
- **Static format string practice**: requiring every format argument to be a literal so untrusted text can only ever be an argument, checked by a compiler diagnostic that compares directives against argument types, at the cost of losing runtime-built formats and annotating every wrapper.
- **Fortified library functions**: library variants that receive compiler-known destination sizes and validate directive use at runtime, aborting on overflow, with fortified formatting refusing the count directive in a writable format, at a small per-call cost.

### Lifetime, ownership and allocation errors

- **Use-after-free**: access through a pointer to memory already returned to the allocator, its stack-storage siblings being use-after-return and use-after-scope.
- **Dangling pointer**: pointer still holding the address of storage whose lifetime has ended, the dangling view and the invalidated iterator being its container-facing forms, and the wild pointer its never-initialized cousin that held no valid allocation at all.
- **Double free**: second release of the same allocation, corrupting allocator bookkeeping or handing one block to two owners, with double destruction as the object-level equivalent.
- **Invalid free**: release of an address the allocator never returned, such as a stack address, an interior pointer after arithmetic, a block from a different allocator family or module, or an array released through the scalar deletion form.
- **Memory leak**: loss of the last reference to an allocation without releasing it, or retention long past last use, whose common cause is an exception unwinding past the release, and whose mirror image is error cleanup that runs twice and frees on each pass.
- **Uncontrolled memory allocation**: allocation whose size or count untrusted input dictates, letting one request exhaust memory.
- **Allocation call defect**: misuse at the allocation site itself, a size computation that wraps so the block is far smaller than the caller will fill, or a return value used without testing for the failure that exhaustion produces.
- **Reallocation misuse**: use of the original pointer after a resize moved the block, loss of the block when the resize fails, or a zero-length request whose release-or-return behavior differs across implementations.
- **Ownership confusion**: two components each believing they own one allocation, a raw pointer retained after ownership was handed away, or an owner released before the dependents pointing into it, producing a double free, a premature release or cleanup that touches freed memory.
- **Container reallocation hazard**: growth of a container that relocates its elements, silently invalidating every outstanding pointer, reference, iterator and view into it.
- **Placement construction misuse**: constructing an object into storage of insufficient size or wrong alignment, failing to destroy it explicitly, or reading one whose contents a move transferred away and left in a valid but unspecified state.
- **Failure to clear sensitive memory before release**: returning a block that still holds secrets, exposing them through later reuse or a core dump.

### Integer and numeric errors

- **Integer overflow**, also **integer underflow**, also **wraparound**: arithmetic result outside its type's range, undefined for signed types in C and C++ and reduced modulo the range for unsigned ones, which turns a large value small, defeats an upper-bound check, and turns a bounded loop counter unbounded.
- **Integer truncation**: assignment of a wider value into a narrower type, discarding high-order bits, the general case being any narrowing or otherwise lossy conversion.
- **Sign conversion error**: conversion, promotion or comparison that changes a value's signed interpretation, covering sign extension of a negative byte into a huge quantity, zero extension of the same byte, a sign change error where a negative value becomes an enormous unsigned one, and the integer promotions and usual arithmetic conversions that make a signed-unsigned comparison pass.
- **Undefined arithmetic operation**: operation the standard leaves without a result, covering negation or absolute value of the most negative number, division by zero, modulo by zero, division overflow, and shifts by a negative or excessive count, left shift overflow and right shift of a negative value.
- **Negative length**: length or count below zero that passes a signed check and becomes an enormous size when converted, the usual cause being a signed type where the platform's size type was required.
- **Size arithmetic overflow**: element count times element size, or header plus payload, wrapping so a small allocation receives a large write, the chain from integer overflow to buffer overflow.
- **Pointer and integer conversion loss**: storing a pointer in an integer type too narrow to hold it, or building a pointer from integer arithmetic so it carries no valid provenance.
- **Declared type range violation**: reliance on the implementation's signedness choice for plain character types and bit-fields, or storage of a value outside an enumeration's defined set that is then used as a table index.
- **Floating point defect family**: the numeric group covering precision loss, exact equality comparison of computed values, conversion outside an integer type's range, ordered comparison against a not-a-number value, domain and range errors, rounding mode dependence and subnormal handling.
- **Incorrect bound check**: range test written with the wrong relational operator, the wrong limit or the wrong operands, admitting out-of-range values.
- **Checked arithmetic**: precondition testing of operand ranges before an operation or postcondition testing of the result after it, by hand, through a compiler intrinsic returning an overflow flag, or by upcasting into a wider type before narrowing, at the cost of extra branches and restructured expressions.
- **Overflow policy selection**: stating explicitly what an out-of-range result does, saturating arithmetic clamping at the type's limits, wrapping arithmetic reducing modularly, or a trap on overflow raising a fault, at the cost of silently wrong magnitudes, hidden genuine overflows, or aborts on benign wraparound.
- **Safe integer library**: type wrapping every operation with automatic range enforcement, or arbitrary-precision representation that grows with the value, at the cost of interface intrusion and runtime overhead.
- **Two's complement representation**: encoding of signed integers in which the high bit carries negative weight, now mandated by C and C++.

### Uninitialized memory and type confusion

- **Uninitialized memory read**: use of an object's value before any write has given it determinate content, whose worst form is an uninitialized pointer dereference and whose usual cause is a result variable left unwritten on a failed path.
- **Uninitialized memory disclosure**: outward copying of storage never written, covering structure padding gaps, freshly allocated blocks, partly filled stack buffers, leftover register contents, core dumps and memory paged to swap or hibernation.
- **Type confusion**, also **object type confusion**: treating memory holding one type as though it held another incompatible type, whether by bad downcast, reading the wrong union member, punning through an incompatible pointer or acting on a mismatched variant tag.
- **Object reuse type confusion**: freeing an object and reclaiming its storage as a different type, so one type's fields reinterpret another's.
- **Object lifetime confusion**: disagreement between components about whether an object is alive, covering use after teardown has begun and virtual dispatch through an unset or destroyed table pointer.
- **Signature confusion**: calling a function through a pointer of a different prototype, mismatching arguments and return handling.
- **Layout assumption error**: code depending on a structure layout that differs between modules or compilations, so fields are read at wrong offsets.
- **Initialization and erasure hardening**: the group of build and library measures that remove residue, covering compiler patterning of uninitialized locals, allocator zeroing on allocation or release, whole-structure initialization to cover padding, stack clearing on return, and non-elidable secret erasure, at costs from redundant writes to touching every returned page.

### Races over memory and over files

- **Race condition**, also **data race**: defect whose manifestation depends on the timing of concurrent operations, the memory-level form being two conflicting unsynchronized accesses to one location, which the language leaves undefined even where an author judges it benign.
- **Atomicity violation**, also **order violation**: interleaving that splits a sequence the programmer assumed indivisible, or reverses one assumed impossible, the common shapes being check-then-act and a lost update from a nonatomic read-modify-write.
- **Time-of-check to time-of-use**, also **TOCTOU**: window between validating a resource and using it during which the resource can be substituted, its privileged form being a double fetch where a length changes between its check and the copy it bounds.
- **Signal handler race**: interruption of a computation by a handler touching the same state, with no locking possible, whose named forms include reentrancy into async-signal-unsafe routines, handler use-after-free and clobbered error indicators.
- **Reference count race**: nonatomic or overflowing count update that loses an increment, wraps, or drops below zero, releasing an object while references remain.
- **Torn access**: read or write of a multiword value observed midway by another thread, including independent writes to adjacent narrow fields that share one machine word.
- **Locking discipline failure**: the defect family covering a missing lock, inconsistent lock choice for one datum, a lock released too early, double unlock, release by a non-owner, recursive acquisition of a nonrecursive lock, and acquisition of two locks in opposite orders.
- **Deadlock**: state where each of a set of threads waits on a resource another holds, so none proceeds, its weaker relatives being livelock, starvation and priority inversion, and its usual cause a circular wait from inverted lock order.
- **Memory ordering misuse**: reliance on the volatile qualifier for atomicity, an omitted fence, a too-weak atomic ordering, compiler reordering of unsynchronized accesses, or an ABA sequence where a value returns to its original so a compare-and-swap succeeds on stale state.
- **Unsafe publication**: making an object visible to other threads before its construction completes, as in double-checked locking, or racing on a lazily initialized object, or reusing from several threads a routine that keeps internal static state.
- **Read-side critical section escape**: retaining a reference obtained under a deferred-reclamation read section past its end, so reclamation frees it underneath.
- **Fork in a multithreaded process**: forking while other threads hold locks, so the child inherits locks no thread exists to release, with asynchronous cancellation of a thread mid-update leaving the same wreckage.
- **Synchronization primitive**: the applied means of ordering concurrent access, an indivisible read-modify-write for single locations and a mutual exclusion lock for larger regions, at the cost of contention, cache traffic, latency and deadlock risk.
- **Sharing avoidance**: removing races by never sharing mutable state, whether through values immutable after construction, per-thread copies, or a single fetch of untrusted data into private storage before validating it, at the cost of copying on update and memory per thread.
- **Safe reclamation scheme**: deferring frees until no reader can hold a reference, by hazard pointers, epochs, grace periods or retried version-counter snapshots, at the cost of memory retained while any thread stalls and retries under write pressure.
- **Symbolic link race**, also **symlink attack**: replacement of a path component with a link between a program's check and its open, redirecting the operation, with hard links and Windows junctions or shortcuts doing the same through other name types.
- **Check-then-open race**: testing a file's properties by name, or checking access rights by path rather than on the opened descriptor, so the name can be rebound in between, the archetype being a predictable temporary name created in a world-writable directory.
- **File interface misuse**: the input and output defect family covering ignored error returns, short reads and writes treated as complete, descriptor leaks and exhaustion, stream update mode errors, text and binary mode confusion, umask dependence, attacker-named devices and reuse without truncation.
- **Exclusive creation**: opening with a flag that fails if the path exists, inside a directory only the owner may write, closing the pre-creation window at the cost of retry logic, setup and cleanup.
- **Descriptor-based operations**: performing checks, changes and path resolution through held descriptors rather than rebuilt name strings, and refusing opens whose final component is a link, at the cost of a lower-level, less portable interface that breaks legitimate link use.

### Turning a memory error into control of the heap

- **Chunk header corruption**: overwrite of the size, flag or trailing size fields the allocator stores beside each block, so it misjudges a block's extent, state or backward neighbour.
- **Freelist corruption**: overwrite of the forward or backward links in a free block so the allocator hands out an attacker-chosen address, whose classic doubly linked form is the unlink attack.
- **Overlapping chunks**: allocator state in which two live blocks share storage, reached by extending or shrinking a recorded size so memory is handed out twice.
- **Fake chunk**: attacker-built block, or whole arena control structure, with valid-looking header and links, placed in writable memory for the allocator to adopt.
- **Named heap-exploitation technique**: the literature's catalog of allocator-specific recipes, the House family from Force, Spirit, Lore, Mind, Prime, Orange, Einherjar, Botcake, Husk, Water, Tangerine, Roman, Storm, Gods, Io, Corrosion and Rust, alongside fastbin duplication, unsorted and large bin attacks, lookaside list attacks and first-fit reuse.
- **Cache poisoning attack**, also **tcache poisoning**: corruption of a per-thread allocation cache, its next pointers, counts, membership markers or management structure, so the next request of a size class returns an attacker-chosen address.
- **Arbitrary free**: inducing the program to release an address the attacker chose, placing allocator-controlled data anywhere.
- **Heap grooming**, also **heap massaging**, also **heap feng shui**: shaping the sequence of allocations and releases, filling holes and defragmenting, so a later allocation lands where the attacker needs it, guided by an allocation oracle that reveals layout without reading memory.
- **Heap spraying**: filling the heap with many copies of chosen data or objects so an uncontrolled pointer is likely to land inside attacker content, its code-generating variant feeding a just-in-time compiler input whose emitted bytes decode as a payload at an unaligned entry.
- **Cross-cache attack**: forcing a freed page to migrate between allocator caches so a block of one type is reissued as another.
- **Use-after-free reclamation**: filling a freed slot with attacker data before the stale pointer is dereferenced.

### Seizing control flow from corrupted memory

- **Pointer subterfuge**: umbrella technique of corrupting a stored pointer so the program's own dereference of it serves the attacker, whether function pointer clobbering, data pointer modification aimed at a later write, or corruption of a saved context.
- **Stack smashing**: overflowing an automatic buffer to overwrite frame control data, the saved return address and saved frame pointer being the targets that seize the function's return and the caller's frame.
- **Code pointer target catalog**: the writable structures whose corruption yields a transfer, covering virtual function table and virtual pointer overwrite, the fake vtable, global offset table overwrite, procedure linkage table hijacking, destructor and exit handler lists, structured and vectored exception handler overwrite, jump buffers, allocator hooks, stream vtables in file stream oriented programming, link maps and callbacks.
- **Shellcode**: short machine-code payload injected into a process, classically one that starts a command interpreter, delivered with alphanumeric encoding, decoder stubs, NOP sleds, egg hunters and markers, staged second halves and stable environment placement to satisfy the input path's bad character constraints.
- **Return-to-libc**, also **ret2libc**: redirecting a return to an existing library function with attacker-arranged arguments, requiring no injected code, with ret2plt, ret2text, ret2syscall, ret2dl-resolve and ret2csu naming the other existing-code destinations.
- **Gadget in code reuse**: short instruction sequence ending in a control transfer, reused as one step of a chained payload, inventoried into a catalog before a chain is built.
- **Gadget chain in code reuse**: ordered sequence of gadget addresses and data placed on a controlled stack so each transfer feeds the next.
- **Return-oriented programming**: computation built entirely from existing return-terminated sequences driven by a controlled stack, reaching Turing completeness with a sufficient gadget set, and driven through a formatting function in its printf-oriented variant.
- **Gadget role vocabulary**: the named parts of a chain, covering stack pivots and lifting, pop-return and pop-pop-return sequences, register-loading and memory-write gadgets, syscall and trampoline gadgets, the one-shot interpreter launcher, the setcontext register loader, dispatchers and frame faking.
- **Jump-oriented programming**: code reuse through sequences ending in indirect jumps driven by a dispatcher, with call-oriented programming as the indirect-call variant, sigreturn-oriented programming restoring every register from a forged signal context, and counterfeit object-oriented programming chaining real virtual methods through forged objects.
- **Data-oriented programming**: exploitation that alters only data, chaining sequences or whole basic blocks the program's own control flow already reaches, from a single privilege flag in a non-control-data attack to bending execution along paths a coarse policy permits.
- **Gadget discovery under secrecy**: finding usable sequences when the binary or its layout is unknown, by inferring them from a restarting service's crash and hang behavior against a stop gadget, or by reading code pages through a disclosure at exploitation time.
- **Exploit primitive**: reliable capability extracted from a bug, arbitrary, relative or limited in its write or read, plus controlled indexing and reference count manipulation, its strongest form giving control of both the value written and its destination address.
- **Information leak primitive**, also **infoleak**: capability to obtain process memory that was never meant to leave the process, its prizes being library bases, stack and heap addresses, kernel pointers and stack canaries, and its weakest form a crash or timing oracle that returns no data.
- **Pointer forging**: construction of a pointer value, or a whole fake object shaped to satisfy the program's expectations, in attacker data the program later trusts, needing only the low bytes where a partial overwrite inherits the unknown high ones.
- **Exploit reliability**: proportion of attempts against a target that succeed without crashing it, its companions being stability, continuation of execution after the payload runs, and dependence on a second weakness paired with the first.
- **Exploit mitigation bypass**: technique whose purpose is defeating a deployed defense rather than the original bug, the named instances including Offset2lib, Control Jujutsu and its argument-corruptible call sites, speculative probing, PACMAN, thread spraying, canary brute force, tag guessing and brute-force address search.

### Memory exploitation inside the kernel

- **Kernel memory corruption**: memory error in privileged code, whose exploitation yields the highest privilege on the machine directly, its own defect shapes being slab cache overflow, kernel stack overflow, page-level use-after-free and refcount overflow.
- **Return-to-user**, also **ret2usr**: redirecting kernel control flow into attacker-prepared code in user address space, with ret2dir reaching the same page through its kernel alias to evade address checks.
- **Kernel heap grooming**: allocating and releasing kernel objects to place a target structure beside a controlled one, using elastic objects, message queues, pipe buffers, socket buffers, extended attributes and key structures as the sprayable material.
- **Credential overwrite**: corruption of a process credential structure, or reclamation of a freed one, so the running task acquires privileged identity.
- **Page table corruption**: obtaining a write into page table entries so arbitrary physical memory becomes mapped and writable, the related move being to make kernel structures reachable through a user mapping.
- **Privileged path overwrite**: writing a kernel string that names a program run with full privilege on a triggering event, such as a module load or a crash handler.
- **Kernel memory disclosure**: leak of kernel contents or addresses to unprivileged code through partly initialized structures copied out, copy-out length confusion, logs, statistics or timing, which undoes kernel layout secrecy.
- **Copy-on-write race**: race between fault handling and mapping teardown that lets a private write reach shared or read-only backing pages.

### Hardening the stack, the allocator and the layout

- **Stack canary**, also **stack cookie**: known value placed between locals and frame control data and verified before return, as a terminator canary, a random canary or an XOR canary combined with the return address, with selective insertion, stack variable reordering and copying arguments to safe locations alongside it, at per-function cost and a secret a disclosure can reveal.
- **Shadow stack**: separate protected region holding only return addresses and compared on return, as software return address copy checking, a hardware shadow stack or a guarded control stack, alongside the safe stack that keeps address-taken buffers off the frame entirely, at memory, instruction and toolchain cost.
- **Guard page**: unmapped page adjoining a region so sequential overruns fault rather than reach the neighbour, extended below the stack as a guard gap and enforced by compiler-emitted stack probing, at address space cost and page granularity.
- **Allocator integrity check**: validation of allocator invariants during use, covering safe unlinking, size and flag consistency, header cookies, cached double-free markers, free-list pointers obfuscated by safe linking, and bookkeeping held out of line away from user data, at cost per operation, per block and in locality.
- **Delayed reuse**, also **quarantine**: withholding freed blocks from reallocation, in the limit never reusing a virtual address, so stale pointers hit unused memory, at the cost of memory and address space retained.
- **Allocator randomization**: randomizing which free block a request receives and where allocations land, as freelist randomization and allocation address randomization, so adjacency cannot be predicted, at the cost of locality, fragmentation and throughput.
- **Heap isolation**: dedicating separate regions per object type, component or size class, as type-isolated heaps and size-class isolation, so reclaimed memory cannot change type and overflows reach only same-sized neighbours, at the cost of memory overhead and fragmentation.
- **Guarded allocation**: giving allocations their own page with unmapped neighbours, for every block in an electric fence allocator or for a sampled fraction in production as the kernel electric fence does, with poisoning on release, at memory and per-release cost.
- **Hardened memory allocator**: allocator designed for exploit resistance rather than peak speed, including hardened allocator flag sets, segment heap designs and terminating on heap corruption when invariants fail, at throughput, memory and availability cost.
- **Data execution prevention**, also **no-execute protection**, also **W^X**: marking data pages unexecutable, giving a process a non-executable stack and heap and never a page both writable and executable, and in the strict form making code executable but unreadable, at the cost of permission flipping in code generators and inline data in code sections.
- **Read-only after initialization**: sealing data pages, dynamic linkage tables and kernel text once startup completes, which requires eager symbol binding, and barring privileged code from user mappings outside explicit windows, at the cost of slower startup, hardware support and no later patching.
- **Address space layout randomization**: randomizing the load addresses of regions so absolute addresses in a payload cannot be predicted, requiring position-independent executables and code, at the cost of limited entropy and total sensitivity to one disclosure.
- **Fine-grained code randomization**: permuting code at function, basic block or instruction level, substituting equivalent encodings in place, shipping structurally different builds, or relocating regions while running so a leaked address expires, at performance, memory, build and pointer-tracking cost.
- **Randomization entropy**: number of independent bits in a layout choice, which bounds how quickly guessing can succeed, which a process that aborts on a failed guess converts into a per-attempt cost, and on which any secret-located metadata region wholly depends.
- **Just-in-time compiler hardening**: constraints on generated code, covering separate writable and executable mappings, constant blinding, random instruction padding, non-executable heaps and per-process instruction encoding, at throughput, address space and code size cost.
- **Platform exploit mitigation**: the vendor-named mitigation family, covering SafeSEH and SEHOP for exception chains, arbitrary code and code integrity guards, export and import address filtering, bottom-up and high-entropy randomization and stack pivot detection, each at compatibility and per-check cost.

### Constraining transfers, pointers and accesses

- **Control-flow integrity**: enforcement that every indirect transfer goes to a target the program's own control-flow graph permits, split into forward edge for calls and jumps and backward edge for returns, at instrumentation cost and a precision that depends on address-taken analysis.
- **Control-flow policy precision**: the grain at which permitted targets are computed, from coarse sets that leave room for chains, through fine-grained per-site sets, to context-sensitive policies, measured by the equivalence class each site allows.
- **Indirect branch tracking**: hardware requirement that every indirect transfer land on a designated landing pad instruction, shipped as branch target identification and as the processor feature set pairing it with a shadow stack, at recompilation and code size cost.
- **Call target validation**: compiler and loader schemes checking an indirect call against a bitmap of valid entries, optionally matching a type signature, verifying returns against a shadow region, and confirming a virtual call's table pointer belongs to the object's hierarchy, at per-call cost and runtime type information.
- **Code-pointer integrity**: policy protecting all code pointers in a separate safe region rather than checking transfers, with a cheaper separation variant that protects only the pointers themselves, at partitioning cost and weaker guarantees.
- **Pointer authentication**: computing a keyed signature over a pointer and its signing modifier, stored in unused bits and verified before use, applied to return addresses and indirect calls with diversified keys, at instruction, bit budget and key management cost.
- **Signing oracle**: attacker-usable code path that signs chosen values, undermining pointer authentication without the key.
- **Read-only dispatch table**: placement of jump and function pointer tables in unwritable pages so corruption cannot redirect them, at the cost of losing runtime registration and patching.
- **Pointer encryption**, also **pointer guard**: storing a sensitive pointer combined with a secret or with its own location so a plain overwrite yields an unusable address, at encode and decode cost per use.
- **Pointer nulling on release**: assigning null to a pointer immediately after its release so later use faults instead of reusing memory, at the cost of missing every other alias.
- **Memory tagging**: association of a small tag with each memory granule and each pointer, checked on access, refreshed per allocation, faulting synchronously or asynchronously, extended to stack slots, and housed in pointer bits the translation ignores, at memory cost and detection limited by tag width.
- **Hardware capability**: unforgeable reference carrying its own bounds, permissions and validity tag, narrowable but never wideable, sealable, and revocable by sweep, at doubled pointer size, porting and scan cost.
- **Fat pointer**: pointer representation carrying bounds alongside the address, whose alternatives keep pointer size by holding bounds in registers, in a side table, in shadow memory, or implicitly through region layout and power-of-two padding, at size, lookup, cache and allocator constraint cost.
- **Memory protection keys**: page tagging that lets a thread cheaply change access rights for a whole key group, at the cost of very few available keys.

### Bounded languages, ownership and error detection

- **Bounds checking**: verification that each access lies inside the target object's extent, mandated by some language definitions, guided by size and element-count annotations, graduated by library hardening modes, and reduced by compiler elimination of provable checks, at a runtime cost per access and annotation burden.
- **Safe C dialect**: variant or instrumentation of C adding checked pointers and enforced bounds while keeping the syntax, the named efforts being Cyclone, CCured, Deputy, Checked C, Fail-Safe C, SoftBound and CETS, at porting, annotation and performance cost.
- **Coding standard conformance**: adoption of a rule set restricting a language to well-defined analyzable constructs, as MISRA C, the bounds and lifetime profiles, or SPARK for Ada with its proofs and subprogram contracts, at the cost of expressiveness, tooling, proof labor and deviation records.
- **Automatic reclamation**: freeing memory without an explicit release, by tracing from roots, by reference counting, conservatively over unmodified C, or in bulk through arenas and regions, removing dangling pointers and double frees at the cost of pauses, footprint, retained garbage and uncollected cycles.
- **Scope-bound resource management**, also **resource acquisition is initialization**: binding a resource's lifetime to an owning object so release happens at scope end, expressed through unique, shared and weak smart pointers, at the cost of destructor-based design, indirection and atomic update.
- **Ownership**: discipline in which each value has exactly one owning binding responsible for releasing it at scope end, transferred by move, divisible by partial move, and pinnable where the value must not relocate, at the cost of restructuring shared access.
- **Borrowing**: temporary access to a value without taking ownership, as a shared read-only reference or an exclusive mutable one, nestable by reborrow, checked so no reference outlives its owner, at expressiveness cost.
