- **Borrow checker**: compile-time enforcer of borrowing and lifetime rules, reasoning over written or elided lifetime relations narrowed to last use, removing dangling references at the cost of rejecting some sound programs.
- **Aliasing rule**: requirement that mutation and aliasing never coexist for one value, whose operational model organizes a location's outstanding borrows as a stack or a tree and thereby yields data race freedom by construction.
- **Drop**: automatic release of a value's resources when its owner leaves scope, in a defined order, checked so release cannot observe a dead borrow, at the cost of implicit ordered side effects.
- **Unsafe block**: region where the compiler permits operations it cannot verify, shifting the obligation to the author through unsafe functions, traits and written safety contracts, and confined behind safe abstractions whose failure to hold is unsoundness, at design and review cost.
- **Interior mutability**: mutation through a shared reference mediated by a wrapper that upholds exclusion statically or by runtime borrow check, at check cost and panics on violation.
- **Thread safety marker**: type property stating that a value may be moved to another thread or referenced from several at once, checked at compile time so unsynchronized sharing never typechecks.
- **Panic discipline**: the failure-path vocabulary of a checked language, covering lock poisoning, panic safety obligations on unsafe code, aborting instead of unwinding, and the undefined behavior of a panic or foreign exception crossing a foreign calling convention.
- **Raw pointer**: unchecked pointer type whose dereference is confined to unsafe code, held to a strict provenance discipline that forbids deriving pointers from integers, at interface cost and the loss of every checked guarantee.
- **Foreign interface boundary**: the point where a safe language calls unchecked code, where every safety guarantee must be reestablished by hand and where a panic or foreign exception crossing the calling convention is undefined.
- **Managed runtime safety**: the guarantees a hosted runtime enforces, covering bytecode verification, array store checks, mandated index validation, absence-typed optionals and compiler-inserted reference counting, at verification, per-access and cycle-leak cost, with an explicit escape hatch that reintroduces every classic error.
- **Exception safety**: the classification of what a function promises about state when it exits by throwing, as the basic, strong or no-throw guarantee, whose violations are throwing destructors, noexcept breaches, slicing catches and swallowed exceptions.
- **Unchecked return value**: ignoring the status a function returns and continuing as though the operation succeeded, whose disciplined counterpart clears and reads the error indicator only where documented, at boilerplate cost per call site.
- **Reference cycle leak**: retention of memory by counted references pointing at one another, unreachable yet never released, which is memory-safe and permanent.

### Finding memory errors before an attacker does

- **Sanitizer as instrumentation**: compiler-inserted runtime detector halting a program at a defined error class with a diagnostic, the family being address sanitizer, memory sanitizer, leak sanitizer, thread sanitizer and undefined behavior sanitizer, with kernel address, kernel memory and kernel concurrency builds and coverage instrumentation, at heavy runtime and memory cost.
- **Redzone**: poisoned padding placed around allocations, between struct members and over returned frames so intra-object, container and stale-frame overflows land in memory the detector marks invalid, and in-code assertions that abort on a violated invariant, at memory and runtime cost.
- **Shadow byte encoding**: compact per-granule record of which bytes are addressable or, in a definedness shadow, initialized, propagated through computation and traceable by origin tracking to the missing write, consulted before each access, at heavy cost that tag-based variants reduce by admitting collisions.
- **Happens-before race detection**: runtime detector tracking vector clocks to decide whether two accesses are ordered, with lockset analysis and lock-order variants that also report deadlock at runtime, at large slowdown and false reports on other synchronization disciplines.
- **Debug allocator**: allocator replacement adding memory fill patterns, guard bytes and free-list validation, in the limit giving every allocation its own guarded page as a page heap, at memory and throughput cost that keeps it out of production.
- **Dynamic binary instrumentation**: rewriting a program's instructions as it runs to insert checks or maintain a shadow value beside every application value, without recompiling, at order-of-magnitude slowdown.
- **Fuzzing**: automated testing feeding a program generated input to provoke crashes and detector reports, as blind fuzzing, mutation-based fuzzing, generation-based fuzzing, and grammar-aware or structure-aware fuzzing driven by a fuzz target over a decoded format, at compute and triage cost.
- **Coverage-guided fuzzing**: input generation steered by the edge coverage or basic block coverage each input reaches, recorded in a collision-prone coverage bitmap with hit count bucketing, at instrumentation cost and a coverage plateau where new inputs stop reaching anything new.
- **Fuzzing campaign vocabulary**: the machinery around a run, covering the fuzz harness and driver generation, seed selection, corpus and test case minimization, seed trimming, mutation operators from bit flips and arithmetic mutation through interesting value substitution, splicing and havoc, dictionaries from automatic token extraction and comparison logging, power and seed scheduling, bug oracles, crash triage and continuous fuzzing.
- **Hybrid fuzzing**: combination of mutation with constraint solving or taint tracking to pass checks random mutation cannot, using input-to-state correspondence and colorization to locate a compared value and checksum neutering to remove the guard entirely, at solver and tracking cost.
- **Fuzzing variant catalog**: the named ways a campaign is driven, in-process and snapshot fuzzing for throughput, emulation-based fuzzing for opaque binaries, kernel syscall and stateful protocol fuzzing for sequenced interfaces, and differential, directed, taint-guided and sanitizer-guided fuzzing for a chosen oracle or target, at setup, fidelity and per-target cost.
- **Symbolic execution**: analysis running a program on symbolic inputs and collecting a path condition per path, solved for witnesses by a satisfiability engine and alternated with concrete runs in concolic testing, at path explosion and solver cost.
- **Abstract interpretation**: analysis computing over abstract domains such as numeric intervals to prove properties for all inputs, resting on a points-to or alias analysis of which objects each pointer may reach, at the cost of imprecision and tuning.
- **Verification of memory properties**: proving a defect class absent by model checking, bounded model checking, deductive proof against written specifications, separation logic or shape analysis, at specification, solver and state-explosion cost.
- **Analysis precision vocabulary**: the terms describing what an analysis promises, soundness reporting every real defect, completeness reporting only real ones, and the false positives and negatives measuring each failure.
- **Type-based memory discipline**: type systems carrying safety facts, covering refinement and dependent types for index safety, linear, affine and uniqueness types against reuse after transfer, and type-state and lifetime inference for allocation protocols, at proof burden cost.

### Untrusted input and the trust boundary

- **Untrusted input**, also **taint**: any data whose content a party outside the program's control influences, taint being the property that a value derives from such data.
- **Improper input validation**: acting on input without confirming the properties the logic assumes, named by what went unchecked, the declared type, a quantity, an index, syntactic correctness, internal consistency, or unsafe equivalence to a rejected value.
- **Improper neutralization**: passing data into another language or protocol without rendering its special elements inert, which the weakness taxonomy cuts one element at a time, by delimiters for parameters, values, records, lines, sections and expressions, input leaders and terminators, quotes, escapes, comments, macro and substitution symbols, and special characters leading, trailing, internal, missing, extra or inconsistent.
- **Downstream interpreter assumption**: weakness class where a component emits text safely for itself while a later consumer parses that same text as syntax.
- **Language-theoretic security**, also **LangSec**: the position that input handling is a recognition problem, so an input language's class, regular, context-free or context-sensitive, fixes whether a recognizer can accept it fully before processing, undecidable for a Turing-complete format, and whose rule is parse, don't validate rather than scatter checks through processing as a shotgun parser does, requiring recognizer equivalence and declarative constraint validation.
- **Filter evasion**: expressing a payload in a form a blocking filter fails to recognize, by case variation, whitespace substitution, keyword splitting, hexadecimal literals, character functions, or any spelling the denylist's author never wrote.
- **Second-order injection**, also **stored injection**: payload stored harmlessly on one request and parsed as code later when another component reads it back, which single-request analysis cannot see.

### Injecting into query and directory grammars

- **SQL injection**, also **SQLi**: attack placing attacker-controlled text into a SQL statement so the database parses it as query syntax rather than data, which lives wherever dynamic SQL assembles query text as a string at runtime, and reaches the parser through a tautology, an inline comment or a broken-out literal.
- **Blind SQL injection**: injection whose results never reach the response, inferred one answer at a time by substring or bitwise extraction from a boolean true or false page, a conditional delay or heavy query, a forced error, or an out-of-band request the database itself makes.
- **Union-based injection**, also **UNION SELECT injection**: injection grafting an attacker-chosen result set onto the original query output, prepared by enumerating column count and text-accepting types and used to read the catalog and fingerprint the product.
- **SQL injection context**: the statement position an injection lands in, quoted string, unquoted numeric, sort clause, row limit, value list, assignment, identifier or stored-procedure body, which decides whether placeholders can cover it and which syntax reaches the parser.
- **Stacked queries**, also **batched queries**: injection appending a statement separator so the driver executes a second attacker-written statement after the original, or piggybacks it on the same round trip.
- **Database host escalation**: injection reaching beyond query semantics through file-loading and export statements, command-executing extended procedures such as xp_cmdshell, user-defined functions, or hostname lookups that exfiltrate values to a controlled name server.
- **NoSQL injection**: attack supplying input a non-relational store interprets as structure, by operator injection where a literal was expected, parameter type confusion supplying an array or object for a scalar, a broken-out string literal, or server-side JavaScript in a query or map-reduce function, inferred blindly where no data returns.
- **Query dialect injection**: the same string-assembly defect in every other query language, HQL, JPQL, Cypher, SPARQL, CQL, Lucene and Solr local parameters, search filters, ORM raw fragments, line terminators that add commands to the Redis or Memcached wire protocol, and JSON injection where a value breaks out of a document built by concatenation.
- **GraphQL abuse**: manipulation of a GraphQL document to reach fields and back ends beyond the intended interface, or to multiply server work through introspection, batching, aliased repetition, cyclic depth and fragment cycles.
- **LDAP injection**: attack inserting directory syntax into a request, changing which entries a search filter matches, redirecting a distinguished name to another subtree, widening a filter with a wildcard, adding attribute assertions, or asserting a tautology to bypass a login, and inferred blindly where no results are shown.
- **XPath injection**: attack inserting node-selection syntax so a query selects nodes the application never intended, inferred blindly one predicate at a time and extended by XQuery function calls, XSLT stylesheet control, and document-loading or extension functions that read files and contact hosts.

### Injecting into commands, code and templates

- **OS command injection**, also **command injection**, also **shell injection**: attack whose input becomes part of a command string handed to a shell, reaching syntax through shell metacharacters, semicolons, ampersands, pipes, newlines, command substitution, or filename expansion that turns planted files into arguments.
- **Argument injection**: attack that cannot add a command but controls arguments to the intended one, supplying values beginning with a dash so they read as switches, reaching archive tool and interpreter options, or exploiting quoting gaps between a runtime and the program that re-splits its command line.
- **Environment and search path injection**: influencing a process's environment or resolution order rather than its arguments, covering Shellshock, field separator manipulation, LD_PRELOAD and redirected library paths, DLL search order hijacking and side-loading, unquoted service paths, and writable directories early in the executable path.
- **Code injection**: attack supplying text the target parses and runs as source in its own language, through eval injection into an evaluation function, unsafe reflection over attacker-named classes and methods, class loaders, dynamic imports, configuration and connection strings, or a file stored where the server will interpret it.
- **Expression language injection**, also **EL injection**: injecting into a small embedded expression language a framework evaluates, named by dialect as OGNL, SpEL and MVEL, and reaching JNDI injection where a naming lookup fetches a remote class, the weakness Log4Shell made famous by expanding lookup syntax inside logged text.
- **Server-side template injection**, also **SSTI**: attack whose input becomes part of a template's source rather than a value passed to it, landing inside an evaluated expression or in literal text where it opens its own delimiters, then escaping the sandbox by walking attributes, subclasses, prototypes, leaked contexts or built-in loaders.

### Injecting into records, documents and object streams

- **CRLF injection**: inserting carriage return and line feed into data a line-oriented protocol treats as a record boundary, which becomes HTTP header injection and response splitting, email header injection, and SMTP, IMAP, FTP and syslog command injection in fields the application forwards, with SMTP smuggling where two mail hops disagree on the end-of-data marker.
- **Host header injection**: attack supplying an unexpected request host so the application builds links, tokens or routing around an attacker's domain, the archetype being a poisoned password reset link, with injected cookies and download filename parameters as its siblings.
- **Log injection**: attack whose input reaches a log as structure rather than content, log forging that writes whole entries, log spoofing that attributes text to another user, splitting records at an aggregator's delimiters, rewriting an operator's terminal through escape sequences, or flooding rotation until the evidence is discarded.
- **CSV injection**, also **formula injection**: attack storing a value that begins with a formula character so a spreadsheet evaluates it when the export is opened, in the worst case reaching the office application's dynamic data exchange feature and the commands it launches.
- **XML injection**: attack inserting markup into a value concatenated into a document, closing elements early, adding attributes, breaking out of a CDATA section, prepending a document type declaration, or exploiting namespace, identifier and signature-reference disagreements to relocate signed content past a verifier, with schema poisoning and schema location hijacking redirecting the rules a validator applies.
- **XML external entity injection**, also **XXE**: attack declaring an entity whose replacement text the parser fetches from a file or network location, returned in band, out of band or inside an error, reached through a parameter entity in an external DTD, and kin to server-side includes, edge side includes and XInclude directives a server, cache or parser expands on the attacker's behalf.
- **XML entity expansion**, also **XML bomb**: denial of service where nested or repeated entity definitions expand to an enormous document, exponentially as billion laughs, linearly as quadratic blowup, or endlessly through recursion or an entity aimed at a blocking source.
- **Insecure deserialization**, also **deserialization of untrusted data**, also **object injection**: reconstruction of program objects from attacker-supplied bytes, so the stream decides which types exist while magic methods, custom read hooks, finalizers and rehashing callbacks run before any application code sees the object, and a nested collection graph can exhaust memory on its own.
- **Gadget in deserialization**: a class already present on the target's classpath whose ordinary behaviour becomes useful to an attacker once its serialized state is chosen, the recurring kinds being transformer chains, dynamic proxies, reflection and bytecode-loading classes, found by call-graph search from a deserialization entry point to a dangerous call.
- **Gadget chain in deserialization**, also **property-oriented programming**: ordered classes arranged so deserializing one triggers the next until a dangerous operation runs, from a kick-off gadget whose callback fires automatically to the sink gadget that executes, universal where built only from a runtime's own standard library.
- **Deserialization by format**: the same weakness named per serializer, covering pickle opcodes and reduce hooks, YAML type tags, Ruby marshal, code-carrying marshalling, polymorphic binding driven by a type discriminator, session and view state blobs, and archive metadata a path operation revives.
- **Prototype pollution**: attack writing to a script runtime's shared ancestor object so every object inherits attacker-chosen properties, reached through reserved key names in a recursive merge or a dotted property path, and turned into effect by a gadget that reads the polluted option, server-side as often as in the browser, with class pollution the class-based analogue.
- **Mass assignment**, also **over-posting**: attack supplying request fields a framework autobinds straight onto a model, setting attributes the form never exposed, with nested parameter names reaching related records outside it.

### Names, encodings and parser disagreement

- **Path traversal**, also **directory traversal**, **external control of file name or path**: attack whose input makes a file operation resolve outside the intended directory, as relative path traversal by dot-dot-slash climbing or absolute path traversal, through alternate separators, percent or doubly encoded dots, extra dots a cleaner half-collapses, or path truncation by a null byte after a validated prefix.
- **Path equivalence weakness**: weakness where two different strings name one file, through UNC network share and drive-relative forms, alternate data streams, legacy short names, reserved device names, trailing dots or spaces, case-insensitive filesystems, resource forks, shortcuts, junctions and symbolic or hard links.
- **File inclusion**: weakness where untrusted input names a file the runtime loads and interprets rather than merely reads, as local file inclusion or remote file inclusion, fed by log poisoning, transformed by a pseudo-protocol stream wrapper, or carried in archive path traversal that extraction writes outside the target directory.
- **Unrestricted file upload**: acceptance of a file without constraining type, name, size or storage, exploited by web shells, doubled or case-varied extensions, null byte extension truncation, upload path traversal, spoofed media types and magic bytes, content sniffing, polyglots, vector images carrying script, payloads hidden in metadata, overwrite of an existing file, and archives or images that expand until memory is gone.
- **Canonicalization error**, also **alternate representation attack**: weakness where a check runs against one representation while use happens against another, the confusions being percent, double and mixed encoding, decoding after validation, overlong UTF-8 and surrogate abuse, best-fit transcoding, Unicode and compatibility normalization mismatch, locale-dependent case folding, lenient base64 and numeric parsing, homoglyph and homograph spellings, invisible or bidirectional override characters, and escape interpretation on load.
- **Parser differential**: security-relevant disagreement between two implementations about what one input means, the protocol or format confusion arising from ambiguous grammars, competing length fields, delimiter and duplicate-field preference, tolerated trailing data and comments, alternate ASN.1 and indefinite-length encodings, URL and path normalization, parameter pollution, multipart boundaries and header folding.
- **HTTP request smuggling**, also **HTTP desynchronization**: attack exploiting disagreement between two servers sharing a connection about where one request ends, named by which hop honours which framing in the CL.TE, TE.CL, TE.TE, CL.CL, CL.0, 0.CL, TE.0 and HTTP/2 downgrade forms, and paying out as request tunneling, response queue poisoning, socket poisoning, front-end control bypass, internal header disclosure and client-side desync.
- **Request framing obfuscation**: the means of making two hops disagree, by transfer encoding header obfuscation, chunk extension and chunk size tricks, a mangled terminating chunk, trailer header smuggling, hop-by-hop header abuse, an expect header, a pause between bytes, HTTP/2 downgrade and cleartext upgrade smuggling, and binary framing injection.
- **Algorithmic complexity attack**: input chosen to drive a program onto an algorithm's worst case, whether colliding hash keys, quadratic scanning or concatenation, or a pattern matcher backtracking exponentially through nested quantifiers and ambiguous alternation, the family the field calls regular expression denial of service.

### Neutralizing untrusted input

- **Input validation**: checking that received data has the properties the program assumes, by type, range, length, character set, format, enumerated token, cross-field consistency, referential existence or declared schema, at the edge or in every layer, at the cost of specifying every field and duplicated rules that drift.
- **Allowlist validation**: accepting only values inside an enumerated or explicitly described permitted set, the positive security model, whose failures are a permissive allowlist, an incorrect or overly restrictive regular expression and a partial string comparison that matches a prefix, at the cost of maintaining the set, where a denylist instead stays permanently incomplete.
- **Neutralization**: rendering special elements inert for one downstream language, by escaping, quoting, output encoding, stripping or substitution chosen from the exact position the value will occupy, after every decoding step has run, at the cost of a distinct transformation per destination.
- **Sanitizer as neutralizing function**: a routine that transforms a value into one its destination parser cannot misread, and so removes taint at that point, at the cost of correctness resting entirely on which parser actually consumes the result.
- **Safe API**: interface separating data from instructions structurally, as parameterized queries with bound values, prepared statements that the driver must not merely emulate, typed query builders, database-specific escaping and identifier allowlists where placeholders cannot reach, and a sink wrapper that parameterizes second-order writes too, at the cost of narrower coverage.
- **Safe process invocation**: launching a child without a shell, by an argument vector, an absolute executable path, a quoting helper, a terminator marking the end of options, command and argument allowlists, a sanitized environment, a library call instead of an interpreter, and a sandbox around the child, at the cost of lost shell features.
- **Database privilege containment**: limiting what an injected statement could reach, by a least privilege account, a separate read-only connection, exposure through restricted views, multi-statement execution disabled, generic error responses that reveal no structure, and stored procedures as the only boundary the application crosses, at the cost of schema and deployment work.
- **Parser and workload hardening**: constraining what a format's machinery may do, refusing document type declarations, entity resolution and include directives, enabling a transformer's secure processing mode, capping expansion and nesting, streaming with bounded buffers, routing consumers through one fail-closed parser generated from a grammar, re-serializing canonically before forwarding, and metering match time, at the cost of tuning and rejected legitimate input.
- **Query workload limiting**: bounding what one query may cost, by static cost analysis before execution, a depth limit, an allowlist of persisted queries, introspection disabled in production, result set limits and a statement timeout, at the cost of rejected legitimate queries and a budget to maintain.
- **Record boundary discipline**: keeping a value from becoming a record or field boundary, by rejecting line terminators in header values, using a header API that validates rather than concatenating, writing structured logs with encoded messages and separated fields, and neutralizing exported values a spreadsheet would evaluate, at the cost of per-sink handling.
- **Upload acceptance policy**: constraining what an upload may be, by an extension allowlist, verifying content type by parsing the bytes, re-encoding the file, capping size, declared dimensions and compression ratio, storing it where nothing executes, and scanning it, at the cost of rejected legitimate files and processing per upload.
- **Evaluation confinement**: keeping untrusted text out of anything a runtime interprets, by static templates and a logic-less template language, template sandboxing, an expression allowlist, literal-only parsing in place of evaluation, a reflection target allowlist, and lookup expansion disabled in logging, at the cost of features authors expect.
- **Canonical form discipline**: converting input to one internal encoding at the edge, decoding and normalizing before any check and never after, refusing a decoder's lenient acceptances, restricting the permitted character repertoire, and detecting confusable spellings, at the cost of rejected legitimate text and per-context conversion.
- **Safe path construction**: canonicalizing a path before any check, extracting only the final filename component, confirming the result lies under a base directory or inside a directory jail, keeping storage outside the served tree, referring to files indirectly by index, and validating each archive member's destination, at the cost of platform-specific resolution and broken links.
- **Unambiguous message framing**: requiring one agreed length for every message on a shared connection, rejecting an ambiguous request that carries two framings, normalizing at the proxy before forwarding, and disabling connection reuse to the back end, at the cost of throughput and rejected clients that frame loosely.
- **Containment of reconstruction and storage**: refusing what should never be built or written, by binding allowlists and denylists, mapping requests into a data transfer object instead of the model, enforcing read-only fields, rejecting reserved keys, using null-prototype objects and frozen prototypes, and type-safe binding to data-only formats, at the cost of lists to maintain.
- **Serialization hardening**: restricting what a stream may rebuild, by a deserialization allowlist, a serialization filter, look-ahead deserialization that inspects a class before instantiating it, a safe loading mode, a restricted unpickler, a serialization proxy, and signing then sealing state so it is integrity-protected, at the cost of lists to maintain and rejected legitimate graphs.
- **Serializable class discipline**: rules for writing a class whose instances cross a stream, keeping the serialization method signatures exact so they are not silently ignored, calling no overridable method during reconstruction, marking secrets transient, copying defensively into the rebuilt object, and pinning a serialization version identifier, at the cost of hand-written read and write methods.
- **Taint tracking**: following untrusted values from sources to sinks through propagators to find flows that reach dangerous operations, statically, dynamically or through an instrumented application that blocks the call, at the cost of a model for every library function and false reports on either side.
- **Model input defense**: measures separating instructions from data, ranking developer instructions above read content, spotlighting retrieved data with explicit boundaries, treating generated text as untrusted, confirming consequential tool calls and scoping an agent's permissions, at the cost of imperfect adherence, imitable markers and approval fatigue.

## Application surfaces

### Origins, sites, and the browser trust boundary

- **Origin**, also **opaque origin**: the tuple of scheme, host, and port a browser uses as the identity of a principal for web content, the opaque form having no usable tuple so that sandboxed and data-URL content matches nothing else.
- **Same-origin policy**, also **SOP**: browser rule allowing script to read a resource only when scheme, host, and port match, at the cost of blocking legitimate cross-domain reads.
- **Site**, also **registrable domain**, also **effective top-level domain plus one**, also **eTLD+1**: the coarser web principal keyed on the shortest name registrable beneath a public suffix, grouping subdomains under one registrant.
- **Public suffix list**: a curated inventory of suffixes under which anyone may register names, consulted to compute site boundaries, at the cost of depending on out-of-band updates.
- **Cross-site request**, also **same-site request**, also **schemeful same-site**, also **cross-origin request**: a request classified by whether initiator and target share a registrable domain, optionally a scheme, or the full origin tuple, which decides cookie attachment and read permission.
- **Document domain relaxation**, also **document.domain**: a legacy setter widening a document's origin to a parent domain so sibling subdomains could script each other.
- **Browsing context**, also **browsing context group**, also **agent cluster**: the nesting of environments that present documents, grouped by script reachability for process assignment and by similar origin for shared memory and single-threaded execution.
- **Secure context**, also **powerful feature**: the delivery condition requiring an authenticated encrypted origin before a page may use a capability restricted for privacy or security reasons, such as geolocation, camera access, or persistent storage, at the cost of excluding plain HTTP deployments.
- **Mixed content**, also **active mixed content**, also **passive mixed content**: insecure subresources on an HTTPS page, active ones being script, styles, and frames a network attacker can replace, passive ones being media he can read or alter.
- **Mixed content policy directives**: the clauses upgrade-insecure-requests, which rewrites insecure subresource URLs to HTTPS, and block-all-mixed-content, which refuses them outright, at the cost of breaking legacy embedded content.
- **Cross-origin resource sharing**, also **CORS**: a server-driven relaxation of same-origin read restrictions using response headers that name permitted origins, at the cost of easy misconfiguration.
- **Preflight request**: an OPTIONS probe the browser sends before a cross-origin request whose method or headers fall outside the safelisted request header set, answered by Access-Control-Allow-Origin, Access-Control-Allow-Credentials, Access-Control-Allow-Methods, Access-Control-Expose-Headers, and an Access-Control-Max-Age that caches the outcome.
- **CORS policy misconfiguration**: granting read access too widely through a wildcard on private data, reflection of the request Origin header, or acceptance of the literal null origin that sandboxed frames present.
- **Fetch metadata request headers**, also **Sec-Fetch headers**: browser-set headers stating a request's site relationship, mode, destination, and user activation, at the cost of needing server rules to matter.
- **Fetch metadata resource isolation policy**: a server rule rejecting cross-site requests whose declared destination makes no sense for the endpoint, at the cost of maintaining allowances for real embeds.
- **Cross-domain policy file**, also **crossdomain.xml**: a legacy plugin-era manifest declaring which foreign domains could read a host's data, governed in turn by the X-Permitted-Cross-Domain-Policies header.

### Cross-origin isolation

- **Cross-Origin Read Blocking**, also **CORB**: a browser rule refusing to deliver data-typed cross-origin responses into a page's process, at the cost of breaking responses with wrong content types.
- **Cross-Origin Resource Policy**, also **CORP**: a response header declaring which sites may embed a resource at all, at the cost of enumerating legitimate embedders.
- **Cross-Origin Opener Policy**, also **COOP**: a header severing the window handle relationship with cross-origin openers, with the softer values same-origin-allow-popups and restrict-properties, at the cost of breaking popup integrations.
- **Cross-Origin Embedder Policy**, also **COEP**: a header requiring every subresource to opt into embedding under require-corp, or to load without credentials under credentialless, at the cost of auditing every third-party dependency.
- **Cross-origin isolation**: the document state reached when opener and embedder policies are both set, unlocking precise timers and shared memory, at the cost of strict embedding constraints.
- **Document-Isolation-Policy**: a header granting a document isolated status without asking its embedded resources to opt in, at the cost of those resources losing credentials.
- **Origin-keyed agent cluster**, also **Origin-Agent-Cluster header**: opting a document into isolation at origin rather than site granularity, at the cost of losing synchronous scripting between same-site frames.
- **Compromised renderer threat model**: the assumption that the process rendering web content can be fully controlled by an attacker, so enforcement must live outside it.
- **Private Network Access**: browser rules restricting requests from public pages to private or loopback addresses, at the cost of breaking local device and intranet integrations.
- **Permissions Policy**, also **Feature Policy**, also **Document Policy**: the headers and iframe allow attribute controlling which powerful features and page behaviors a document and its frames may use, costing an inventory of every feature a page needs.

### Framing, embedding, and interface redressing

- **Clickjacking**, also **UI redressing**: tricking a user into activating a control on a hidden or disguised embedded page, typically by stacking a nearly invisible victim frame above bait content.
- **Clickjacking variants**: the named forms of the technique, covering cursorjacking, likejacking, tapjacking on mobile, double-clickjacking across the two events of a double press, and cross-domain drag-and-drop extraction.
- **Frame busting**: script that detects being framed and breaks out or blanks the page, bypassed through sandboxing, navigation counting, handler overrides, unload interference, or nested framing, at the cost of blocking legitimate embedding.
- **X-Frame-Options**: a legacy response header declaring whether a document may be framed at all or only by its own origin, at the cost of allowing no per-origin list.
- **frame-ancestors**: a policy directive naming the origins permitted to embed a document, at the cost of requiring every legitimate embedder to be enumerated.
- **iframe sandbox attribute**: an embedding restriction stripping script, forms, plugins, navigation, and origin from a nested document, relaxed by tokens such as allow-same-origin, allow-top-navigation, and allow-popups-to-escape-sandbox, at the cost of functionality restored one token at a time.
- **Sandbox escape by token combination**: granting both script execution and original origin to a sandboxed frame, which lets the frame remove its own sandbox attribute and reload.
- **srcdoc document**: a frame whose markup the embedder supplies inline, inheriting the embedder's origin unless sandboxed.
- **Credentialless iframe**, also **anonymous iframe**: an embed loaded with an empty credential store so cookies never reach it, at the cost of breaking embeds that need sign-in.
- **Fenced frame**: an embed that cannot communicate with its embedder, isolating advertising and measurement content, at the cost of losing ordinary embedder integration.
- **Interface obstruction primitives**: the page features that cover or capture what a user aims at, naming top layer overlays painted above all content, autofocus abuse aimed at a hidden field, and fullscreen interface spoofing once allowfullscreen hands over the screen.
- **User activation requirement**: gating a sensitive action on a genuine user gesture in the same document, at the cost of blocking automated and deferred legitimate flows.
- **Data URL navigation restriction**: a browser rule blocking top-level navigation to data URLs, at the cost of breaking legacy generated-document flows.

### Content Security Policy and script-execution hardening

- **Content Security Policy**, also **CSP**: a declared allowlist of the sources a page may load and execute, written as directives governing one resource type each and source expressions naming origins, schemes, keywords, nonces, and hashes, at the cost of breaking inline code.
- **script-src**, also **script-src-elem**, also **script-src-attr**: the load-bearing directive controlling which scripts may execute, splittable so elements and inline handler attributes are configured apart, at the cost of a longer policy.
- **CSP fetch directives**: the clauses governing non-script loads, naming default-src as fallback, style-src, connect-src, object-src, frame-src, and worker-src, at the cost of enumerating every source a page needs.
- **CSP document directives**: the clauses constraining the document itself, naming base-uri for the resolution root, form-action for submission targets, and the sandbox directive, at the cost of forbidding dynamic rewriting.
- **CSP unsafe keywords**: the relaxations unsafe-inline, unsafe-eval, and unsafe-hashes, applied to keep inline code, string evaluation, or hashed handlers working, at the cost of restoring the capability injection needs.
- **Nonce-based policy**, also **hash-based policy**: a policy admitting only scripts carrying a per-response random token or matching a listed digest, at the cost of dynamic response generation or digest updates on every change.
- **strict-dynamic**: a keyword letting an already-trusted script create further scripts while host allowlists are ignored, at the cost of trusting whatever a loaded library injects.
- **Strict CSP**: the modern configuration of nonces or hashes plus strict-dynamic with object-src and base-uri locked down, replacing host allowlists, at the cost of substantial code refactoring.
- **CSP bypass**: defeating a policy through a trusted-code gadget, a callback-wrapping endpoint on an allowlisted host, path relaxation after a redirect, or exfiltration and reuse of a nonce.
- **CSP violation reporting**: the report-uri and report-to directives delivering structured violation messages through the Reporting API, alongside Network Error Logging for failed loads, with the Report-Only header measuring a candidate policy, at the cost of report noise and no protection while observing.
- **Trusted Types**: a mechanism forcing dangerous DOM sinks to accept only values from a reviewed policy object, switched on by require-trusted-types-for, at the cost of migrating every sink assignment.
- **Subresource Integrity**, also **SRI**: an attribute carrying a digest of an external script or stylesheet so altered content is refused, required wholesale by the Integrity-Policy header, at the cost of breakage on any upstream change.
- **X-XSS-Protection**: a legacy header controlling a browser's built-in reflected-script filter, removed because the filter leaked information and could be steered to disable legitimate script.

### Cross-site scripting and output handling

- **Cross-site scripting**, also **XSS**: a flaw letting an attacker run script in a victim's browser under the target application's origin, inheriting its data and privileges.
- **Cross-site scripting delivery classes**: the named routes to execution, covering reflected echo in a response, stored content served to later visitors, DOM-based flow through client code alone, the reflected and stored DOM variants, self injection, and blind injection detected out of band.
- **Mutation cross-site scripting**, also **mXSS**: execution arising when the browser's parser rewrites sanitized markup during reserialization into something that carries script.
- **Universal cross-site scripting**, also **UXSS**: a browser or extension defect letting an attacker run script in any origin, breaking the same-origin boundary itself.
- **Client-side template injection**: attacker data interpreted as a template expression by a front-end framework, yielding evaluation inside the page through an expression sandbox escape.
- **Injection context**, also **context breakout**: the grammatical position attacker data lands in, such as element text, attribute value, event handler, script string, or URL, and the closing of that construct so following characters are parsed as code.
- **Scripting URL schemes**: the javascript and data URL forms whose bodies execute or render in whatever context navigates to or imports them, a common sink for injected values.
- **Contextual output encoding**: escaping untrusted values for the exact position they occupy, whether markup entities, script string escapes, or URL encoding, at the cost of correct context tracking through templates.
- **HTML sanitization**, also **Sanitizer API**: parsing untrusted markup and keeping only an allowlisted set of elements, attributes, and schemes, whether in a library or the platform interface, at the cost of parser-difference bypasses and lost formatting.
- **X-Content-Type-Options nosniff**: a header forbidding the browser from inferring a response's type from its bytes, which stops content sniffing and the charset sniffing attack that overrides a declared charset, at the cost of breaking endpoints that declare their content types wrongly.
- **Injected-script abuse**: what execution is used for, covering self-propagating worms, drawn credential forms, keystroke capture, storage poisoning for persistence, and markup-only defacement known as HTML injection or content spoofing.

### Client-side data flow, gadgets, and scriptless attacks

- **Sink**, also **source**, also **client-side taint flow**: the path attacker data travels from a browser interface that returns influenceable values, such as the fragment or a received message, into an interface that turns its argument into markup, code, or a request.
- **Execution sinks**: the browser interfaces that compile or parse their argument, naming innerHTML, document.write, eval, the Function constructor, string-valued timers, and navigation property assignment.
- **DOM clobbering**: injecting named markup elements so named property lookups on the document or window return those elements instead of the expected variables.
- **Script gadget**: existing trusted code in a page or library that converts attacker-supplied markup or data into execution, defeating filters and policies.
- **Client-side prototype pollution**: writing to a shared object prototype from attacker-controlled property paths, so unrelated code reading a polluted option inherits attacker-chosen values.
- **Dangling markup injection**: injecting an unterminated tag or attribute so the following page content, including secrets, is swallowed into an attacker-bound request.
- **Scriptless attack**: extracting data or forging interface state with markup, styling, and layout alone, through injected style rules, attribute-prefix selectors that leak a value character by character, styled keylogging, font ligature measurement, and visited-link history sniffing.
- **Markup-only redirection**: sending a user or a page's requests elsewhere without script, through an injected base element, a meta refresh directive, or an overridden form submission target.
- **DOM-based manipulation**: client code building a navigation, link, cookie, request path, or frame reference from attacker-controlled input, covering the open redirect, cookie, link, path traversal, and cross-frame variants.

### Client-side controls and browser-held data

- **Transmitting data via the client**: sending application state out to the browser and accepting it back, whether as a hidden form field, an obfuscated blob, or serialized framework state such as ViewState, which makes that state attacker-controlled.
- **Client-side state signing**: keying a digest over data handed to the browser, as ViewState message authentication does, so the server detects edits, at the cost of key rotation across a server farm and larger payloads.
- **Server-side revalidation**: recomputing every security-relevant value on the server rather than trusting what the client returned, at the cost of extra lookups per request.
- **Client-side control reliance**: enforcing a rule only in the browser, so it disappears for any composed request, covering validation, disabled and length-limited inputs, authorization decisions, session timeouts, and referring-URL checks.
- **Client bundle disclosure**: what shipped front-end code reveals, covering source maps, markup comments, privileged interface code sent to unprivileged users, dependency versions listed exactly, build-time secret inlining, and hardcoded credentials.
- **Client-side obfuscation**: transforming shipped code to resist reading, which delays analysis, at the cost of size, debuggability, and no real protection.
- **Debug exposure**: leaving diagnostic behavior reachable in production, covering debug endpoint exposure through an undocumented request or build flag, a console or profiler route, and framework debug mode in production with its error overlay and state inspector.
- **Sensitive data in the URL**: placing tokens, session identifiers, or personal data in query strings and paths, where browser history, referrer headers, and proxy logs retain them.
- **Browser cache of sensitive responses**: storing authenticated pages on disk where later users of the device read them, addressed by no-store directives at the cost of losing all caching benefit.
- **Autofill harvesting**: laying out off-screen or covered fields that a browser or password manager fills automatically, capturing stored credentials and payment data without the user noticing.

### Cookies, sessions, and cross-site state

- **HTTP cookie**: a name and value the server asks the browser to store and resend on matching requests, discarded at session end without an expiry date and kept on disk with one, at the cost of a credential the browser attaches by itself.
- **Cookie scope**: the domain and path pair deciding which requests carry a cookie, confined to one host without a domain attribute and widened to every subdomain with one, at the cost of exposure to every host beneath the named domain.
- **Cookie security flags**: the delivery restrictions Secure, which withholds a cookie from plain HTTP, and HttpOnly, which hides it from page script, at the cost of insecure development contexts and front-end code that reads the value.
- **SameSite attribute**: the flag deciding cross-site delivery, withholding entirely under Strict, allowing top-level safe navigation under Lax, restoring it under None, and relaxing briefly under lax-allowing-unsafe, at the cost of broken federated flows.
- **Cookie prefix**: a naming convention making the browser enforce attribute requirements before acceptance, with Secure- demanding a secure origin and Host- demanding host-only root scope, at the cost of renaming existing cookies.
- **Cookie jar attacks**: manipulating a victim's cookie store from a weaker channel or related host, covering tossing or shadowing from a sibling domain, forcing a chosen value, overflowing the per-domain limit to evict entries, and cookie bombs that break requests.
- **Cookie parsing attacks**: exploiting disagreement between browsers and server parsers over header structure, covering the cookie sandwich around a target entry, empty-named cookies reassembled as another, and reserved prefix confusion.
- **Session identifier**: the value naming a server-side session, whose secrecy stands in for the user's credentials, and which is derivable when generated from guessable inputs and reusable when replayed.
- **Session hijacking**: taking over an authenticated session, whether by stealing the identifier, predicting it from a weak generator, reading it from storage, capturing it at a proxy, fixing a known one on the victim before login, donating the attacker's own, or puzzling a session variable into asserting a state never reached.
- **Session lifecycle controls**: the server-side rules bounding a session, naming renewal on authentication change, idle and absolute timeouts, logout invalidation, concurrent session limits, and binding to client attributes, at the cost of interrupted work and false expiries.
- **Client-side session**, also **server-side session store**: the choice between holding all session state in a signed or encrypted cookie and holding it in a backend the identifier references, at the cost of lost immediate revocation or of shared storage and replication.
- **Third-party cookie**, also **partitioned cookie**, also **CHIPS**: a cookie set or sent in an embedded context, optionally stored under a key that includes the embedding site so each embedder gets a separate jar, at the cost of shared cross-site state.
- **Storage partitioning**: keying client state, connections, and caches to the top-level site as well as the origin, through double-keyed caches, network partition keys, declared related website sets, and the Storage Access API prompt that escapes it, at the cost of breaking embedded features.
- **Cross-site tracking techniques**: rejoining a user across sites without shared storage, through identifiers appended to outbound links, redirect chains read as navigational tracking, and subdomains aliased onto third-party infrastructure by CNAME cloaking.
- **Privacy Sandbox interfaces**: the browser-mediated replacements for cross-site identity, naming Topics, Protected Audience, Attribution Reporting, Private State Tokens, Shared Storage, and a privacy budget, at the cost of in-browser machinery and new leak surfaces.

### Cross-site request forgery, navigation, and cross-site leaks

- **Cross-site request forgery**, also **CSRF**, also **session riding**: causing a victim's browser to issue an authenticated state-changing request the user never intended, including login cross-site request forgery and logout cross-site request forgery.
- **Forgery-enabling endpoint behavior**: server habits that widen forgery reach, naming safe-method state change, content type confusion in forgery where a body is parsed whatever its declared type, JSON cross-site request forgery through payloads shaped as legal form submissions, and HTTP method override driven by a header.
- **Forgery token patterns**: the secret-in-the-request defenses, naming the synchronizer token pattern, the per-request token, the double-submit cookie and its signed double-submit cookie form, and the encrypted token pattern, at the cost of stateful storage, key management, and broken parallel tabs.
- **Request provenance checks**: admitting a cross-origin credentialed mutation only after Origin header validation or Referer validation, a custom header requirement no cross-site form can meet, or reauthentication for sensitive actions, at the cost of absent referrer information, forced preflights, and friction.
- **Forgery protection failures**: the ways a token defense stops applying, covering forgery token leakage through referrers and logs, a token not bound to session, token validation skipped by method or content type, and over-broad sharing policy.
- **Open redirect**: an endpoint forwarding the browser to a destination taken from request input, reached through protocol-relative targets, parser disagreement over userinfo and encoding, or substring domain checks.
- **Redirect allowlist**: validating a redirect target against a fixed inventory of permitted destinations, at the cost of maintenance whenever legitimate targets change.
- **Tabnabbing**, also **reverse tabnabbing**: rewriting a page the user has looked away from, either a background tab or the opener a new tab still holds a handle on, so they return to a convincing forgery.
- **noopener**, also **noreferrer**: link relations severing the opener handle, the second also suppressing the referrer header, at the cost of losing intentional cross-window communication and analytics attribution.
- **Referrer leakage**: disclosing the current URL, and any secrets inside it, to every host a page links to or fetches from, including fragments carried through a redirect.
- **Referrer-Policy**: a header and attribute controlling how much of the current URL accompanies outgoing requests, commonly strict-origin-when-cross-origin, at the cost of breaking referrer-dependent integrations.
- **Address deception**: making the displayed location misrepresent the document, through chrome spoofing, a fake browser window drawn inside a page, lookalike internationalized names, encoded or shortened URLs, and history rewriting.
- **Cross-site leak**, also **XS-Leak**: inferring a user's state on another site from a browser-observable difference, a leak oracle answering one yes-or-no question about a response nobody ever read.
- **Cross-site search**, also **XS-Search**: repeatedly querying another site's search feature from an attacker page and reading a side signal to recover which terms match the victim's data.
- **Timing leak channels**: oracles built on elapsed work, naming the resource timing leak, the execution timing attack, hybrid timing attacks and timeless comparison, the frame load timing oracle, cross-window timing, event loop contention, connection pool exhaustion, the stream concurrency limit leak, port scanning through fetch timing, and shared-memory clocks.
- **Response-shape leak channels**: oracles built on what a load produces, naming the error-event leak, status code detection, script error message leak, frame counting, image dimension leak, media metadata leak, response size leak, scrollbar detection, style-based leak, identifier attribute leak, memory measurement leak, CORS error leak, policy violation oracle, and array-literal reads known as JSON hijacking.
- **State-probing leak channels**: oracles built on retained state, naming cache probing and storage quota probing, the history length leak, the redirect count leak, the maximum URL length leak, prefetch and download bar detection, navigation detection, redirect detection, the window reference leak, Web Locks probing, document viewer detection, resource policy detection, the text fragment leak, and background script registration probing.
- **Cache partitioning**: keying the browser cache by the top-level site so probes cannot read another site's entries, at the cost of lower cache hit rates.

### Messaging, workers, storage, and platform capabilities

- **Web messaging**, also **postMessage**: a sanctioned channel passing structured data between documents of different origins, at the cost of explicit origin and window handle checks on both ends.
- **Cross-document message weaknesses**: the ways the channel is misused, covering receivers that act without verifying the sender's origin, the postMessage broadcast leak where a wildcard target origin is read by whatever occupies the window, and payloads written into markup or code.
- **Worker contexts**: the scripting environments beside the document, naming web workers, shared workers reachable from every tab of an origin, rendering and audio worklets, and the BroadcastChannel, MessagePort, and Web Locks channels between them, at the cost of shared mutable state across tabs.
- **Service worker**: a script intercepting an origin's network requests under a registered path scope, widened by the Service-Worker-Allowed header, at the cost of a persistent updatable proxy inside the origin.
- **Service worker abuse**: turning that proxy hostile, by registering a worker from an injection or upload, poisoning the script-visible response cache, pulling further code in at runtime, or preloading requests nobody wanted.
- **Push and background execution interfaces**: origin code running with no page open, naming the Push API and its per-browser subscription endpoint, payload encryption, silent push, and the Background Sync, Periodic Sync, and Background Fetch interfaces, at the cost of activity with no user session behind it.
- **Client storage interfaces**: the per-origin stores readable by any script in the origin, naming localStorage, sessionStorage, IndexedDB, in-memory blob URLs, and the Clear-Site-Data header that discards them, at the cost of losing legitimate local state.
- **WebSocket**: a persistent bidirectional connection the same-origin policy does not restrict, hijackable cross-site because the browser authenticates the handshake with cookies, at the cost of server-side origin checks and a maintained permitted list.
- **Peer connection surface**: the browser-to-browser media and data path, whose session descriptions are untrusted input, whose connectivity candidates disclose private addresses, and whose relay credentials and signaling channel are both abusable, at the cost of traffic the application cannot inspect.
- **Device access interfaces**: the prompt-gated capabilities reaching hardware, naming WebUSB, Web Bluetooth, Web Serial, WebHID, Web MIDI, Web NFC, the File System Access API, Screen Capture, the Idle Detection API, clipboard read permission, and the Payment Request flow, at the cost of one mistaken grant.
- **Fingerprinting channels**: deriving a stable device identity from measurable platform differences, naming canvas rendering, audio graph output, installed font metrics, battery state, and motion sensor samples.

### HTTP message handling, caches, and server-side request forgery

- **HTTP response splitting**: inserting line terminators into header-bound data so the client receives headers, a body, or a second authoritative response the application never intended.
- **Host header abuse**: supplying host or forwarding values the application trusts, through injected Host headers, absolute request lines, or client address headers, to redirect users, poison caches, or poison password reset links.
- **HTTP parameter pollution**: supplying a parameter more than once, hiding a value inside a delimiter one parser splits on, or sending an unexpected method, so components disagree about what was asked.
- **Web cache poisoning**: getting a shared cache to store an attacker-influenced response for other users, exploiting inputs outside the cache key, bodies on read methods, and normalization differences, and readable through cache hit indicators.
- **Web cache deception**: causing a shared cache to store a personalized authenticated response under a path that looks static, usually by appending a suffix the application ignores.
- **Cache control directives**: the response clauses bounding shared storage, naming Vary to widen the key, private and no-store to withhold a response, and s-maxage to set shared lifetime apart, at the cost of fragmented caches and lost caching benefit.
- **Server-side request forgery**, also **SSRF**: making a server issue a request to an attacker-chosen destination, borrowing its network position and identity, and exploited blind or semi-blind through out-of-band interaction when no response returns.
- **Server-side request forgery targets**: what the borrowed position reaches, naming services bound to private interfaces, instance metadata credentials, non-HTTP schemes such as file and gopher, plain-text backends spoken to by protocol smuggling, and fetching renderers, webhooks, and link previews.
- **Server-side request forgery filter bypass**: defeating destination validation with decimal or hexadecimal address forms, mapped address families, hostnames resolving inward, rebinding between check and connect, or a permitted URL that redirects.
- **Server-side request forgery containment**: bounding where a forged request can go, through resolve-then-connect validation, egress filtering, a forwarding proxy for all outbound fetches, token-required and hop-limited metadata, and callback destination validation, at the cost of maintained allowances and a new dependency.

### Uploaded content, framework rendering, and disclosure

- **Upload validation**: constraining submitted files by an upload type allowlist over extension and media type, magic byte validation, filename sanitization or server-generated storage names, and an upload size quota, at the cost of rejecting formats and names users expect to work.
- **Archive and decoder bombs**: submitted content whose expansion dwarfs its stored size, naming zip slip and the archive symlink entry that escapes the extraction directory, decompression bombs, and the image decompression bomb whose declared dimensions force an enormous pixel buffer.
- **Hostile stored content**: files that act when served or processed, naming the polyglot file valid under two formats, scriptable vector image upload, uploaded documents the browser renders as active markup, executable path storage, and server-side document conversion abuse.
- **Stored content delivery controls**: serving user files safely through out-of-webroot storage, a separate content origin, forced download disposition with an inert declared type, content re-encoding and metadata stripping, a quarantine state with upload malware scanning, retrieval authorization or a signed retrieval URL, and direct-to-storage upload, at the cost of lost inline preview.
- **Framework rendering surface**: the security consequences of rendering a view on both server and client, naming the hydration mismatch, serialized render payloads, props over-serialization, and cross-request state pollution from state a module reuses.
- **Framework sinks and route exposure**: the framework constructs that publish or execute what a caller supplies, naming trusted-HTML escape hatches, the sanitizer bypass helper, selector injection, template literal injection, framework-generated route exposure from generated server functions and file-layout routes, static generation of private data, edge middleware authorization bypass, shadow DOM piercing, and custom element hijacking.
- **Client application state attacks**: manipulating a single-page application's own machinery, through state store poisoning, client-side deserialization of state that chooses its own types, event bus injection into an internal channel, and client-side route guard bypass.
- **Disclosure hygiene**: what a deployment reveals without being asked, naming server banner disclosure and technology fingerprinting, the verbose error message and stack trace disclosure, directory listings, backup file exposure and version control directory exposure, default and sample content, and developer comments in delivered markup.
- **Application mapping**: building the inventory an attacker tests against, through proxied manual browsing, automated crawling, content discovery and forced browsing, hidden parameter and verb enumeration, response fingerprinting, and virtual host discovery.

### HTTP API authorization, inventory, and edge controls

- **Broken object level authorization**, also **BOLA**: an endpoint acting on the object named in the request without confirming the caller is entitled to that object, harvested by object identifier enumeration wherever a predictable resource identifier is issued.
- **Broken object property level authorization**, also **BOPLA**: reading or writing fields the caller has no right to, through request bodies bound wholesale onto internal objects, excessive data exposure where full records are returned for client-side field filtering, or fields hidden only in the interface.
- **Broken function level authorization**, also **BFLA**: exposing an operation to callers whose role should not reach it, commonly administrative endpoint exposure on the same surface as user operations, found by guessing a route or a method.
- **Interface contract controls**: enforcing shape at the boundary, through a response field allowlist per role, request schema validation accepting only declared fields and types, and an indirect object reference map handing sessions opaque handles, at the cost of serializer and schema upkeep.
- **API inventory failures**: operating interfaces nobody tracks, naming the undocumented shadow API, the zombie endpoint left after deprecation, version sprawl with weaker checks in older releases, interface specification exposure to anyone who asks, and blind trust in an upstream response from a partner.
- **API inventory**: maintaining an authoritative register of endpoints, versions, owners, and data classes, with an announced deprecation and sunset policy, at the cost of continuous discovery, curation, and client migration work.
- **Graph interface abuse**: turning a query language against its server, through introspection abuse and field suggestion leakage, the query depth attack, the batching attack, alias overloading and the field duplication attack that defeat per-request counters, fragment cycles, global object identifier enumeration, and read-method query exposure.
- **Graph interface controls**: bounding what a query may ask, through introspection disabling, depth limiting, query cost analysis before execution, a persisted query allowlist of pre-registered operations, and resolver-level authorization, at the cost of broken tooling and pervasive repeated checks.
- **Rate limiting**: capping calls per identity over an interval, whether by token or leaky bucket, fixed or sliding window, with quotas, throttling, load shedding, size and timeout caps, and jittered backoff, at the cost of tuning against legitimate bursts.
- **Rate limit bypass**: spreading calls across rotating identities, addresses, headers, or endpoints, so no single counter keyed to any one of them reaches its threshold.
- **Webhook verification**: confirming an inbound callback came from the claimed sender, through a keyed digest over the delivered bytes, a bounded replay window against resent events, and idempotency keys that collapse duplicates, at the cost of secret management and clock synchronization.
- **API key**: a long-lived opaque string identifying a calling application, rotated on a schedule or after exposure, at the cost of being a bearer secret with no user context and of overlap windows for every consumer.

### Business logic, automated abuse, and resource exhaustion

- **Business logic flaw**: a defect in what an application allows rather than how it parses, covering sensitive business flow abuse, workflow bypass by requesting later steps directly, forced state transition through a submitted stage parameter, and the state machine violation that an assumption of client sequence permits.
- **Flow race condition**: a race condition in a business flow exploiting the gap between a check and its effect, as a single-endpoint race, a multi-endpoint race, a double submission, or time-of-check to time-of-use in a flow, producing limit overrun on a single-use allowance.
- **Value manipulation in a flow**: value tampering with an amount the application takes from the request rather than recomputing it, covering negative value abuse, rounding abuse, currency confusion, discount stacking, and refund abuse without delivery.
- **Flow integrity controls**: recomputing totals, entitlements, and transitions server-side from stored records, with a transaction integrity check on every write and an optimistic concurrency check rejecting writes whose base version has changed, at the cost of extra reads, slower flows, and retries surfaced to callers.
- **Automated threat event**: a named misuse of ordinary features at machine speed, covering carding and card cracking, cashing out, token cracking, scalping, denial of inventory and inventory hoarding, expediting and sniping, scraping, footprinting, account creation and aggregation, free-tier abuse, skewing, spamming, and advertising fraud.
- **Anti-automation controls**: answering insufficient anti-automation by keeping a human-scale flow human-scale, through human verification challenges, client proof of work, headless client detection, and scoring, at the cost of accessibility failures, false accusations, and defeat by paid solvers and residential proxies.
- **Application-layer resource exhaustion**: unrestricted resource consumption where a small request costs a large amount of work, through worst-case algorithmic input such as backtracking patterns, hash collisions, entity expansion, the JSON depth bomb, and normalization growth, or through unbounded result sets, rendering pipelines, cache stampedes, and metered third-party calls.

### Application requirement standards and risk categories

- **Application Security Verification Standard**, also **ASVS**: a catalog of testable application security requirements arranged by chapter and graded into verification levels that fix how much of it an application at a given risk must satisfy, at the cost of interpretation work.
- **Web Security Testing Guide**, also **WSTG**, also **Mobile Application Security Testing Guide**, also **MASTG**: the companion catalogs of test procedures for verifying web and mobile requirements, at the cost of rapid staleness as platforms change.
- **OWASP Top Ten**: the periodically rebuilt list of the ten most consequential web application risk categories, naming broken access control, security misconfiguration, insecure design, integrity and logging failures, exceptional condition mishandling, and vulnerable components.
- **Sibling top-ten lists**: the same form applied to other surfaces, naming the API Security Top Ten, the Mobile Top Ten with its improper credential usage, insufficient cryptography, insecure communication, and insufficient binary protections categories, and the Top Ten for large language model applications naming prompt injection and sensitive information disclosure.
- **Mobile Application Security Verification Standard**, also **MASVS**, also **MASWE**: mobile requirements grouped as storage, crypto, auth, network, platform, code, resilience, and privacy, mapped to the Mobile Application Security Weakness Enumeration, at the cost of interpretation work per platform.

### Mobile application surfaces

- **Insecure mobile data storage**: insecure data storage keeping credentials, tokens, or records where other software or a person holding the phone reads them, covering shared storage exposure outside private application storage, the unencrypted local database, temporary file exposure, backup leakage, and the app group container shared with extensions.
- **Device-side leakage channels**: paths by which displayed or entered data escapes the application, naming screenshot leakage on backgrounding, clipboard leakage, system log leakage, crash report leakage, keyboard cache leakage, broadcast eavesdropping, third-party software development kit data collection, autofill service abuse, lock screen notification content, and application widget exposure.
- **Mobile data protection measures**: platform facilities for holding secrets and limiting exposure, naming the keychain accessibility attribute, hardware-backed keystore attestation with biometric gating, key invalidation on enrolment change, file protection classes, screen capture prevention, clipboard restriction, backup exclusion, and data wipe on sign-out, at the cost of failed background access and re-enrolment.
- **Cross-application attack surface**: reaching an installed application from another one, through exported component exposure, the exported activity and exported service, broadcast receiver exposure, content provider exposure, implicit intents, intent spoofing, intent redirection, pending intent hijacking, task hijacking, permission re-delegation and squatting, fragment injection, the dirty stream attack, local socket exposure, and the screen overlay attack chained with accessibility service abuse.
- **Deep link claiming**: binding an application to incoming links, where a custom URL scheme claim any application may make invites deep link hijacking and deep link parameter injection, while a universal link claim with digital asset link verification proves domain ownership, at the cost of hosting and upkeep of the published statement.
- **WebView**: an embedded browser sharing the host application's identity, where insecure WebView settings enable file access from file URLs and a JavaScript bridge open to bridge abuse, hardened by marking only intended bridge methods, refusing a transport error handler override, and system browser handoff for external links, at the cost of losing interface control.
- **Mobile transport trust**: constraining which servers and anchors a client accepts, through certificate or public key pinning with a backup pin, a network security configuration declaring trust anchors, and refusal of the cleartext traffic permitted by default, at the cost of outages on rotation and transport security exceptions that outlive their reason.
- **Mobile tampering and analysis**: what an attacker does with a distributed package, covering application package extraction of resources and a hardcoded secret in a mobile binary, reverse engineering, repackaging and resigning, side-loading, a debuggable build shipped, dynamic instrumentation and method hooking, native library tampering, signature block confusion, and pinning or detection bypass.
- **Mobile client resilience**: raising the cost of running on a hostile device, through root, jailbreak, emulator, and debugger detection, runtime integrity and signature verification, in-application monitoring, device binding, obfuscation, string encryption, and white-box cryptography, at the cost of false positives and defeat once the checker is patched.

### Prompt injection, output handling, and retrieval

- **Prompt injection**: supplying text a model reads as instruction rather than data, whether direct prompt injection by the requester or indirect prompt injection through retrieved pages, multimodal injection in attachments and images, prompt template injection, special token injection, hidden characters no reviewer can see, and conversation memory poisoning that acts on later sessions.
- **Jailbreak**: a prompt inducing output a model's policy training was meant to refuse, built by the persona attack and Do Anything Now framing, refusal suppression and prefix injection, the many-shot jailbreak, the crescendo attack, payload splitting, encoding obfuscation, cross-lingual jailbreak, ASCII art jailbreak, token smuggling, invisible character injection, and adversarial suffixes from greedy coordinate gradient search.
- **System prompt leakage**: a deployment's hidden text reaching the requester, whether recited under prompt extraction or reconstructed by prompt stealing, covering a prompt-embedded secret, hidden context disclosure, and tool schema disclosure, so instruction confidentiality fails against a determined requester.
- **Model input and output controls**: the layers around a model, naming instruction hierarchy, content delimiting and datamarking of untrusted passages, provenance tagging of context, structured prompt assembly, an injection detection classifier, input guardrail and output guardrail with content filtering, safety alignment, and model output encoding for the consumer, at the cost of latency, guardrail bypass, and over-refusal.
- **Improper output handling**: passing generated text into a renderer, interpreter, query engine, or shell without validation, so model-generated code execution, generated query execution, insecure output rendering, and rendered link exfiltration follow from ignoring the untrusted output assumption.
- **Jailbreak defenses**: checks placed around a model to blunt optimized attacks, naming the self-reminder, the perplexity filter, the paraphrase defense, randomized smoothing of prompts, the constitutional classifier, and representation rerouting, at the cost of inference expense, distorted legitimate requests, and over-refusal.
- **Automated red teaming**: searching for jailbreaks by machine rather than by hand under the adaptive attacker assumption, through greedy coordinate gradient suffix optimization, attacker models that refine and prune candidates, and best-of-N jailbreaking, judged by attack success rate for a universal jailbreak or a transferable attack prompt, at the cost of compute and judge model manipulation.

- **Retrieval augmented generation**: answering from documents fetched at query time and placed in the prompt, at the cost of trusting whatever the retriever returns.
- **Retrieval poisoning**: getting hostile text into what a retriever selects, through corpus poisoning of any indexed source, vector store poisoning with crafted records, similarity manipulation that sits text near many queries, reranker manipulation, and hidden text in a document.
- **Retrieval leakage**: an index handing back what its source would not, through a vector store permission gap, cross-tenant retrieval leakage from one shared collection, the stale index record left after deletion at source, and embedding inversion back to text.
- **Retrieval controls**: document-level authorization in retrieval filtering passages by the requester's rights, source allowlisting for retrieval, ingestion review, retrieved content sanitization, and retrieval provenance display, at the cost of index metadata upkeep, lower recall, and thin-source refusals.

### Agent security and model assurance
