- **Excessive agency**: granting a model more permissions, tools, or autonomy than its task requires, so agent privilege compromise makes a confused deputy in an agent act at that authority, with the lethal trifecta of private data, untrusted content, and outbound communication marking the blast radius of an agent that one manipulation reaches.
- **Model Context Protocol**, also **MCP**: a published interface by which a model client discovers and calls tools, reads resources, and fetches prompt templates from separate servers, at the cost of trusting every server a session loads.
- **Tool and protocol attacks**: attacking what an agent calls, through tool poisoning in a description or schema, tool output injection, tool misuse, parameter injection into a tool call, tool registry squatting, tool shadowing, server impersonation, plugin compromise, sampling request abuse, resource poisoning, prompt template injection through a server, line jumping, and tool session hijacking.
- **Token passthrough**: a tool server forwarding a caller's access token to a downstream service without checking that the token was issued for the server itself.
- **Rug pull**, also **silent tool redefinition**: a tool provider that behaves correctly during review and changes its description, parameters, or behavior after approval, so later calls act outside what the user allowed.
- **Cross-server exfiltration**: one tool server reading data a second server produced in the same session and sending it outward through a call of its own.
- **Agent behavior failures**: an autonomous run going wrong, covering memory poisoning of an agent reaching later tasks, shared knowledge store poisoning and agent communication poisoning, autonomous loop abuse, agent resource overload, cascading hallucination, a rogue agent, agent identity spoofing, human manipulation by an agent, unexpected code execution by an agent, agent code execution escape, and actions nobody can attribute.
- **Agent hijacking**, also **goal hijacking**: redirecting an autonomous run's objective through injected instruction, so it keeps acting with its own credentials toward an attacker's goal.
- **Zero-click agent exploitation**: compromising an agent through content it ingests on its own schedule, with no user action at any point.
- **Insecure inter-agent communication**, also **agent in the middle**: messages between cooperating agents carried without authentication or integrity, letting a participant be impersonated, instructions be rewritten in flight, and privilege abuse in a delegation chain go unnoticed.
- **Agent controls**: bounding a run, through agent identity, scoped delegation, capability scoping, tool allowlisting, per-tool credential minimization, agent egress control, sandboxed tool execution, tool call provenance, the rule of two, human in the loop behind an irreversible action gate, an agent audit log, and an agent kill switch, at the cost of throughput, approval fatigue attacks, and enumeration work.
- **Agent design patterns**: the arrangements that keep untrusted text away from authority, naming the action-selector pattern, plan then execute, the code-then-execute pattern, the context-minimization pattern, the dual model pattern, and the map-reduce agent pattern, at the cost of capability, engineering complexity, and many model calls per task.

- **Model training and supply chain attacks**: corrupting what a model learns or ships as, through availability poisoning and targeted poisoning, label flipping, clean-label poisoning, backdoor poisoning whose backdoor trigger wakes sleeper behavior in a trojaned model, model poisoning of weights, split-view poisoning and frontrunning poisoning, fine-tuning data poisoning, the fine-tuning attack, safety degradation from fine-tuning, unsafe model deserialization, and model repository squatting.
- **Model bill of materials**: an inventory of the datasets, base checkpoints, and libraries inside a shipped model, kept with data provenance records, a dataset integrity digest, training data sanitization, and model artifact provenance signing, at the cost of facts suppliers do not publish.
- **Federated learning poisoning**: a participant in collaborative training submitting crafted updates, whether a model replacement attack overwriting the shared model or a free-rider attack contributing nothing, while gradient leakage and the data reconstruction attack read the other participants' data.
- **Byzantine-robust aggregation**: combining collaborative training updates by a rule tolerating a hostile minority, through coordinate-wise median aggregation, trimmed mean aggregation, or Krum selection, at the cost of slower convergence and discarded honest work.
- **Inference-time attacks on models**: attacks needing only queries or inputs, covering the adversarial example within a perturbation budget and imperceptibility constraint, the white-box attack by fast gradient sign method, projected gradient descent attack, or Carlini and Wagner attack, and the black-box attack by surrogate model, boundary attack, or query-based attack, aiming at targeted misclassification, sometimes through a universal perturbation.
- **Physical-world adversarial input**: an object, sticker, or marking placed in a scene so a sensor-fed model misreads it, including the adversarial patch that works wherever it appears, the audio adversarial example, the problem-space attack, and the adversarial malware sample that stays a working file.
- **Model extraction**, also **model stealing**: rebuilding a hosted model from the answers it gives, whether functionality stealing that trains a substitute on its outputs, parameter extraction of the weights themselves, or hyperparameter stealing of the training settings.
- **Membership inference**: deciding from a model's behavior whether one specific record was in its training data, alongside property inference about the whole set, attribute inference about a person, and model inversion that reconstructs a representative input.
- **Training data extraction**: pulling a model's training text back out verbatim, resting on memorization, prompted by the divergence attack that pushes it off its usual distribution, and reaching prompt retention exposure across customers.
- **Model defenses and assurance**: measures against those attacks, naming adversarial training, certified robustness and randomized smoothing, differentially private training, input purification and feature squeezing, backdoor detection by trigger inversion, activation clustering, and spectral signature detection, fine-pruning, machine unlearning, model weight watermarking and model fingerprinting, red teaming with adaptive evaluation, and the MITRE ATLAS matrix, at the cost of accuracy and compute.
- **Generated content watermarking**: marking model output so its origin can be checked later, by biasing generation to carry a statistical mark or attaching content provenance metadata signed with edit history, alongside synthetic media detection against voice cloning fraud, at the cost of quality loss and the watermark removal attack.
- **Unbounded consumption**: letting a deployment spend inference capacity without limits, so token flooding, the sponge example, context window exhaustion, self-referential expansion, the energy-latency attack, cost harvesting, and distillation through an interface turn ordinary usage into denial of wallet or denial of machine learning service.
- **Inference quota**: a ceiling on calls or tokens per identity and interval, with a spending cap, input length limit and output length limit, step budget, inference timeout, per-request cost accounting, and tenant capacity isolation, at the cost of tuning against legitimate bursts and jobs halted part-finished.
- **Hallucination**: generated content no source supports, stated as confidently as grounded output, becoming misinformation, package hallucination an attacker registers, cascading hallucination through later steps, and overreliance where automation bias lets unverified text pass review.

## Cryptography, from primitives to deployment

### What a primitive guarantees

- **Perfect secrecy**, also **information-theoretic security**: property that a ciphertext's distribution is identical for every possible message, which Shannon's theorem shows forces a key space at least as large as the message space.
- **Unicity distance**: amount of ciphertext beyond which one key alone stays consistent with meaningful plaintext, fixed by how far a language falls below the maximum information a symbol could carry.
- **Semantic security**, also **IND-CPA**, also **indistinguishability under chosen-plaintext attack**: requirement that an adversary holding an encryption oracle cannot tell which of two plaintexts it chose was the one encrypted.
- **Chosen-ciphertext security**, also **IND-CCA2**, also **IND-CCA1**, also **lunchtime security**: requirement that indistinguishability survives an adversary having ciphertexts of its choice decrypted, at any time in the adaptive form and only before the challenge in the lunchtime form.
- **Non-malleability**: requirement that no adversary can turn a ciphertext into another one whose plaintext is meaningfully related to the first.
- **Ciphertext integrity**, also **INT-CTXT**, also **plaintext integrity**, also **INT-PTXT**: requirement that a receiver accept no ciphertext the sender never produced, weakened in the plaintext form to accepting no message that was never sent.
- **Authenticated encryption security**: combined notion demanding chosen-plaintext indistinguishability together with ciphertext integrity.
- **Key commitment**: property that a ciphertext opens under one key only, so a single blob cannot be decrypted two meaningful ways.
- **Nonce-misuse resistance**: property that repeating a nonce leaks only that two messages were equal rather than destroying confidentiality outright.
- **Unforgeability under chosen-message attack**, also **EUF-CMA**, also **strong unforgeability**, also **SUF-CMA**: requirement that nobody with a signing or tagging oracle produce a valid authenticator on a fresh message, and in the strong form not even a new one on an old message.
- **Existential forgery**, also **selective forgery**, also **universal forgery**, also **total break**: the ladder of signature failure from a valid signature on some message, to one on a message fixed in advance, to signing anything on demand, to recovery of the private key.
- **Collision resistance**, also **preimage resistance**, also **second-preimage resistance**: the three hash goals of finding no colliding pair, no input matching a given digest, and no partner for a given input.
- **Chosen-prefix collision**: collision between messages carrying two arbitrary attacker-supplied prefixes, the form that breaks document and certificate formats, weaker as identical-prefix, near, free-start, and semi-free-start variants, and multiplied into multicollisions, expandable messages, and herding structures against iterated hashes.
- **Key indistinguishability**: requirement that a derived or agreed key be indistinguishable from an independent random key.
- **Key privacy**, also **anonymity of a ciphertext**, also **unlinkability**, also **untraceability**: properties that a ciphertext hides which public key it was made for, that two uses of one credential cannot be tied together, and that a transaction cannot be traced to its origin.
- **Security game**: formal interaction between adversary and challenger whose winning probability defines a notion, posed in real-or-random or left-or-right form and measured by the adversary's advantage over blind guessing.
- **Security level**: bits of work the best known attack should cost, driven by a security parameter, bounded by a negligible success function, stated concretely in queries and time or asymptotically, and compared across primitives by security level equivalence, also called comparable strength matching.
- **Provable security**: practice of reducing any attack on a scheme to an algorithm against a stated hard problem, where a tight reduction preserves the nominal level and a loose one forces larger parameters.
- **Random oracle model**: analysis setting treating a hash as a public truly random function, joined by the ideal cipher, generic group, algebraic group model, and quantum random oracle settings, by the assumption-only standard model, and by indifferentiability as the licence to substitute a real construction.
- **Birthday bound**: point near the square root of a block or state space where collisions become likely and security claims stop, which beyond-birthday-bound constructions are built to pass.
- **Universal composability**: framework proving a protocol indistinguishable from an ideal functionality in every environment, established by exhibiting a simulator.

### Adversary models and cryptanalysis

- **Chosen-plaintext attack**, also **ciphertext-only attack**, also **known-plaintext attack**, also **chosen-ciphertext attack**: the ladder of adversary access from intercepted ciphertext alone, through unchosen plaintext pairs, to encryptions and decryptions of its own choosing, adaptively when each query follows the last answer, and in chosen-key and known-key models to the key itself.
- **Adaptive adversary**, also **static adversary**: adversary choosing each move from earlier answers, against one fixing every choice before the interaction, the same split that separates adaptive from selective security.
- **Multi-user security**: notion measuring advantage against many keys at once, since breaking any single one of them may be enough, beside leakage resilience which holds when the adversary also learns a bounded function of secret state.
- **Computational hardness assumption**: stated belief that a specific problem admits no efficient algorithm, on which a proof depends, strengthened where random self-reducibility makes the average instance as hard as the worst.
- **Model gap**: distance between what a proof covers and what a deployment does, so an attack outside the model through timing or error behavior violates no proven statement, and where a security proof assumption, a protocol specification ambiguity, or an implementation-specification gap leaves room for one.
- **Distinguisher**: algorithm telling a primitive's output from random better than chance, weighed by the data, time, and memory it needs, by whether it reaches the full round count or only a reduced one, and by whether its cost is merely certificational.
- **Exhaustive key search**, also **brute-force search**: trial of candidate keys until one succeeds, the baseline cost every cipher must push out of reach, divisible almost linearly across machines and cheapened by time-memory tradeoffs whose rainbow table form inverts unsalted password hashes.
- **Meet-in-the-middle attack**: matching partial computations from both ends of a construction, the reason cascade encryption under two keys buys little strength, extended over further rounds by the biclique refinement.
- **Birthday attack**: collision search succeeding after roughly the square root of the output space many samples, distributable through distinguished points with little memory per machine, and widened by Wagner's generalized birthday algorithm to finding several values that cancel.
- **Length-extension attack**: appending chosen data to a message using only its digest and length, which forges any authenticator formed by hashing a key before its message.
- **Weak key**: key under which a cipher behaves badly by collapsing rounds or becoming self-inverse, alongside semi-weak pairs whose encryptions undo each other, equivalent keys that shrink the effective space, and related-key attacks that use keys whose relation the attacker chooses.
- **Differential cryptanalysis**: tracing how a chosen input difference propagates through rounds along a characteristic of known probability, in truncated, impossible, higher-order, boomerang, rectangle, and differential-linear forms.
- **Linear cryptanalysis**: approximating a cipher by linear relations over bits that hold with a measurable bias, combined across linear hulls and multiple approximations, or used with exactly zero correlation to discard wrong keys.
- **Integral cryptanalysis**, also **square attack**: summing outputs over a structured input set so a predictable value appears, with distinguishers located by division property search and by statistical saturation.
- **Algebraic attack**: writing a primitive as a polynomial system and solving it by cube summation, interpolation, or Grobner basis computation, resisted in proportion to a function's algebraic immunity.
- **Slide attack**: exploiting self-similar rounds so a cipher's own encryptions align with each other, joined by invariant subspace, nonlinear invariant, and rebound attacks that ride structure the round function preserves.
- **Correlation attack**: exploiting statistical dependence between keystream and one register's output, sharpened by decoding in its fast form and by guess-and-determine reasoning, with the shortest equivalent register recovered by the Berlekamp-Massey algorithm.
- **Frequency analysis**: solving a substitution from symbol counts against known language frequencies, aided by the index of coincidence and Kasiski examination for a repeating key length and by crib dragging for known fragments.

### Block and stream cipher families

- **Symmetric-key cryptography**, also **private-key cryptography**: setting where one shared secret both protects and recovers a message, stated formally as an encryption scheme triple of key generation, encryption, and decryption routines bound by correctness, and costing establishment of that secret beforehand.
- **Block cipher**: keyed invertible map on a fixed-width block, whose block size bounds the data one key may safely protect and whose key size puts exhaustive search out of reach, costing a mode and padding for real messages.
- **Substitution-permutation network**, also **SPN**: structure alternating a nonlinear substitution layer with a linear mixing layer every round, costing a separate inverse implementation for decryption.
- **Feistel network**: structure splitting a block and updating one half with a keyed function of the other so decryption never inverts that function, with unbalanced, generalized, and Lai-Massey variants trading round count against per-round work.
- **Key schedule**: expansion of a master key into per-round subkeys separated by round constants, applied for a round count set above the highest count analysis has broken, costing setup time on every rekey and a security margin bought with throughput.
- **Key whitening**: exclusive-or of extra key material at a cipher's input and output, raising exhaustive-search cost for a small amount of extra key.
- **Confusion**, also **diffusion**: paired design goals of nonlinear key dependence from substitution boxes with high nonlinearity and low differential uniformity, and of spreading each input bit across the block, gauged by avalanche criteria, branch number, and the wide trail strategy, costing table memory or gate area.
- **ARX construction**: primitive built only from addition, rotation, and exclusive-or, fast and naturally constant-time, often implemented bitsliced as a Boolean circuit, and harder to bound analytically than a table-based design.
- **Tweakable block cipher**: block cipher taking a public tweak beside the key so each tweak yields an independent permutation, costing a new primitive or a mode built around one.
- **Pseudorandom permutation**, also **PRP**, also **pseudorandom function**, also **PRF**, also **strong pseudorandom permutation**: keyed map no efficient adversary distinguishes from random, strongly so when inverse queries are allowed, interchangeable with a random function only up to the birthday limit, and built provably from public permutations by the Even-Mansour and Luby-Rackoff results.
- **Pseudorandom generator**: deterministic routine stretching a short seed into a longer string no efficient test tells from random, its expansion factor fixing how much one seed supplies, built from a one-way permutation and a hard-core predicate that the Goldreich-Levin theorem always yields, extended to long inputs by the GGM construction, and costing an unproven gap.
- **Constrained pseudorandom function**: keyed function admitting derived keys that evaluate only inputs matching a predicate, narrowed to a puncturable pseudorandom function losing one chosen input and to a key-homomorphic pseudorandom function whose outputs combine as their keys do, costing larger keys and speed.
- **One-time pad**, also **Vernam cipher**: combination of every message symbol with a fresh uniform key symbol, unbreakable and costing key material as long as all traffic plus a guarantee that no segment is ever reused.
- **Classical cipher**: pre-machine scheme built from substitution and transposition, covering the shift, Vigenere, autokey, running key, Playfair, Hill, columnar, and fractionating forms, the nomenclator and codebook, and the rotor machines ending in Enigma.
- **DES**, also **Data Encryption Standard**: sixteen-round Feistel cipher on 64-bit blocks with a 56-bit key, now a compatibility choice only, extended historically by DESX whitening and by two-key or three-key Triple DES in encrypt-decrypt-encrypt order at low speed.
- **AES**, also **Advanced Encryption Standard**, also **Rijndael**: the standard 128-bit block cipher with 128, 192, or 256 bit keys, built from byte substitution, row shifting, column mixing, and round-key addition, universally supported and accelerated in hardware, needing a mode for messages longer than a block.
- **AES-generation alternative cipher**: 128-bit block cipher standardized beside AES, namely Serpent, Twofish, MARS, RC6, Camellia, ARIA, SEED, SM4, Kuznyechik, and Anubis, sound in design and costing thin library support or confinement to one jurisdiction.
- **Legacy 64-bit block cipher**: cipher whose narrow block caps the data one key may protect, namely IDEA, Blowfish, RC2, RC5, CAST-128, TEA, XTEA, Skipjack, SAFER, Khazad, KASUMI, and MISTY1, retained only where a format or a handset demands it.
- **Lightweight cipher**: primitive designed for small area, low energy, or single-cycle latency, namely PRESENT, GIFT, SIMON, SPECK, SKINNY, PRINCE, Midori, LED, CLEFIA, NOEKEON, HIGHT, Piccolo, TWINE, KATAN, QARMA, MANTIS, SPARX, LEA, and CRAFT, buying that thrift with reduced margins and narrow blocks.
- **Stream cipher**: cipher combining plaintext with a keystream position by position, needing no padding, self-synchronizing when the keystream depends on prior ciphertext, and failing completely if a keystream is ever repeated.
- **Keystream reuse**, also **two-time pad**: encryption of two messages under one keystream, which reveals the exclusive-or of their plaintexts.
- **Linear feedback shift register**, also **LFSR**: register whose next bit is a linear combination of its current bits, extremely cheap in hardware and maximal in period under a primitive feedback polynomial, and predictable alone unless filtered, combined, decimated, or irregularly clocked.
- **ChaCha20**: stream cipher of the ARX form refining Salsa20 with better per-round diffusion, fast and constant-time without cipher hardware, extended through HChaCha20 subkey derivation to the long-nonce XChaCha20 form, and demanding a unique nonce per message.
- **RC4**: byte-oriented stream cipher over a permutation state, extremely compact, biased in its early keystream, leaky in key setup, and only partly repaired by discarding initial bytes.
- **Named stream cipher family**: keystream generator standardized or shortlisted outside the ChaCha line, namely A5/1, A5/2, E0, SNOW 3G, SNOW-V, ZUC, Trivium, Grain, MICKEY, Rabbit, HC-128, and SOSEMANUK, costing either mandated use in one ecosystem or almost no library support.

### Modes of operation and authenticated encryption

- **Mode of operation**: rule for applying a block cipher repeatedly to a message of any length, costing state, an initial value, and a set of failure modes the cipher alone does not have.
- **Electronic codebook mode**, also **ECB**: mode encrypting each block independently, trivially parallel and leaking which plaintext blocks repeat, which is enough to expose the shape of structured data.
- **Cipher block chaining**, also **CBC**: mode exclusive-oring each plaintext block into the previous ciphertext before encryption, needing an unpredictable initial value and serial encryption, and in the propagating variant giving up random access entirely.
- **Cipher feedback mode**, also **CFB**: mode turning a block cipher into a self-synchronizing stream cipher, removing padding and keeping encryption serial.
- **Output feedback mode**, also **OFB**: mode generating keystream by iterating the cipher on its own output, removing padding and forbidding any reuse of the starting value.
- **Counter mode**, also **CTR**: mode encrypting a nonce and position counter to produce keystream, fully parallel with random access and catastrophic if a counter block ever repeats.
- **Initialization vector**, also **IV**, also **nonce**: nonsecret starting value making repeated encryption of one plaintext differ, drawn randomly or counted upward, costing bandwidth per message and strict uniqueness discipline across every sender.
- **PKCS number seven padding**: filling of a final block with bytes equal to the pad length, simple to strip and a classic source of oracle leakage, beside the X9.23, ISO 7816-4 bit, and zero padding alternatives that trade ambiguity against handling cost.
- **Ciphertext stealing**, also **CBC-CS**: ending a chaining mode on a partial block without expansion by borrowing bits from the previous block, saving the padding bytes and complicating every implementation.
- **XTS**, also **XEX-based tweaked codebook mode with ciphertext stealing**: disk encryption mode deriving a tweak per sector through an exclusive-or mask, giving random access and no integrity or freshness, superseding LRW and the salted ESSIV workaround.
- **Wide-block mode**: sector-wide encryption in which any ciphertext change scrambles the whole block, realized by EME, CMC, and accordion cipher modes, costing two passes over the data and no parallelism.
- **Key wrapping**: encryption of one key under another with integrity and a bound usage field, standardized as AES key wrap with its padded form and as tweakable key wrap, costing a distinct algorithm, a key hierarchy, and several passes.
- **Authenticated encryption with associated data**, also **AEAD**, also **AE**: single primitive giving confidentiality and integrity together and binding cleartext header bytes to the ciphertext, removing composition mistakes and costing tag bytes, nonce discipline, and a decision about what counts as associated data.
- **Encrypt-then-MAC**: composition authenticating the ciphertext so forgeries are rejected before decryption, generically secure and costing two keys and two passes, against the fragile plaintext-first and side-by-side orders that force decryption first or leak plaintext equality.
- **Galois counter mode**, also **GCM**: authenticated encryption pairing counter mode with the GHASH polynomial authenticator, very fast where carryless multiply exists, usable in authentication-only GMAC form, and catastrophic on nonce reuse.
- **AES-GCM-SIV**: nonce-misuse-resistant authenticated encryption deriving per-message keys and authenticating with the POLYVAL hash, safer under repeated nonces and slower than plain counter modes because it makes two passes.
- **Synthetic initialization vector**, also **SIV**, also **deterministic authenticated encryption**, also **DAE**: construction deriving the starting value from the message itself, needing no nonce at all, safe for key wrapping, and costing two passes, no streaming, and visible equality of repeated messages.
- **CCM**, also **counter with CBC-MAC**: authenticated encryption stacking counter mode over a CBC authenticator, widely standardized in constrained profiles and requiring the message length in advance, beside EAX which accepts streaming input at the same doubled cipher work.
- **OCB mode**: single-pass parallel authenticated encryption over a tweakable cipher, near raw cipher speed and held back for years by patents.
- **ChaCha20-Poly1305**: authenticated encryption pairing the ChaCha20 keystream with the Poly1305 authenticator, fast without cipher hardware and fatally broken by a repeated nonce.
- **Ascon**: sponge-based lightweight authenticated cipher and hash family standardized for constrained devices, compact in hardware and slower than AES modes on large processors, chosen over the Elephant, GIFT-COFB, Grain-128AEAD, ISAP, PHOTON-Beetle, Romulus, Sparkle, TinyJAMBU, and Xoodyak finalists.
- **CAESAR portfolio cipher**: authenticated cipher selected by public competition for lightweight, high-performance, or defense-in-depth use, namely AEGIS, Deoxys-II, COLM, ACORN, OCB, and Ascon, beside the MORUS, NORX, Ketje, Keyak, OTR, CLOC, SILC, JAMBU, Tiaoxin, Minalpher, SCREAM, and POET candidates, costing AES round hardware or near-zero deployment.
- **Committing AEAD**: authenticated encryption binding key, nonce, and message so one ciphertext cannot be reinterpreted, reaching robust security under arbitrary misuse at the cost of a longer tag or extra hashing.
- **Online authenticated encryption**: authenticated encryption emitting output as input arrives, which enables streaming, forfeits misuse resistance, and releases unverified plaintext that turns any downstream parser into an oracle.

### Hash functions

- **Cryptographic hash function**: one-way map from any input to a fixed-length digest for which collisions and preimages are infeasible, costing a full pass over the data.
- **Merkle-Damgard construction**: iteration of a compression function over padded blocks with the message length in the final block, inheriting collision resistance from its core, exposing internal state in its output, and refined by wide-pipe states and by the HAIFA counter and salt.
- **Davies-Meyer construction**: compression function encrypting the chaining value under the message block and adding the input back, cheap and exposing fixed points, beside the Matyas-Meyer-Oseas and Miyaguchi-Preneel arrangements that cost a key schedule per block.
- **Sponge construction**: hash from one permutation absorbing input into part of a state and squeezing output from it, where the rate traded against the capacity sets both speed and security, and whose duplex form serves a whole session under strict call ordering.
- **Extendable-output function**, also **XOF**: hash producing output of any requested length, usable as a keystream and needing a length or domain tag to stay unambiguous, standardized as SHAKE with the cSHAKE, TupleHash, and ParallelHash variants.
- **Cryptographic domain separation**: distinct prefixes, customization strings, and a label input for distinct uses of one function so instances behave independently, with the same context binding applied wherever a value could be replayed into another use, costing a convention every caller must follow.
- **Tree hashing**: combination of block digests through a tree, giving parallelism and incremental update at the cost of extra digest storage.
- **Hash-then-sign**: signing a digest rather than a message, keeping signature cost constant and making the scheme only as strong as the hash, hardened by randomized hashing whose target collision resistance holds when the message precedes the hash key, and offered as a prehashed signing mode whose external message representative the signer never sees whole.
- **Cryptographic hash agility**: designing formats so a digest function can be replaced later, whether by algorithm identifiers, by a hash function combiner as one instance of a robust combiner surviving either component's failure, or by constants derived from a publicly explainable source, costing negotiation logic and doubled work.
- **SHA-2**: hash family with 224 to 512 bit outputs over a Merkle-Damgard core, the conservative default everywhere, vulnerable to length extension except in the truncated SHA-512/256 form, and slower on wide processors in its 32-bit member.
- **SHA-3**: standardized sponge family over the Keccak permutation, immune to length extension and slower than SHA-2 in software, with the reduced-round KangarooTwelve offering speed outside the federal standard.
- **BLAKE2**: hash family of the ARX form with keying, salting, and personalization built in, fast on both wide and small platforms, extended by the tree-based parallel BLAKE3, and lacking federal approval.
- **MD5**: 128-bit hash still common in checksums, ruled out of any signature or certificate use by affordable chosen-prefix collisions, alongside the broken MD2, MD4, RIPEMD, SHA-0, HAVAL, Snefru, Tiger, and Panama designs and the retired SHA-1.
- **Whirlpool**: 512-bit hash from an AES-like cipher in Miyaguchi-Preneel mode, standardized and slow in software, beside the Skein, Grostl, JH, and MD6 competition finalists, the regional Streebog and SM3 standards, and the constrained-device Ascon-Hash, PHOTON, SPONGENT, Quark, and Lesamnta-LW designs.

### Message authentication and key derivation

- **Message authentication code**, also **MAC**: short keyed tag proving a message reached the verifier unmodified from a holder of the key, costing a shared secret and tag bytes whose length trades bandwidth against per-attempt forgery odds.
- **HMAC**: nested keyed hash with inner and outer padding, provably secure from a weak assumption on its hash and costing two extra compression calls, replacing the secret-prefix and secret-suffix constructions that length extension and offline collision search break.
- **KMAC**: sponge-based authenticator built from cSHAKE with the key and lengths encoded, immune to length extension and running at sponge speed.
- **CMAC**, also **OMAC1**: block cipher authenticator masking the final block with two derived subkeys, standardized and inherently serial, superseding plain CBC-MAC and its EMAC, XCBC, TMAC, and retail banking variants.
- **PMAC**: parallelizable block cipher authenticator using per-block offsets, scaling across cores, joined by LightMAC for length-independent security and ZMAC for tweakable primitives.
- **Poly1305**: one-time polynomial authenticator over a prime field, near-optimal in speed as a Wegman-Carter construction over an almost-universal hash family, and fatally broken if its per-message value repeats.
- **SipHash**: keyed short-input pseudorandom function built against hash flooding, fast on tiny messages and too short in output to serve as a general authenticator, beside the microcontroller-oriented Chaskey.
- **Key derivation function**, also **KDF**: function turning one secret into several keys with stated independence, safest when it first extracts entropy into a uniform key and then expands it, costing a context convention at every call site.
- **HKDF**: extract-and-expand derivation over HMAC where a salt concentrates entropy and a counter with an info string generates output, cheap and useless unless callers supply distinct context labels.
- **Counter mode key derivation**: generation of key blocks by keyed hashing of an incrementing counter with fixed context, parallel and needing careful field encoding, beside the serial feedback, double-pipeline, X9.63, and concatenation forms that turn an agreement's shared secret value and other information string into keys.
- **Password hashing function**: deliberately slow one-way function storing a password verifier, tuned by a work factor and iteration count and mixed with a per-record salt and optionally a site-wide secret pepper, costing login latency and server memory.
- **PBKDF2**: password derivation iterating HMAC many times, supported everywhere and cheap for an attacker with parallel hardware, beside its superseded predecessor and the password-based encryption modes built on it.
- **bcrypt**: password hash built on an expensive Blowfish key schedule, resistant to plain hardware parallelism and truncating input near seventy-two bytes.
- **Argon2**: password hashing competition winner with memory, time, and parallelism parameters, strongly memory-hard in its data-dependent, data-independent, and recommended hybrid orderings, requiring tuning per deployment.
- **Memory-hard function**: function whose evaluation needs a large working array so attackers must spend silicon area rather than gates, realized by scrypt with its ROMix core and by yescrypt, balloon hashing, Catena, Lyra2, and Makwa, and demanding that same memory from the defender.
- **NT hash**: unsalted MD4 digest of a password used by Windows authentication, trivial to crack offline and required by legacy protocols, alongside the LM, crypt, MD5-crypt, and SHA-crypt hashes kept only for old accounts.

### Randomness and generators

- **Cryptographically secure pseudorandom number generator**, also **CSPRNG**, also **deterministic random bit generator**, also **DRBG**: generator whose output is indistinguishable from random without its seed, standardized as a random bit generator construction of noise source, conditioning, and deterministic expansion with defined reseed limits, and costing a trusted entropy input and protected state.
- **Entropy source**: physical or system process yielding unpredictable data, counted in min-entropy rather than the overstating Shannon measure, assessed by entropy source validation on an independent and identically distributed track, and costing live entropy source access, a vetted conditioning component, and far more samples per bit of full entropy output.
- **Health test**: continuous check that a noise source still behaves as characterized, by repetition count, sliding-window proportion, and a restart test, catching failures at the price of occasional false alarms.
- **Seeding**: absorption of enough entropy before any output is produced, stated as a seed material requirement met at generator instantiation with a personalization value, repeated under a reseeding policy to recover from state compromise, and costing a blocked startup until the requirement is met.
- **Backtracking resistance**, also **prediction resistance**: properties that a captured generator state reveals neither earlier output nor, once fresh entropy is mixed in, later output, whose absence is exploited by state compromise extension.
- **CTR-DRBG**: standardized generator running a block cipher in counter mode, fast with cipher hardware, beside the conservative Hash and HMAC variants, the pool-based Fortuna and Yarrow designs, the slow provable Blum Blum Shub and Blum-Micali generators, the X9.31 construction, and the trapdoored Dual EC DRBG.
- **Hardware random number generator**, also **TRNG**: circuit deriving bits from physical noise such as oscillator jitter, exposed through instructions like RDRAND and RDSEED, giving true entropy and costing health testing plus trust in an unverifiable core.
- **getrandom interface**: operating system call returning kernel generator bytes and blocking until the pool is seeded, safe by default and stalling at early boot, beside the always-available device and the arc4random wrapper whose behavior differs across platforms.
- **Fork safety of a generator**: guarantee that a process and its child never emit identical output, which requires reseeding or a generation counter and whose absence produces duplicate keys from cloned images, containers, and entropy-starved boots.
- **Rejection sampling**: discarding out-of-range draws to sample uniformly from a range that is not a power of two, removing modular reduction bias at an unpredictable running time.
- **Statistical randomness test suite**: battery of tests checking output for detectable structure, cheap to run and never evidence of cryptographic strength.

### Public-key primitives and hard problems

- **Public-key cryptography**, also **asymmetric cryptography**: cryptography with separate public and private keys, built on trapdoor permutations and other one-way structures, removing shared-secret setup at a large computational and key-validation cost.
- **Key encapsulation mechanism**, also **KEM**, also **KEM-DEM paradigm**: asymmetric algorithm producing a random symmetric key with its ciphertext, paired with authenticated symmetric encryption of the payload, giving public-key convenience at symmetric speed and requiring two sound layers.
- **RSA**: public-key scheme whose security rests on the difficulty of factoring a modulus built from two secret primes, universally supported and slow at safe key sizes, and never usable in its unpadded textbook form.
- **Optimal asymmetric encryption padding**, also **OAEP**: randomized RSA encryption padding with provable chosen-ciphertext security, costing two mask generation passes and exact implementation.
- **PKCS number one version one point five padding**: legacy RSA padding for encryption and signature, still widely deployed, fragile under any decryption oracle, and easy to verify sloppily on the signature side.
- **CRT-RSA**: private RSA operation performed modulo each prime and recombined, roughly four times faster, open to fault injection, and usually blinded with a random factor to remove input-dependent timing at extra exponentiations.
- **Coppersmith attack**: use of lattice reduction to find small roots of modular polynomials, which breaks RSA when part of a key or message is known and underlies the Wiener, Boneh-Durfee, Hastad broadcast, Franklin-Reiter, short pad, and partial key exposure results.
- **ROCA vulnerability**: weakness in a library's structured prime generation that lets an attacker factor affected moduli, beside the batch greatest common divisor attack that factors many keys at once from primes accidentally shared between them.
- **General number field sieve**: the fastest general factoring algorithm and the one that sets RSA key size guidance, preceded by trial division, Pollard rho, the p minus one and p plus one methods, Fermat and continued fraction factoring, the quadratic sieve, and the elliptic curve method for medium factors.
- **Miller-Rabin test**: probabilistic primality test whose error falls exponentially in the rounds it costs, combined with Lucas testing in the Baillie-PSW check and used with trial division to generate probable primes, safe primes, or provable primes at higher cost.
- **Discrete logarithm problem**: task of recovering an exponent from a group element and a generator, attacked generically by baby-step giant-step, Pollard rho, and the kangaroo method, reduced over smooth orders by Pohlig-Hellman, and solved subexponentially in finite fields by index calculus with reusable precomputation.
- **Diffie-Hellman key agreement**: derivation of a shared secret from exchanged public group elements, authenticating nothing by itself, named by a key agreement scheme notation counting each side's ephemeral and static pairs, so ephemeral Diffie-Hellman and elliptic curve ephemeral Diffie-Hellman give forward secrecy, a one-pass key agreement gives it on one side, and static Diffie-Hellman gives none, costing an exponentiation per session.
- **Public key validation**: invalid point validation and a subgroup membership check on a received value before use, with group parameter validation against a named group requirement, refusing a degenerate public key and applying an all-zero shared secret check, which blocks small subgroup confinement and the invalid curve attack that unvalidated key exchange parameters invite, and costing an exponentiation per exchange.
- **ElGamal encryption**: randomized public-key encryption from Diffie-Hellman in a prime-order group, homomorphic and malleable and doubling ciphertext size, hardened by Cramer-Shoup for standard-model chosen-ciphertext security and packaged as the DHIES and ECIES hybrid schemes.
- **Elliptic curve cryptography**, also **ECC**: public-key cryptography in the group of points on a curve, giving short keys and costing careful parameter handling, whose scalar multiplication must be made uniform through a Montgomery ladder or fixed windows rather than plain double-and-add, and sped by the GLV method where a curve endomorphism splits the scalar.
- **Edwards curve**: curve form with complete unified addition formulas that remove exceptional cases, beside the x-only Montgomery form for agreement and the short Weierstrass form most standards use, converted between forms at the cost of a birational map.
- **Cofactor**: ratio of a curve's order to its prime subgroup order, small by design and a source of subtle protocol bugs, whose cofactor handling clears it by multiplication or avoids it with a prime-order group encoding such as Ristretto, at extra arithmetic and changed results in some protocols.
- **Curve25519**: Montgomery curve with rigid parameter provenance and a cofactor of eight, exposed as the X25519 agreement function, beside Curve448 for margin, the mandated NIST P-256, P-384, and P-521 curves, secp256k1, Brainpool, and the SM2 curve, each costing either compliance friction or speed.
- **Supersingular curve**: curve with a very small embedding degree, unusable for plain curve cryptography and central to pairings and isogenies, alongside anomalous and binary-field curves excluded by parameter checks after the SSSA, MOV, Frey-Ruck, and Weil descent reductions.
- **Hash-to-curve**: deterministic map from a byte string to a curve point, needed by pairing and password protocols, implemented in constant time through the simplified Shallue-van de Woestijne-Ulas map or the invertible Elligator encoding, costing a few field operations.
- **Bilinear pairing**: map taking two group elements to a third linearly in each argument, enabling aggregation and advanced schemes, computed by Miller's algorithm and a final exponentiation over pairing-friendly curves such as BN and BLS12-381, resting on bilinear, q-strong, and knowledge of exponent assumptions, generalized to a multilinear map on more inputs, at heavy cost.
- **Montgomery multiplication**: modular multiplication in a transformed domain that avoids trial division, paired with Barrett reduction, modular reduction by Solinas primes, Karatsuba multiplication, Toom-Cook multiplication, Schonhage-Strassen multiplication, Kronecker substitution, square-and-multiply with sliding windows, and the Tonelli-Shanks algorithm or Cipolla's algorithm for square roots, costing conversions and operand-independent care.

### Digital signatures

- **Digital signature**: value only a private key holder can produce and anyone holding the public half can check, costing key distribution, verification time, and a private key whose exposure forfeits all authenticity.
- **RSA-PSS**, also **probabilistic signature scheme**: randomized RSA signature padding with a tight security proof, preferred for new work and unsupported by some old verifiers, alongside the full domain hash construction it generalizes.
- **DSA**, also **Digital Signature Algorithm**: discrete logarithm signature over a prime-order subgroup descended from the ElGamal scheme, compact in output and fatal if its per-signature secret ever fails.
- **ECDSA**: elliptic curve form of DSA, standard in every ecosystem, malleable unless verifiers accept only the smaller of two equivalent scalars, and dependent on a perfect secret value per signature.
- **Per-signature nonce**: secret scalar used once per DSA or ECDSA signature, where signature nonce reuse yields the private key by algebra and a biased signature nonce yields it by lattice reduction across many signatures.
- **Deterministic nonce generation**, also **deterministic nonce derivation**: derivation of the per-signature scalar from key and message by a keyed hash, removing dependence on the generator and costing the randomization that protects against fault attacks unless fresh entropy is hedged back in.
- **Schnorr signature**: signature from a three-move proof of knowledge made noninteractive, short and provable with a tight reduction, and long held back by patents.
- **EdDSA**: Schnorr-style signature over Edwards curves with deterministic nonces and the public key hashed into the message, fast and fixed to one curve and hash, instantiated as Ed25519 and Ed448 and inconsistent between cofactored and cofactorless verification.
- **BLS signature**: pairing-based signature that is short, deterministic, and aggregatable into one value for many signers, requiring pairing arithmetic, a hash to curve, and proofs of possession to stop rogue key attacks, and verifiable in batches that report only that the batch failed.
- **Multisignature**: single signature proving that a fixed group of signers all approved one message, produced by protocols such as MuSig and MuSig2 that yield an ordinary signature, costing interactive rounds and strict nonce commitment.
- **Blind signature**: signature on a message the signer cannot read, realized as blind RSA signing from Chaum's blinding, measured by one-more forgery security and broken by the ROS attack across concurrent sessions, and costing careful blinding validation plus the signer's loss of any content check.
- **Ring signature**: signature proving that one member of an ad hoc set signed without revealing which, giving anonymity at a size growing with the set and revealing repeat signers only in the linkable variant.
- **Group signature**: signature on behalf of a managed group that an authority can open, giving accountable anonymity and requiring that trusted manager plus tracing keys in the traceable variant.
- **Designated verifier signature**: signature convincing one chosen party only, giving deniability and losing transferability, beside undeniable signatures needing the signer's cooperation, chameleon signatures resting on a trapdoor hash, sanitizable signatures with an editing key, and signature key blinding for unlinkability.
- **Forward-secure signature**: scheme evolving its private key so past signatures stay valid after a compromise, requiring strict key evolution and epoch tracking.
- **Signcryption**: single primitive delivering confidentiality and origin authentication together, costing a joint proof of two goals that a naive composition of the two would not give.

### Post-quantum cryptography

- **Post-quantum cryptography**: cryptography designed to resist quantum computers, selected through a multiround public standardization process that rates parameter sets by comparison with breaking AES or SHA-2, and costing larger keys plus young implementations.
- **Shor's algorithm**: quantum algorithm factoring integers and computing discrete logarithms in polynomial time, which ends RSA and elliptic curve security, where Grover search and Simon period finding only halve symmetric strength or break particular constructions.
- **Learning with errors**, also **LWE**: problem of recovering a secret from noisy linear equations, the workhorse lattice assumption together with short integer solution, learning with rounding, and the NTRU ratio assumption, made compact and structurally riskier over ideal and module lattices.
- **Lattice reduction**: transformation of a basis into a shorter and more orthogonal one, the main lattice attack tool through LLL and BKZ with sieving inside, joined by Babai rounding and the Babai nearest plane algorithm and by Kannan embedding onto the unique shortest vector problem, whose predicted cost from the root Hermite factor and the Gaussian heuristic sets every parameter set.
- **Discrete Gaussian sampling**: drawing of lattice noise from a bell-shaped distribution over integers, essential to trapdoor signatures and identity-based schemes, replaced by centered binomial noise in key encapsulation, its precision bounded by a Renyi divergence argument, and costing a classic side-channel hazard.
- **ML-KEM**, also **Kyber**: standardized module lattice key encapsulation over the passively secure K-PKE core, with public matrix seed expansion, an encapsulation key held publicly and a decapsulation key kept secret, fast with number theoretic transform arithmetic and larger in key and ciphertext than curve agreement.
- **ML-DSA**, also **Dilithium**: standardized module lattice signature using Fiat-Shamir with aborts, challenge ball sampling for a sparse polynomial, high and low bit decomposition of the commitment with a hint vector repairing it, and a norm bound check before release, fast to verify with kilobyte keys and signatures.
- **FN-DSA**, also **Falcon**: standardized signature in the GPV framework over an NTRU trapdoor, signing by fast Fourier sampling of a discrete Gaussian, the most compact post-quantum signature and the hardest to implement because it needs reproducible floating-point arithmetic.
- **FrodoKEM**: unstructured learning with errors encapsulation avoiding all ring structure for maximum conservatism at very large keys, beside NTRU Prime and its streamlined form, the superseded NewHope, SABER, LAC, Round5, and Three Bears candidates, and the withdrawn BLISS signature.
- **Decryption failure**: event in which a lattice ciphertext decodes incorrectly because noise exceeded the bound, exploitable by reaction attacks that observe which ciphertexts fail, and suppressed by the Fujisaki-Okamoto transform with an implicit rejection secret at one extra encryption.
- **Classic McEliece**: code-based encapsulation hiding a decodable Goppa code behind a scrambling transform, unbroken since 1978 and megabyte-scale in public keys, beside its Niederreiter dual, the quasi-cyclic BIKE and HQC, the rank-metric ROLLO and RQC, and the Wave signature.
- **Information set decoding**: best known generic attack on code-based schemes, exponential in the code parameters, refined from Prange's algorithm through Stern's algorithm, ball collision decoding, and the BJMM algorithm that sets code parameters.
- **Unbalanced oil and vinegar**, also **UOV**: multivariate signature splitting variables so signing is easy, giving short signatures with very large public keys, compressed by QR-UOV, SNOVA, and MAYO after Rainbow, LUOV, HFE, and GeMSS lost ground to key recovery attacks.
- **Isogeny-based cryptography**: cryptography built on maps between elliptic curves, extremely compact and shaken when the Castryck-Decru attack on torsion points broke SIDH and its SIKE encapsulation, leaving the group-action CSIDH and the slow SQIsign signature.
- **MPC-in-the-head**: proof technique simulating a multiparty computation, or VOLE in the head, so a signature rests on symmetric primitives or a fresh assumption, used by Picnic, FAEST, AIMer, SDitH, RYDE, MQOM, and Mirath over MinRank, permuted kernel, code equivalence, and lattice isomorphism, at kilobyte signatures.
- **Hash-based signature**: signature resting only on hash properties, built from Lamport and Winternitz one-time keys and few-time HORS or FORS trees under a Merkle root, standardized as stateful XMSS and LMS whose leaf index must never repeat and as stateless SLH-DSA with far larger signatures.

### Splitting trust across parties

- **Secret sharing**: division of a secret into shares revealing nothing below a threshold, classically by Shamir's polynomial with Lagrange reconstruction and also by Blakley hyperplanes, residue and additive schemes, replicated sharing, ramp secret sharing, packed secret sharing, hierarchical secret sharing, and a weighted threshold structure, costing an access rule fixed at sharing time.
- **Verifiable secret sharing**, also **VSS**: sharing in which each holder checks its share against published commitments, Feldman's exponentiated coefficients or Pedersen's perfectly hiding form, blocking a cheating dealer and supporting cheater detection, robust reconstruction, and proactive refresh and committee resharing, available as asynchronous verifiable secret sharing, at extra proofs and a live protocol among holders.
- **Distributed key generation**, also **DKG**: protocol producing a shared private key with a public counterpart that no party ever holds whole, as in Gennaro-Jarecki-Krawczyk-Rabin key generation and asynchronous distributed key generation, requiring rounds, broadcast, and complaint handling, and open to a biased key generation attack by a late aborter unless contributions are committed first.
- **Threshold signature**: signature produced by a quorum of key shareholders and verified as an ordinary one, easy as threshold Schnorr signing through FROST and as threshold BLS signing by interpolation, heavy for ECDSA through GG18, GG20, the DKLs protocol, the CMP protocol, and Lindell two-party signing, costing signing rounds, presignature state, and identifiable abort machinery.
- **Secure multiparty computation**, also **MPC**: protocol letting parties compute a function of private inputs while revealing only the result, stated against semi-honest, covert, or malicious adversaries with an honest or dishonest majority, narrowed to secure aggregation where only a sum is revealed, and costing very high communication plus a choice between abort and guaranteed output.
- **Garbled circuit**: encrypted Boolean circuit one party evaluates blindly on encrypted input labels, generic in scope and large per gate, shrunk by point-and-permute, free-XOR, row reduction, half-gates, three-halves garbling, stacked garbling, and arithmetic garbling, and hardened by authenticated garbling, LEGO garbling, dual execution, or cut-and-choose at multiplied cost.
- **Oblivious transfer**, also **OT**: protocol giving a receiver one of the sender's messages while neither learns the other's choice or contents, based on the Naor-Pinkas transfer or Chou-Orlandi transfer and multiplied by IKNP, the KOS extension, SoftSpokenOT, and the silent Ferret protocol, costing public-key work per base transfer and heavy local computation to avoid it.
- **Beaver triple**: preprocessed shared triple whose product relation makes an online multiplication cheap, consumed one per multiplication, produced by MASCOT, Overdrive, TinyOT, Gilboa multiplication, or a pseudorandom correlation generator emitting oblivious linear evaluation and vector oblivious linear evaluation correlations, checked by a sacrifice step, and shifting cost into an offline phase rather than removing it.
- **SPDZ protocol**: dishonest-majority computation over shares under information-theoretic share authentication with preprocessed randomness, fast online and expensive offline, beside the GMW, BGW, BMR, and Damgard-Nielsen protocols with player elimination, and joined to mixed-protocol conversion where share conversion and arithmetic to boolean conversion move values between representations.
- **Oblivious pseudorandom function**, also **OPRF**: evaluation of a keyed function where the server never learns the input and the client never learns the key, proven with a key-use proof in the verifiable form, shared across holders as a distributed pseudorandom function beside threshold symmetric encryption and threshold Paillier decryption, costing a round trip and public-key work.
- **Private set intersection**: protocol revealing only the elements two private sets share, with cardinality-only and union variants, circuit private set intersection, unbalanced private set intersection, and a Diffie-Hellman based intersection open to guessing on small item spaces, costing bandwidth that scales with set size.
- **Private information retrieval**, also **PIR**: fetching of a record without revealing which one, as single-server private retrieval at the price of homomorphic work over the whole database or as multi-server private retrieval across noncolluding servers, with batch private retrieval and keyword private retrieval, extended by function secret sharing and distributed point functions.
- **Oblivious RAM**, also **ORAM**: reshuffling of memory on every access so the pattern of reads and writes reveals nothing about the program, built as square-root oblivious RAM or path oblivious RAM over a tree, extended to garbled RAM and to doubly oblivious access that hides the client's own accesses, costing logarithmic overhead per access.

### Commitments, proofs, and structured encryption

- **Commitment scheme**: mechanism fixing a party to a value while keeping it secret until opening, perfectly or computationally hiding and binding, run as commit and reveal where the value follows its placeholder, realized by Pedersen group elements, salted hashes, or trapdoor forms one holder can reopen, costing a stored opening and an extra message.
- **Merkle tree**, also **hash tree**: tree of hashes whose root commits to every leaf, giving membership proofs logarithmic in leaf count plus absence and append-only consistency proofs in sparse, mountain range, and prefix variants, costing the tree be built and kept.
- **Polynomial commitment**: commitment to a polynomial with proofs of its value at chosen points, constant-sized through the pairing-based KZG scheme, setup-free through inner product arguments, cheapened by a batched opening and a linearization step that collapses many claims into one, and generalized to vector commitments, Verkle trees, and accumulators, all at public-key cost per proof.
- **Zero-knowledge proof**: protocol convincing a verifier a statement holds while revealing nothing else, sound statistically or only computationally, and proven by an extractor that recovers the witness by rewinding under the forking lemma and a simulator that produces transcripts without it, costing rounds or heavy proving work.
- **Sigma protocol**: three-move commit, challenge, and response proof of knowledge with special soundness, the shape of the Fiat-Shamir identification protocol, Guillou-Quisquater identification, the Okamoto identification protocol, the Chaum-Pedersen protocol, and the Schnorr identification scheme, composable into disjunctive and conjunctive proofs, and needing a fresh unpredictable challenge each run.
- **Fiat-Shamir transform**: replacement of the verifier's challenge with a hash of the whole transcript, removing interaction and demanding transcript binding over every public input, since the frozen heart vulnerability forges proofs wherever one is omitted.
- **Range proof**: proof that a committed value lies in an interval, beside shuffle proofs, commit-and-prove links, verifiable encryption, and deterministic nullifiers that prevent a commitment being used twice, each adding proof bytes per value.
- **zk-SNARK**: succinct noninteractive argument with tiny proofs and fast verification, realized by Groth16, PLONK, Pinocchio, Marlin, and Sonic, paid for by heavy proving and a reference string whose generation secret must be destroyed in a ceremony, with universal and updatable setups easing that burden.
- **zk-STARK**: transparent succinct argument built from hashes and Reed-Solomon proximity testing through the FRI protocol, needing no trusted setup and producing far larger proofs, beside Aurora, Ligero, Brakedown, Basefold, Binius, Halo2, Spartan, Hyrax, Libra, and Virgo.
- **Circuit arithmetization**: expression of a computation as algebraic constraints a proof system consumes, as rank-one constraint systems, quadratic arithmetic programs, a multilinear extension or low-degree extension, or PLONKish layouts over a Lagrange basis with custom gates, lookup arguments, a copy constraint enforced by a grand product argument, and a quotient polynomial divided by a vanishing polynomial, priced in prover time.
- **Recursive proof composition**: verification of one proof inside the circuit of another, giving incrementally verifiable computation, proof-carrying data, and aggregation, cheapened by folding schemes, cycles of proof-friendly curves, and an arithmetization-friendly hash such as MiMC, Poseidon, Rescue, Griffin, or Anemoi, at a narrowed parameter choice.
- **Fully homomorphic encryption**, also **FHE**: encryption permitting computation on ciphertexts, kept alive by gate bootstrapping or programmable bootstrapping as the noise budget drains and tuned by hybrid key switching, relinearization, modulus switching, and packing, realized by additive Paillier, Goldwasser-Micali, Benaloh, and Damgard-Jurik schemes and by GSW, BGV, BFV, CKKS, and TFHE, at thousandfold slowdown.
- **Approximate homomorphic arithmetic**: homomorphic evaluation over encrypted real numbers where each multiplication is followed by rescaling down a modulus chain, held in a residue number system representation, and defended by noise flooding against indistinguishability under chosen-ciphertext-with-decryption-oracle attack, costing precision loss and a homomorphic parameter standard to pin choices.
- **Identity-based encryption**, also **IBE**: encryption where any string serves as a public key, removing certificate distribution and giving a private key generator that can decrypt everything, extended by attribute-based, predicate, and functional encryption whose keys release only what a policy permits.
- **Proxy re-encryption**: conversion of ciphertexts from one key to another by a semi-trusted proxy that never sees plaintext, simplifying sharing beside broadcast encryption, traitor tracing, updatable and puncturable schemes, and costing re-encryption key management.
- **Anonymous credential**: credential proving attributes without revealing identity or linking two showings, built on Camenisch-Lysyanskaya, BBS, or U-Prove signatures with selective disclosure and predicate proofs, issued as unlinkable tokens under anonymous rate limiting, and costing heavy proof machinery plus issuer cooperation.
- **Verifiable random function**, also **VRF**: keyed function whose output looks random yet carries a public proof that it is the only output for that key and input, shareable across a quorum and usable as a randomness beacon, at signature-level cost per evaluation.
- **Proof of work**: demonstration that effort was spent, as a hash below a difficulty target, a memory-hard variant, a client puzzle, or a serial verifiable delay function or time-lock puzzle, and in storage form as proofs of space, replication, and retrievability, wasting the resource it prices.
- **Format-preserving encryption**, also **FPE**: encryption whose output keeps the input's alphabet and length, standardized as the FF1 and FF3-1 Feistel modes with cycle walking and rank-then-encipher, fitting legacy fields and leaving no room for randomization or a tag.
- **Order-preserving encryption**, also **OPE**: encryption whose ciphertexts sort like their plaintexts, allowing range queries and leaking the whole ordering, beside deterministic encryption that reveals repeats, order-revealing and property-preserving variants, blind indexes, and tokenization that replaces a value with an unrelated surrogate.
- **Searchable symmetric encryption**, also **SSE**: encryption letting a server match keyword tokens against ciphertext, extended to public-key keyword search, dynamic searchable encryption, multi-user searchable encryption, and whole structured data, and costing access-pattern and volume leakage that reconstruction attacks exploit unless forward privacy, backward privacy, a volume-hiding scheme, and response padding are paid for.
- **No-cloning theorem**: result that an unknown quantum state cannot be copied, which underwrites quantum money and quantum coin flipping and, with the quantum bit commitment impossibility result, bounds what quantum protocols can achieve.
- **Quantum key distribution**: establishment of a shared secret whose secrecy rests on the no-cloning theorem, realized by the BB84 protocol, the B92 protocol, and entangled E91 protocol, by the decoy state method against the photon number splitting attack, and as continuous variable key distribution, measurement-device-independent key distribution, and device-independent key distribution, costing dedicated links, short distances, error reconciliation, and privacy amplification.

### Secure channels and protocols

- **Secure channel**: abstraction in which two endpoints exchange messages outsiders cannot read, alter, reorder, or replay, established by a key agreement or key transport run that also names the parties and confirms the derived key explicitly, closed by a close notification whose omission is a truncation attack, and costing credentials, round trips, and per-message state at both ends.
- **Key confirmation**: explicit exchange proving both parties derived the same key, as unilateral key confirmation when one side demonstrates possession and bilateral key confirmation when both do, joined by assurance of public key validity, assurance of private key possession, and assurance of domain parameter validity, and costing an added round trip.
- **Forward secrecy**, also **perfect forward secrecy**, also **PFS**: property that later seizure of a long-term private key does not decrypt traffic recorded earlier, because the session secret came from ephemeral values that were destroyed.
- **Post-compromise security**, also **future secrecy**, also **break-in recovery**: property that a session heals after an attacker reads a party's state, once fresh key material is exchanged again.
- **Channel binding**: tying an inner authentication exchange to the outer channel carrying it through an exporter secret, so captured credentials cannot be presented elsewhere, and costing both endpoints implementing the same recipe.
- **Rekeying**, also **key update**: replacement of live traffic keys mid-connection so no key protects too much data, signalled by a key update message, and costing brief state duplication and bookkeeping for records in flight.
- **Transport Layer Security**, also **TLS**, also **SSL**, also **Secure Sockets Layer**: the standard protocol wrapping a byte stream in authentication, encryption, and integrity through a handshake protocol, a record protocol, and an alert protocol whose fatal alert and warning alert taxonomy names every failure, with a labelled key schedule deriving an early secret, handshake secret, master secret, and per-direction traffic secrets, costing handshake latency and version maintenance.
- **ClientHello**: opening handshake message offering versions, suites, groups, key shares, and extensions, answered by a server hello or a retry request and followed by certificate, transcript signature, and finished messages, with a middlebox compatibility mode sending a dummy ChangeCipherSpec so old inspection devices let the connection pass.
- **Cipher suite**: named bundle of key exchange, authentication, and encryption algorithms used for one connection, settled by cipher suite negotiation under a cipher suite policy, offering PSK with ephemeral key exchange or an RSA key transport handshake, costing a test matrix and downgrade exposure wherever an export cipher suite, anonymous cipher suite, or null cipher suite stays enabled.
- **Transport Layer Security extension**: typed field in a handshake message negotiating one capability, covering the supported_versions extension, supported groups extension, signature algorithms extension, signature_algorithms_cert extension, status_request extension, encrypt_then_mac extension, max_fragment_length extension and record size limit, padding extension and record padding, cookie extension, early_data extension, extended master secret, renegotiation indication extension, and the retired heartbeat extension, costing a parser for each one.
- **Server Name Indication**, also **SNI**: client-supplied hostname in the opening message so one address serves many certificates, leaking the requested site to every observer unless the sensitive fields are encrypted to a published key, which costs key distribution and a fallback path.
- **Session resumption**: reconnection using an earlier connection's secret to skip authentication work, held either in server-side state or in a client-held ticket sealed under a rotated ticket key and presented with a PSK binder computed from a binder key over an obfuscated ticket age, costing forward secrecy for the resumed pair unless fresh exchange is mixed in.
- **Zero round-trip time data**, also **0-RTT**, also **early data**: application bytes sent in the first flight under a resumption key, with an early exporter secret available to the application, costing replay exposure that only an anti-replay cache and a narrow freshness window bound, and an early data forward secrecy gap for those bytes.
- **Mutual TLS**, also **mTLS**: requirement that both endpoints present certificates so service calls authenticate cryptographically, extended by post-handshake requests, delegated credentials, bare public keys, and the workload certificate issued as a SPIFFE Verifiable Identity Document, and costing issuance, rotation, and revocation for every client.
- **Datagram Transport Layer Security**, also **DTLS**: adaptation of the stream protocol to unreliable packet transport with explicit sequence numbers and retransmission, defended against flooding by a cookie exchange, and paralleled by QUIC which encrypts nearly every packet field, costing reordering, amplification, and header protection work.
- **Internet Key Exchange**, also **IKE**: negotiation protocol authenticating peers and creating a child security association with traffic selectors that selector narrowing trims, offering a configuration payload, an anti-clogging cookie against flooding, and MOBIKE for address changes, superseding the main mode and quick mode exchanges of its first version, and costing its own state machine and rekeying.
- **IPsec**: network-layer protection applying an encapsulating security payload or authentication-only header in tunnel or transport mode, driven by a security policy database, a security association database, and a peer authorization database, with extended sequence numbers feeding an anti-replay window in a protected tunnel, and costing traversal encapsulation plus a bump in the stack or bump in the wire placement.
- **Noise protocol framework**: construction kit building handshakes from named patterns of key transmission and mixing, identified by a Noise protocol name, run over a CipherState, SymmetricState, and HandshakeState, with pre-shared key and fallback modifiers, rating each message by authentication grade, confidentiality grade, and identity hiding grade, as used by WireGuard, and costing no negotiation or algorithm agility at all.
- **Noise handshake pattern**: pattern token sequence naming which keys each side sends and mixes, from the one-way N pattern and X pattern through the NK pattern, NX pattern, XK pattern, XX pattern, IK pattern, and KK pattern, with a pre-message pattern for known keys, deferred patterns, a PSK modifier, and Noise pipes, costing a choice fixed before any message is sent.
- **STARTTLS**: upgrade of a plaintext application session to an encrypted one on the same port after a command, costing a cleartext phase whose advertisement an attacker can strip, as opportunistic encryption does wherever the offer can be suppressed.
- **Password-authenticated key exchange**, also **PAKE**, also **zero-knowledge password proof**: protocol deriving a strong shared key from a low-entropy secret with nothing usable left for offline guessing, balanced as in SPAKE2 and CPace or augmented as in SRP and OPAQUE where the server stores only a verifier, costing extra rounds and per-user state.
- **Bellare-Rogaway model**: formal setting for key exchange security where an adversary controls sessions and must distinguish a real key from random, extended by the Canetti-Krawczyk models that reveal ephemeral and long-term secrets separately to capture key compromise impersonation and unknown key-share failures.
- **Injective agreement**: strongest level of Lowe's authentication hierarchy, where every completed run matches exactly one run of the peer, above non-injective agreement on roles and data, weak agreement on the peer's participation, and bare aliveness, each stated as a correspondence assertion or trace property whose computational soundness carries it into the computational setting.
- **Freshness**, also **recency**: property that a message belongs to the current run rather than a recorded one, proven by an unpredictable challenge, by a timestamp against a synchronized clock, or by a monotonic counter with a replay window, and costing round trips, clock trust, or per-peer state.
- **Protocol state machine**: explicit enumeration of permitted states and transitions that an implementation follows, whose divergence from the messages actually exchanged lets duplicated, omitted, or out-of-order messages skip checks, and costing code that mirrors the specification exactly.
- **Version negotiation**: agreement on which protocol version and parameters to use, safe only when every negotiated choice is covered by the transcript both sides verify, worked around by GREASE where version intolerance, extension intolerance, and cipher suite ossification break new values, and costing a signaling cipher suite value, downgrade sentinels, and minimum protocol version enforcement that locks out old clients.
- **Formal verification of a protocol**: proof that stated goals hold across every execution against a stated adversary, in a symbolic model with a Dolev-Yao adversary, in a computational model with a bounded algorithm, or in BAN logic, by mechanized protocol analysis with a tool-produced attack trace, up to a machine-checked proof of a handshake and a verified implementation, costing an exact model and expert effort.

### End-to-end messaging and group keys

- **End-to-end encryption**, also **E2EE**: arrangement where only the conversing devices hold keys so the relaying service carries ciphertext it cannot read, costing server-side search, recovery, and abuse handling.
- **Double ratchet**: message keys advancing by one-way derivation from a per-direction chain while the root secret refreshes on every fresh public value from the peer, giving forward secrecy and healing, extended across a user's devices by Sesame, and costing per-device state, retained skipped keys, and desynchronization when chains diverge.
- **Extended Triple Diffie-Hellman**, also **X3DH**: initial agreement mixing long-term, signed medium-term, and one-time published keys so a sender starts while the recipient is offline, extended post-quantum as PQXDH, and costing prekey upkeep plus weaker freshness once the one-time pool is exhausted.
- **Sealed sender**: concealment of the sender's identity from the relaying server by moving it inside the encrypted envelope, part of a wider metadata protection effort that is anonymity preserving, with padding, cover traffic, and message unlinkability, and costing delivery tokens and weaker server-side abuse controls.
- **Deniability**: property that a transcript proves to no third party who wrote it, giving message repudiation over content and participation repudiation over presence, achieved by malleable encryption and published authentication keys as in Off-the-Record messaging, and undercut deliberately by message franking.
- **Key fingerprint**: short human-comparable digest of a public key, shown as a safety number or short authentication string and checked out of band, whose absence leaves trust on first use, and costing a manual step users routinely skip.
- **Continuous group key agreement**, also **CGKA**: group scheme where any member injects fresh secrets so the group key heals, realized by TreeKEM inside Messaging Layer Security through a ratchet tree of path secrets, epochs, proposals, and commits, and costing replicated tree state and strict ordering to prevent group state divergence.
