- **Messaging Layer Security**, also **MLS**: group messaging protocol carried by a delivery service and an authentication service, changing membership through an add proposal, remove proposal, update proposal, or ReInit proposal committed into a new epoch, admitting an external join, identifying members by a basic credential or certificate credential, and costing a secret tree and sender ratchet per member.
- **Group key agreement**: protocol deriving one shared secret among many parties at once, constant-round in the Burmester-Desmedt protocol where each member broadcasts one value derived from its two neighbours, and costing rounds or bandwidth growing with the group.
- **Logical key hierarchy**, also **LKH**: central tree of key encryption keys so removing one member rekeys only its path, cheaper than pairwise fan-out to every member and costing full trust in the server that holds the tree.
- **Insider security**: property that a group member learns nothing about messages sent before it joined or after it was removed, alongside participant consistency, speaker consistency, causality preserving delivery, and a global transcript order that keep every member's view of membership and history the same.

### Identity of keys and certificates

- **Public key infrastructure**, also **PKI**: arrangement of issuers, documents, directories, and rules by which a stranger's public key becomes trustworthy, costing governance, operations, and lifecycle work at every layer.
- **Certification authority**, also **CA**: party whose signature asserts that a named subject controls a certified key, split into an offline root, intermediates, issuing tiers, and registration roles, held to the Baseline Requirements and Network and Certificate System Security Requirements, publishing a certificate policy document on the RFC 3647 framework, and costing custody of keys whose theft breaks everything below.
- **Trust anchor**: key a verifier accepts by direct configuration rather than by validating anything, curated as a root store whose additions, root distrust, and graduated distrust must reach every device, and costing trust store divergence between clients plus total exposure wherever an interception root is installed.
- **X.509 certificate**: signed document binding a public key to a distinguished name with a validity window, an algorithm identifier, and a serial number meeting a serial number entropy requirement, profiled for the internet as the PKIX profile, and costing issuance, renewal, and a dense encoding that parsers get wrong.
- **Subject alternative name**, also **SAN**: extension holding the typed names a certificate covers, checked against the name the client asked for, where a wildcard label matches exactly one level and the untyped common name no longer counts, costing reissuance whenever the name list changes.
- **Certificate extension**: optional typed field carrying constraints, marked critical when an unparsing verifier must reject, covering key usage, path length limits, policy identifiers, and an extended key usage naming a server authentication key purpose, client authentication key purpose, code signing key purpose, email protection key purpose, time stamping key purpose, or OCSP signing key purpose, costing verifier code per field.
- **Name constraints**: extension restricting which name spaces a subordinate authority may issue inside through permitted and excluded subtrees, costing constraints planned before issuance and correct implementation in every verifier before it protects anything.
- **Distinguished Encoding Rules**, also **DER**: canonical binary encoding of certificate structures defined in ASN.1 so a signed object has one byte representation, wrapped as text in PEM and carried in signing request, key bundle, and certificate-set containers, costing strict parsers that reject sloppy encoders.
- **Path validation**: checking of every certificate from the presented one to a configured anchor for signature, dates, names, constraints, and status, driven by an initial policy set, an explicit policy requirement, a maximum path depth, and a valid policy tree that anyPolicy widens, with chain building that backtracks over alternative parents, costing a strict algorithm naive implementations get wrong.
- **Cross-signing**: second authority signing an existing authority's key so chains validate under two anchors, extended by bridge authorities joining separate hierarchies into a mesh rather than a tree, costing path discovery that explores far more branches.
- **Domain validation**, also **DV**: issuance on proof of control over a name, with organization validation and extended validation adding manual vetting under the Extended Validation Guidelines, bounded by a validation data reuse period, high-risk request screening, internal name deprecation, and a reserved address prohibition, and costing oversight of every delegated third party that performs it.
- **Domain control validation**: proof of authority over a name by answering an HTTP challenge, a DNS challenge, a TLS-ALPN challenge, or an email challenge validation, optionally through CNAME delegation for validation, with a fresh random value in domain validation, multi-perspective issuance corroboration, and CAA checking against the Certification Authority Authorization record, costing automation on the name's own infrastructure.
- **Automated Certificate Management Environment**, also **ACME**: protocol in which a client proves domain control under an ACME account key, an ACME order, and an ACME authorization, each request carrying a replay nonce and optionally an external account binding, beside the Certificate Management Protocol, Certificate Management over CMS, and Simple Certificate Enrollment Protocol, costing an agent with credentials on every host.
- **Certificate lifecycle management**: tracking of every certificate from issuance to replacement through certificate automation, certificate renewal, certificate rekey, and certificate reissuance, kept honest by a certificate inventory, expiry monitoring, and linting of certificates, and costing tooling whose absence produces a certificate expiry outage.
- **Certificate revocation list**, also **CRL**: signed list of retired serial numbers, published as a base certificate revocation list with a CRL number and a revocation reason code such as the key compromise reason or a certificate hold, refreshed through a delta certificate revocation list, kept small by CRL partitioning or issued as an indirect certificate revocation list, and costing a growing download.
- **Online Certificate Status Protocol**, also **OCSP**: signed answer about one certificate's status from an OCSP responder or a delegated OCSP responder marked OCSP no-check, freshened by an OCSP nonce, spared the client by OCSP response caching and OCSP stapling that a must-staple TLS feature extension demands, and costing revocation latency while a response stays valid.
- **Revocation checking policy**: rule for what a client does when status is unavailable, as soft-fail revocation checking that proceeds or hard-fail revocation checking that refuses, avoided by a pushed revocation set such as CRLite or by revocation by expiry under short lifetimes, and costing a revocation checking privacy leak wherever the client asks a third party.
- **Certificate transparency**, also **CT**: requirement that every certificate appear in an append-only certificate transparency log named on a log list, sharded by temporal sharding into a log shard, watched by a log monitor and log auditor under a certificate transparency policy, served through the static Certificate Transparency API in transparency log version two, and costing certificate transparency enforcement that breaks private hierarchies.
- **Signed certificate timestamp**: log's signed promise to include a certificate within a maximum merge delay, with signed certificate timestamp delivery as an embedded signed certificate timestamp in a pre-certificate, in the handshake, or in a status response, checked by a log inclusion proof, signed certificate timestamp auditing, and witness cosignature, and costing larger handshakes plus exposure to log poisoning.
- **Misissuance**: authority signing a certificate it should not have, as a rogue certificate, an unauthorized certificate for a domain, a backdated certificate past a distrust rule, one from a certificate collision attack or a name constraint escape, or one from certification authority compromise or subordinate authority abuse, all reported through a certificate problem report on a revocation timeline requirement.
- **Public key pinning**: restriction of a service to a pin set so any other chain is refused, by static pinning or dynamic pinning learned from a header, as in the withdrawn HTTP Public Key Pinning and in certificate pinning in mobile applications, and costing a backup pin, a pinning denial of service risk, and a pinning bypass through local anchors.
- **HTTP Strict Transport Security**, also **HSTS**: site instruction that clients use the encrypted scheme for a period and refuse click-through warnings, closing SSL stripping through a subdomain inclusion directive and a preload list covering the first request, paralleled by mail transfer agent strict transport security with TLS reporting and by the withdrawn Expect-CT declaration, and costing slow list changes, delivery failures, and subdomains locked out.
- **Key transparency**: publication of every key a directory hands out into a verifiable append-only record with signed tree heads, inclusion and absence proofs, and gossip between clients so a split view collides, costing log infrastructure and self-monitoring by every user.
- **Web of trust**: decentralized model where users sign each other's keys and validity follows from enough trusted signers and introducers, as in OpenPGP with its subkeys, revocation certificates, and unauthenticated key servers, now bypassed by Web Key Directory, Autocrypt, and Stateless OpenPGP, costing trust decisions users cannot reason about and keys that poisoning renders unusable.
- **SSH host key**: server's long-term key identifying the machine to clients and recorded in a local known-hosts file, replaceable by short-lived certificates from a signing authority with named principals, costing manual reverification or custody of that authority key.
- **DNS-Based Authentication of Named Entities**, also **DANE**: publication of a service's key or issuer constraint as a naming-system record secured by zone signatures, costing a signed zone, validating resolvers, and a record update on every key change.

### Key lifecycle and custody

- **Key management**: discipline of creating, distributing, storing, using, replacing, and destroying keys under a key management policy and key management practices statement, tracked through the pre-activation state, active state, suspended state, deactivated state, compromised state, destroyed state, and destroyed compromised state, entered by key activation and key reactivation, and costing record keeping.
- **Cryptographic module**: defined cryptographic boundary around hardware, firmware, or software inside which algorithms and keys are contained, rated by a module security level, running an approved mode of operation on a stated operational environment, holding critical security parameters and public security parameters, entering an error state on failure, and costing algorithm validation testing.
- **Self-test**: check a module runs on itself before or during service to confirm its own integrity and algorithms, as a known-answer test against stored vectors, a pairwise consistency test on a fresh key pair, and a continuous generator test for repeated output, costing startup delay and vectors per algorithm.
- **Key generation**: production of a key from a source of unpredictability with the length and structure its algorithm needs, done inside the protected boundary where it will be used, costing a trustworthy random source and, for in-module generation, no ability to back the key up.
- **Key distribution**: delivery of a key to every party that needs it without exposure in transit or at rest, followed by registration with a directory that verifies who submitted it, costing a protected channel or a wrapping key already in place.
- **Key storage**: holding of a secret at rest as a key blob or sealed secret so only authorized use retrieves it, with restricted permissions on key material, a backup holding key recovery information, an archive for old data, and optionally escrow, costing further holders an attacker can target just as well.
- **Crypto period**: total time a key is authorized for use, chosen from the volume it protects and its exposure, and split between the shorter span for protecting new data and the longer one for opening data protected earlier, with crypto period shortening as the answer to greater exposure, costing replacement work at every period's end.
- **Key rotation**: replacement of a key on a schedule so none protects unbounded data or time, with versions labelled in the data and a rollover window where both are honored, costing re-encryption or versioning, and changing nothing for data keys when only the tier above rotates.
- **Key destruction**, also **key zeroization**: overwriting of every copy of a key so what it protected becomes unreadable, used deliberately as crypto shredding to delete data, and costing certainty that no copy was missed and a fight with runtimes that duplicate buffers.
- **Key compromise recovery**: procedure for replacing a leaked key, re-protecting affected data, and revoking whatever vouched for it, costing a rehearsed key compromise recovery plan and downtime.
- **Key inventory**: maintained record of every key with its purpose, owner, custodian, location, and dates, named by a key handle or key label the data carries and asserting a trusted association to its owner, costing discovery, continuous reconciliation, and an access log the key holder cannot edit.
- **Split knowledge**: division of a secret so no one person holds enough to reconstruct it, enforced as dual control or by secret sharing for key custody under a share reconstruction quorum, exercised in air-gapped signing from a key ceremony script before a ceremony witness, and costing availability whenever the key must be used.
- **Hardware security module**, also **HSM**: dedicated device generating and using keys internally and returning operations rather than key material, reached through PKCS#11 or the Key Management Interoperability Protocol, loaded at initial key injection from a key loading device, and costing expense, throughput limits, and awkward backup.
- **Key hierarchy**: tiers in which key encryption keys wrap the data keys below, so a high rotation rewrites no data, realized as envelope encryption and in payments as a zone master key, terminal master key, base derivation key giving a derived unique key per transaction, working key, PIN encryption key, and PIN verification key, costing depth on every access path.
- **Key separation by purpose**: distinct key per operation, direction, and context, separating signature, authentication, data encryption, key transport, key agreement, and authorization keys in symmetric, private, and public forms and as static or ephemeral key agreement key pairs, bound by a domain-separating label, and costing more keys to generate, distribute, and rotate.
- **Bring your own key**, also **BYOK**, also **hold your own key**, also **HYOK**: importing a key into a service or keeping it entirely under your own control so the service calls out for each operation, costing either trust in a holder you cannot inspect or latency, availability risk, and lost features.
- **Secrets management**: central service or password vault issuing a dynamic secret under secret leasing instead of scattering secrets in files, with secret injection at runtime, secret versioning, secret rotation, secret revocation, a break-glass credential, and a secret access audit trail, costing a secret zero bootstrap credential that cannot come from the store itself.
- **Hardcoded key**: key embedded in source or a shipped binary, identical on every installation and extractable by anyone holding the artifact, as is a vendor default key, a secret in a configuration file, a secret in a container image layer, a secret in an environment variable, a secret in version control, or any long-lived static credential.
- **Crypto agility**: designing systems so an algorithm can be replaced without redesign, through labelled objects, a negotiated versus fixed algorithm design, an approved algorithm list, a minimum key length policy, and a cryptographic inventory or cryptographic bill of materials built by cryptographic discovery scanning, costing negotiation surface, more code paths, and a backward compatibility trap at every version.
- **Algorithm transition**: managed move from one approved algorithm or key size to a stronger one, passing through algorithm deprecation, a deprecated algorithm period, protocol version deprecation with an algorithm sunset date, restricted algorithm use, legacy-use decryption, and a disallowed algorithm, held above a security strength floor, and costing interoperability during the overlap plus a migration flag day.
- **Post-quantum migration**, also **quantum-safe migration**: replacement of key exchange and signature schemes with quantum-resistant ones, urgent because of harvest now, decrypt later, staged through hybrid key exchange, hybrid signature, and dual-stack certificate deployment with composite certificates and downgrade protection during migration, and costing a post-quantum readiness assessment, larger messages, and a multi-year program.
- **Cryptographic configuration baseline**: named versioned set of algorithm and protocol settings that deployments are held to, as a systemwide crypto policy applied at a crypto policy enforcement point with server preference ordering, checked by cryptographic monitoring of live traffic, and costing a legacy compatibility exception wherever a deprecated algorithm still enabled cannot be removed, plus crypto configuration drift to reconcile.

### Signed and encrypted message formats

- **JSON Object Signing and Encryption**, also **JOSE**: family of text-friendly formats for signing, encrypting, and publishing keys, with compact, general, and flattened serializations, with direct, wrapped, key agreement with key wrapping mode, or PBES2 key management content keys, and with a use parameter and key_ops parameter on each published key, costing an algorithm registry and a wide option space that invites misconfiguration.
- **JSON Web Token**, also **JWT**: compact signed or encrypted claims set naming the issuer, subject, audience, validity window, unique identifier, and any key the presenter must prove, readable by every holder unless encrypted, and costing verification discipline plus no easy revocation.
- **Protected header**: header bytes covered by the signature or tag, carrying the algorithm, content encryption, key identifier, type, content type, critical-extension, and b64 header parameter fields, with a detached payload signed but not carried, against unprotected headers anyone in the path may alter, costing strict validation in every consumer.
- **Bearer token**: credential authorizing whoever presents it with no further proof, simple and lost entirely once intercepted or logged, replaced by sender-constrained forms bound to a client-held key or the connection certificate, costing per-request proof objects and key management on every client.
- **PASETO**: token format fixing one algorithm per version and refusing in-band algorithm choice, costing a version bump to change scheme, beside the binary COSE and CBOR Web Token formats for constrained devices and macaroons whose appended caveats narrow a credential without contacting the issuer.
- **Cryptographic Message Syntax**, also **CMS**: container behind signed and encrypted mail, code signing, and timestamping, carrying SignedData, EnvelopedData, AuthEnvelopedData, EncryptedData, or DigestedData with signed and unsigned attributes including a signing time attribute, a KeyTransRecipientInfo, KeyAgreeRecipientInfo, KEKRecipientInfo, or PasswordRecipientInfo per recipient, and triple wrapping, costing a large parsing surface and divergent canonicalization.
- **Secure Multipurpose Internet Mail Extensions**, also **S/MIME**: mail format signing and encrypting message bodies under certificates, offering clear signing that leaves the text readable or opaque signing that wraps it, marked by an smime-type parameter and able to request a signed receipt, costing certificate distribution to every correspondent.
- **OpenPGP packet**: tagged length-prefixed unit from which keys, signatures, and messages are assembled, covering the public key encrypted session key packet, symmetric key encrypted session key packet, symmetrically encrypted integrity protected data packet with its modification detection code, string-to-key specifier, ASCII armor, and cleartext signature framework, costing the legacy symmetrically encrypted data packet with no integrity.
- **OpenPGP signature subpacket**: typed field inside a signature carrying a key flags subpacket, key expiration subpacket, preferred algorithm subpackets, notation data, or a mark of trust signature, exportable certification, or primary key binding signature, alongside the user identifier packet it certifies, costing implementations that disagree on which subpackets they honor.

### Token and signature misuse

- **Algorithm confusion attack**: making a verifier process an object under a different scheme than the issuer used, whether by declaring no signature at all or by naming a symmetric algorithm so a known public key is fed into a keyed check that anyone can compute.
- **Header-driven key selection**: trusting the object's own header to name the verification key, as an embedded key, a remote key set location or x5u header, an unvalidated certificate chain, or an injected key identifier pointing at attacker-controlled storage, so the attacker supplies both key and signature.
- **Weak shared secret for token authentication**: signing tokens with a short or guessable symmetric secret, so an offline search recovers it and forges tokens at will.
- **Unvalidated audience**: accepting a token minted for another service, or with an unchecked issuer, expiry, or not-before field, so a credential works sideways or forever.
- **Unverified claim reading**: parsing and acting on a token's contents before or without checking its signature, so attacker-written claims reach application logic.
- **Type confusion between token kinds**, also **cross-token confusion**: presenting a token minted for one purpose where another kind is expected, exploiting verifiers that ignore the declared type.
- **Token replay**: presenting a captured valid token again, or substituting one issued to another subject or client, which succeeds wherever the verifier records nothing and checks authenticity without binding.
- **Signature wrapping attack**, also **XML signature wrapping**: relocating signed content inside a document so the verifier checks the original element while the application reads an injected one, beside outright removal of the signature and reference targets that point away from what will be processed.
- **Surreptitious forwarding**: passing a signed and encrypted message to another recipient so the signer appears to have written to someone never addressed, the consequence of leaving the recipient identity out of the signed content and of signing before or after encryption without binding the two.
- **Decompression bomb in a token**: small compressed payload, declared by a zip header parameter, that expands enough to exhaust memory when a receiver inflates it.

### Composition, nonce, and randomness misuse

- **Cryptographic doom principle**: rule that any processing of a message before its authenticity is verified will eventually be turned into an attack, which is why decrypting or parsing first, choosing an unauthenticated mode, accepting malleable or truncated ciphertext, and calling a signature primitive without its padding all fail.
- **Nonce reuse**: use of one nonce value twice under one key, which in counter and stream modes reveals the difference of two plaintexts and in polynomial-authenticated modes hands over the authentication key, arising from predictable starting values, timestamps, restarted or wrapped counters, and colliding short nonces.
- **Data volume limit per key**: bound on how much one key may protect before birthday collisions or mode-specific weaknesses appear, which key wear-out reaches silently unless rekeying machinery respects it, costing coordination and a key hierarchy.
- **Associated data omission**: leaving context such as version, sender, record number, or length outside the authenticated portion, so a message replays into another context or records are split, merged, reordered, or opened under a second key as the invisible salamander case showed.
- **Insecure random source for keys**: generating keys from a general-purpose random function, a predictable seed, generator state duplication, or entropy starvation at boot, which yields a low-entropy key, shared prime factors across a population, a weak key population from a patched generator, and keys that structurally flawed key generation, trapdoored parameters, or a kleptographic backdoor lets its author recover.
- **Roll-your-own cryptography**: assembling a scheme from primitives instead of using a reviewed construction, the crypto misuse taxonomy covering insecure library defaults, copied example cryptography, verification disabled for testing, a constant salt for password-based encryption, insufficient iteration count, a password used directly as a key, a keyed hash used as an authentication code, and comparisons stopping at the first wrong byte.
- **Misuse-resistant interface**, also **hard-to-misuse interface**: library design offering one safe call per task with no nonce, mode, or padding choice exposed, following misuse-resistant primitive design to lower the crypto API usability barrier, and costing expert control and a migration for callers of the old surface.
- **Cryptographic misuse detection**: static or dynamic search for known-bad cryptographic calls, driven by rule-based misuse detection over a crypto usage rule specification per library, and costing rules that lag new interfaces plus false positives on deliberate low-level use.

### Protocol oracles and named attack classes

- **Padding oracle**: receiver behavior distinguishing bad padding from bad authentication, letting an attacker decrypt a byte at a time, one of the decryption oracles that also arise from distinct error messages, format checks, rejection timing, downstream writes, cache effects, and legacy implementations sharing a key.
- **Oracle silencing**: making every failure indistinguishable in message, timing, and observable behavior, including substitution of a random plaintext for a failed key-transport decryption, costing constant-time handling of every error path and diagnostics that tell operators nothing.
- **POODLE**: downgrade to a version whose padding goes unchecked followed by byte-at-a-time decryption, the padding-oracle class that also holds the original Vaudenay result, the Zombie POODLE and GOLDENDOODLE behavioral variants, the Lucky Thirteen and Lucky Microseconds timing forms, and the BEAST attack on predictable chaining values.
- **Bleichenbacher oracle**, also **million message attack**: recovery of a key-transport ciphertext by submitting many altered versions and learning which are accepted as well formed, the formatting-oracle class that returned as ROBOT and DROWN and appears as the Manger, Marvin, Klima-Pokorny-Rosa, and BERserk results against padding and encoding checks.
- **CRIME**: recovery of a secret from the compressed length of a message mixing it with attacker-chosen text, with BREACH on responses and TIME and HEIST inferring length from timing or a browser, beside Sweet32, Bar Mitzvah, and RC4 NOMORE exploiting block collisions and keystream bias.
- **FREAK**: attack making a server use a weakened export key exchange and breaking it offline, the downgrade class with Logjam, shared-group precomputation, version rollback, downgrade dances, and insecure fallback, all needing a machine-in-the-middle able to replay, reflect, interleave, delay, predict, or roll back clocks.
- **SMACK**: class of attacks driving a handshake state machine through forbidden sequences to find skipped checks, as in SKIP-TLS omitting authentication, CCS injection activating empty keys, renegotiation prefix injection, the triple handshake, the Selfie attack, Terrapin message deletion, and key reinstallation repeating nonces.
- **Goto fail**: validation flaw whose control flow skipped the signature check so every certificate passed, the implementation class with Curveball accepting supplied curve parameters, null-prefix names, rogue authority certificates from hash collisions, the SLOTH transcript collision, Heartbleed reading adjacent memory, and failure returned as success.
- **ALPACA**: redirection of an encrypted connection to another service sharing a certificate whose protocol reads the bytes differently, the confusion class with virtual host confusion, cross-protocol key reuse, chosen-protocol design, EFAIL, Raccoon and DHEat, invalid curve and twist attacks, Dragonblood, and Zerologon.

## Identity and authority

### Identity, its parties, and its names

- **Identification**: asserting a claimed identity to a system, such as supplying a username, before any evidence for the claim is offered.
- **Authentication**, also **authn**: checking evidence that a party controls the identity it claimed, which identification alone never provides, costing user friction and a credential lifecycle to operate.
- **Verification**: confirming that presented evidence matches what a system recorded for the claimed identity, costing stored comparison data and per-attempt processing.
- **Authentication parties**: the claimant whose identity is tested, the verifier that checks its authenticator output, the subscriber holding issued authenticators in a subscriber account, and the credential service provider that enrols subscribers, keeps an address of record for reaching them, and maintains their records.
- **Digital identity**: the set of recorded attributes that distinguishes one subject from every other subject in a given context.
- **Identity attribute**: a single recorded property of a subject, such as a name, a birth date, or an employee number, which one party may assert to another as an attribute statement, released whole or as a derived attribute value such as an age range, and grouped with others as an attribute bundle.
- **Claim**: a statement about a subject carried inside a token or assertion, drawn from a standard claim set, marked an essential claim when a relying party requires it, and delivered as an aggregated claim signed by another issuer or a distributed claim fetched from one.
- **Identifier**: a string naming exactly one subject inside a namespace, where reassignment after an account closes is the identifier reuse hazard that leaves old references resolving to a new person.
- **Pairwise pseudonymous identifier**: an identifier that differs for each relying party so one subject cannot be correlated across them, costing cross-service account linking and a published sector identifier grouping the sibling domains that share one value.
- **Subject, the identified party**: the person, device, or process that a digital identity refers to.
- **Subject, the active party in an access decision**: the process or account that requests an operation, named in the rule that permits or refuses it.
- **Principal, the authenticated entity**: the named entity that an authenticated identity denotes inside a security protocol.
- **Principal, the holder of rights**: the account, service, or group to which access rights attach and about which an authorization decision is made.
- **Object**: the passive resource in an access decision, the thing whose use is being requested.
- **Credential**: the object or record binding an authenticator to an identity, costing issuance, storage, a credential expiry policy, authenticator inventory review, and revocation on suspicion.
- **Non-human identity**, also **machine identity**, **workload identity**, **non-person entity**: an authenticating principal that is a device, service, job, or running process rather than a person, costing issuance and rotation at fleet scale.
- **Service account**: an account used by software rather than a person, costing weak lifecycle ownership and standing privilege that nobody reviews.
- **Shared account**: one credential used by several people, costing the loss of attribution and of any individual revocation.
- **Account provisioning**: creating an account and its attributes in a target system from an authoritative source of identity, pushed by the cross-domain identity management protocol and kept aligned by identity synchronization, or created at first federated sign-in as just-in-time provisioning, costing an integration per system.
- **Deprovisioning**: removing or disabling an account when a person's relationship ends, choosing between account disable and delete, catching what was missed through stale account detection and orphaned account review, costing coverage of every downstream system and an interval in which a credential still works.

### Authenticators, their factors, and their strength

- **Authenticator**: something a claimant holds and controls, used to prove control of an identity, costing issuance, replacement, and revocation handling.
- **Authentication factor**: a category of evidence, being knowledge the claimant remembers, possession of an object they hold, or inherence in a trait measured from them.
- **Single-factor authentication**: authentication resting on evidence from one factor category, costing complete failure once that single secret is stolen.
- **Multi-factor authentication**, also **MFA**, also **two-factor authentication**, also **2FA**: authentication requiring evidence from two or more distinct factor categories in one act, costing enrolment effort, support load, and recovery complexity.
- **Step-up authentication**: demanding stronger evidence when a session attempts a more sensitive operation, costing interruption at the moment of highest user intent.
- **Adaptive authentication**, also **risk-based authentication**: varying the evidence demanded according to observed signals such as device fingerprinting, impossible travel detection, velocity checking, and login provenance, raising a silent risk challenge only for scored attempts, costing a signal pipeline, constant tuning, and false challenges to legitimate users.
- **Passwordless authentication**: authenticating with a held or inherent authenticator and no memorized secret, costing recovery design and authenticator provisioning.
- **One-time password**, also **OTP**: a value valid for one use or a short interval, computed from a shared seed by counter as HOTP or by clock step as TOTP, costing seed provisioning, a verifier store worth stealing, and a tolerance window for drift that widens reuse.
- **Software token**, also **authenticator app**: a program computing one-time passwords on a general-purpose device, costing exposure to malware on that device.
- **Hardware one-time password token**: a dedicated device that displays generated codes, issued either as a single-factor one-time password device or as a multi-factor one-time password device that unlocks with a PIN or biometric first, costing purchase, distribution, battery life, and replacement logistics.
- **Look-up secret**, also **transaction authentication number**, also **TAN**: a printed or issued list of single-use values read by index to authorize one step, costing physical custody and eventual exhaustion.
- **Out-of-band authenticator**: an authenticator reached over a channel separate from the one being authenticated, delivered by text message, voice call, or mailbox, costing that channel's own security and restriction by verifiers where it is a public telephone network.
- **Push notification approval**: authenticating by accepting a prompt on a registered device, costing a device dependency and habitual reflex approval.
- **Number matching**: requiring the user to type a number shown by the sign-in attempt into the approving app, beside the requesting application, location, and time, costing one step and defeating blind approval.
- **Single-factor cryptographic authenticator**: a key that signs a challenge with no local activation step, held as single-factor cryptographic software on a general-purpose machine or inside a single-factor cryptographic device, costing full use by whoever holds the host or the device.
- **Multi-factor cryptographic authenticator**: a key that signs a challenge only after a PIN or biometric unlocks it locally, held as multi-factor cryptographic software or inside a multi-factor cryptographic device, costing unit price, enrolment effort, and a lockout path.
- **Activation secret**, also **client PIN**: a PIN or biometric that unlocks an authenticator locally and is never sent to the verifier, costing a reset path and permanent credential loss when its client PIN retry counter empties.
- **Challenge-response authentication**: proving control of a secret by transforming a fresh challenge, costing state for issuing challenges and tracking their freshness.
- **Replay resistance**: the property that an authenticator output belongs to the current protocol run, obtained by including a nonce so a recorded exchange cannot be submitted again.

### Proofing, enrolment, and assurance

- **Identity proofing**: establishing that a real-world person corresponds to the identity being registered, costing evidence collection, review effort, and applicant abandonment.
- **Identity evidence**: a document or record offered as proof of a claimed real-world identity, validated for genuineness and then verified against the person presenting it by document to face comparison, costing verification services and manual review.
- **Electronic machine readable travel document**: a passport or identity card whose signed chip is read and verified, unlocked by basic access control keyed from the printed page or by password authenticated connection establishment, shown genuine and uncloned through chip authentication and active authentication, and releasing sensitive data only after terminal authentication, costing reader hardware.
- **Injection attack on remote proofing**: feeding pre-recorded or generated imagery into the capture pipeline as virtual camera injection, rather than presenting anything to a real sensor, and registering a fabricated person that way as deepfake enrolment.
- **Synthetic identity**: a fabricated identity assembled from real and invented attributes, or from a generated face or voice, submitted in volume to obtain accounts before manual review can react.
- **Enrolment**: the process by which a proofed applicant becomes a subscriber holding issued authenticators, costing email control verification at signup and an administrative path attackers will target.
- **Binding**: associating an authenticator with an identity record, at enrolment, when a second is added, or as authenticator rebinding when a lost one is replaced at the same assurance, costing proof that the account holder authorized it.
- **Unauthorized binding**: an adversary registering its own authenticator against a legitimate account, by enrollment of a rogue authenticator, directory account attribute abuse, or an alternate identifier claim naming another subject, so its access survives a password reset.
- **Authenticator threats**: the enumerated ways an authenticator fails, being authenticator theft, authenticator duplication into a working clone, eavesdropping on its output, a side channel measured from it, endpoint compromise driving it, social engineering of the subscriber holding it, and verifier compromise yielding the stored data.
- **Compromise resistance**: the property that stolen verifier data, a counterfeit verifier, or possession of the device alone yields nothing an adversary can reuse to authenticate.
- **Identity assurance level**, also **authenticator assurance level**, also **federation assurance level**: a graded statement of how thoroughly an identity was proofed, how strongly its authentication resists guessing, theft, and phishing, and how well a federated statement is protected between the parties.
- **Assurance level selection**: choosing required levels from an impact assessment rather than by default, fixing the minimum authentication strength and the interval before identity must be proven again, costing an analysis step before deployment.

### Memorized secrets and password policy

- **Password**, also **memorized secret**: a string the claimant recalls and enters to prove identity, costing user memory burden and exposure to guessing and to reuse across services.
- **Passphrase**, also **Diceware**: a memorized secret of several randomly chosen words, buying measurable length without added memory load, costing typing effort on constrained devices.
- **Personal identification number**, also **PIN**: a short numeric memorized secret protected by attempt limits rather than by length, costing total dependence on the limiter.
- **Password entropy**: a measure of how unpredictable a secret is to a guesser who models the skewed way humans choose, stated as guessing entropy, as min-entropy for the likeliest single attempt, or as the guess number a value occupies.
- **Password composition policy**: rules constraining allowed secrets by minimum length, maximum length, and required character classes, costing usability, predictable substitution patterns, and passphrase support lost to a low ceiling.
- **Minimum strength policy**: requiring a candidate to pass a strength estimator such as zxcvbn, shown live during entry, instead of a character-class rule, costing maintained dictionaries and rejections the user must be told how to fix.
- **Password blocklist**, also **password denylist**: refusing secrets found on a list of common or context-specific choices, costing list maintenance and rejection friction.
- **Breached-credential screening**: checking a chosen or submitted secret against corpora from public breaches, costing a data feed, a privacy-preserving lookup, and forced resets when accounts appear.
- **Password rotation**, also **password expiration**: forcing periodic change on a schedule and keeping old digests to refuse reuse, costing predictable incremental variants, retained secret material, and support calls for little guessing benefit.
- **Password reuse**: using one secret at several services, so a single breach becomes a key to the others.
- **Password manager**: software that generates, stores, and fills a distinct secret per site behind one master secret, costing a single high-value vault and autofill that must check each form's origin.
- **Default password**, also **hardcoded credential**: a factory-set or embedded secret identical on every unit, readable from documentation or firmware and unchangeable without a rebuild.
- **Honeywords**, also **honeychecker**: decoy password digests stored beside the real one, with an isolated service knowing which is genuine so use of a decoy raises an alarm, costing another component to protect.
- **Authentication scheme evaluation framework**: rating a scheme against fixed usability, deployability, and security benefits so alternatives compare on one grid, naming benefits such as nothing-to-carry, easy recovery from loss, and resilience to observation, guessing, and leaks from other verifiers, costing agreement on the benefit list.

### Credential guessing, cracking, and login abuse

- **Brute-force attack**: trying every candidate secret in a space until one is accepted, bounded only by its length and alphabet.
- **Dictionary attack**: guessing from a curated wordlist of likely secrets, built from leaks, language corpora, terms specific to the target, and variations of a secret they leaked elsewhere, rather than from the whole space.
- **Rule-based attack**: applying transformation rules to every dictionary word, in named variants that append brute-forced characters, follow a positional character-class mask, or concatenate entries from two lists.
- **Trained guessing model**: ordering candidates by character transition probabilities, learned structural templates, or a neural model fitted to leaked password corpora, so likely secrets are tried first.
- **Precomputation attack**, also **rainbow table**, also **time-memory tradeoff attack**: performing the expensive search before the target digest is seen, so recovery costs lookups rather than computation, which a unique salt defeats.
- **Offline cracking**: guessing against a stolen verifier file at attacker-controlled speed, on graphics hardware or across many machines, with no server present to throttle it.
- **Credential stuffing**: replaying username and password pairs taken from other breaches at scale and sorting out the live hits, exploiting reuse rather than guessing.
- **Password spraying**: trying a few very common passwords across many accounts, or one likely password against many usernames, to stay below per-account lockout thresholds.
- **Credential harvesting**: collecting secrets in bulk from users, endpoint memory, and credential stores through infostealer malware, keystroke logging, form grabbing inside the browser, or sniffing unprotected traffic.
- **Replay attack**: resubmitting a captured authentication message so it is accepted as fresh, in named variants that obtain a response before its challenge is issued, forward an exchange between unwitting parties, or present it to a second verifier.
- **Login throttling**, also **rate limiting on login**: slowing or capping accepted attempts per account or source, with a growing delay after each failure, until online guessing is uneconomic, costing legitimate retries and per-identifier state.
- **Account lockout**: disabling authentication for an account after a threshold of failures, whether until an administrator acts or for a period that expires, costing help-desk load and a denial-of-service lever against many accounts.
- **CAPTCHA**, also **proof of work challenge**: demanding a task easy for a human or measurable computation per attempt so bulk guessing carries a cost, costing accessibility, latency, and defeat by paid solving services.
- **Account enumeration**: learning which identities exist, as username enumeration from a verbose authentication error, response-difference enumeration, timing-based enumeration, forgot-password enumeration, registration enumeration, or rate-limit behavior enumeration, each turning the sign-in surface into an account existence oracle.
- **Uniform authentication failure response**: answering every failed attempt with one message and one timing, keeping a constant-work authentication path that hashes whether the identity exists or not, and returning a uniform reset acknowledgement, costing computation spent on garbage input and diagnostic clarity for genuine users.
- **Honeytoken credential**, also **canary password**, also **honey account**: a planted credential or unused account whose use signals that a credential store was read or an intruder is present, costing upkeep so it looks real.
- **Account takeover detection**: recognizing a hijacked account from post-authentication signals such as sudden contact changes and mass actions, supported by credential stuffing detection across the login surface, failed-login pattern analysis, breach corpus monitoring with forced reset of leaked secrets, session anomaly detection, and recovery abuse monitoring, costing tuned rules per application.
- **Authentication event logging**: recording every attempt with its outcome, source, and method so anomalous sign-in detection has a baseline to compare against, costing storage volume, personal data handling, and the device recognition drift that turns ordinary client updates into alerts.

### Phishing resistance and credential binding

- **Credential phishing**: luring a user to a counterfeit sign-in page and capturing whatever they type, tailored to one person or role as spear phishing.
- **Smishing**: credential or code phishing delivered by text message, by voice call, or as a scannable image code carrying the malicious destination.
- **Counterfeit sign-in host**: a login page under attacker control, reached through a visually identical or near-miss domain name, tampered name resolution, or a fake browser window drawn inside a page.
- **Adversary-in-the-middle phishing**, also **real-time phishing proxy**: relaying each step of a genuine sign-in between victim and service, which defeats one-time codes and yields the authenticated session material.
- **Verification code phishing**, also **one-time password bot**: tricking a user into reading a legitimate code aloud or typing it into an attacker's form, often by automation that telephones them posing as the service.
- **Multi-factor downgrade attack**: steering a victim toward a weaker enrolled method the attacker can defeat, or onto a legacy interface that never enforces the account's second factor.
- **MFA fatigue attack**, also **push bombing**: flooding a victim with repeated approval prompts until one is accepted out of annoyance or confusion.
- **Phishing resistance**: the property that an authenticator cannot produce a usable response for any party other than the origin it was registered to.
- **Origin binding**: computing the authenticator response over the requesting site's origin so a counterfeit site receives nothing usable, costing breakage across sibling domains.
- **Sender-constrained token**, also **token binding**, also **proof of possession**: a token usable only by a party proving control of an associated key, by a signed possession proof per use carrying a server-supplied DPoP nonce, by the client certificate presented at issuance, or by a key held in device hardware, costing key management and enforcement at every resource.
- **Bearer credential**, also **bearer assertion**: a credential honoured on presentation alone, with no check that the presenter holds anything else, costing total compromise of the authority it carries whenever it leaks.

### Public key authenticators and passkeys

- **FIDO2**: the standard pairing a browser interface with an authenticator protocol for phishing-resistant sign-in, superseding the earlier second-factor and passwordless FIDO standards, costing authenticator provisioning and recovery planning.
- **WebAuthn**: the browser interface by which a site asks an authenticator to create or use a public key credential, costing origin-scoped design and a fallback path for clients that lack it.
- **Client to Authenticator Protocol**, also **CTAP**: the protocol a client speaks to an external authenticator over the human interface device transport, near field transport, or Bluetooth transport, its second generation adding local verification and discoverable credentials, costing transport support and newer hardware.
- **Public key credential**: a key pair created for one relying party, the private half retained by the authenticator and found again through an opaque credential identifier the site stores, costing per-site registration.
- **Relying party identifier**, also **RP ID**: the domain scope a credential is bound to, checked on every use so no other origin can invoke it, widened only by a declared list of sibling domains.
- **Assertion, the authenticator's signed response**: the signature covering challenge, origin, request type, relying party hash, and status flags, proving the authenticator still holds the private key for a registered credential.
- **Authenticator attestation statement**: a signed claim made at registration about the authenticator's model and how it protects keys, offered in a form that names no unit, a form shared across a manufacturing batch, a form mediated by a third party, or an enterprise form disclosing a device-unique identifier.
- **User presence**: evidence that someone physically interacted with the authenticator, typically a touch, which shows deliberate human intent and proves no identity.
- **User verification**: evidence that the authenticator locally checked who the user is, by PIN or biometric, reported to the site in a signed flag, costing an extra local step on every use.
- **Discoverable credential**, also **resident key**: a credential the authenticator can locate without being told its identifier, so sign-in needs no username first, costing on-device storage slots.
- **Passkey**: a discoverable public key credential intended to replace the password outright, costing recovery design and dependence on platform ecosystems.
- **Synced passkey**: a passkey replicated across a user's devices by a provider's credential service, costing trust in that service and in the strength of its own account security.
- **Device-bound credential**: a credential whose private key never leaves the device that generated it, costing loss of access when that device is gone.
- **Roaming authenticator**, also **security key**, also **cross-platform authenticator**: a portable hardware authenticator carried between machines, costing procurement, distribution, carrying, and spares for loss.
- **Platform authenticator**: an authenticator built into one device and unusable anywhere else, costing lockout when that device is lost.
- **Hybrid transport**, also **cross-device authentication**: authenticating on one device using an authenticator on another over a proximity-checked channel, costing setup and radio availability.
- **Secure payment confirmation**: binding a payment amount and payee into a public key authentication ceremony so the signed response proves exactly what the user approved, costing merchant and browser support.

### Biometrics

- **Biometric authentication**: recognizing a person from a measured physical or behavioural trait, costing irrevocable identifiers and error rates that never reach zero.
- **Biometric template**: the stored feature representation built from an enrolment sample, against which a probe captured at authentication time is compared to produce a similarity score, whose accuracy decays through template ageing as the enrolled sample stops resembling the person.
- **Biometric error rates**: the proportions of impostor comparisons wrongly accepted and genuine ones wrongly rejected, their crossing as the equal error rate, their curve as a detection error tradeoff plot, the separation of the two score distributions as the decidability index, and the proportions who cannot enrol or acquire a sample, all moved by the accept threshold.
- **Biometric verification and identification**: the one-to-one comparison confirming a claimed identity against the one-to-many search of a gallery, the second run as a watchlist search or as biometric deduplication at enrolment, costing accuracy that falls as the population grows.
- **Multibiometric fusion**: combining several biometric sources into one decision, at feature-level fusion before comparison, score-level fusion over the comparison scores, or decision-level fusion over separate verdicts, costing weight tuning, a bespoke matcher, or coarse information at each level.
- **Biometric menagerie**: a classification of users by how readily they match their own template and other people's, naming sheep who match reliably, goats who fail against their own, lambs who are easily impersonated, and wolves who impersonate others.
- **Biometric modality**: the trait a system measures, being face geometry, fingerprint ridges, iris texture, subsurface vein pattern, vocal characteristics, or behavioural patterns such as typing rhythm and gait, each costing its own sensors and its own exposure to recordings, imagery, or latent prints.
- **Presentation attack**: presenting a fabricated or altered trait to the sensor through an instrument whose species is a printed image, a mould, a mask, or a replayed recording, in place of the bona fide presentation a cooperating person makes, so the system accepts a genuine subject.
- **Presentation attack detection**, also **liveness detection**: distinguishing a live trait from an artefact, by prompting the subject to act or inferring liveness passively, measured by the attack presentation classification error rate, the bona fide presentation classification error rate, and the impostor attack presentation match rate, graded by a presentation attack detection level naming the attack potential level it withstands, costing extra sensing and latency.
- **Deepfake presentation attack**: presenting synthesized imagery or audio of a target person to a remote biometric check.
- **Morphing attack**: enrolling a blended image so that two different people both match the same stored reference.
- **Template attack**: defeating a stored reference by refining a synthetic sample against returned scores, reconstructing an approximation of the trait from a stolen template, presenting a masterprint built from commonly occurring features, or mounting a wolf attack whose input matches many templates through a weakness in the comparison algorithm.
- **Template protection**: storing a transformed representation that cannot be inverted, called irreversibility, cannot be correlated across applications, called unlinkability of templates, and can be replaced after compromise, called renewability, built as a cancelable transform, a fuzzy extractor, a fuzzy vault, a fuzzy commitment over a secure sketch, or biohashing, costing accuracy and per-application key management.
- **Biometric cryptosystem**: a design that releases or derives a key from a biometric reading instead of returning a match score, by key binding that locks an independent key with the trait or by key generation from a biometric that derives one from the reading itself, costing higher error rates.
- **Biometric revocability problem**: the fact that a compromised trait cannot be reissued, unlike a password or a key.
- **Local biometric comparison**: matching on the user's own device, often to release a locally held key rather than to serve as the evidence itself, so no template leaves the device, costing trust in that device's protections.

### Enterprise ticket and digest authentication

- **Kerberos**: a network authentication protocol in which a key distribution centre holding every principal key issues a ticket-granting ticket after checking a long-term key, then exchanges it for service tickets that carry a session key, costing time synchronization and a central service whose loss is total.
- **Ticket policy**: the configured validity, renewal limit, and clock skew tolerance for issued tickets, with the flags marking one forwardable, proxiable, renewable, or its service trusted to receive delegated credentials, costing replay room when loose and outages when tight.
- **Kerberos pre-authentication**: proving knowledge of the long-term key before any ticket is issued, by encrypted timestamp pre-authentication or by certificate under PKINIT, its absence permitting offline cracking and its presence still allowing pre-authentication brute force, account enumeration through error codes, and KDC spoofing where the reply goes unvalidated, costing clock synchronisation.
- **Privilege attribute certificate**, also **PAC**: the authorization data carried inside a Windows Kerberos ticket describing the principal's group memberships, confirmed with the domain controller at the cost of a round trip per authentication.
- **Pass-the-ticket**: importing a ticket extracted from a compromised host's memory into a session to act as its owner, or using a stolen password-derived key to request a fresh one, without ever knowing the password.
- **Ticket forgery**: minting or editing tickets with a stolen key, as a golden ticket from the domain-wide key, a silver ticket from one service key, or a diamond or sapphire ticket whose authorization data is swapped inside a legitimately issued one.
- **Ticket cracking**: obtaining password-derived encrypted material and recovering the secret offline, from service tickets for accounts with registered service names, from initial replies for accounts exempt from pre-authentication, or from time synchronization messages.
- **Kerberos delegation abuse**: obtaining tickets that impersonate arbitrary users by capturing a forwarded ticket from a host trusted for full delegation, using a compromised account's permitted targets, writing a delegation attribute on the target object, or defeating a non-forwardable restriction.
- **Certificate-based logon abuse**: logging on as another principal by attaching a key credential to its directory object, by certificate template escalation where a template lets the requester name any subject, by enrollment agent abuse, by a golden certificate forged with a stolen issuing key, or by certificate mapping confusion, then recovering the account's key with UnPAC-the-hash.
- **Authentication coercion**: forcing a machine or service to authenticate toward an attacker-chosen host, known as coerced machine authentication, including relay to an enrollment endpoint that will issue a certificate for the victim, so its credential can be captured or relayed.
- **NTLM authentication**: a challenge-response family in which a client proves knowledge of an unsalted password digest without sending it, answering with a Net-NTLMv1 response or a Net-NTLMv2 response and, on legacy systems, a LAN Manager hash, costing the absence of mutual authentication and permanent exposure to relay.
- **Pass-the-hash**: authenticating with a stolen password digest directly, because the protocol never requires the plaintext.
- **NTLM relay**: forwarding a captured challenge-response exchange to a third service as SMB relay, or into a different protocol sharing the credential as cross-protocol relay, so the attacker authenticates as the victim without cracking anything, the exchange usually captured by name resolution poisoning or web proxy discovery abuse.
- **Relay hardening**: message signing enforcement, extended protection for authentication binding an exchange to its outer transport channel, a directory signing requirement that refuses unsigned binds, and NTLM blocking that disables the legacy protocol outright, costing performance, per-service configuration, and application remediation.
- **Host credential exposure**: secrets recoverable after code execution on a machine, from the security account database, cached domain credential material, cleartext credential caching kept for legacy protocols, access token theft from another process, and directory database extraction through directory replication credential theft.
- **Host credential protection**: keeping those secrets out of reach by credential store isolation in a separate secure process, a local secret protection service refusing untrusted access, restricted administrative logon that sends no reusable credential, a protected account class barred from legacy protocols, and local administrator password randomization, costing compatibility with older tooling.
- **HTTP authentication scheme**: the named challenge and credential forms a server may demand, being basic authentication sending an encoded password with every request, digest authentication answering a nonce with a hash, the negotiate mechanism agreeing on an underlying protocol, and bearer token presentation, costing downgrade to the weakest form both sides accept.

### Network, service, and workload authentication

- **Directory bind authentication**: authenticating by attempting a directory connection with a name and secret, costing transmission of that secret and directories that treat an absent or empty one as success.
- **Network port authentication**, also **802.1X**: requiring authentication before a switch or access point grants a device network access, carried by extensible authentication protocol methods that exchange certificates or tunnel an inner method, costing supplicant configuration and clients that accept any server certificate.
- **Remote authentication dial-in user service**, also **RADIUS**: a protocol carrying network access decisions between devices and a central server under a per-device shared secret, costing weak response integrity that a chosen-prefix collision can forge, with TACACS+ and Diameter as its administrative and telecommunications counterparts.
- **Mutual TLS authentication**, also **client certificate authentication**: both endpoints proving identity with certificates during the handshake, costing issuance, distribution, and renewal on every service.
- **Public key authentication for remote shell**: authenticating with a key pair whose public half the server lists, costing key distribution, review of a list where one added line is a permanent way in, and exposure wherever a key agent is forwarded.
- **Secure production identity framework**: giving every workload a SPIFFE identifier inside a trust domain and an X509 identity document or JWT identity document fetched from a local workload API once node attestation and workload attestation by selectors succeed, with a trust bundle of anchors for verifying peers, costing issuing infrastructure and platform-specific attestation.
- **Workload identity federation**: exchanging a platform-issued projected service account token or a build system's job token for credentials in another system so no static secret is stored, called pipeline identity federation in the build case, costing trust configuration, audience restriction on every token, and reliance on its claims.
- **Instance metadata credential**: a credential a workload retrieves from a local platform endpoint, costing exposure to any request-forging flaw in the workload and a metadata service token requirement proving locality that older clients cannot meet.
- **Short-lived credential**: a credential valid for minutes or hours so theft has limited value, replaced on schedule with two accepted during changeover, costing renewal traffic and reliable clocks.
- **Request signing**: authenticating each call by signing a canonical request built from its method, path, signed header set, and body, standardised as HTTP Message Signatures and issued ahead of time as a presigned link, costing client complexity, encoding pitfalls, a signature timestamp window, and a server-side nonce cache to refuse replay inside it.
- **API key authentication**: authenticating a caller with a static string, costing full compromise when the string leaks and no user context in the request.

### Federation and single sign-on

- **Single sign-on**, also **SSO**: authenticating once and gaining access to many applications without repeating it, costing a shared point of failure, careful scoping of the session it creates, and a session management specification with a check session interface for applications that must notice a remote sign-out.
- **Federated identity**: relying on another organization's authentication of a subject rather than holding credentials locally, an identity provider issuing statements that a service provider consumes under a federation trust agreement that allowlists a relying party or leaves each release to a runtime decision, sometimes through a federation proxy, costing trust configuration and dependence on the partner.
- **Federation metadata**: the published description of a party's endpoints, keys, and supported bindings, protected by metadata signing so keys and endpoints cannot be swapped, read by an identity provider discovery service to offer the user a choice, costing refresh discipline and an overlap at every metadata key rotation.
- **Identity provider initiated sign-on**: a flow starting at the provider, which delivers an unsolicited statement to an application, costing the loss of request correlation and the injection check that correlation provides.
- **Service provider initiated sign-on**: a flow starting at the application, which redirects the user with a request identifier the response must echo back, costing redirect handling and state for every pending request.
- **Security Assertion Markup Language**, also **SAML**: a markup standard for exchanging authentication and attribute statements between organizations, carried by the redirect binding, the POST binding as an auto-submitted form, or the SOAP binding as a direct server call, most often under the web browser single sign-on profile, costing intricate signature handling and canonicalization pitfalls.
- **Assertion, the identity provider's signed statement**: the statement carrying the subject's name identifier, the conditions limiting its use, a subject confirmation method of bearer confirmation or holder-of-key confirmation, how and when they authenticated, and the attributes released, passed by front-channel presentation through the browser or fetched by back-channel presentation against an assertion reference.
- **Assertion validation**: checking that a statement names this application as its audience, arrived at the endpoint it names, falls inside its validity window, answers a request this application made, and was never consumed before, costing exact configuration, synchronised clocks, and shared state across instances.
- **Assertion replay**: submitting a captured statement again to obtain a second application session.
- **Signature bypass on a signed statement**: making a verifier check a signature over different bytes than the application reads, by signature wrapping that restructures the document, a parser differential attack, reference redirection, transform abuse, namespace injection on a signature, digest confusion, splitting an identifier with a comment, removing the signature, or supplying the signing certificate.
- **Golden SAML attack**: stealing an identity provider's signing key and minting valid statements for any subject at any federated application.
- **Identity provider confusion**: making an application accept a statement issued by a different provider it also trusts, or by one the attacker chose.
- **Single logout**: propagating one sign-out to every application in the federation, begun as relying party initiated logout and carried by front-channel logout that loads each endpoint in the browser or by back-channel logout delivering a signed logout token, costing post-logout redirect validation, logout token replay, silent failures, and retries for unreachable participants.
- **Verifiable credential**: a signed claim set a holder keeps in a wallet and shows to unrelated verifiers as a verifiable presentation under the issuer holder verifier model, avoiding the phone-home problem of telling the issuer each use, costing a revocation registry, a trust registry of recognized issuers, wallet attestation, and over-collection by a verifier that asks for everything.
- **Decentralized identifier**, also **DID**: an identifier that resolves without a central registry to a DID document listing the keys, verification methods, and service endpoints tied to its subject, costing resolution infrastructure for every method it is registered under.
- **Selective disclosure**: revealing only chosen claims from a credential while the issuer's signature still verifies, as a selective disclosure JWT withholding salted claim digests, as a mobile document, or by proving a predicate such as being over an age, costing scheme complexity, a key binding proof from the holder, and unlinkable presentation only in the harder schemes.

### Delegated authorization flows and their attacks

- **OAuth 2.0**: a delegated authorization framework in which a resource owner lets a client obtain limited tokens from an authorization server for use at a resource server, extended by User-Managed Access for policy set in the owner's absence and restated by the Grant Negotiation and Authorization Protocol, costing a sensitive central component and frequent misuse as an authentication protocol.
- **Access token, the delegated authority**: the credential a client presents to a resource server for access within a granted scope, issued opaque or under the JWT access token profile and narrowed by naming the resource it is meant for, costing a separate token per resource a client talks to.
- **Refresh token**: a long-lived credential exchanged for new access tokens without user involvement, reissued on each use so that a second use of the old one reveals refresh token theft, costing high value to a thief, false revocations after network retries, and refresh token flooding that fills the token store.
- **Authorization code grant**: the flow where the browser returns a short-lived code that the client exchanges for tokens over a direct call, the code refused on second use, costing a redirect round trip.
- **Proof key for code exchange**, also **PKCE**: binding an authorization code to a secret the requesting client generated and reveals only at exchange, so an intercepted code is useless, costing support in every client.
- **Withdrawn legacy grants**: the implicit grant that returned tokens in the redirect itself and the password credentials grant that had the client collect the user's password, removed for exposing tokens in the address bar and for training credential sharing.
- **Client credentials grant**: the flow where a client obtains a token for itself with no user involved, costing careful scope limits since no person consented, and a client secret whose leakage in a public client, brute force, or reuse permits client impersonation.
- **Device authorization grant**: the flow letting an input-constrained device obtain tokens while the user approves on a second device, with client-initiated backchannel authentication asking the provider to reach the user directly, costing polling endpoint abuse and a pending request an attacker can offer to a victim as cross-device flow abuse.
- **Scope and consent**: the named extent of access a token is issued for, approved by the resource owner on a consent screen or described as structured authorization details under Rich Authorization Requests, costing user attention, comprehension, repeated prompts when requested incrementally, an overbroad scope request nobody reads, and a scope upgrade attack at exchange.
- **Redirect URI validation**: comparing the requested return destination against registration with no redirect pattern weakness from wildcards or prefixes, closing open redirector abuse, and answering native application redirect hijacking with a loopback redirect requirement, claimed scheme verification, and an external user agent requirement, costing exact registration of every valid destination.
- **OpenID Connect**, also **OIDC**: an identity layer over the delegated framework that returns a signed identity token asserting a named user authenticated at a stated time by stated means, published in a provider discovery document, offered also as a hybrid flow and hardened as the financial-grade profile, costing correct token validation in every client.
- **Identity token validation**: checking a token's issuer, audience, subject identifier, and signature, matching the nonce parameter carried from the request, and reading the authentication time claim, the authentication methods reference, and the authentication context class reference so a maximum authentication age and a strength can be demanded, costing per-request client state.
- **Authorization code interception and injection**: capturing a code from the browser or from the operating system's handler registry, then authorization code injection of a code obtained elsewhere into the victim's session, authorization code replay of one already spent, or cross-site request forgery on the redirect endpoint, all bound by an opaque state value echoed back and by a one-time authorization code.
- **Mix-up attack**: confusing a client about which authorization server answered, whether through discovery or by metadata poisoning, so it sends the code or token to the wrong party, which authorization server issuer identification in the response would settle.
- **Token leakage**: exposure of issued tokens through fragment leakage in the address bar, referrer leakage of authorization parameters, browser history, access token leakage through logs, or a token in browser storage that any script on the origin can read, which a backend for frontend pattern keeps out of the browser.
- **Token substitution attack**: presenting a token issued for one client or audience at a resource whose issuer validation failure leaves it unchecked, harvested by a counterfeit resource server that replays what clients send it, and forged where signature algorithm confusion lets the attacker pick the algorithm.

### Sessions, recovery, and account takeover

- **Session**: the server-recognized continuation of one authenticated interaction across many requests, keyed by an unguessable identifier and held either in server storage or in signed data travelling with the client, costing storage, coordination, and revocation that cannot take effect early.
- **Session fixation**: forcing a victim to use a session identifier the attacker already knows, so the victim's authentication elevates the attacker's session.
- **Session identifier rotation on authentication**: issuing a new identifier at the moment privileges change, costing one write and defeating fixation outright.
- **Session timeout**: the bounds that end a session, a hard expiry measured from creation, an expiry after a period without activity, and periodic reissue during long use, costing lost work, repeated sign-ins, and coordination across concurrent clients.
- **Session invalidation**: destroying server-side session state on logout, on password change, and across every device on demand, costing a store operation, difficulty in stateless designs, and the user's other devices signed out.
- **Account recovery**: restoring access after an authenticator is lost, by a pre-issued recovery code, trusted device recovery, a recovery contact, an escrowed recovery key, out-of-band recovery verification, or delayed recovery the real owner can interrupt, costing a fallback authentication path that becomes the weakest way into the account and a mail account as the root of trust.
- **Password reset flow**: the sequence replacing a forgotten secret through a single-use password reset token sent as a reset link in email, which must expire and must avoid reset token predictability, reset token leakage through referrer, and password reset poisoning by a tampered host header, costing a path as strong as sign-in itself.
- **Security question**, also **knowledge-based verification**: a challenge drawn from a preregistered question or, as dynamic knowledge-based verification, from records held about the person, costing answers that are researchable, purchasable, guessable, or forgotten.
- **Recovery channel hijack**: capturing all future recovery for an account by email change hijack where no change-of-contact confirmation or recovery attempt notification reaches the old address, by phone number recycling, or through an orphaned recovery channel its holder no longer controls.
