- **Account takeover**: gaining full control of another person's account, by credentials, by recovery abuse, by session theft, by a pre-hijacking attack that claims the account before its owner registers, by unauthorized account linking of an attacker-controlled federated identity, or through account deletion and reclamation of the identifier.

### Models of authority

- **Access control**: mediation of every attempt to use a resource, granting or refusing it against a stated rule, costing an enforcement point on every path to everything protected.
- **Authorization**: determining which operations an already identified party may perform on which resources, the decision that begins where authentication ends, costing policy that must be written and kept current.
- **Access control matrix**: a representation with one row per active party, one column per resource, and the permitted operations written into each cell, its contents being the protection state that every decision reads.
- **Safety problem**: whether a given right can ever reach a given party in a system where rights may be copied, undecidable in the general matrix formalism of Harrison, Ruzzo, and Ullman, and decidable in the take-grant, schematic, and typed variants that restrict how rights move.
- **Discretionary access control**, also **DAC**, also **identity-based access control**: a scheme where whoever owns a resource decides who else may use it and, holding the grant option, may pass that decision on, with ownership transfer moving the right and cascading revocation withdrawing whatever was granted onward, costing authority that spreads wherever holders choose, unlike originator controlled access control keeping the source's say over onward release.
- **Mandatory access control**, also **MAC**, also **non-discretionary access control**: a scheme where a system-wide rule decides access and no holder of a resource can override it, costing labels on everything and workflows nobody can locally adjust.
- **Role-based access control**, also **RBAC**, also **RBAC96**: a model where permissions attach to named job functions, standardised as core RBAC, hierarchical RBAC, and constrained RBAC, with user assignment and permission assignment as its relations, a role hierarchy passing permission inheritance from senior roles down to junior roles, and role activation in each session, costing role explosion unless role mining and role engineering curate the catalogue.
- **Attribute-based access control**, also **ABAC**: a model computing each decision from named properties of the requester, the resource, the action, and the surrounding situation such as network zone or device posture, as in context-based, history-based, and risk-adaptive access control, costing attribute providers whose assurance and veracity must be judged, values that go stale, and rule sets whose reach nobody can predict.
- **Policy-based access control**, also **PBAC**, also **XACML**: centrally authored policy documents evaluated as targets, conditions, and effects, reconciled by a combining algorithm such as deny-overrides, permit-overrides, or first-applicable, returning permit, deny, not-applicable, or indeterminate, and attaching obligations and advice the enforcement point must discharge, costing verbosity and specialist tooling.
- **Next Generation Access Control**, also **NGAC**: expressing policy as a graph of assignments, associations, and prohibitions computed by a general policy machine, costing a graph model in place of stored permission lists.
- **Relationship-based access control**, also **ReBAC**: a model deciding from the chain of relationships connecting requester to resource, stored as relation tuples naming a userset, expanded by computed userset and tuple-to-userset rewrites, and read at a consistency token called a zookie so the new enemy problem cannot serve a stale answer, costing unbounded fan-out on deep graphs.
- **Access control list**, also **ACL**: a list where each access control entry names a security identifier and an access mask, held in a security descriptor alongside the system access control list that drives auditing, kept in canonical ACL order so refusals precede grants, with ACL inheritance to new children, costing a walk of everything to find one party's effective permissions.
- **Permission bits**, also **mode bits**: the Unix encoding of read, write, and execute for owner, group, and everyone else, with the setuid bit and setgid bit switching identity on execution and the sticky bit stopping one party deleting another's files in a shared directory, costing granularity so coarse that a POSIX access control list with its mask entry must extend it.
- **Capability token**: a transferable token that both names a resource and carries the authority to use it in stated ways, unforgeable either by kernel protection or by being a large random value, costing tracking of where copies have gone.
- **Object capability model**: a model where the only way to affect anything is to invoke a reference already held, so naming a resource and being allowed to use it are one act, costing a capability-safe language and a world with no ambient authority to fall back on.
- **Confused deputy**: an attack in which a privileged program is tricked into applying its own ambient authority to a resource the requester chose but could not reach alone.
- **Attenuation**: handing on a weaker form of a held authority, as a facet exposing part of an object, a forwarder hiding the real reference, a caretaker that can be told to stop, or a membrane revoking a whole subgraph at once, costing interposition at every boundary.
- **Bell-LaPadula model**: a confidentiality model whose simple security property lets labelled parties read only at or below their level and whose star property lets them write only at or above it, so information never descends, with tranquility of labels assumed, trusted subjects exempted, and System Z the counterexample showing the rules alone insufficient.
- **Biba model**: an integrity model whose strict integrity policy holds no write up and no read down, so untrusted data never contaminates trusted data, with an invocation property limiting what a party may call and a low-water-mark policy that drops a reader's level instead of refusing.
- **Clark-Wilson model**: a commercial integrity model in which constrained data items change only through certified transformation procedures that carry them between valid states as well-formed transactions, converting unconstrained data items at the boundary, confirmed by integrity verification procedures, authorized as an access triple of party, procedure, and data, and governed by numbered certification rules and enforcement rules.
- **Chinese Wall model**, also **Brewer-Nash model**: a model whose simple security rule stops a party reading any company dataset in a conflict-of-interest class it has already read from, and whose star property blocks writes that could carry data between them, with sanitised information exempt.
- **Lattice-based access control**: labels ordered by a dominance relation over sensitivity levels and compartment sets, permitting flows only upward in that order and none in either direction between incomparable labels.
- **Noninterference**: the Goguen-Meseguer noninterference property that what a low-level observer sees is unaffected by anything a high-level party does, whether the flow is explicit or carried by a control decision, weakened to non-inference and generalized noninterference for nondeterministic systems, strengthened to separability, and to the restrictiveness and hook-up property that survive composition.

### Policy architecture and enforcement

- **Policy decision architecture**: the separation between the policy decision point that evaluates rules, the policy enforcement point in the request path that asks and obeys, the policy administration point where rules are authored, the policy information point supplying attribute values a request lacks, and the policy retrieval point holding the rules, costing latency and an authorization availability dependency.
- **Externalised authorization**: moving decisions out of application code into centralised enforcement by a shared service, sidecar enforcement beside each workload, gateway enforcement in front of many services, or embedded enforcement evaluated in process, costing a network call, a declarative policy language to learn, and policy bundle distribution.
- **Default deny**: a posture refusing anything not explicitly permitted, so silence is an implicit deny, reading rules as a closed world policy against the open world policy where anything unmentioned is allowed by default permit, costing an explicit grant for every legitimate case and an outage whenever one is missed.
- **Fail-closed authorization**: refusing requests when no decision can be obtained, keeping the policy at the cost of availability during an evaluator outage, where fail-open authorization keeps availability by discarding the policy exactly when something is wrong.
- **Authorization decision caching**: reusing an earlier decision for an identical request within a stated decision lifetime, and caching refusals as negative decision caching, cutting latency and load at the cost of a stale permission honoured until decision cache invalidation, which continuous access evaluation avoids by deciding on every request.
- **Policy as code**: keeping rules in version-controlled text under review, with policy unit tests asserting a request yields a stated decision, policy coverage measuring what those tests reach, policy simulation against recorded traffic, and shadow enforcement recording what new rules would refuse, costing a build and release pipeline for policy.
- **Insecure direct object reference**, also **IDOR**: a weakness where an identifier supplied by the requester selects a record with no check that they are entitled to it, beside missing authorization where no check runs at all, incorrect authorization where one runs against the wrong identity, resource, or rule, and insufficient granularity of access control.
- **Authorization bypass technique**: reaching a guarded operation by requesting an unprotected administrative interface directly, parameter tampering on an identifier or role field, a path-based bypass writing the route in an equivalent form, a method-based bypass changing the verb, a referer-based access decision, or client-side enforcement of server-side security.
- **Resource-based policy**: an authorization statement attached to the resource listing which actors may use it, evaluated alongside an identity-based policy on the actor and a trust policy naming who may assume a role, where an explicit deny beats every allow and a condition key tests context for tag-based authorization, costing a second place to look.
- **Role assumption**: exchanging one's own identity for a role's authority for a limited period, narrowed by a session policy, extended across accounts by role chaining and cross-account access, and guarded by an external identifier against a confused deputy, costing a chain of records to attribute the actions afterwards.
- **Tenant isolation of authority**: ensuring no grant inside one customer's space can name a resource in another's, by carrying a tenant identifier through every internal call and scoping every query to it, whether tenants sit in a silo model, pool model, or bridge model, costing tenant context propagation through every asynchronous path.
- **Row-level security**: a predicate-based row filter attached to a table so each query sees only permitted rows, with column-level security, cell-level security, and field-level authorization narrowing the grain, label-based row security comparing row labels to a session label, and dynamic data masking obscuring values, costing query plan surprises.
- **View-based access control**: granting rights on a restricted view or by procedure-based access instead of the base tables, filtered by a security barrier view and by query rewriting for authorization adding the entitlement predicate, run under definer's rights or invoker's rights with ownership chaining skipping checks on commonly owned objects, costing view maintenance.
- **Graph reachability check**: deciding a relationship query by walking the tuple graph from requester to resource under a recursion depth limit, answered in reverse by lookup subjects and lookup resources, which meet the filtered listing problem and hot spot tuples, costing a denormalized set index to answer at speed.
- **Revocation latency**: the interval between withdrawing an authority and the withdrawal taking effect everywhere, from the eventual consistency of authority between services, a race between revocation and use, or a session outliving an entitlement, shortened by revocation list checks, a short credential lifetime as revocation, and session invalidation on privilege change.
- **Authorization conflict**: two rules yielding opposite effects for one request, settled by deny precedence or by specificity precedence between a negative authorization and a broader grant, exposed by policy conflict detection and policy reachability analysis as policy shadowing, policy redundancy, an unreachable policy rule, or overlapping rule ambiguity.

### Privilege, its management, and its review

- **Privilege**: an ability to act beyond the restrictions normally applied to an account, conferred by the system rather than by a resource owner, distinct from a permission pairing one operation with one resource and from an entitlement recorded in an entitlement catalogue for governance.
- **Principle of least privilege**: the rule that a party hold only the rights sufficient for its task and only as long as needed, receiving just the references it must reach, alongside the need-to-know rule demanding a task requirement beyond sufficient clearance.
- **Privilege escalation weakness**: gaining rights beyond those assigned, vertically into a higher level of authority, horizontally into a peer's data, or locally from a foothold to the machine's administrative account, by privilege chaining several small grants into a larger one, or through a privilege dropping error, whether by defect, misconfiguration, or abuse of a legitimate feature.
- **Excessive privilege**: rights held beyond a party's function, written as a wildcard grant matching every resource or action, held permanently by an account that needs them rarely, or left by incorrect default permissions and insecure inherited permissions, named a least privilege violation and execution with unnecessary privileges, widening whatever a compromise reaches.
- **Process capability set**: the superuser's powers split into individually holdable units, governed by the permitted set a process may hold, the effective set currently in force, the inheritable set passed to programs it runs, and a bounding set no descendant can exceed, costing helpers that gain more than intended.
- **Delegation**: an arrangement letting one party exercise authority belonging to another with a record of who authorised what, unlike impersonation which hides the actor, requested as on-behalf-of authorization by token exchange, constrained delegation limiting the targets a service may reach where unconstrained delegation limits nothing, costing enforcement at every hop of the delegation chain.
- **Privileged access management**, also **PAM**: privileged session brokering with privileged session recording, credential vaulting and credential checkout rotating secrets per use, just-in-time access leaving zero standing privilege, and break-glass access kept for an emergency access procedure, costing the most attractive target in the estate.
- **Administrative tier model**, also **enterprise access model**: a division of administration into tiers so that authority over tier zero, the systems controlling identity itself, is never used from a lower tier, worked from a privileged access workstation under the clean source principle with credential tiering and administrative account separation, costing hardware and duplicated accounts.
- **Permission boundary**: a cap fixing the maximum authority any grant to a party can confer, and an organisation-wide authorization guardrail no account-level grant can exceed, allowing delegated administration safely at the cost of denials no local team can explain.
- **Separation of duties**, also **SoD**, also **segregation of duties**: policy splitting a sensitive task so no one person can complete it alone, enforced as static separation of duty over mutually exclusive roles, dynamic separation of duty at activation, and object-based or history-based separation of duty over a record or workflow, reinforced by rotation and mandatory leave, costing a maintained list of toxic combinations and staffing for every step.
- **Two-person rule**, also **dual control**, also **four-eyes principle**: requiring a designated action to be performed or approved by two parties, whether through split knowledge, a quorum, any m of n named approvers, a maker and a separate checker, or a countersignature, costing coordination and leaving collusion as the residual failure.
- **Access review**, also **access recertification**, also **entitlement review**: a periodic access review campaign in which a named entitlement owner reaffirms or withdraws each right held, defended by last-used access data showing when each was exercised and kept as access certification evidence, costing reviewer time and rubber-stamp approval because removal risks breaking work.
- **Permission creep**, also **privilege creep**: growth of a person's rights as their tasks change, because additions are made and matching removals are not, leaving privilege accumulation, role drift, orphaned permissions whose purpose is gone, and dormant accounts nobody uses.
- **Joiner-mover-leaver process**: granting birthright access on hire, making a mover adjustment that replaces rather than supplements rights on transfer, and deprovisioning them on departure, costing an authoritative feed of those events, a connector per system, and the leaver lag in which a credential still works.

### Release of information under policy

- **Multilevel security**: running one system for information of several classifications at once, relying on labels enforced by a minimal privileged kernel, with domain and type enforcement, multi-category security, and assured pipelines expressing the rules, whether dedicated to one level, operated at system high, compartmented, or truly multilevel, costing a verified core and restricted functionality.
- **Declassification**: the authorized decision that information no longer needs its protection, carried out by a regrade of the item's label downward under a write-down authorization, sanitising or redacting what it contains, and vested in a trusted downgrader, unlike spillage, which is its accidental arrival somewhere unauthorized, costing an accountable human judgement per item.
- **Cross-domain solution**: a controlled path between networks of different classifications, either an access cross-domain solution presenting one desktop onto several or a transfer cross-domain solution moving data through a filter chain doing data type verification and deep content inspection behind a protocol break, or a device carrying data one way only, costing accreditation and throughput.
- **Inference control**: restricting which aggregate answers a statistical database gives, by query set size restriction and query auditing of the history, so a tracker attack or a differencing attack cannot isolate one record, bounded by the fundamental law of information recovery holding that enough accurate answers reveal individuals, costing refused analysis.
- **Statistical disclosure control**: the body of methods for releasing statistics without revealing individual contributions, using cell suppression on sensitive cells caught by a threshold rule, dominance rule, or p-percent rule, complementary suppression so margins cannot recompute them, and controlled rounding or random rounding of counts, costing accuracy in the released figures.
- **Data swapping**: exchanging attribute values between similar records so no released record is a real person's, beside rank swapping, the post-randomisation method, micro-aggregation, noise addition, the cell key method, and fully synthetic data or partially synthetic data replacing records outright, costing distorted relationships between attributes.
- **Generalisation**: replacing a precise value with a broader category, applied as global recoding across a whole release, local suppression of single risky values, top-coding and bottom-coding of the tails, or sampling only a fraction of records, with de-identification removing direct identifiers and pseudonymisation replacing them by consistent artificial values, costing analytic detail.
- **k-anonymity**: the property that every released record shares its quasi-identifiers with at least a stated number of others, strengthened by l-diversity and t-closeness over the sensitive values, defeated by a homogeneity attack, a background knowledge attack, or a linkage attack against named data to achieve re-identification, and measured as prosecutor risk, journalist risk, or marketer risk.
- **Differential privacy**: the guarantee that one person's presence or absence between neighbouring datasets changes released answers by at most a factor set by the privacy parameter epsilon, pure differential privacy in strict form and approximate differential privacy with a small failure probability, holding post-processing invariance, extended by group privacy, spent from a privacy budget, costing accuracy.
- **Global sensitivity**: the largest change one individual's record can make to a query's exact answer, which sets how much noise a mechanism must add, distinguished from the local sensitivity of the dataset at hand and the smooth sensitivity bound that can be used safely.
- **Laplace mechanism**: adding noise from a Laplace distribution scaled to a query's global sensitivity for the pure guarantee, with the Gaussian mechanism giving the approximate one and the discrete Gaussian mechanism and snapping mechanism defeating the floating point attack on noise sampling, costing accuracy and constant-time sampling to close a timing channel in a private mechanism.
- **Exponential mechanism**: choosing among discrete options with probability weighted by a utility score, as report noisy maximum returns the largest of several noisy counts, the sparse vector technique answers a stream of threshold queries paying only for those that pass, and propose test release checks first whether local sensitivity is low, costing accuracy.
- **Local differential privacy**: applying the guarantee on each person's device before anything is sent, as randomised response does with a coin flip, removing the need to trust the central model's collector, with the shuffle model, amplification by shuffling, and privacy amplification by subsampling recovering some accuracy, costing far more noise.
- **Sequential composition**: the accounting rule that the privacy loss random variable adds across queries on the same data, with parallel composition charging only the largest loss on disjoint parts, advanced composition and optimal composition bounding many releases tightly, the moments accountant tracking higher moments, and a privacy filter and odometer running to privacy budget exhaustion.
- **Rényi differential privacy**: a restatement of the guarantee as a divergence between output distributions, giving tighter accounting over many releases, alongside concentrated and zero-concentrated differential privacy bounding the loss's mean and spread, Gaussian differential privacy stating it as a hypothesis test, and Bayesian differential privacy assuming a prior.
- **Pufferfish framework**: a customisable privacy definition naming which secrets must stay hidden and which assumptions the data satisfies, instantiated as Blowfish privacy with publisher-declared constraints and as metric differential privacy scaling protection by distance, beside label differential privacy, joint differential privacy, personalised differential privacy, and pan-privacy.
- **Differentially private gradient descent**, also **DP-SGD**: clipping and noising per-example gradients so a trained model satisfies the guarantee against a membership inference attack, with objective perturbation noising the learning objective instead, subsample and aggregate combining disjoint computations, and private aggregation of teacher ensembles training a student on privately aggregated votes, costing accuracy.

## Boundaries and what crosses them

### Mediation and the trusted base

- **Reference monitor**: mechanism that intercepts every attempted access to a resource and decides it against a stated policy before the access proceeds, anchoring the trusted computing base whose failure voids the policy, at the cost of checking work on every access.
- **Reference monitor requirements**, also **tamper-proof mediation**, **non-bypassable mediation**, **verifiable mediation**: the conditions a deciding mechanism must satisfy, being unmodifiable by the code it judges, unskippable, small enough to analyze, and consulted afresh rather than inferred from an earlier check.
- **Trusted computing base minimization**: shrinking what must be correct for protection to hold, at the cost of pushing function across new interfaces that must then be guarded.
- **Trusted computing base inflation**: growth of the set of components whose failure breaks protection, usually by adding privileged helpers, drivers, or injected sidecars inside the boundary.
- **Security kernel**: minimal protected core that decides all resource requests for a system and is kept small enough to verify, at the cost of constrained functionality.
- **Protection domain**, also **domain isolation**: the property that code in one domain cannot read, write, or divert code in another outside defined interfaces, together with the boundary and the permitted resource set that express it.
- **Sandbox**, also **confinement**: environment that runs untrusted code with a deliberately narrow set of reachable resources for the whole of its execution, at the cost of broken functionality wherever the narrowing is wrong.
- **Login spoofing**: presentation of a counterfeit credential prompt by an unprivileged program in order to harvest a password.
- **Guard**, also **data diode**, **unidirectional gateway**, **air gap**: elements placed between separately classified systems, from a content-inspecting relay through one-way hardware to no connection at all, at the cost of accreditation effort, lost acknowledgements, and manual transfer.
- **Object-capability model**: protection model in which a program's only authority is the set of unforgeable references it holds, at the cost of pervasive plumbing to pass references.
- **Ambient authority**, also **confused deputy problem**, **permission re-delegation**: authority a program holds for who it is rather than what it was handed, and the resulting flaw where a privileged component performs a protected action for a caller that lacks the right.
- **Capability leak**: accidental hand-off of a reference to a less trusted party, permanently widening that party's authority.
- **Capability attenuation on delegation**, also **revocable forwarder**, **attenuating forwarder**, **membrane**, **powerbox**, **sealer and unsealer pair**: handing on a weakened, switchable, or opaque derivative of an authority instead of the authority itself, at the cost of a wrapper on every delegated resource.
- **Isolation cost**, also **boundary crossing cost**, **compartment granularity trade-off**, **diagnosability loss under confinement**: the throughput, memory, and diagnostic clarity lost to mediated calls and duplicated state, which pushes designs toward fewer and coarser compartments.
- **Sandbox policy drift**, also **policy widening under pressure**, **escape hatch**: divergence between a confinement policy and a program's real needs, settled in practice by loosening whichever rule caught the last failure until the boundary permits the original incident.

### Privilege levels and the kernel boundary

- **Protection ring**, also **ring 0**, **ring 3**, **ring minus one**, **ring minus two**, **ring minus three**: hardware hierarchy of execution privilege holding the kernel at zero and applications at three, with informal names below zero for a hypervisor, system management mode, and an out-of-band management coprocessor.
- **Privilege level**, also **current privilege level**, **supervisor mode**, **user mode**, **machine mode**, **exception level**: the processor's current authority setting, named differently per architecture, deciding which instructions and which mappings are legal.
- **Privileged instruction**, also **sensitive instruction**: instruction that faults outside a sufficiently privileged mode, or whose behavior depends on privilege, which is why an unmodified guest cannot simply be run deprivileged.
- **Mode switch**, also **trap**, **gate descriptor**, **call gate**: controlled transition into a more privileged level through a named checked entry point rather than an ordinary jump, at the cost of slow transitions and descriptor bookkeeping.
- **Kernel boundary**, also **system call boundary**: the line separating kernel code and data from user code, crossed only at defined entry points, where every argument arrives untrusted.
- **Confused kernel argument handling**: kernel code treating a user-supplied pointer or length as trustworthy, which lets user code direct kernel accesses.
- **User pointer accessor discipline**: requiring kernel code to reach user memory only through checked helper routines, at the cost of verbosity and of accessors that must themselves be audited.
- **Privileged access restriction on user memory**, also **SMEP**, **SMAP**, **privileged access never**, **privileged execute never**: processor features faulting kernel execution of user pages and kernel reads or writes of user memory outside deliberately unmasked windows, at the cost of annotating every intended access.
- **Kernel page-table isolation**, also **KPTI**, **KAISER**: giving user mode a page table that omits kernel mappings, at the cost of an address-space switch on every kernel entry and exit.
- **Address space isolation**, also **split address space**, **shadow address space**: mapping only what a privilege level legitimately needs, at the cost of mapping churn, copying across the split, and coherence bookkeeping between mappings.
- **Kernel lockdown**, also **kernel module signing**, **kernel self-protection**: closing the interfaces and load paths by which a privileged user could modify the running kernel, at the cost of broken debuggers, out-of-tree drivers, and hibernation.
- **Monolithic kernel as one protection domain**: architecture in which all kernel code shares full authority, so one bug in the system calls, device nodes, control commands, or parsers reachable by unprivileged code yields the whole machine.
- **Shatter attack**: privilege escalation by sending window messages from a low-privilege process to a higher-privilege window on the same desktop.
- **Window and desktop isolation**, also **user interface privilege isolation**, **session zero isolation**, **private object namespace**: blocking messages from lower to higher privileged windows, placing services in a session with no desktop, and giving each process its own view of kernel object names, at the cost of broken accessibility, automation, and cross-process rendezvous.
- **Microkernel**, also **microkernel minimality principle**, **multiserver system**, **exokernel**, **user-space device driver**: architecture keeping only scheduling, address spaces, and communication privileged while drivers and services run as restartable unprivileged processes, at the cost of message-passing overhead on every interaction.
- **Separation kernel**, also **partitioning kernel**, **multiple independent levels of security**, **time partitioning**, **space partitioning**: minimal kernel whose only job is fixing each partition's memory and processor windows and permitting exactly the declared channels between them, at the cost of a configuration frozen before deployment.
- **Partition separation properties**, also **data isolation between partitions**, **information flow control between partitions**, **periods processing**, **fault isolation between partitions**: the guarantees a partitioned system claims, namely no cross-partition access, communication only over configured channels, sanitization of shared resources between uses, and no propagation of one partition's failure.
- **Formally verified kernel**, also **functional correctness proof**, **refinement proof**, **integrity theorem**, **confidentiality theorem**: kernel carrying a machine-checked proof that its implementation meets a specification of its isolation properties, at the cost of a frozen feature set and heavy proof effort.
- **Verification assumption set**: the conditions a kernel proof rests on, such as correct hardware, compiler, and specification, outside which the proof establishes nothing.
- **Time protection**: kernel enforcement that partitions cannot influence one another through shared hardware timing state, at the cost of flushing and statically partitioning microarchitectural resources.

### Separating and dropping privilege

- **Privilege separation**, also **privilege separation broker**, **monitor process**, **worker process**, **target process**: splitting a program so the part handling untrusted input holds no authority and asks a retained privileged part for the few operations it needs, each right taken just before the operation that needs it and dropped after, at the cost of restructuring and an interface that must validate everything.
- **Process-per-task isolation**, also **process-per-library isolation**, **utility process**, **instance-per-request isolation**: giving each unit of work or each risky parser its own short-lived process reached by messages, at the cost of memory footprint, startup latency, and serialization on every call.
- **Zygote process**, also **fork-then-restrict pattern**, **spare renderer**: pre-initialized template process forked to create restricted children cheaply, at the cost of shared template state and a window before restrictions take effect.
- **Privilege dropping at startup**, also **irreversible privilege drop**, **supplementary group clearing**, **deferred privileged initialization**, **privilege revocation**: shedding elevated identity, groups, and rights immediately after the operations that required them, at the cost of rigid startup ordering since some rights cannot be regained.
- **Incomplete privilege drop**, also **saved set-user identifier**, **real, effective, and saved identity triple**: the failure in which a process keeps a stored identity or group membership that lets it reacquire the authority it appeared to surrender.
- **Set-user-identifier binary**, also **set-group-identifier binary**, **set-user-identifier sandbox helper**: executable running with its owner's or its file's identity rather than its caller's, and therefore inheriting attacker-controlled environment, descriptors, and arguments.
- **Environment and descriptor sanitization before privilege use**, also **descriptor closing before privilege change**, **descriptor passing**: clearing loader and locale variables and inherited descriptors before a privileged action, and handing a restricted process only already-opened resources, at the cost of breaking callers that relied on inheritance.
- **Linux capabilities**, also **capability permitted set**, **capability effective set**, **capability inheritable set**, **capability bounding set**, **ambient capabilities**, **file capabilities**: division of root's authority into separately grantable units held in per-process sets and on executables, at the cost of coarse units and inheritance rules that surprise.
- **Capability that grants the machine**, also **CAP_SYS_ADMIN**, **CAP_SYS_MODULE**, **CAP_SYS_PTRACE**, **CAP_DAC_OVERRIDE**, **CAP_SETUID**, **CAP_NET_RAW**, **CAP_SYS_CHROOT**: individual Linux powers broad enough that holding one defeats the surrounding confinement, covering kernel code loading, arbitrary process inspection, permission bypass, identity change, raw sockets, and root pivoting.
- **Capability dropping profile**, also **drop all capabilities baseline**, **no-new-privileges flag**, **securebits**, **disallow privilege escalation**: keeping an explicit list of retained powers and forbidding any later execution from gaining more, at the cost of discovering the true list through production failures.
- **Debugger access restriction**, also **Yama ptrace scope**, **non-dumpable process attribute**, **protected process light**: limiting which processes may attach to, dump, or open a handle to which others, at the cost of broken debuggers and lost crash diagnostics.
- **Restricted token**, also **job object restrictions**, **AppContainer**, **jail token for a content process**: launch-time restriction bundle applied to a process before it handles content, with groups disabled, declared capabilities, private storage, and limits on the process group, at the cost of packaging requirements and opaque access failures.
- **Dedicated service user**, also **dynamic service identity**, **least-privilege service account for a workload**, **privileged helper daemon**, **service unit hardening directive**: running each service under its own minimal identity with declarative restrictions applied by the service manager, at the cost of enumerating rights, unstable file ownership, and a standing privileged helper to validate.

### What a process may ask, and who decides

- **System call filtering**, also **system call interposition**, **seccomp**, **filter-based secure computing mode**, **strict secure computing mode**: restricting for a process's remaining life which kernel requests it may issue, decided from the request number and register arguments, at the cost of hard failures when a needed request is absent.
- **System call allowlist**, also **system call denylist**, **seccomp profile**, **default runtime filter profile**, **filter stacking**: a named policy listing permitted or forbidden requests for a workload, layered so it can only tighten, at the cost of breaking on library updates that reach for new requests and of missing requests the author never considered.
- **Filter verdict**, also **filter action kill**, **filter action errno**, **filter action trap**, **filter action trace**, **filter action log**, **user notification filtering**: the choice a filter has on a matched request, being terminate, fail with an error, signal the program, hand it to a supervisor, or permit and record, at the cost of mishandled errors and no protection while only recording.
- **Pointer argument blindness**: the inability of a register-level filter to examine the memory a request points at, which is why path and structure checks cannot live inside one.
- **Filter bypass by equivalent request**, also **multiplexed request as a filter hole**, **device control request as a wildcard**, **compatibility mode request path**, **architecture-specific numbering divergence**, **virtual dynamic shared object**: reaching filtered functionality through a differently numbered request, an argument-dispatched multiplexer, a driver control command, a legacy calling interface, or code the kernel serves without an entry at all.
- **Interposition time-of-check race**, also **argument copy race**, **path resolution mismatch**, **symbolic link race against a monitor**: a monitor and the kernel disagreeing about what a request refers to, because the argument, the resolved path, or a link component changed between the inspection and the use.
- **Self-imposed access restriction**, also **Landlock**, **pledge**, **promise**, **unveil**, **Capsicum capability mode**, **descriptor rights limiting**: facilities by which a program narrows its own reachable paths, operation classes, and descriptor rights in layers that never widen, at the cost of coarse classes and enumerating paths in advance.
- **Sandbox profile language**, also **application sandbox profile**, **Seatbelt profile**, **extended Berkeley Packet Filter security module**: declarative or loadable policy naming the operations and paths a process may use, applied at launch or attached to kernel decision hooks, at the cost of opaque rules and hard failure diagnosis.
- **Mandatory access control enforcement module**, also **Linux security module framework**, **security module stacking**: kernel component applying an administrator-set policy to every operation regardless of the file owner's wishes, at the cost of policy authoring, hook coverage gaps, and unclear denial attribution.
- **Type enforcement**, also **security context**, **domain transition**, **label transition**, **multi-category separation**, **simplified label enforcement**, **path-based mandatory access control**, **profile-based confinement**: tagging subjects with domains and objects with types or paths and permitting only the operations a rule joins, at the cost of a large rule base and filesystem passes to relabel objects.
- **Enforcement mode choice**, also **permissive mode**, **enforcing mode**, **complain mode**, **profile learning**, **policy boolean**, **audit denial record**: running a policy so denials take effect, or so they are only recorded while a profile is grown from observed behavior, at the cost of no containment during the learning period.
- **Policy over-permission**, also **unconfined domain**, **privileged tier exemption**: writing rules broad enough to make the system work, or exempting a namespace from the restricted profile, which quietly removes the containment the mechanism was installed to provide.
- **Mandatory integrity control**, also **integrity level**, **low integrity process**, **untrusted integrity level**, **no-write-up rule**: labeling processes and objects from untrusted through system and denying every write to a higher label, at the cost of confusing failures when labels mismatch.
- **Integrity measurement architecture**, also **file appraisal**, **verified filesystem integrity**, **per-file integrity verification**: hashing files or whole read-only images as they are read and refusing those that fail an appraisal rule, at the cost of read overhead and metadata maintained across every legitimate update.
- **Application control policy**, also **managed installer**, **signed-only execution posture**, **code integrity guard**, **arbitrary code guard**, **child process policy**, **image load restriction policy**, **extension point disable policy**: host allowlisting of executable code plus per-process restrictions locked at creation, at the cost of heavy rule maintenance, blocked legitimate software, and broken just-in-time compilation.

### Filesystem views, namespaces and containers

- **Jail**, also **change root**, **chroot**, **zone**, **global zone**, **branded zone**, **hierarchical jails**: binding a process tree to a filesystem subtree with restricted process, address, and device views on a shared kernel, at the cost of an administrative instance that owns them all and, in the weakest form, no protection against a privileged process.
- **Mount option confinement**, also **read-only mount**, **read-only root filesystem**, **no-execute mount option**, **no-set-identifier mount option**, **no-device mount option**: attaching filesystems so writes, execution, identity bits, or device nodes are inert, at the cost of breaking software that expects to write, run, or ship device nodes there.
- **Bind mount**, also **root directory pivot**, **mount propagation control**, **private mount propagation**: assembling a confined filesystem view and detaching the old root while keeping mount events from spreading to the host, at the cost of exposing everything under a bind source and subtle sharing when defaults are left in place.
- **Filesystem view minimization**, also **masked path**, **hidden process view**, **device allowlist**, **private temporary directory**, **minimal image without a shell**: presenting only the files, kernel interface paths, and device nodes a workload actually uses, at the cost of breaking in-container tuning, monitoring tools, and any diagnosis from inside.
- **Namespace**, also **mount namespace**, **network namespace**, **process identifier namespace**, **interprocess communication namespace**, **UTS namespace**, **control group namespace**, **time namespace**: kernel mechanism giving a process group its own instance of a global name space, at the cost of no protection wherever one is shared or missing.
- **User namespace**, also **identifier mapping**, **subordinate identifier range**, **identity remapping for containers**, **rootless container**: private mapping of user and group numbers letting a process be privileged inside while unprivileged outside, at the cost of ownership confusion on shared volumes and restricted networking, storage, and devices.
- **Unprivileged user namespace attack surface**: the kernel code paths that unprivileged identifier mapping makes reachable, historically a productive source of privilege escalation.
- **Control group**, also **cgroup**, **control group version two unified hierarchy**, **control group delegation**, **memory limit**, **process count limit**, **processor quota**, **block input-output throttling**, **resource quota per tenant**: kernel hierarchy accounting and capping a workload's resource use so it cannot exhaust the host, at the cost of terminations and throttling whenever a limit sits below real demand.
- **Resource exhaustion as a confinement failure**, also **denial of service inside a sandbox**, **fork bomb**, **noisy neighbor as a denial channel**: the case in which a boundary holds for confidentiality and integrity yet fails for availability, because confined code can still consume the shared capacity its neighbors need.
- **Container**, also **container image**, **container runtime isolation**, **container runtime interface**: process group with private namespaces, resource limits, filters, and a packaged layered filesystem, presented as an isolated machine while sharing the host kernel, at the cost of separation weaker than virtualization and defaults that favor compatibility.
- **Shared kernel as a single point of failure**, also **container isolation as a boundary rather than a barrier**: the fact that every container on a host runs one kernel, so a single kernel flaw crosses all of those boundaries at once.
- **Image supply discipline**, also **image provenance enforcement**, **signature verification on image pull**, **image scanning in the pipeline**, **immutable workload discipline**: admitting only signed, scanned images from named build systems and replacing rather than modifying running containers, at the cost of blocked emergency builds, failed pulls, and lost in-place debugging.
- **Stale image**, also **image drift**, **rogue container**, **registry-supplied malicious image**, **image with embedded credential**, **layer history disclosure**: an image deploying superseded content, a container modified away from its image, a workload started outside the pipeline, or a packaged secret still readable in a lower layer.
- **Pod-level isolation**, also **security context**, **sidecar trust assumption**, **sidecar injection**, **init container privilege window**, **ephemeral debug container**: treating a co-scheduled group as one trust unit sharing namespaces internally, with per-workload restriction settings that default to permissive when omitted, at the cost of no separation at all between members.
- **Admission control for workloads**, also **validating admission webhook**, **mutating admission webhook**, **admission enforcement mode**, **admission webhook failure policy**, **workload security standard tiers**, **run as non-root requirement**: rejecting or rewriting a workload specification that breaks isolation rules before it is scheduled, at the cost of blocked deployments and a failure policy trading cluster availability against unchecked admission.
- **Multi-tenancy strength**, also **soft multi-tenancy**, **hard multi-tenancy**, **namespace as a tenancy boundary**, **node isolation for a tenant**, **grouping containers by sensitivity**, **scheduling constraint for isolation**: separating tenants either with logical controls that a determined tenant could cross or with dedicated nodes and virtualization, at the cost of lost packing efficiency and duplicated operation.
- **Runtime class selection**, also **sandboxed workload**, **virtualized container**, **two-layer isolation**, **confidential container**: scheduling untrusted input onto a stronger runtime that gives the workload its own kernel or its own encrypted machine, at the cost of slower start, higher memory use, and compounded overhead.
- **Workload credential scoping**, also **service account token projection**, **token automount disabling**, **cluster join token**, **node authorization scoping**, **workload identity attestation**: issuing short-lived audience-bound credentials only to workloads that need them and proving which workload is asking, at the cost of refresh logic in every consumer and bootstrap complexity.
- **Host agent compromise as cluster compromise**, also **unbounded administrative access to an orchestrator**, **orchestrator node trust assumption**, **mixed workload sensitivity on one host**, **escape blast radius on a shared node**: the fact that a node's agent can run any workload scheduled to it and read its secrets, so one stolen credential or unscoped administrator reaches every workload on that node.

### In-process compartments and language sandboxes

- **Software fault isolation**, also **SFI**, **address masking**, **address sandboxing by guard region**, **binary rewriting for confinement**, **static validator**, **instruction alignment requirement**, **control transfer confinement**: confining untrusted code inside one address space by rewriting its accesses and transfers into a validated region or surrounding that region with unmapped guard pages, at the cost of inserted checks, precise disassembly, and rejected legitimate code patterns.
- **Trampoline**, also **springboard**, **service runtime**, **compartment call boundary**, **register scrubbing at a boundary**, **stack separation between compartments**: the trusted entry and exit sequence through which confined code reaches its host, copying arguments, clearing registers, and switching stacks, at the cost of cycles and memory on every crossing.
- **Memory protection key**, also **protection key**, **key-tagged memory domain**, **protection key register write control**: per-page access tag switched by one register write to give cheap in-process domains, at the cost of few available keys and the need to stop confined code from executing the switch itself.
- **Hardware-enforced compartment**, also **capability hardware compartment**: in-address-space region whose reachable memory the processor itself checks through unforgeable bounded pointers, at the cost of a changed pointer representation and recompilation of everything inside.
- **In-process sandbox**, also **library sandboxing**, **compiled-to-sandbox dependency**, **memory-safe compartment**, **taint-tracked boundary interface**: containing a risky third-party library inside the host's own address space by recompilation, rewriting, or rewriting it in a safe language, at the cost of weaker guarantees, marshalling, and validating every value that returns.
- **WebAssembly sandbox**, also **linear memory**, **linear memory isolation**, **bounds check elision by guard pages**, **base plus offset addressing**, **absence of raw host pointers**: bytecode environment whose instructions name nothing outside one contiguous byte array and its declared imports, at the cost of a bounds check or large reserved range per instance.
- **Module validation**, also **structured control flow**, **type checking of indirect calls**, **function table**, **protected call stack**, **sandbox trap**: static and dynamic checks that make arbitrary jumps and forged code addresses unrepresentable and turn a violation into a clean abort, at the cost of load-time work and a check on every dynamic call.
- **Import as the only authority**, also **WebAssembly system interface**, **preopened directory**, **absence of ambient authority**, **shared-nothing linking**, **component model isolation**, **resource handle**, **world**: the rule that a module reaches the world only through handles and functions its launcher supplies, and that composed components exchange only copied values and unforgeable handles, at the cost of declaring authority in advance and copying at every boundary.
- **Host function flaw as an escape**, also **runtime bug as an escape path**, **compiler as trusted component**, **just-in-time compiler as a sandbox hole**: a bug in a supplied host function, in the engine's memory management, or in generated code, granting confined code effects the sandbox itself would never permit.
- **Intra-sandbox memory corruption**, also **shadow stack inside linear memory**, **writable and executable memory as an isolation failure**: exploitation of a buffer error inside a confined module, which corrupts that module's own data and control state even though the host stays protected.
- **Language runtime sandbox**, also **bytecode verifier**, **class loader namespace isolation**, **stack inspection permission check**, **interpreter isolate**, **realm**, **frozen intrinsics**, **runtime heap sandbox**, **pointer compression cage**: managed runtime that refuses unsafe operations, gives each guest its own globals and heap, and confines object pointers to a reserved region, at the cost of memory per instance and total breaks when its own native code is flawed.
- **Multi-process browser architecture**, also **renderer process**, **browser process**, **broker and target model**, **network service process**: splitting a browser so content parsing runs in a restricted process assumed to hold attacker code, which requests privileged actions from a broker that validates them, at the cost of memory use and a hand-audited request interface.
- **Site isolation**, also **site as an isolation unit**, **registrable domain as the isolation unit**, **process-per-site-instance**, **out-of-process iframe**, **partial site isolation**, **origin lock on a content process**: placing content from different sites in separate processes locked to their assigned site, at the cost of many more processes and complex cross-process layout, input routing, and painting.
- **Cross-origin isolation policy**, also **cross-origin isolated state**, **storage partitioning by top-level site**, **frame sandbox attribute**: headers and document states that sever references, refuse sensitive cross-site data, and strip an embedded document's abilities before content reaches a process, at the cost of broken embeds, pop-ups, and cross-site sign-in.
- **Interprocess communication hardening**, also **message validation at a broker**, **interface definition language for messages**, **capability-style message handle**, **interface broker**, **commit-time origin check**, **renderer kill on policy violation**: treating every message from a restricted process as hostile input, handing it only the endpoints it may use, and terminating it when a message contradicts its permitted state, at the cost of schema and validation work on every message.
- **Attack surface migration**, also **full-chain exploit**, **font parsing relocation**, **graphics process isolation**, **kernel graphics interface lockdown**: the effect by which hardening one process pushes attackers into the services it must still call, so an escape chains a content compromise with a flaw in a broker, driver, or kernel interface.

### Virtualization as a boundary

- **Hypervisor**, also **virtual machine monitor**, **type-one hypervisor**, **type-two hypervisor**: layer giving each guest an illusory machine while controlling every real resource, running either directly on hardware or as a program on a host system, at the cost of a privileged layer whose flaws expose all of its guests.
- **Trap-and-emulate**, also **binary translation for virtualization**, **paravirtualization**, **ring aliasing**, **address space compression**: running a guest deprivileged and emulating, rewriting, or replacing the privileged instructions it attempts, at the cost of per-trap overhead, a modified guest, and hypervisor memory or privilege state the guest can observe.
- **Hardware-assisted virtualization**, also **guest mode and host mode**, **virtual machine control structure**, **virtual machine exit**, **virtual machine entry**: processor support for a guest execution mode that exits automatically on sensitive operations, at the cost of exit latency and a control structure whose misconfiguration is itself an isolation failure.
- **Second-level address translation**, also **nested paging**, **shadow page tables**, **extended page table permission control**: translation of guest physical addresses to host physical addresses so a guest cannot address memory outside its assignment, at the cost of translation overhead and trapped page table writes where hardware nesting is absent.
- **Hypercall**: guest-initiated request into the hypervisor, and the primary attacker-reachable interface of the virtualization layer.
- **Device emulation**, also **device emulation attack surface**, **paravirtualized device interface**, **split driver model**, **driver domain**: software imitation of hardware for a guest, or a shared-memory ring and separate driver machine replacing it, at the cost of a broad parsing surface in privileged code and a backend that must validate every entry.
- **Input-output memory management unit**, also **IOMMU**, **interrupt remapping**, **kernel direct memory access protection**: hardware translating and restricting the memory a device may reach and the interrupts it may raise, at the cost of translation overhead, mapping management, and unsupported peripherals at boot.
- **Direct memory access attack**, also **Thunderclap class attack**, **pre-boot direct memory access window**, **peripheral firmware as trusted code**: a peripheral reading or writing host memory directly, before translation hardware is programmed or through the mapped windows a driver legitimately opened.
- **Device passthrough**, also **single-root input-output virtualization isolation**, **virtual function isolation weakness**, **translation unit group**, **peer-to-peer transaction bypass**, **address translation service abuse**, **function level reset gap**: assigning a physical device or one of its functions to a guest for speed, at the cost of trusting the device's own separation and of paths where peer traffic, pre-translated claims, or leftover state escape the checker.
- **Virtual machine snapshot**, also **snapshot rollback hazard**, **live migration**, **confidential workload migration**, **migration agent**, **container checkpoint and restore**: saving, restoring, or moving a running machine's complete state, at the cost of live secrets inside the image, revoked credentials reinstated on rollback, and trust in the destination host.
- **Virtual machine introspection**, also **semantic gap**, **hypervisor-based monitoring**: inspecting a guest's memory and state from below so guest-level compromise cannot disable the monitor, at the cost of interpreting raw memory as system structures and a privileged component that reads guest secrets.
- **Lightweight machine boundary**, also **MicroVM**, **minimal device model**, **jailer process**, **unikernel**, **library operating system**, **user-space kernel**, **sentry-style emulating process**, **gofer-style file proxy**: giving a workload its own kernel or its own reimplementation of kernel interfaces with only a handful of devices, at the cost of unsupported hardware, incomplete compatibility, and slower requests.
- **Disposable virtual machine**, also **micro-virtualization**, **template-based virtual machine**, **compartmentalized desktop operating system**, **trusted graphical domain**, **network domain isolation**, **firewall domain**, **cross-domain copy control**, **remote browser isolation**: building each activity from a short-lived machine derived from a read-only template, with a thin trusted compositor and mediated copying between them, at the cost of memory, latency, and constant cross-machine friction.
- **Hypervisor attack surface minimization**, also **disaggregated hypervisor**, **formally verified hypervisor**, **firmware-level guest attack surface**: stripping unused emulated devices and virtual firmware, splitting the layer into unprivileged components, and proving its isolation properties, at the cost of lost guest compatibility, inter-component latency, and heavy verification effort.

### Escapes, the walls that failed

- **Sandbox escape**, also **sandbox bypass**: exploited flaw, or use of a legitimate but overlooked interface, giving confined code authority outside the environment built to contain it.
- **Change root escape**, also **directory descriptor escape**, **nested change of root escape**: leaving a restricted filesystem root by holding a directory descriptor opened before the change, or by changing root into a subdirectory and walking upward past the original.
- **Jail escape**: obtaining authority outside a jail, typically through a kernel flaw, an exposed device node, or a mounted host path.
- **Container escape**, also **container breakout**, **cross-container attack**: obtaining execution or authority on the host from inside a container, or reaching a neighboring container through the shared kernel, network, or volume.
- **Host resource exposure**, also **host filesystem mount exposure**, **root filesystem mount as total exposure**, **host process namespace sharing**, **host network namespace sharing**, **host interprocess communication sharing**, **shared volume as a lateral channel**: configuration handing a container a host path, process view, interface set, or shared segment, so escape is a matter of using what was granted.
- **Container runtime socket exposure**, also **orchestrator credential exposure inside a workload**, **metadata service access from a workload**, **node agent interface exposure**, **anonymous authentication to a node agent**, **pod escape to node**, **node-to-cluster escalation**: a workload reaching the runtime's control socket, a cluster token, the instance metadata endpoint, or an unauthenticated node interface, and thereby the node's identity and the cluster behind it.
- **Privileged container**: container run with every capability, unmasked kernel interfaces, and device access, which removes practically every boundary between it and the host.
- **Release agent escape**, also **core pattern escape**, **kernel module load escape**, **raw memory device exposure**: writing a host-executed helper or crash handler path from inside a workload, loading kernel code, or using a device node that grants direct physical memory access.
- **Runtime-mediated escape**, also **runtime binary overwrite escape**, **symbolic link exchange escape**, **working directory leak escape**, **runtime hook abuse**, **image entrypoint as host execution**, **storage driver escape**, **device passthrough escape**: escapes through the privileged runtime itself, by overwriting its executable, racing a mount destination, inheriting a host descriptor, or feeding it lifecycle commands and layers that it parses on the host.
- **Kernel-mediated container escape**, also **binary format handler registration escape**, **namespace file descriptor leak**, **kernel exploit as escape**, **capability-driven escape**, **filesystem race escape through the process filesystem**, **debug interface escape**: escapes using the shared kernel rather than any runtime bug, by registering a host interpreter for a file format, rejoining a host namespace through a leaked descriptor, exploiting a kernel flaw, or using a granted capability.
- **Virtual machine escape**, also **guest-to-host escape**, **guest-to-guest attack**, **host-to-guest intrusion**: code inside a guest obtaining execution or memory access in the virtualization layer or a co-resident guest, or the host reading and altering guest memory.
- **Emulated device escape**, also **VENOM**, **floppy controller escape**, **Cloudburst**, **display device escape**, **network device emulation escape**, **hypervisor memory disclosure**: escape through a memory error in emulated hardware reached by writing a virtual device's registers, including legacy controllers the guest appears never to use.
- **Guest interface escape**, also **hypercall interface flaw**, **hypercall double fetch**, **ring buffer validation flaw**, **shared clipboard and drag-and-drop escape**, **shared folder escape**, **guest tools as attack surface**, **emulated device state machine confusion**: escape through argument handling in the interfaces a guest is meant to call, including arguments changed after the hypervisor validated them.
- **Hyperjacking**, also **Blue Pill**, **SubVirt**, **hypervisor rootkit**, **thin malicious hypervisor**, **system management mode rootkit**, **management coprocessor compromise**: installing malicious code beneath a running operating system so it becomes a guest, or inside firmware and management processors that no guest-level tool can inspect.
- **Escape detection by host monitoring**: watching host-level process and file activity for the signatures of a breakout, at the cost of false positives and of monitoring blinded once the kernel itself is compromised.

### Enclaves, trusted execution and encrypted memory

- **Trusted execution environment**, also **TEE**, **enclave**: hardware-backed execution context whose code and data the hosting operating system cannot read, at the cost of a restricted programming model, expensive transitions, and dependence on one vendor's design.
- **Enclave memory and transitions**, also **enclave page cache**, **enclave page cache map**, **enclave entry call**, **enclave exit call**, **asynchronous enclave exit**, **state save area**, **enclave transition cost**: processor-owned metadata recording which enclave owns each protected page, with controlled calls in and out and register state saved out of the operating system's reach, at the cost of a resident memory limit and cycles on every crossing.
- **Enclave identity**, also **enclave measurement**, **signer identity measurement**, **security version number**, **enclave identity policy**: the digest of an enclave's initial code and layout, the digest of the key that signed it, and the version field by which policy refuses older builds, at the cost of painful updates or trust in an author's future binaries.
- **Sealed storage**, also **sealing key derivation**, **sealing to platform state**, **sealing to signer identity**, **monotonic counter**: encrypting data under a processor-derived key tied to a platform and an identity, with a counter proving the stored copy is current, at the cost of no portability, limited write endurance, and loss on hardware replacement.
- **Enclave rollback attack**, also **enclave forking attack**: replacing an enclave's persisted state with an older sealed copy, or running two instances in parallel and feeding each a different view, since sealing establishes neither freshness nor uniqueness.
- **Iago attack**: attacking a protected component by returning malicious but well-formed results from the operating system services it is obliged to call.
- **Untrusted host interface hardening**, also **untrusted memory pointer sanitization**, **enclave shielding runtime**, **shielded execution**, **enclave library operating system**: copying and validating every value a protected environment receives from a host that is expected to lie, behind a shim letting unmodified applications run, at the cost of a large trusted layer inside the boundary.
- **Enclave debug mode**, also **enclave malware concern**, **enclave denial of service by the host**: launch mode that provides no confidentiality at all, alongside the symmetric facts that protection also hides attacker code and that the host remains free to starve an enclave of resources.
- **Secure world**, also **normal world**, **secure monitor**, **secure monitor call**, **trusted application**, **trusted operating system**, **TrustZone for microcontrollers**, **nonsecure callable region**, **secure gateway instruction**, **veneer function**, **stack sealing**, **banked stack pointers**, **secure partition**, **secure partition manager**: two-state design in which a small monitor owns the only path between an ordinary system and isolated trusted services, entered on small devices at marked gateway instructions with separate stacks, at the cost of a total single point of failure.
- **World-partitioned hardware**, also **world-partitioned memory controller**, **world-partitioned peripheral control**, **peripheral security assignment**, **security attribution unit**, **realm-style third state**, **granule protection table**, **physical memory protection region**: hardware assignment of memory regions and devices to exactly one world or privilege mode so any cross-world access faults, at the cost of losing shared device use, few available regions, and added translation latency.
- **Boomerang attack**, also **world-shared buffer double fetch**: abuse of a trusted application's write access to normal-world memory as a proxy to reach memory the caller could not touch, including buffers the untrusted side changes between two reads.
- **Secure element**, also **embedded secure element**, **secure enclave coprocessor**, **secure coprocessor boot chain**, **tamper-resistant key store**, **hardware-backed keystore**: separate tamper-resistant processor with its own memory and verified boot that generates and uses keys which never enter application memory, at the cost of tiny capacity, slow operations, no key export, and a closed vendor interface.
- **Trusted user interface**, also **protected confirmation**, **screen capture protection**: display and input path owned by the trusted state, producing a signed statement of what the user approved, at the cost of limited graphics, developer effort, and no defense against a camera.
- **Confidential computing**, also **data-in-use protection**, **operator exclusion from the trusted base**, **enclave model versus confidential machine model**: running a workload in hardware-isolated attested memory the operator cannot read, either as part of a process or as a whole machine, at the cost of hardware dependence and attestation infrastructure.
- **Memory encryption**, also **total memory encryption**, **transparent memory encryption default**, **multi-key memory encryption**, **per-virtual-machine encryption key**, **address-tweaked encryption**, **integrity-protected memory**, **memory encryption engine**, **memory replay protection**: encrypting and version-checking memory as it leaves the processor under a platform key or per-guest keys mixed with the block address, at the cost of key slots capping protected guests, counter storage, and forbidden page migration.
- **Encrypted virtual machine**, also **secure encrypted virtualization**, **encrypted state extension**, **secure nested paging**, **reverse map table**, **page validation**, **trust domain**, **trust domain extensions**, **secure arbitration mode**, **confidential compute realm**, **realm management monitor**: guest whose memory and register state are encrypted under keys the hypervisor never holds, with ownership of each physical page recorded and accepted by the guest, at the cost of a large new trusted component and redesigned host interfaces.
- **Shared versus private page marking**, also **guest-hypervisor communication block**, **bounce buffer for confidential input-output**, **paravisor**, **enlightened guest**, **virtual machine privilege level**, **trusted device interface**, **peripheral link encryption**: the pages and layers through which a confidential guest deliberately exposes what the host or a device must see, at the cost of a copy per transfer and exposure wherever a page is marked wrongly.
- **Host attack on a confidential guest**, also **SEVered attack**, **nested page remapping attack**, **unencrypted register state exposure**, **CipherLeaks attack**, **ciphertext side channel**, **CacheWarp attack**, **malicious interrupt injection**, **malicious communication exception injection**: attacks from the privileged layer that relocate a guest's pages, read or alter its register state, infer plaintext from deterministic ciphertext, drop its modified cache lines, or inject exceptions to steer its control flow.
- **Attested launch**, also **launch measurement**, **guest launch policy field**, **runtime measurement register**, **workload owner as trust root**, **versioned chip endorsement key**, **trusted computing base recovery event**: measuring a confidential guest's initial memory, firmware, and policy so its owner decides which configurations receive secrets, at the cost of measurements changing with configuration and forced re-attestation after a firmware version bump.
- **Acknowledged side-channel limit**: the stated position that hardware protected environments do not defend against timing and microarchitectural leakage, leaving that boundary to the workload itself.

### Boot integrity and roots of trust

- **Hardware root of trust**, also **root of trust for measurement**, **root of trust for storage**, **root of trust for reporting**, **root of trust for update**, **root of trust for detection**, **root of trust for recovery**: the elements whose integrity is assumed rather than checked, being the first code that measures, the protected place measurements are kept, the key that signs statements about them, and the logic that authorizes updates, finds corruption, and restores firmware.
- **Chain of trust**, also **boot read-only memory**, **immutable first stage**, **processor-verified first stage**, **low-level bootloader**, **iBoot**, **broken link in the chain**: sequence in which each stage verifies the next from unchangeable silicon upward, nullified in full by any stage that transfers control without verifying its successor.
- **Measured boot**, also **platform configuration register**, **register extension**, **measurement event log**, **measurement order dependence**, **measurement transcript**, **static root of trust for measurement**, **dynamic root of trust for measurement**, **measured launch environment**, **authenticated code module**, **launch control policy**: recording each stage's digest into append-only protected registers without deciding whether to proceed, optionally restarting the record at a late launch, at the cost of needing a separate verifier and of boot failures after legitimate platform change.
- **Verified boot**, also **secure boot**, **signature verification at boot**, **allowed signature database**, **forbidden signature database**, **shim loader**, **machine owner key**, **platform key**, **key exchange key**, **authenticated firmware variable**, **setup mode**, **custom key enrollment**, **physical presence requirement**: refusing to execute a stage whose signature fails against firmware-held key and revocation databases that only signed updates may change, at the cost of key custody, enrollment burden, and blocked alternative systems.
- **Bootloader bypass class**, also **signed but vulnerable component**, **signed loader configuration flaw**, **boot logo parsing vulnerability**, **test key shipped in production firmware**: flaws in properly signed boot code that make it execute unverified content, and signed components too widely deployed to revoke.
- **Anti-rollback counter**, also **rollback index**, **verified boot metadata**, **one-time programmable fuse**, **boot configuration lock**, **unlocked bootloader state**, **boot state colors**: stored version values and burned fuses refusing any image older than the highest ever run, and the reported state when verification is disabled, at the cost of no downgrade even when a new version is broken.
- **Firmware write protection**, also **firmware update authentication**, **firmware update capsule authentication**, **management memory lock**, **firmware resilience**, **golden recovery image**, **firmware corruption detection**: latching firmware storage read-only after early boot and accepting only verified updates with a protected copy to restore from, at the cost of bricking risk, duplicate storage, and settings that cannot be undone.
- **Firmware boot phase exposure**, also **pre-initialization firmware phase**, **driver execution phase**, **option read-only memory**, **resume boot script attack**, **system management mode callout**, **device firmware upgrade mode**, **recovery mode as a boot path**: the phases and alternative paths where unverified modules, peripheral-supplied firmware, replayed configuration scripts, or a weaker recovery target run with full platform privilege.
- **Trusted platform module**, also **firmware trusted platform module**, **virtual trusted platform module**, **platform hierarchy**, **endorsement hierarchy**, **storage hierarchy**, **null hierarchy**, **primary seed**, **storage root key**, **enhanced authorization**, **policy digest**, **authorization value**, **dictionary attack lockout**, **locality**: chip or firmware service providing measurement registers, seed-derived key hierarchies, sealing, and a policy language binding an object's use to platform state and user secrets, at the cost of slow operations, policies hard to audit, and sealed data lost after any legitimate update.
- **Boot-time physical attack**, also **evil maid attack**, **bus sniffing against a discrete module**, **register forgery by module reset**: installing a boot-stage implant on a powered-off machine, reading secrets crossing the bus to a measurement chip, or resetting that chip while the platform runs so chosen measurements can be replayed into cleared registers.
- **Hypervisor-enforced code integrity**, also **virtualization-based security**, **credential isolation**, **isolated user mode**, **trustlet**, **kernel integrity protection**, **page protection layer**, **secure page table monitor**, **trusted execution monitor**, **fast permission restrictions**: using a monitor below the kernel to own page table updates, keep kernel text unwritable, and hold authentication secrets out of reach, at the cost of driver breakage, transition overhead, and another trusted component.
- **Signed system volume**, also **system integrity protection**, **sealed key protection**, **system software authorization**, **personalization nonce**, **Image4 manifest**, **unique device key**, **group device key**, **data vault**, **effaceable storage**: read-only system volume under a verified hash tree, with install images personalized to one device by an online signing service and storage keys bound to device and boot state, at the cost of connectivity, blocked downgrade for recovery, and data lost after unexpected state change.

### Attestation, its roles and its flow
