- **Remote attestation**, also **local attestation**: proving to a distant party, or to another protected component on the same machine, what software and hardware configuration is actually running, at the cost of verification infrastructure and exposure of platform identity.
- **Attester**, also **attesting environment**, **target environment**: the party making claims about its own state, divided into the part that collects and signs the claims and the part whose state is being reported.
- **Verifier**, also **verifier owner**, **attestation appraisal policy**, **third-party attestation service**, **self-hosted attestation with cached collateral**: the party appraising evidence against rules its owner sets, run either as a vendor service or locally from downloaded certificates and revocation data, at the cost of an external dependency or collateral that must be kept fresh.
- **Relying party**, also **relying party owner**, **attestation result**: the party that acts on a verifier's signed conclusion under its own policy, such as releasing a secret or admitting a workload.
- **Attestation evidence**, also **attestation report**, **quote**, **entity attestation token**, **software identification tag**, **concise software identification**, **concise reference integrity manifest**: the signed statements a target produces about itself, carried in standard token and manifest formats, before any appraisal decides whether they are acceptable.
- **Endorser**, also **endorsement**, **endorsement key**, **endorsement certificate**, **reference value provider**, **reference values**, **golden measurements**: the manufacturer vouching for an attesting environment's keys and capabilities, and the party publishing the expected measurements evidence is compared against.
- **Passport model**, also **background-check model**, **two-step appraisal**: the two attestation flows, one where the attester obtains a result and presents it to relying parties and one where the relying party forwards evidence to a verifier itself, at the cost of freshness management or of verifier availability per request.
- **Attestation freshness**, also **nonce-based challenge**, **epoch identifier**: binding a report to a recent challenge value or a periodically published epoch so an old report cannot stand in, at the cost of a round trip before evidence can be made or a distribution channel for epochs.
- **Attestation replay attack**, also **attestation relay attack**, **attestation proxying**: presenting a genuine but stale report from an earlier acceptable state, or forwarding a healthy machine's report as one's own.
- **Attestation key**, also **attestation identity key**, **provisioning certification**, **quoting enclave**, **provisioning certification enclave**, **platform certificate chain**: the hardware-held signing key and the certified short-lived keys derived from it whose signature makes a report trustworthy, at the cost of provisioning services, chain validation, and revocation infrastructure.
- **Group signature attestation**, also **direct anonymous attestation**, **privacy certification authority**: signing reports as an anonymous member of a hardware group, or through an intermediary that converts a unique identity into an unlinkable one, at the cost of complex cryptography, harder revocation, and a party able to relink.
- **Layered attestation**, also **device identifier composition engine**, **compound device identifier**, **alias key**, **device identity key**, **composite device attestation**, **device measurement protocol**: attestation in which each boot layer derives its identity from its own measurement and certifies the next, and a lead attester combines component reports into one, at the cost of firmware support in every layer and device.
- **Attestation-gated secret release**, also **secure key release**, **key release policy**, **attestation-based admission**, **hardware root of trust for container hosts**: releasing a key or admitting a node only when a report satisfies policy, at the cost of workloads failing whenever measurements legitimately change and outages when no verifier answers.
- **Runtime attestation**, also **measurement of mutable state as a limitation**: repeated appraisal during execution rather than only at launch, which still reports what was loaded rather than what memory now contains, at the cost of measurement overhead.

### Application platform boundaries

- **Mobile application sandbox**, also **per-application account**, **per-application data directory**, **application identifier binding**, **shared application identity**, **scoped storage**, **user-chosen file access**, **file-based encryption**, **credential-encrypted storage**, **direct boot**, **work profile**, **per-user isolation on a shared device**: confining each application to its own identity, storage, and per-file keys tied to its package name and signing key, at the cost of a mediated mechanism for every legitimate exchange and duplicated storage per profile.
- **Permission model**, also **install-time permission**, **runtime permission**, **permission group**, **one-time permission**, **foreground-only grant**, **coarse location alternative**, **signature-level permission**, **settings-gated special permission**, **unused permission auto-revocation**, **entitlement**, **restricted entitlement**, **consent fatigue**, **privacy indicator**: naming each capability so an application holds it only when granted, with grants narrowed by time, visibility, precision, or signer, at the cost of asking users for decisions they cannot evaluate and features failing without explanation.
- **Code signing enforcement**, also **page-granular signature validation**, **library validation**, **hardened runtime**, **notarization**, **Gatekeeper policy**, **quarantine attribute**, **code directory hash**, **team identifier**, **just-in-time compilation restriction**, **write-xor-execute enforcement on a mobile platform**, **dynamic code loading restriction**: kernel refusal to execute pages that fail to validate against an application's signature and the platform's countersignature, with no mapping both writable and executable, at the cost of blocked plug-ins, generated code, and unsigned local builds.
- **Interprocess boundary on a mobile platform**, also **Binder**, **Binder transaction**, **Parcel**, **Binder token**, **caller identity check at a Binder boundary**, **Mach port right**, **content provider**, **exported component**, **file provider**, **temporary content grant**, **pending intent**: kernel-mediated calls carrying the caller's identity and unforgeable handles, through which all cross-application interaction passes, at the cost of an access-control surface on every exposed component and care in restoring a cleared identity.
- **Cross-application authority confusion**, also **implicit message hijacking**, **message redirection**, **pending intent hijacking**, **custom scheme hijacking**, **custom permission squatting**, **task affinity hijacking**, **parcel mismatch vulnerability**, **accessibility service abuse**, **overlay attack**, **tapjacking**: attacks in which another application claims a name, scheme, permission, or task, or draws over the interface, so a request, its authority, or the user's tap arrives at the attacker.
- **Platform distribution and integrity control**, also **sideloading**, **unknown sources restriction**, **pre-publication application review**, **remote application removal**, **device management policy enforcement**, **lockdown mode**, **integrity verdict**, **device integrity attestation**, **application attestation**, **root detection**, **hooking framework as a threat**: gating what may be installed and retained on a device and requiring a platform-signed judgment that device and application are unmodified, at the cost of a vendor kill switch, blocked independent distribution, and checks that instrumentation defeats when made client-side.

### The wire and the adversary's position on it

- **Man-in-the-middle attack**, also **MITM**, **machine-in-the-middle**, **meddler-in-the-middle**, **on-path attacker**, **active network attacker**: adversary relaying traffic between two parties, able to read, drop, and rewrite it while each party believes the other is direct.
- **Off-path attacker**, also **blind spoofing**, **non-blind spoofing**, **off-path injection**: adversary that cannot observe the victims' traffic and must predict header values well enough that an endpoint accepts its packets.
- **Eavesdropping**, also **passive interception**, **wiretapping**, **packet sniffing**, **promiscuous mode capture**: reading traffic in transit while transmitting nothing, breaking confidentiality with no change for the endpoints to notice.
- **Traffic injection**, also **packet crafting**, **replay attack on the wire**, **message deletion attack**, **message reordering attack**, **packet delay attack**: forging, resending, dropping, reordering, or holding packets so an endpoint acts on a message the legitimate sender did not send at that moment.
- **Relay attack**, also **reflection attack on a challenge protocol**, **near-field communication relay attack**, **ghost and leech**, **contactless card emulation abuse**: forwarding an authentication exchange between a genuine prover and verifier that are nowhere near each other, or returning a verifier's own challenge so it answers its own question.
- **Distance bounding**, also **distance bounding protocol**: proving physical closeness by timing rapid challenge and response rounds, at the cost of specialized radios and very tight timing tolerances.
- **Network tap**, also **port mirroring**, **SPAN**, **packet broker**, **out-of-band inspection**, **sensor placement**, **network detection coverage**, **east-west traffic visibility**: copying link or port traffic to monitors that can alert but not block, at the cost of backplane capacity, frames dropped under load, and blind spots wherever no collection point sits.
- **Flow record**, also **NetFlow**, **IPFIX**, **sFlow**, **flow sampling**, **passive asset discovery**: summary of a conversation's endpoints, ports, timing and byte counts exported by a router or sensor instead of full packets, enough to see who talked to whom and to find hosts nobody registered, at the cost of losing content and of sampling that hides short flows.
- **Encrypted traffic analysis**, also **handshake fingerprinting**, **metadata-only detection**, **encrypted traffic visibility loss**, **protocol anomaly alerting**, **network detection and response**: inferring what a session carries from its unencrypted handshake fields, sizes and timing once inspection can no longer read content, at the cost of inference rather than proof and of fingerprints that change with every client release.
- **Network forensics**, also **session reconstruction**, **rolling capture buffer**, **capture integrity hashing**, **capture deduplication**, **sensor clock synchronisation**, **capture retention cost**: recovering what crossed a link from stored traffic, reassembling sessions and hashing the capture so it stands up later, at the cost of storage that bounds how far back questions can reach and of clocks that must agree across sensors.

- **VLAN hopping**, also **double tagging attack**, **switch spoofing attack**, **dynamic trunking protocol abuse**, **private VLAN proxy attack**: reaching a virtual network the attacker's port does not belong to, by nesting tags, negotiating a trunk on an access port, or bouncing frames off the router back to a neighbor.

- **Local name resolution spoofing**, also **LLMNR and NBT-NS spoofing**, **multicast DNS spoofing**, **WPAD hijacking**, **proxy auto-configuration script abuse**, **zeroconf name claim race**, **name squatting**, **service discovery poisoning**, **port squatting**, **service location protocol poisoning**, **fake captive portal**, **service impersonation on a local network**: answering the lookups, discovery probes, and sign-in interceptions a host performs when it cannot find a service, so credentials and traffic reach the claimant.
- **First-hop hardening**, also **DHCP snooping**, **dynamic ARP inspection**, **IP source guard**, **port security**, **sticky MAC learning**, **BPDU guard**, **root guard**, **loop guard**, **storm control**, **router advertisement guard**, **DHCPv6 guard**, **neighbour discovery inspection**, **secure neighbour discovery**, **private VLAN isolation**, **native VLAN pruning**, **static ARP entries**, **MACsec**, **broadcast domain minimisation**: switch features accepting configuration replies, address bindings, and topology messages only where policy expects them, with per-hop frame encryption where available, at the cost of per-port configuration everywhere and outages when equipment moves.
- **Source address spoofing**, also **IP source spoofing**, **connectionless source spoofing**, **decoy source addresses**, **source routing abuse**, **loose source routing attack**, **strict source and record route abuse**, **IP options abuse**, **record route reconnaissance**, **IP identification field inference**: placing a false source address or a chosen path in a packet so replies go elsewhere, filters are bypassed, and the sender cannot be identified.
- **Source address validation**, also **ingress filtering**, **BCP 38**, **egress filtering**, **unicast reverse path forwarding check**, **strict reverse path forwarding**, **loose reverse path forwarding**, **SAVI**, **bogon filtering**, **martian address filtering**, **anti-spoofing access control list**, **hop count filtering**: dropping arriving or departing packets whose source address cannot legitimately come from that direction, at the cost of breaking asymmetric routing and multihoming and of lists that must be kept current.
- **IPv6 boundary weakness**, also **neighbor cache exhaustion**, **routing header type zero abuse**, **hop-by-hop option processing denial**, **advertisement guard evasion by fragmentation**, **transition tunnel abuse**, **public tunnel relay abuse**, **dual-stack policy gap**, **address family translation bypass**, **all-nodes multicast probing**, **address space size as an assumed defense**, **hardware address disclosure by interface identifier**: attacks and gaps particular to the larger address family, where controls cover one family only, tunnels carry traffic past inspection, and a large subnet is mistaken for protection.

- **Fault attack family**, also **differential fault analysis**, **Bellcore attack**, **safe-error attack**, **computational safe-error attack**, **memory safe-error attack**, **ineffective fault analysis**, **statistical ineffective fault analysis**, **statistical fault attack**, **persistent fault analysis**, **fault sensitivity analysis**, **algebraic fault attack**, **sign change fault attack**, **invalid curve fault attack**, **twist curve fault attack**, **collision fault analysis**, **fault on a deterministic signature nonce**, **secure boot bypass by glitching**, **signature verification skip**, **software-induced fault injection**, **CLKSCREW**, **Plundervolt**, **VoltPillager**, **frequency and voltage overdrive attack**, **undervolting fault against a victim process**: key recovery from faulty, unchanged, or biased outputs, and the software-driven variants that reach a protected computation by pushing frequency and voltage past their stable range.
- **Rowhammer**, also **memory disturbance error**, **single-sided hammering**, **double-sided hammering**, **one-location hammering**, **many-sided hammering**, **non-uniform hammering pattern**, **half-double**, **RowPress**, **TRRespass**, **Blacksmith**, **ZenHammer**, **target row refresh bypass**, **error correcting code bypass in rowhammer**, **ECCploit**, **RAMBleed**, **memory massaging**, **page grooming**, **page table entry corruption by bit flip**, **Flip Feng Shui**, **Drammer**, **SMASH**, **GLitch**, **Throwhammer**, **Nethammer**: corruption of memory a process cannot address by repeatedly activating physically neighboring rows, steered onto a chosen victim structure and triggered from sandboxed script, a graphics processor, or remote traffic.
- **Emanation and air-gap channel**, also **air gap covert channel**, **power analysis**, **simple power analysis**, **differential power analysis**, **correlation power analysis**, **difference of means test**, **mutual information analysis**, **selection function**, **electromagnetic analysis**, **near-field probing**, **screaming channels**, **photonic emission analysis**, **software-based power analysis**, **energy reporting interface leakage**, **PLATYPUS**, **Hertzbleed**, **frequency throttling channel**, **thermal covert channel**, **collide and power**, **remote power side channel in shared logic**, **ring oscillator sensor**, **long wire crosstalk leakage**, **acoustic cryptanalysis**, **keyboard acoustic emanation**, **AirHopper**, **GSMem**, **Fansmitter**, **DiskFiltration**, **BitWhisper**, **SATAn**, **RAMBO**, **screen brightness covert channel**, **indicator light covert channel**, **accelerometer keystroke inference**, **gyroscope acoustic eavesdropping**: recovery of secrets from a device's current draw, radiated field, light, heat, or sound, and deliberate modulation of those emissions to carry data across a boundary that has no network path at all.

### What a developer applies against a channel

- **Constant-time programming**, also **data-independent execution time**, **secret-independent control flow**, **secret-independent memory access**, **branchless programming**, **constant-time selection**, **conditional move usage**, **arithmetic masking of a branch**, **constant-time comparison**, **linearized branch**, **loop bound padding**, **dummy operation insertion for timing**, **bitslicing**, **full table scan lookup**, **scatter-gather table layout**, **cache-line-confined table**, **table preloading**, **secret type discipline**, **optimization barrier**, **data operand independent timing mode**: writing code whose instruction sequence and touched addresses depend only on public data, at the cost of speed, readability, difficult review, and optimizers that reintroduce the branch the source removed.
- **Constant-time algorithm choice**, also **Montgomery ladder**, **regular exponentiation**, **fixed-window exponentiation**, **complete addition formulas**, **unified point addition**, **randomized projective coordinates**, **constant-time modular inversion**, **constant-time rejection sampling**, **Path ORAM**, **oblivious data structure**, **data-oblivious algorithm**, **access pattern padding**, **oblivious enclave execution**: algorithms that perform the same operations and touch the same addresses whatever the secret, up to hiding the entire address sequence, at the cost of doubled work, logarithmic bandwidth multipliers, and large slowdowns.
- **Masking**, also **boolean masking**, **arithmetic masking**, **multiplicative masking**, **affine masking**, **higher-order masking**, **masking order**, **share**, **mask refreshing**, **boolean to arithmetic mask conversion**, **masked substitution box**, **table recomputation**, **threshold implementation**, **non-completeness**, **uniformity of sharing**, **domain-oriented masking**, **inner product masking**, **code-based masking**, **consolidated masking scheme**, **low-latency masking**, **private circuit construction**, **non-interference composability**, **strong non-interference**, **probe isolating non-interference**: splitting every sensitive value into random shares so no computed quantity depends on the secret, with constructions that survive hardware glitches and compose without extra refreshing, at the cost of large slowdowns, area, and constant demand for fresh randomness.
- **Hiding, blinding and redundancy**, also **hiding**, **noise injection**, **shuffling**, **random delay insertion**, **dummy round insertion**, **dual-rail logic**, **blinding**, **message blinding**, **exponent blinding**, **scalar blinding**, **base point blinding**, **key refreshing**, **fresh re-keying**, **leakage resilience**, **leakage-resilient pseudorandom function**, **redundant computation**, **verification after signing**, **infective computation**, **instruction duplication**, **temporal redundancy**, **spatial redundancy**, **control flow signature check**, **error detecting code protection**, **glitch detector**: making measurements noisier or misaligned, randomizing inputs so intermediates differ every run, and repeating or checking a computation so one fault is caught, at the cost of throughput, randomness, area, and defeat by averaging or a second fault.
- **Leakage assessment**, also **test vector leakage assessment**, **Welch t-test leakage detection**, **fixed versus random test**, **fixed versus fixed test**, **specific leakage test**, **chi-squared leakage detection**, **moment-based leakage detection**, **security order estimation**, **leakage certification**, **perceived information**, **normalized inter-class variance**, **multiple comparison correction in leakage testing**, **statistical timing test**, **formal verification of constant-time code**, **static analysis for timing leaks**, **taint tracking for secret data**, **symbolic execution for constant-time analysis**, **binary-level constant-time analysis**, **self-composition**, **relational verification**, **product program construction**, **abstract interpretation of cache behaviour**, **verified cryptographic implementation**, **leakage simulator**, **leakage contract fuzzing**: deciding whether measurements depend on secrets at all, by statistical detection over crafted input classes or by machine-checked proof over source and compiled code, at the cost of large trace counts, false alarms, and analysis that stalls on real programs.
- **Residual leakage of a protected implementation**, also **order reduction attack**, **mask reuse leakage**, **randomness cost of masking**, **horizontal attack on a masked implementation**, **probing model**, **glitch-extended probing model**, **robust probing model**, **random probing model**, **noisy leakage model**, **bounded leakage model**, **only computation leaks model**, **second fault defeating a check**, **compiler-induced timing leak**, **cross-device model degradation**, **detection threshold on a test statistic**, **worst-case leakage evaluation**: the ways a defended implementation still leaks, when glitches, transitions, reused randomness, or many samples in one trace pull its effective order below the claimed one, and the formal models written to capture that gap.
- **Channel-aware isolation**, also **cache partitioning**, **way partitioning**, **cache coloring**, **page coloring**, **secure page coloring for sensitive pages**, **way partitioning through a quality of service mechanism**, **cache randomization**, **randomized skewed cache**, **randomized index encryption in a cache**, **randomized fully associative emulation**, **mapping rekeying**, **rekeying interval tuning**, **cache flush on context switch**, **cache line locking**, **multithreading disable**, **core scheduling**, **core reservation for an enclave**, **enclave control of its own page mappings**, **asynchronous exit counting by an enclave**, **transactional page fault hiding**, **timer coarsening**, **fuzzy time**, **high-resolution timer restriction**, **shared memory primitive restriction**, **increased refresh rate**, **guard row allocation**, **allocation-level domain separation**, **physical memory domain isolation**, **refresh management**, **per row activation counting**, **BlockHammer**, **ANVIL**, **probabilistic adjacent row activation**, **hammering detection by performance counters**: partitioning, randomizing, flushing, refreshing, or reserving the shared hardware that carries a channel, and blunting the clocks that measure it, at the cost of lost capacity, throughput, hit rate, and precise timing for legitimate users.

## Operations and delivery

### Intrusion models and adversary tactics

- **Intrusion kill chain**: model laying out an intrusion as ordered phases, from reconnaissance and weaponization through delivery, exploitation, installation, and command and control, to action on the objective.
- **Adversary behavior knowledge base**, also **ATT&CK**: public taxonomy organizing observed adversary techniques under the tactical goals they serve, with separate matrices for enterprise, mobile, and industrial control systems.
- **Tactic, technique and procedure**, also **TTP**: description of adversary behavior at three levels, the goal pursued, the method used, and the specific way it was carried out.
- **Reconnaissance**: pre-compromise tactic of gathering victim host information, victim identity information, victim network information, and victim organization information, by active scanning, address block scanning, wordlist scanning, phishing for information, search of open technical databases, and attack surface enumeration.
- **Open source intelligence**, also **OSINT**: intelligence produced from publicly available records, publications, and postings, gathered by passive reconnaissance through registration record research, passive domain name system research, certificate transparency mining, scan database reuse, search engine dorking, code repository reconnaissance, social media reconnaissance, job posting analysis, and document metadata harvesting.
- **Resource development**: tactic of building, renting, or stealing what an operation needs, covering infrastructure acquisition, infrastructure compromise, account establishment, persona development, capability development, capability acquisition, capability staging, exploit purchase, code signing certificate acquisition, and an operational relay box network of residential and rented proxies.
- **Initial access**: tactic covering the step by which an adversary first obtains a foothold, whether through an exposed application, valid accounts, a lure, a drive-by page, removable media, or a trusted third party.
- **Execution**: tactic covering every means of making adversary code run on a victim, through command and scripting interpreter abuse, shell command execution, script host abuse, native application programming interface invocation, shared module loading, exploitation for client execution, system service execution, container deployment, serverless execution, cloud administration command, and user execution of a malicious file.
- **Persistence**: tactic of keeping access across reboots, credential changes, and eviction attempts, through boot or logon autostart execution, event triggered execution, service creation and service hijacking, account manipulation, and traffic signaling that wakes an implant on a chosen packet.
- **Privilege escalation tactic**: tactic of gaining rights beyond those first held, through kernel and service flaws, elevation control mechanism abuse, domain policy modification, group policy modification, domain trust modification, file and directory permission modification, unconstrained delegation abuse, constrained delegation abuse, certificate template misconfiguration abuse, and privileged directory object permission abuse.
- **Defense evasion**: tactic whose purpose is to avoid detection, blocking, and attribution, covering binary padding, compilation after delivery, indicator removal from tools, fileless storage, embedded payload and steganographic payload delivery, version downgrade attack, indicator blocking, virtual instance execution, direct volume access, debugger evasion, deobfuscation at runtime, and exploitation for defense evasion.
- **Credential access**: tactic of obtaining usable authentication secrets from a compromised environment, including a credential in a configuration file, a secret in an environment variable, a command history credential, an unattended installation credential, and forced authentication that makes a host reveal material to adversary infrastructure.
- **Discovery**: tactic of learning a compromised environment's composition, covering system information discovery, account discovery, permission group discovery, process discovery, software discovery, security software discovery, file and directory discovery, network share discovery, remote system discovery, network service scanning, domain trust discovery, and cloud service discovery.
- **Internal reconnaissance**: methodical mapping of an environment after access, covering system network configuration discovery, system network connection discovery, system owner discovery, system service discovery, system time discovery, configuration store querying, browser information discovery, peripheral device discovery, policy object discovery, device driver discovery, and log enumeration, assembled by attack path mapping into a route to a target.
- **Lateral movement**: tactic of progress from one compromised host to others, through remote service execution, remote scheduled task, administrative share abuse, service control remote execution, remote management shell movement, secure shell lateral movement, remote desktop hijacking, shared content tainting, internal spearphishing, software deployment tool abuse, and hands-on-keyboard activity.
- **Pivoting**: routing of attacker traffic through a compromised host to reach networks not directly reachable, by port forwarding, reverse port forwarding initiated outbound, SOCKS proxying, tunneling through a compromised host, and jump host abuse of a gateway built to administer every segment.
- **Collection**: tactic of gathering data of interest inside a compromised environment, covering screen capture, keylogging, clipboard capture, audio capture, video capture, browser session collection, email collection, email forwarding rules, database dumping, cloud storage collection, and automated collection, with data staging and archiving of collected data before one transfer.
- **Exfiltration**: tactic of moving collected data out of an environment, by exfiltration over the command channel, exfiltration over an alternative protocol, exfiltration over a web service, exfiltration to cloud storage, transfer of data to a cloud account inside one provider, DNS-based exfiltration, encrypted exfiltration, steganographic exfiltration, traffic duplication, and physical media exfiltration.
- **Data transfer size limit evasion**: shaping of an outbound flow so it stays beneath the volume thresholds that would alert, by chunked exfiltration into small pieces, low and slow exfiltration over a long period, scheduled transfer timed to resemble routine synchronization, and automated exfiltration on the tooling's own trigger.
- **Impact**: tactic of destroying, altering, denying, or exposing rather than gaining or holding access, covering disk structure wipe, disk content wipe, encryption for extortion, data manipulation, defacement, endpoint denial of service, compute hijacking, bandwidth hijacking, cloud service hijacking, messaging service pumping, service stops, and recovery inhibition.
- **Dwell time**: elapsed period between an adversary's first access and their eviction from the environment, measured beside breakout time, the shorter interval to their first successful move onto a second host.

### Initial access, lures and social engineering

- **Valid account abuse**: use of genuine credentials obtained elsewhere to sign in as an authorized user, which produces no exploit and no unfamiliar binary for a defender to find.
- **Drive-by compromise**, also **drive-by download**: infection of a visitor by code a merely opened page delivers, reached through watering hole sites, malvertising, search result poisoning, fake update overlays, or an exploit kit that profiles the browser.
- **Malicious document**: file weaponized to execute code when opened or enabled, covering macro-enabled lures, macro code stomping, remote template injection, dynamic data exchange fields, and shortcut files disguised with document icons.
- **Mark-of-the-web bypass**: delivery through an archive, disk image, or nested container chosen so the payload arrives without the origin marking that would otherwise force a warning or a policy check.
- **Hypertext markup language smuggling**: assembly of the malicious file inside the browser from script in a page, so no file crosses the mail or web gateway.
- **Paste-and-run lure**: instruction that persuades a victim to copy a supplied command into their own terminal or run box and execute it, so the delivery carries no attachment.
- **Trusted relationship abuse**: entry through a partner, contractor, or managed provider that already holds legitimate access into the target, including use of a supplier's own remote connection.
- **Phishing**: fraudulent message crafted to make a recipient reveal secrets or run attacker content, turning on a lure hook that makes the target act, tailored as spearphishing to a named person, as whaling to an executive, and as thread hijacking inside a real conversation.
- **Vishing**, also **quishing**: social engineering by voice call, text message, or scanned code, each moving the victim onto a channel no mail gateway inspects, including the callback lure that carries no link and has the victim telephone the attacker.
- **Adversary-in-the-middle phishing kit**, also **reverse proxy phishing**: proxy serving the genuine site through itself and relaying the login in real time, capturing session tokens along with the password and any second factor.
- **Phishing kit**, also **phishing as a service**: packaged pages, logic, and collection endpoints reproducing a brand's login flow, sold or rented so an operator needs no skill of their own.
- **Consent phishing**, also **illicit consent grant**: lure asking the victim to approve a hostile application's access to their account data, whether by request or by clickjacking the consent screen, leaving access that a password reset does not remove.
- **Device code phishing**, also **QR code login hijack**: lure that has the victim enter an attacker-generated device authorization code or scan the image carrying it, handing over an authenticated session without any credential crossing the wire.
- **Push notification fatigue**, also **MFA fatigue**: repeated approval prompts sent until a worn-down victim accepts one.
- **Business email compromise**, also **BEC**: fraud in which instructions sent as a trusted insider, executive, or supplier redirect money or data, including payroll diversion, invoice fraud, and messages from a genuinely compromised vendor mailbox.
- **Advance fee fraud**: scheme promising a large payout in return for a small up-front payment that is never recovered, one of a family of consumer frauds with the technical support scam, sextortion, the investment grooming scam known as pig butchering, and the recovery scam that revisits known victims.
- **Pretext**: the false situation a lure supplies to justify its request, drawn from authority, urgency, scarcity, and fear, or from a reciprocity pretext, a social proof pretext, a liking pretext, a commitment pretext, or a synthesized voice, and judged by its pretext plausibility against the target's real context.
- **Lookalike domain**, also **homoglyph attack**: registered name visually close to a trusted one, built from confusable characters, added words, or a hijacked domain's subdomains so forged mail and pages pass a glance.
- **Pharming**: redirection of a victim to an adversary's site by corrupting name resolution rather than by a link, related to angler phishing that impersonates a brand's support account on a social platform and to brand impersonation that reproduces an organization's visual identity to borrow its trust.
- **Security awareness training**: instruction and simulated lures teaching staff to recognize and report manipulation, delivered as embedded training or just-in-time awareness training, measured by phish-prone rate, simulation click rate, simulation reporting rate, the resilience ratio between them, and the repeat clicker population, costing working time and goodwill and decaying without repetition.
- **Suspicious message reporting**, also **abuse mailbox**: monitored channel through which staff submit doubtful mail for analysis, which buys early warning at the price of a triage queue somebody must staff.

### Malware classes, payloads and anti-analysis

- **Malware**, also **malicious code**: software written or repurposed to act against the interests of the owner of the system running it.
- **Virus**: malicious code replicating by inserting copies of itself into host files or boot structures, in boot sector, master boot record, file infector, macro, multipartite, cavity, companion, and memory-resident forms.
- **Worm**: self-replicating program spreading from host to host with no user running an infected file, by mass mailing, exploitation of listening network services, or copying onto removable media.
- **Trojan horse**: program presenting itself as something the user wants while carrying a hidden hostile function, including the remote access trojan that hands an operator interactive control of shell, files, screen, and camera.
- **Backdoor**: hidden access path that bypasses normal authentication and leaves no ordinary record, including an undocumented fixed credential built into a product's own code.
- **Rootkit**: code that hides itself and other artifacts by subverting the system's own reporting of files, processes, and connections, in user-mode, kernel-mode, bootkit, firmware, UEFI, and hypervisor forms, reached by pre-operating-system boot abuse, bootloader modification, or component firmware modification.
- **Ransomware**: code denying an owner access to data or systems and demanding payment, whether by encrypting files in place, locking the device interface, or destroying data behind a ransom note promising recovery that never existed.
- **Extortion economy**: the criminal division of labor behind modern ransom operations, covering toolkit rental to affiliates, revenue splits, data leak sites, double and triple extortion, big game hunting, and initial access brokers.
- **Wiper**: malicious code whose purpose is irreversible destruction of data, boot structures, or firmware rather than gain, sometimes lying dormant as a logic or time bomb until a chosen condition or date arrives.
- **Infostealer**: code that sweeps a host once for credentials, tokens, cookies, wallets, and documents and then exits, with the harvest traded in bulk as stealer logs.
- **Banking and payment fraud malware**: implants that steal and alter payment sessions, covering keyloggers, form grabbers, screen scrapers, web injects, man-in-the-browser transaction alteration, automatic transfer systems, terminal memory scrapers, checkout skimmers, mobile overlays, and clipboard address rewriting.
- **Botnet**: network of compromised hosts under coordinated remote control, each an enrolled bot awaiting instructions, rented or directed as one resource by a bot herder.
- **Greyware**, also **riskware**, also **potentially unwanted program**: software that degrades or monetizes a machine against its owner's interest without being unambiguously hostile, covering adware, spyware, stalkerware, scareware, and rogue security products.
- **Payload staging**: split of delivery into a dropper, stager, loader, or downloader that fetches the real capability at run time, as against a stageless artifact carrying the whole capability in one file.
- **Implant**: attacker-controlled code left running on a compromised host for continued access, tasked at each check-in, often modular so capability plugins load on demand, built from a template with a campaign watermark, and identified by its mutex name as an indicator and its victim identifier generation scheme.
- **Fileless malware**: operation that writes no executable to disk, living instead in process memory, interpreted scripts, or configuration store values read back by a small launcher.
- **Living off the land binary**, also **LOLBin**: legitimate signed tool already present on the system, used to execute, download, or proxy attacker code so no unfamiliar program appears and path-based trust rules accept it.
- **Packer**, also **crypter**, also **protector**: wrapper that compresses or encrypts an executable and restores it in memory at run time, entered through thread local storage callback abuse or from a code cave and leaving for the original code by a tail jump, defeating pattern matching on the file.
- **Polymorphic malware**, also **metamorphic malware**: code that changes its encrypted appearance on every propagation or rewrites its own instruction sequence for each copy, leaving no constant body to match.
- **Binary obfuscation**: restructuring of compiled code to make it costly to read while preserving behavior, covering control flow flattening, opaque predicates, junk insertion, string encryption, stack string construction, mixed boolean arithmetic obfuscation, function name hashing, import by ordinal, and bytecode for a bundled interpreter.
- **Anti-analysis gating**: refusal of a sample to act until its environment looks real, judged by a hypervisor bit check, a hardware fingerprint check, an analysis artifact check, geography and language, human interaction, long sleeps, and a guardrail key derived from the intended host.
- **Anti-debugging**: checks inside a sample that make interactive inspection fail, by a debug flag check, a global flag check, a debug register check, breakpoint byte scanning, an instruction counter timing check, a parent process check, anti-attach state, exception-based anti-debugging, and a self-integrity checksum that notices a patched byte.
- **Malware behavior catalog**, also **MBC**: published enumeration of malware objectives and behaviors used to describe a sample uniformly, joined by malware attribute enumeration and characterization for a sample's attributes and relationships and by capability detection rules that name what a binary can do.

### Command and control

- **Command and control**, also **C2**: the channel and infrastructure by which an operator directs compromised hosts and receives their output, held on a team server that queues tasking and stores collected data.
- **Beaconing**: implant behavior of contacting its controller at a configured interval, with jitter randomizing the timing so the traffic never forms a fixed rhythm a defender can key on.
- **Egress-only channel**: control design in which the implant always initiates outbound contact, needing no inbound reachability, with slow long-haul and fallback paths kept for recovering access and a multi-stage channel separating tasking from transfer.
- **Protocol blending**: shaping of control traffic to pass as ordinary use, over encrypted web requests, name lookups, diagnostic echo packets, mailbox drafts, a non-standard port, non-application layer protocol control, or protocol tunneling inside a permitted protocol, with data encoding of control traffic and junk data padding, following an operator-editable traffic profile.
- **Cloud service abuse for control**: use of a legitimate hosted storage, chat, or code service as the relay for tasking and output, as one-way web service communication that only pushes or bidirectional web service communication that also collects tasking, alongside remote access software abuse of ordinary support tooling.
- **Ingress tool transfer**: bringing of adversary tooling into an environment after access is established, downloaded by an implant, copied across an existing session, or fetched by a system utility already trusted to reach the network.
- **Dead drop resolver**: retrieval of the true controller address from a comment or profile field on a benign public service, so the implant ships with no hostile address in it.
- **Domain generation algorithm**, also **DGA**: routine computing many candidate controller names from a seed so defenders cannot block the set in advance.
- **Fast flux**: rapid rotation of the addresses behind a single malicious name, and in the double form the authoritative name servers too, so blocking any one host achieves nothing.
- **Operational infrastructure hardening**: adversary practice of hiding controllers behind redirectors, bulletproof hosting, domain fronting, aged and shadowed domains, an internal proxy on a foothold, an external proxy, a multi-hop proxy, residential proxy abuse, anonymizing infrastructure, peer-to-peer tasking, and routine rotation of names and certificates.
- **Sinkholing**: redirection of malicious names to a controlled server that answers harmlessly and records callers, paired with domain registration takedown, which cuts off tasking and enumerates victims at the cost of legal coordination and registrar cooperation.

### Persistence, escalation and evasion techniques

- **Windows persistence mechanisms**: launch points and event triggered execution the platform consults automatically, turned into adversary launchers, covering the registry run key, startup folder, scheduled task, service creation, application shimming, component object model hijacking, image file execution options injection, winlogon helper, accessibility feature abuse, screensaver hijack, windows management instrumentation event subscription, port monitor persistence, and print processor persistence.
- **Unix and macOS persistence mechanisms**: the same idea on Unix-like systems, covering cron persistence, systemd unit persistence, systemd timer persistence, a launch agent, a launch daemon, login item persistence, shell profile modification, trap command persistence, and an implanted authorized key.
- **Library load order abuse**: execution gained by placing a malicious library where the loader looks before the real one, covering dynamic library search order hijacking, library side-loading beside a trusted signed program, phantom library hijacking of a library a program requests but lacks, dynamic linker preloading, profiler library injection, and path environment interception.
- **Server and mailbox persistence**: adversary code added to a running application rather than to the operating system, covering server software component persistence, web server module persistence, transport agent persistence, stored procedure persistence, browser extension persistence, office template persistence, and mailbox rule persistence with an email hiding rule that buries the replies.
- **Cloud identity persistence**: addition of attacker credentials, application secrets, keys, or federated trust to a tenant, with rogue device registration enrolling an attacker's own device and internal image implantation compromising every instance launched from a golden image, so access outlives every rebuilt machine.
- **Web shell**: script planted on a web server that turns ordinary requests into command execution on the host.
- **Account creation and manipulation**: creation of new local, domain, or cloud accounts, or alteration of an existing account's groups, credentials, and authentication factors, to hold access independent of any implant.
- **Bring your own vulnerable driver**, also **BYOVD**: loading of a legitimately signed but flawed driver in order to reach kernel privilege through its flaw, as against kernel driver loading of the adversary's own module.
- **Local privilege escalation**: rise from user to administrative or kernel rights on a host already held, through unquoted service path abuse, weak service permission abuse, writable path hijack, setuid binary abuse, sudo misconfiguration abuse, token impersonation, access token manipulation, parent process identifier spoofing, named pipe impersonation, privileged group abuse, user account control bypass, and a kernel exploit for escalation.
- **Credential dumping**: extraction of authentication material from a compromised host, covering credential store memory dumping, security account manager dumping, directory database extraction, cached credential extraction, browser credential theft, session cookie theft, keychain extraction, data protection interface abuse, private key theft, password manager database theft, and instance metadata credential theft.
- **Kerberos ticket and assertion forgery**: authentication material minted from stolen key material rather than a password, covering golden and silver tickets, diamond and sapphire tickets, forged enterprise certificates, forged federation assertions, skeleton key patching, and shadow credentials written to a directory account.
- **Directory replication abuse**, also **DCSync**: request of password material from a domain controller by impersonating a replication partner, and in the rogue domain controller variant pushing changes through replication so no directory audit record appears.
- **Kerberoasting**, also **AS-REP roasting**: request of service tickets or pre-authentication responses for chosen accounts, then offline cracking of the returned material to recover their passwords.
- **Session token replay**, also **pass the cookie**: reuse of a stolen session artifact, application token, or relayed authentication so the adversary inherits an authenticated session without ever holding the password.
- **Process injection**: execution of attacker code inside another process to borrow its identity, by library and shellcode injection, hollowing, doppelganging, ghosting, atom bombing, queued procedure calls, thread hijacking, module stomping, early bird queueing, section mapping, and reflective loading from memory.
- **Endpoint agent blinding**: defeat of in-process monitoring by restoring hooked library code, hand-written and indirect system calls, patching the script scanning interface, disabling event tracing, and processor mode transitions the monitor does not follow.
- **Security control tampering**: attack on the defensive stack itself, covering security tool termination, disabling logging, audit policy tampering, driver-based tool unloading, safe mode boot abuse, firewall rule modification, execution allowlist bypass, cloud log disabling, cloud firewall modification, trust control subversion, code signing policy modification, quarantine attribute bypass, and security alerting spoofing.
- **Tamper protection**: vendor-enforced lock on a security agent's settings, processes, and drivers, reinforced by protected process isolation that keeps unprivileged code out of a sensitive process and by kernel integrity enforcement that refuses unsigned kernel code, at the cost of broken debugging, legacy drivers, and slower legitimate administration.
- **Masquerading**: presentation of attacker code under a trusted name, path, or icon, using lookalike filenames, double extensions, text direction override characters, system directories, hidden files and directories, a hidden window, a hidden user account, and stolen or abused code signing certificates.
- **Indicator removal**: deletion or alteration of the traces an intrusion leaves, covering log clearing and selective record deletion, timestomping, file and artifact wiping, shell history clearing, network connection history clearing, mailbox data clearing, process argument spoofing, encoded commands, and persistence artifact clearing at the end of an operation.
- **Mobile platform technique set**: adversary methods particular to phones, covering device administrator and accessibility abuse, notification reading for one-time codes, foreground persistence, icon hiding, removal prevention, runtime code download, synthetic input injection, application repackaging, and sideloading.
- **Industrial control technique set**: adversary methods against process control, covering the unauthorized command message, spoofed reporting message, blocked command message, controller tasking modification, program download, program upload, operating mode change, parameter modification, alarm setting modification, control logic hooking, project file infection, safety instrumented function manipulation, point and tag identification, and a rogue master.
- **Manipulation of control**: alteration of the commands a control system issues so a physical process behaves as the adversary chooses, alongside manipulation of view falsifying operator displays, denial of control, denial of view, and the consequences loss of control, loss of view, loss of protection, loss of safety, loss of productivity and revenue, damage to property, and theft of operational information.

### Detection engineering

- **Detection engineering**: discipline of building, testing, and maintaining the logic that turns recorded activity into alerts, at a continuing cost in authorship, validation, and upkeep.
- **Indicator of compromise**, also **IOC**: observable fact whose presence shows a system has already been affected by hostile activity.
- **Indicator of attack**, also **IOA**: observable pattern of behavior showing hostile activity in progress, whether or not it has yet succeeded.
- **Indicator type**: the three grades of observable, being atomic facts such as an address, computed values such as a file digest, and behavioral descriptions of what an actor does in sequence.
- **Pyramid of pain**: ranking of indicator types by how much effort an adversary must spend when a defender denies each one, from hashes at the base to behavior at the top.
- **Detection rule**: encoded expression of what activity should raise an alert, written in a packet inspection rule language, a network event scripting language, an event query language, a structured pattern expression, or as a search-based detection query or streaming detection query, carrying ongoing cost in tuning and in the noise it produces.
- **Open cybersecurity schema framework**, also **OCSF**: vendor-neutral event schema so a detection written once applies across sources, joined by the Elastic common schema for field naming and the common event format for delimited transport, at the cost of mapping every producer into it.
- **Portable detection rule**, also **Sigma rule**: detection logic written in a vendor-neutral format and translated per platform, which eases sharing at the cost of platform-specific precision.
- **Byte-pattern rule**, also **YARA rule**: expression matching sequences and structures inside files or memory, which catches variants of a lineage at the cost of authoring skill and scan time.
- **Signature-based detection**: matching of observed data against known-bad descriptions and digests, which is cheap and exact and is defeated by a single changed byte or an undescribed variant.
- **Behavioral detection**: alerting on sequences of actions rather than artifacts, which survives adversary tooling changes at a high cost in telemetry volume and tuning, and includes heuristic judgment about suspicious structure.
- **Anomaly detection**: alerting on departure from a model of normal, built from statistical baselines and outlier measures, which surfaces novel activity and produces volume wherever normal is broad or the baseline period was dirty.
- **User and entity behavior analytics**, also **UEBA**: scoring of accounts and devices against their own history and their peers, which finds insider and credential misuse at high data and tuning cost.
- **Detection as code**: management of detection logic in version control through a detection engineering lifecycle of detection requirements, detection peer review, shadow deployment of a rule, a rule promotion pipeline, detection versioning, and rule retirement, which brings engineering discipline and process overhead to every small change.
- **Detection unit test**: automated check that a rule fires on a recorded positive and stays silent on recorded negatives, extended by emulation-based validation that executes the real technique in a safe environment, at the cost of maintaining test data and tooling.
- **Alerting and detection strategy**, also **ADS**: documentation template recording a detection's goal, its logic, the false positives it is known to produce, how it was validated, and the response expected, at the cost of writing discipline for every rule.
- **Tuning**: adjustment of a detection's conditions and thresholds to reduce noise, by named allowlists and suppression rules, which protects analyst attention and creates blind spots an adversary can occupy.
- **Detection coverage mapping**: recording of which adversary behaviors current detections address, which exposes gaps and invites false confidence when coverage is counted rather than validated.
- **Detection robustness**: property of a detection that keeps firing when an adversary changes tooling, as against detection brittleness that keys on an incidental detail, detection fidelity meaning an alert means what its author intended, and decay as the environment moves beneath it.
- **Funnel of fidelity**: model describing how a large volume of events narrows through triage, investigation, and remediation to few real actions, measured along the way by the positive predictive value of an alert, escalation rate, alert volume per analyst, and the signal-to-noise ratio of a source.
- **Detection maturity level model**: scale describing how deep into adversary behavior an organization's detections reach, beside the hunting maturity model for hunt capability and the detection engineering maturity matrix rating a program's people, process, and technology.
- **File and message detection analytics**: the branch judging artifacts rather than behavior, covering file hashing for detection, file content rules, file content analysis, emulated file analysis, homoglyph detection, locator analysis, message content analysis, identifier reputation analysis, domain name reputation analysis, and sender reputation analysis, at the cost of stale lists and false verdicts.
- **Network traffic detection analytics**: the branch judging traffic, covering protocol metadata anomaly detection, network traffic signature analysis, client-server payload profiling, connection attempt analysis, relay pattern analysis, remote terminal session detection, community deviation analysis, resolution traffic analysis, application protocol command analysis, passive certificate analysis, and file carving from traffic, at the cost of blindness to encrypted channels.
- **Process and platform detection analytics**: the branch judging hosts, covering system call analysis, process lineage analysis, process spawn analysis, file creation analysis, file access pattern analysis, script execution analysis, memory boundary tracking, process self-modification detection, process code segment verification, shadow stack comparison, scheduled job analysis, system firmware verification, and database query string analysis, at the cost of very high event volume.
- **File integrity monitoring**, also **FIM**: comparison of watched files against recorded baselines so modification is reported, extended to service binary verification and system file analysis against vendor content, at the cost of alert volume through every legitimate update and baselines that must be re-established after patching.
- **Identity behavior detection analytics**: the branch judging accounts, covering authentication event thresholding, authorization event thresholding, domain account monitoring, local account monitoring, session duration analysis, web session activity analysis, resource access pattern analysis, job function access pattern analysis, geolocation logon pattern analysis, user data transfer analysis, and credential compromise scope analysis, at the cost of learning periods and role churn.
- **Modeling the defended environment**: maintenance of the pictures detection depends on, covering software inventory, configuration inventory, network node inventory, data inventory, hardware component inventory, asset vulnerability enumeration, network mapping, logical link mapping, physical link mapping, network traffic policy mapping, access modeling, system dependency mapping, service dependency mapping, and data exchange mapping, at the cost of collection that ages with the estate.

### Telemetry, logging and audit integrity

- **Endpoint telemetry**: stream of process, file, configuration, module load, and network events reported from a host, including the process tree that gives each action a parentage, whose value scales with the storage and agent overhead it demands.
- **Command line telemetry**: capture of the full argument string each process was started with, which is invaluable for detection and records every secret an operator or a script passed as an argument.
- **Network telemetry**: observation of connections drawn from devices and sensors, as flow records summarizing one conversation or as full packet capture, covering unmanaged hosts at the cost of content blindness or very large storage.
- **Audit log**: record of security-relevant actions produced by a configured auditing facility, whose completeness trades directly against volume, performance, and the risk of overwriting history when undersized.
- **Log coverage gap**: part of an environment producing no usable records, so activity there can be neither detected nor reconstructed.
- **Tamper-evident logging**: recording arranged so alteration of past records is detectable, which raises evidentiary value and adds verification machinery to every read.
- **Hash-chained log**: log in which each record commits to the previous one, so removal or edit breaks the chain, at the cost of verification work and careful handling of the chain's own anchor.
- **Write-once storage**, also **WORM storage**: medium or policy permitting a single write per unit, which guarantees retention and makes deletion for privacy or error correction hard.
- **Log forwarding**: shipment of records off the machine that produced them, which preserves evidence when that host is compromised and requires reliable transport and capacity to keep.
- **Log normalization**: conversion of records from many producer formats into common fields and schemas, through log parsing into named fields, log conversion into the analysis tier's format, event filtering, and event aggregation, which enables shared detections and loses detail the source format carried.
- **Log retention**: period for which records stay available, managed through log rotation, log archival, log compression, and log reduction, with log preservation beyond it for a hold, set against investigation need, storage cost, privacy obligation, and the records a filter or sampler discards forever.
- **Log management**: the operational program around records, setting a log generation policy and log source prioritization, running the collection and storage infrastructure, and adding log file integrity checking, log clearing detection, log viewing, and log reporting, at the cost of capacity planning and a tier that must itself stay available.
- **Clock synchronization for correlation**: keeping system clocks aligned and times normalized to one reference so event ordering across hosts is trustworthy, dependent on an authenticated time source and correct zone information.
- **Correlation rule**: logic raising a finding only when several distinct events occur inside a chosen window, which cuts noise and fails silently when one contributing source stops or the window is set wrong.
- **Security information and event management**, also **SIEM**: central platform collecting, normalizing, searching, and alerting on security records, at substantial licensing, tuning, and engineering cost.

### Alerting, operations and threat intelligence

- **Alert triage**: rapid assessment of an alert to decide whether it is real and how urgent it is, which is the main consumer of analyst hours and the work that severity criteria and enrichment exist to shorten.
- **Alert fatigue**: erosion of analyst attention and judgment caused by a sustained volume of alerts that mostly turn out to be nothing.
- **False positive**: alert asserting activity that did not happen or was benign, the counterpart of the false negative where hostile activity ran and the monitoring meant to catch it stayed silent.
- **Mean time to detect**, also **MTTD**: average interval between the start of hostile activity and a defender noticing it, reported beside mean time to respond and mean time to contain.
- **Security operations center**, also **SOC**: standing function that monitors, triages, and responds to security events, whether tiered by depth of work, rostered on call, or staffed across time zones, carrying continuous staffing cost and a handoff at every boundary.
- **Security orchestration, automation and response**, also **SOAR**: platform executing multi-step response workflows across tools, which cuts repetitive work and demands continuous integration upkeep.
- **Containment automation**: response action taken without human approval, such as cutting a machine off the network, which is fast and can disable a critical system on a false positive.
- **Threat hunting**: deliberate search through telemetry for activity no alert fired on, driven by a stated hypothesis, by reporting on an actor, or by the assets whose loss would matter most, which finds what detections miss and consumes senior analyst time.
- **Threat intelligence**: assessed knowledge about adversaries and their methods produced to inform decisions, written strategically for leaders, operationally for defense planners, or tactically for detection, and valuable only while current.
- **Threat information exchange standards**: the agreed carriers of shared threat data, being a structured model for threats and indicators, a protocol for serving and pulling them, and markings stating how widely each piece may be passed, at the cost of modeling effort and reliance on recipient discipline.
- **Attribution**: assignment of observed activity to a responsible actor, reached by clustering intrusions that share methods, tooling, or infrastructure and naming the cluster, which risks merging distinct actors, confuses everyone when vendors name the same one differently, and is misled by a false flag planting evidence that points at someone else.

### Deception

- **Deception technology**: defensive practice of planting false assets and data that only an intruder would touch, as a decoy object, a decoy network resource, a whole decoy environment, or a decoy public release that misleads reconnaissance, which yields alerts with almost no false positives and needs careful placement to stay believable.
- **Honeypot**: system placed to be attacked and watched with no production purpose, emulated cheaply and recognizably or run as a real host for deep insight, at a cost in build effort and containment if it is turned into a launch point.
- **Honeynet**: network of decoy systems with monitored connectivity, run as a standalone honeynet, a connected honeynet wired to production for plausibility, or an integrated honeynet among real neighbors, at significant construction cost and the risk that an intruder pivots out of it.
- **Honeytoken**: piece of data planted so its use anywhere reveals that someone took it, cheap to seed and worthless unless the alerting path is watched.
- **Canary token**: honeytoken that reports back the moment it is opened, resolved, or authenticated with, giving fast notice and revealing the defender's monitoring to an alert adversary.
- **Decoy credential**, also **decoy session token**: username, secret, or session artifact that grants nothing and alarms on any use, cheap to plant and useless once an adversary can spot the difference.
- **Decoy file**, also **honeyfile**: document with inviting content and no real value, whose access indicates collection activity, requiring realistic content to attract interest.
- **Decoy account**, also **decoy persona**: identity created to be discovered and targeted, so every authentication attempt against it is hostile, requiring enough history and contact to look genuine.
- **Tarpit**: service that responds so slowly that automated scanning and spreading stall on it, which wastes adversary time and holds defender resources too.
- **Breadcrumb**: planted trace such as a saved connection or a history entry that leads an intruder toward a decoy rather than a real asset, at the cost of confusing legitimate users and tools.

### Incident response

- **Incident**: occurrence that has harmed or threatens the confidentiality, integrity, or availability of systems or data, distinguished from the wider set of adverse events, precursors, and indications by the judgment that real harm needs a coordinated response.
- **Incident response lifecycle**: the standard sequence in which response work is organized, running from preparation through detection and analysis, containment, eradication, and recovery, to review of what happened.
- **Preparation**: the phase of readiness work done before anything happens, covering policy and authority, plans, tooling, access, retainers, and training.
- **Detection and analysis**: the phase of establishing that something is happening and determining what it is, which sets the direction of all later action.
- **Scoping**: determination of exactly which accounts, hosts, and data are affected, the prerequisite to effective containment and the finding that most often expands.
- **Containment**: the phase that stops an intrusion from spreading or progressing, first by crude immediate measures and then by sustainable ones that hold while normal operation resumes.
- **Host isolation**: cutting of a machine's network access while leaving it running and reachable to responders, which stops spread and interrupts whatever that machine served.
- **Credential containment**: revocation, reset, or forced re-authentication of compromised identities and their cached tokens, which cuts adversary access and locks out real users and automation during the change.
- **Eradication**: the phase of removing the adversary's tooling, footholds, and unauthorized changes from the environment, whose completeness depends entirely on the accuracy of scoping.
- **Eviction and restoration actions**: the concrete steps of cleanup, covering process suspension and termination, session termination, account locking, credential revocation, authentication cache invalidation, object eviction, file eviction, configuration key deletion, email removal, resolution cache eviction, host reboot, host shutdown, disk erasure, disk reformatting, and disk repartitioning, each trading evidence for certainty before restoration begins.
- **Object restoration**: return of data and artifacts to a known-good state from a trusted copy, covering file restoration, configuration restoration, database restoration, mailbox restoration, software restoration, and disk image restoration, with access restoration, account unlocking, and credential reissue for identities that response disabled, at the cost of losing changes made since the copy.
- **Recovery**: the phase of returning systems and services to trustworthy operation, from tactical restoration of minimum service to a strategic rebuild, with verification under heightened monitoring before normal scrutiny resumes.
- **Post-incident review**: the phase of establishing what happened and what must change, conducted on the premise that people acted reasonably given what they knew, and worth nothing unless its conclusions get owners and funding.
- **Incident prioritization**: ordering of open incidents by functional impact, information impact, and recoverability effort so scarce responders work the worst first, at the cost of judgment calls made under incomplete information.
- **Playbook**, also **runbook**: prepared guidance for a class of incident or a specific task, covering what to assess and who decides, which speeds response and goes stale against the systems it describes.
- **Incident commander**: single named person holding decision authority and coordination for a response, supported by a scribe recording actions and times, which prevents paralysis at the cost of two trained people doing no technical work.
- **Communications plan**: prearranged channels, templates, and approvals for internal, customer, and regulatory notification during an incident, which prevents improvised messaging and needs an out-of-band path for when the systems themselves are untrusted.
- **Tabletop exercise**, also **functional exercise**: rehearsal of a scenario, either as a discussion with the real decision makers or as an operation of real tooling against a simulated incident, cheap in the first form and heavy in preparation for the second.

### Forensics and malware analysis

- **Digital forensics**: discipline of recovering and interpreting data from systems so findings about past events withstand scrutiny, at a cost in specialist time, rigor, and prior arrangement of the logging that must already exist.
- **Forensic acquisition**: obtaining of a defensible copy of data, whether a sector-level disk image behind a write blocker or a targeted triage collection from a running host, ordered by volatility and verified by digests, which preserves the original and takes the system out of use.
- **Chain of custody**: documented record of who held evidence, when, and why, which sustains admissibility and imposes strict handling discipline on every responder who touches it.
- **Memory forensics**: examination of captured physical memory for processes, network state, injected code, and secrets that exist nowhere on disk, drawn also from page, hibernation, and crash dump files, requiring version-specific structure knowledge.
- **Timeline analysis**: ordering of dated artifacts into one sequence to reconstruct events, comprehensive in the super timeline form that consumes every timestamp source, and defeated by unreliable clocks or aggressive filtering.
- **Execution artifact**: any trace left by a program having run, covering prefetch files, compatibility and experience caches, user assistance counters, background activity records, resource usage databases, jump lists, and shell bags.
- **Windows file system and registry artifact**: evidence produced by the platform's own bookkeeping, covering master file table and journal entries, volume shadow copies, zone identifier streams, recycle bin records, account and configuration hives, transaction logs, and removable device histories.
- **Unix and macOS artifact**: evidence on Unix-like systems, covering filesystem event and unified logs, quarantine and knowledge databases, Spotlight metadata, property lists, audit daemon records, journals, login accounting, privilege elevation logs, and known hosts files.
- **Cloud audit artifact**: provider-side evidence that exists nowhere on a host, covering the control plane audit log, data plane access log, sign-in log, unified audit log, consent grant record, mailbox audit record, key usage log, function invocation log, storage access log, instance metadata access record, and a cloud snapshot as evidence.
- **Anti-forensics**: deliberate action taken to prevent, mislead, or invalidate later examination, covering log wiping, secure deletion, forged timestamps, hidden file systems, and encryption of tooling and collected data.
- **Malware analysis**: examination of hostile code to establish what it does, what it talks to, and how to detect it, statically with entropy analysis and header anomaly analysis, dynamically under instruction tracing, interface call monitoring, and inline hooking for instrumentation, or by emulation and symbolic reasoning, with import and fuzzy hashing grouping samples into lineages, requiring scarce specialist skill.
- **Reverse engineering**: reconstruction of a program's logic from its compiled form through manual unpacking, original entry point recovery, import table reconstruction, process memory dumping for analysis, disassembly, decompilation, control flow graph recovery, library function identification, binary diffing, and configuration decryption and structure recovery, at very high time cost.

### Vulnerability and exposure management

- **Vulnerability management**: continuing process of finding, judging, and fixing weaknesses across an estate, resting on an asset inventory with named owners, at a permanent cost in staffing and change work.
- **Vulnerability scanning**: automated probing of systems for known weaknesses, performed unauthenticated, with credentials for accuracy, or by a resident agent, which gives broad coverage cheaply and produces noise and load on the targets.
- **External attack surface management**: continuous discovery of an organization's internet-reachable assets from the outside, which finds forgotten exposure and misattributes assets that are not yours.
- **Exposure management**, also **continuous threat exposure management**: judging weaknesses by whether they are actually reachable and exploitable in context, cycling through scoping, discovery, prioritization, validation, and mobilization, which sharpens priority and demands environment knowledge scanners lack.
- **Risk-based prioritization**: ordering of fixes by exploitability and business consequence rather than raw severity, whether by a qualitative risk rating method banding likelihood against impact or by exploitation data, which directs limited effort well and depends on data that is often absent.
- **Patch management**: process of obtaining, testing, and deploying vendor fixes across an estate, inside scheduled windows or outside them when waiting is worse, at a steady cost in testing, downtime, and unplanned work forced by out-of-band releases.
- **Virtual patching**: blocking of exploitation at a gateway or runtime layer while the flawed component stays unchanged, which buys time and leaves the flaw present to be found again.
- **Exception and risk acceptance**: recorded decision to leave a known flaw unfixed with named accountability and an expiry, which permits business reality and becomes permanent when nobody revisits it.
- **Remediation verification**: rescanning or retesting to confirm a fix is actually in place, which catches failed, partial, and regressed deployment and adds a second pass to every cycle.
- **Security assessment**: structured judgment of a target's posture by examination techniques such as documentation review, ruleset review, system configuration review, and log review as assessment, and by testing techniques such as network discovery as assessment, port and service identification, and target vulnerability validation, at the cost of an assessment policy and plan agreed first.
- **Penetration test**: authorized attempt to compromise a defined target to demonstrate exploitability, run with no information, partial information, or full source and credentials, through planning, discovery, attack, and reporting phases, which proves risk unlike a vulnerability assessment that only identifies known flaws, inside a scope the adversary would not respect and only as far as testers reached in the time allowed.
- **Red team engagement**: goal-oriented exercise in which an authorized team pursues an objective against a defending organization, optionally emulating a named actor's techniques, starting from an assumed breach, or working openly with defenders, which tests detection and response and costs weeks of skilled effort.
- **Bug bounty**: standing offer of payment for externally reported flaws under a published disclosure policy and safe harbor promise, which buys many eyes at variable cost and generates a triage workload of duplicates and out-of-scope reports.

### Vulnerability identifiers, scoring and disclosure
