- **Vulnerability identifier**, also **CVE identifier**: unique public label assigned to a specific reported flaw by a numbering authority so everyone refers to the same thing, held in a vulnerability record that identifier reservation holds back before publication and that may become a disputed record, a rejected record, or the later enrichment of a record.
- **Weakness identifier**, also **CWE identifier**: label for a class of coding or design mistake, describing the kind of flaw rather than one occurrence, and ranked periodically as a weakness top list.
- **Vulnerability scoring**: standard scheme producing a numeric severity from an exploitability metric group of attack vector, attack complexity, attack requirements, privileges required, and user interaction, an impact metric group covering scope and vulnerable and subsequent system impact, recorded in a scoring vector string and adjustable for exploit maturity, remediation level, report confidence, and one deployment's security requirements.
- **Supplemental metric group**: the scoring additions that describe context without changing the number, being an automatable metric, a recovery metric, a value density metric, a vulnerability response effort metric, a provider urgency metric, and a safety metric, beside a modified base metric restating a base value for one deployment and a severity rating scale naming the resulting band.
- **Exploit prediction scoring**, also **EPSS**: model estimating the probability that a flaw will be exploited in the near term, which focuses remediation and says nothing about impact.
- **Known exploited vulnerability catalog**: published list of flaws confirmed to be exploited against real targets, a strong prioritization signal with deliberately narrow coverage.
- **Stakeholder-specific vulnerability categorization**, also **SSVC**: decision-tree method producing an action rather than a number for each flaw, which encodes local judgment and requires somebody to define the trees.
- **Common weakness scoring system**, also **CWSS**: scoring method for weaknesses in code rather than for vulnerable products, weighted for business context by the common weakness risk analysis framework.
- **Common attack pattern enumeration and classification**: published catalog of attack patterns organized by mechanism and by domain.
- **Common platform enumeration**, also **CPE**: structured naming scheme for products and versions so advisories can be matched against an inventory.
- **Silent patch**: fix shipped without an advisory or identifier, leaving deployers unable to judge urgency and inviting comparison of patched and unpatched binaries to find the flaw.

### Dependencies and supply chain attacks

- **Software supply chain**: the full set of people, code, tools, and processes that contribute to a released software artifact, and the upstream maintainers who decide what a downstream consumer ships.
- **Supply chain attack**: intrusion that reaches a target by compromising something the target trusts and installs rather than attacking the target directly.
- **Transitive dependency**, also **indirect dependency**: component pulled in by a project's dependencies rather than declared by the project, one of several kinds alongside direct, development, optional, peer, build-time, and runtime dependencies.
- **Dependency graph**: the directed structure of every component a build resolves, whose depth, resolution rules, and version conflicts produce the diamond problem, dependency hell, and growth beyond what the project actually uses.
- **Phantom dependency**, also **undeclared dependency**: code a project imports without declaring, so inventory, pinning, and scanning never see it.
- **Single-maintainer risk**, also **single upstream maintainer risk**: exposure created when one unpaid account can publish a widely used component, compounded by low bus factor, open source stewardship risk, deprecation, an end-of-life dependency whose defects stay unrepaired, and concentration risk in a dependency that many products share.
- **Dependency confusion**, also **substitution attack**: publication of a public package under an internal name so a resolver prefers the attacker's copy, aided by namespace shadowing and implausibly high version numbers a resolver treats as newest.
- **Typosquatting**: registration of a package name that is a plausible misspelling of a popular one, alongside combosquatting, homoglyph names, brandjacking, starjacking, plain name squatting, and slopsquatting of names that language models hallucinate.
- **Dependency hijacking**: seizure of an already-trusted package rather than publication of a new one, through maintainer account takeover, lapsed maintainer domains, claimed abandoned repository paths, chained upstream accounts, and reuse of deleted namespaces.
- **Malicious package publication**: release of a component whose purpose is to run attacker code on machines that install it, whether trojanized behind advertised behavior, harvesting tokens and cloud metadata, spreading itself through stolen publishing credentials, or disrupting consumers as protest.
- **Install-script abuse**: attacker code placed in a package's lifecycle scripts or build steps, which run with the installing user's privileges before any import, test, or review touches the output.
- **Long-con contributor**: attacker who builds maintainer trust over months of legitimate contribution before introducing a backdoor, or who simply persuades an overworked maintainer to hand over publish rights.
- **Source-artifact divergence**: published release archive containing code absent from the tagged source it claims to build from, including registry metadata that disagrees with the archive it describes.
- **Registry cache poisoning**, also **malicious mirror**: attacker-controlled replica or proxy cache that serves altered artifacts under legitimate names, or withholds updates from clients that trust it.

### Component inventory and exploitability

- **Software bill of materials**, also **SBOM**: machine-readable record of the components in a release and how they relate, carrying a document namespace, supplier, name, version, identifiers, relationships, author, and timestamp, costing generation, distribution, bill of materials assembly from supplier documents, bill of materials merge, and format translation per build.
- **Build SBOM**: inventory produced by the build itself, recording what was actually resolved and linked, as against design, source, analyzed, deployed, and runtime inventories taken at other moments and differing in depth, completeness, and drift from the artifact.
- **SPDX**: standardized format for expressing component inventory, licensing by short license identifier, and element relationships, alongside CycloneDX for delivery pipelines and software identification tags installed with a product, at the cost of large specification surfaces and format-specific tooling.
- **Package URL**, also **purl**: naming scheme identifying a package by ecosystem, namespace, name, and version in one portable string, which exists because mismatched naming schemes are why components fail to match vulnerability records.
- **Specialized bills of materials**: inventories of things other than software components, being a cryptographic bill of materials of algorithms and keys, a service bill of materials of hosted services and data flows, a hardware bill of materials, a manufacturing bill of materials, an operations bill of materials, and an artificial intelligence bill of materials of models, weights, and datasets.
- **Vulnerability exploitability exchange**, also **VEX**: machine-readable supplier statement about whether a known vulnerability actually affects a product, asserting a not affected status with an impact statement, an affected status with an action statement, a fixed status, or an under investigation status, at the cost of per-vulnerability analysis.
- **Not-affected justification**: the named reason a supplier gives for asserting a vulnerability does not apply, being that the subcomponent is absent, the defective code is absent, it is unreachable, an adversary cannot control its input, or a shipped control already blocks it.
- **CSAF**, also **Common Security Advisory Framework**: machine-readable advisory format carrying product trees with product identification helpers, remediation categories, threat categories, and an exploitability exchange profile between suppliers and consumers, joined by standalone exploitability statements, an open vulnerability schema keyed to package ecosystems, and advisory feeds, at the cost of ingestion tooling per feed.
- **Reachability analysis**: determination of whether a vulnerable function in a dependency can be called from the product's own code, which cuts findings that do not matter at the cost of analysis accuracy and compute.
- **Software composition analysis**, also **SCA**: identification of the third-party components a project includes and matching them against known vulnerability and license data, at the cost of noisy matches and inventory that must stay accurate.

### Dependency hygiene and registry controls

- **Lockfile**: generated file recording the exact resolved version and digest of every component so installs repeat, at the cost of merge conflicts and a graph that goes stale until somebody regenerates it.
- **Version pinning**: requirement of one exact version, and in the full graph form of every transitive component too, rather than a range or a movable tag whose contents can change under a fixed reference, which stops unreviewed publishes at the cost of deliberate patching work.
- **Digest pinning**, also **integrity field**: reference to a component by the hash of its contents rather than by a mutable name, with artifact digest verification against a trusted expected value before use, so an altered artifact fails installation, at the cost of readability and churn on every legitimate update.
- **Vendoring**: copying of dependency source into the project's own repository, or mirroring upstream sources locally, so builds fetch nothing and an upstream deletion cannot break them, at the cost of manual upgrades and storage.
- **Private registry**: internally controlled package repository, commonly fronted by a pull-through cache for public packages, which creates one inspection and allowlisting point at the cost of hosting, backup, and access administration.
- **Package quarantine**: holding of a newly fetched component unusable until it is reviewed or until a cooldown interval has passed, so malicious releases are caught upstream first, at the cost of delaying fixes along with everything else.
- **Namespace reservation**: registration of an organization's internal package names publicly, or publishing under an owner-qualified scope, so nobody else can claim them, at the cost of clutter, renewal, and longer identifiers.
- **Install-script disabling**: configuration of the package manager to skip package-supplied lifecycle scripts, which closes the commonest execution path into developer machines and breaks components that genuinely need them.
- **Dependency review**: human inspection of what a proposed change adds to the graph, whether raised by hand or by an update bot, which catches hostile additions and loses its value to update fatigue once the volume of proposals exceeds attention.
- **Package immutability**: registry rule forbidding republication of an already-published version, with yanking as the only withdrawal, which prevents silent swaps and leaves a bad release needing a new number.
- **Publishing two-factor enforcement**: requirement of a second authentication factor, or a narrowly scoped automation token, before a release can be pushed, which blocks stolen-password publishing at the cost of maintainer friction and token administration.

### Provenance, attestation and signing

- **Provenance**: verifiable record of where an artifact came from, who produced it, and from which inputs, bound to one output file by digest and complete only when it names every input and parameter that influenced that output.
- **Build provenance**: record of the build that produced an artifact, naming the builder identity, the declared build type, the source revision in a source-to-build binding, the external and internal parameters, the resolved dependency digests, and the run details, distributed with the artifact so its authenticity can be checked.
- **Attestation**: signed machine-readable claim about an artifact, issued so a consumer can check it instead of trusting a promise, at the cost of signing infrastructure and the key custody it implies.
- **Artifact attestation statement**: the signed body binding one or more subjects, each named by digest, to one typed predicate, carried in an envelope that commits to the payload type so it cannot be reinterpreted as another kind of claim.
- **In-toto layout**: signed specification of a supply chain naming its steps with their expected commands, expected materials, and expected products, the functionary keys authorized for each, the signer threshold enforced by threshold verification, the artifact rules that match, create, delete, allow, and require files, and the verifier's own inspection commands, at the cost of key distribution and expiry re-signing.
- **Link metadata**: signed record a functionary emits after performing a step, listing the materials it consumed, the products it produced, its byproducts, environment record, and return value record, checked by link authentication against the keys the layout authorized for that step.
- **Verification policy**: rules a consumer applies to decide whether an artifact's attestations suffice, stated as consumer expectations and verifier expectations such as builder identity and source repository, applied by the verifier role to what the producer role emitted, at the cost of policy authorship and exception handling.
- **Verification summary attestation**, also **VSA**: signed statement that a verifier already checked an artifact against a policy, so downstream parties need not repeat the work, at the cost of having to trust that verifier's judgment.
- **Supply chain levels for software artifacts**, also **SLSA**: graded framework for verifiable integrity whose build track rises through build level 1 with generated provenance, build level 2 with provenance signed by a hosted build platform, and build level 3 with tenant isolation keeping build code away from the signing material, adopted at rising engineering cost.
- **SLSA threat model**: classification of supply chain threats by where they enter, as a source threat putting unauthorized code into the repository, a build threat making the build produce something the source does not specify, a distribution threat swapping the artifact in transit, and a usage threat installing a real artifact nobody intended.
- **Source track**: the graded requirements on how revisions are authenticated, reviewed, and retained, covering a source control platform's source provenance attestation, two-person review, retained history, an immutable source reference, branch continuity from a stated revision, and a continuously enforced control proved across a range of revisions, at the cost of platform support for each proof.
- **Code signing**: marking of executable code with a verifiable producer identity so recipients can refuse code from anyone else, carried inside the artifact's format or detached beside it as a signature manifest, at the cost of key custody and revocation handling.
- **Keyless signing**: signing with a short-lived credential issued against a verified human or workload identity instead of a stored key, which removes key theft as a concern at the cost of depending on an identity provider, a timestamped record, and identity resolution at verification time.
- **Release signing key custody**: the rules for who holds release keys and how they are used, covering rotation, offline ceremonies with witnesses, threshold signing by several holders, and countersigned timestamps, at the cost of ceremony, coordination, and a compromise recovery that is slow and rarely complete.
- **Transparency log**: publicly readable append-only record of published artifacts and signatures, proved by inclusion and consistency proofs and summarized in signed checkpoints that monitors read and witnesses countersign so a split view served to different clients is caught, at the cost of operating and watching the log.
- **Trusted publisher**: registry configuration accepting releases only from a named build workflow's identity, paired with a publication attestation linking the version to its build and with signature checks at admission, which removes standing publish tokens at the cost of tying releases to one platform.

### Build integrity and reproducibility

- **Hermetic build**: build declaring all inputs in advance and fetching nothing while it runs, in a sealed build environment with egress blocked, an isolated build environment sharing no writable state, and an ephemeral build environment destroyed afterward, as against a non-hermetic build, at the cost of local mirrors, cold caches, and build input allowlisting maintained up front.
- **Pinned toolchain**: fixing of compiler, linker, and tool versions by digest on a reviewed golden build image whose own toolchain provenance is recorded in a build environment attestation, so the programs a build trusts cannot change silently, at the cost of manual upgrades and image maintenance.
- **Build system compromise**: attacker control of the machinery that compiles and packages releases, so honest source yields malicious output, reached by build script injection, build parameter injection, build cache poisoning of a shared cache, misplaced remote execution trust, artifact substitution after the build, and artifact tampering in transit that improper artifact integrity validation never catches.
- **Reproducible build**: build any party can repeat from the same source and inputs for a byte-identical result, normalized against clock, path, locale, and archive metadata and checked by independent rebuilders whose agreement raises confidence in a published binary, at the cost of removing every source of variation.
- **Build nondeterminism**: any behavior making repeated builds of identical source differ, arising from embedded timestamps and build identifiers, absolute and temporary paths, locale and character encoding dependence, filesystem and link order dependence, parallelism, random seeds, hash iteration order, symbol table ordering, archive member metadata, uninitialized memory in output, toolchain version recording, and network access during build.
- **Reference build timestamp**, also **source date epoch**: declared build time every tool honors in place of the real clock, applied with build path prefix mapping, deterministic archive mode, archive metadata normalization, build identifier stripping, and a published build information file recording the conditions a rebuilder must reproduce, at the cost of tool support that must exist end to end.
- **Trusting trust attack**: attack in which a compiler inserts a backdoor into the programs it compiles, and recognizes its own source so it reinjects itself when rebuilt, leaving the visible source of both compiler and product clean.
- **Diverse double-compiling**: rebuilding of a compiler with a second independent compiler and comparison of the results to expose a self-reproducing backdoor, supported by bootstrappable chains that start from one tiny auditable seed binary, at the cost of obtaining a genuinely independent toolchain.

### Update security

- **Secure update channel**: delivery path that authenticates the party entitled to issue an update and checks the download against a signed expected hash and length before installing, at the cost of provisioning verification keys on every client and keeping that expectation fresh.
- **The update framework**, also **TUF**: design for update systems that survives repository and key compromise by splitting authority across the root role, targets role, snapshot role, and timestamp role, with delegations, thresholds, offline keys, metadata expiration, consistent snapshots, and key compromise resilience, at the cost of substantial metadata machinery.
- **Delegated targets**: handing of authority over a subset of target files from a targets role to another key holder, expressed as path pattern delegation, terminating delegation, hashed bin delegation, succinct delegation, or multi-role delegation, at the cost of a deeper trust graph and delegation metadata to generate.
- **Root key rotation**: publication of new root metadata signed by the old keys so clients move their trust anchor forward without reinstalling, resting on trusted root bootstrapping at install, metadata threshold signing, an offline role key for rare use and an online role key for frequent signing, and monotonic metadata version numbers, at the cost of scheduled re-signing ceremonies.
- **Uptane**: vehicle update framework splitting trust between an image repository holding available images and a director repository naming which images each unit installs, with a primary control unit distributing to secondary control units that perform full verification or partial verification and report a vehicle version manifest, at the cost of two repositories and an update freshness service.
- **Rollback attack**: service of an older version and its metadata so a client installs a downgrade believing it is current, including update replay of a previously valid signed package and downgrade to a vulnerable version whose defect the attacker already knows.
- **Mix-and-match attack**: service of a combination of metadata and files that never coexisted on the repository, beside the freeze attack, the fast-forward attack, the endless data attack, the slow retrieval attack, the wrong software installation attack, the extraneous dependencies attack, the arbitrary software attack, and the mirror withholding attack.
- **A-B partition update**, also **dual-bank update**: writing of a new image to an inactive partition or flash bank and switching at next boot, backed by a recovery image and a monotonic anti-rollback counter, which survives a failed write at the cost of doubled storage and no retreat to an earlier release.
- **Staged rollout**: release to a growing fraction of a fleet, as canaries and then scheduled waves by region, device class, or ring, with a lever to halt distribution in flight, which bounds the reach of a bad build at the cost of prolonged version spread.

### Pipeline hazards and delivery controls

- **Poisoned pipeline execution**, also **PPE**: getting a build system to run attacker-chosen instructions and then using the job's credentials and outputs, by editing the pipeline definition itself, a script the pipeline invokes, workflow trigger abuse producing the event it fires on, or public poisoned pipeline execution through a contribution anyone may open against a public repository.
- **Insufficient flow control**: pipeline condition in which a change reaches production without passing any control that could stop it, one of the recognized pipeline weakness classes with inadequate pipeline identity management, insecure pipeline system configuration, ungoverned third-party service usage, and insufficient pipeline logging and visibility.
- **Workflow injection**: attacker text reaching a place where a pipeline treats it as code, such as a branch name, issue title, or other untrusted value interpolated straight into a command an interpreter then parses.
- **Pull-request-target hazard**: trigger that runs a workflow with repository secrets in the base branch context while checking out an untrusted contributor's code, the fork workflow secret exposure that is the reason automation triggered by outside contributions must never hold the project's secrets.
- **Self-hosted runner abuse**: use of a build machine the project operates to reach its internal network, its filesystem, or the tools, caches, and processes a previous job left behind, including runner label hijacking that claims a label a privileged job selects.
- **Pipeline cache poisoning**: writing of malicious content into a shared build cache from a low-privilege job so a later, more privileged job restores it as trusted input, the same move as cross-workflow artifact poisoning through a published artifact.
- **Mutable action reference**: naming of a reusable build step by an identifier its owner can repoint, so what executes changes with no change in the calling project, compounded by composite action risk where that step calls further steps nobody reviewed and by reusable workflow trust granting a shared definition the caller's privileges.
- **Pipeline privilege escalation**: use of a build job's own permissions to obtain wider ones, through a default token permission broader than any job needed, deploy keys used outside their purpose, pipeline bot account abuse of standing rights, federated trust policy misconfiguration, and commit status spoofing that satisfies a required check.
- **Secret in source history**: credential committed once and preserved in the repository's recorded history, reachable from every clone whatever the current files say, with build logs, image layers, and caches keeping copies of their own.
- **Action pinning**: reference to a build step by an immutable commit identifier rather than a movable tag, which freezes exactly what runs at the cost of manual updates whenever the step is improved.
- **Ephemeral runner**: build machine created for one job and destroyed afterward, brought up by just-in-time runner registration with a single-use token so a compromised runner cannot rejoin, with runner autoscaling isolation keeping each scaled instance fresh, which removes carried-over state at the cost of startup latency and repeated dependency downloads.
- **Least-privilege pipeline token**: grant of only the permissions a job needs, scoped as a branch-scoped secret or to a named environment and issued short-lived or federated from the platform's workload identity, the pipeline-based access control that credential hygiene completes, at the cost of per-job credential design.
- **Secret scanning**: searching of code, history, logs, and artifacts for credential patterns, refused locally at commit or centrally at push, at the cost of false positives, documented bypass paths, and blindness to unrecognized formats, with revocation the only dependable remedy once a secret is out.
- **Branch protection**: server-enforced rules on who may write to a branch and under what conditions, imposing a required review by someone other than the author, a required status check, a signed commit the platform reports as a verified commit, and a protected tag, which keeps unreviewed change out at the cost of slower emergency fixes.
- **Artifact promotion**: movement of one already-built artifact between environments by immutable artifact digest instead of rebuilding, gated by environment gating, an environment protection rule, and a manual approval gate, with separation of build and deploy privileges, an immutable release, a deployment audit trail, and rollback capability, at the cost of registry plumbing and handoffs.
- **Drift detection**: comparison of running infrastructure against its declared definition to surface unmanaged change, paired with infrastructure as code review that puts configuration definitions through the same approval as application code, at the cost of noise from legitimate out-of-band operations.

### Secure development and supplier governance

- **Secure development lifecycle**, also **SDL**: development process with security activities at every phase, covering security requirements and security user stories, a bug bar, banned functions, secure coding standards, a threat modeling gate and design review gate, a security gate in a pipeline, security champions, security training, a definition of done including security, and documented gate exceptions, at the cost of process weight and schedule.
- **Secure software development framework**, also **SSDF**: published set of secure development practices in four practice groups, prepare the organization, protect the software, produce well-secured software, and respond to vulnerabilities, whose named tasks cover development roles, a supporting toolchain, secure development environments, code access protection, release integrity verification, secure coding and build configuration, and vulnerability root cause analysis, at the cost of tailoring each practice.
- **Application security testing**: the automated checks wired into development, covering static application security testing, dynamic application security testing of a running application, interactive application security testing from an instrumented runtime, infrastructure as code scanning, container image scanning, and admission control policy and policy as code gates, at the cost of a scanner's false positive burden teaching teams to disregard the output.
- **Distroless image**: container image carrying an application and its runtime with no package manager or shell, the end of a line that runs from base image provenance through a minimal base image and pins the result by image digest reference, at the cost of difficult inspection and absent debugging tools.
- **Secure supply chain consumption framework**, also **S2C2F**: graded framework for taking in external open source through one enforced path, covering ingestion, inventory, updating, auditing, scanning, rebuilding from source, and fixing upstream, judged alongside repository practice scorecards and ecosystem criticality scores, at the cost of a governance program to run it.
- **Repository practice scorecard**: automated rating of a project's security-relevant repository practices, checking for committed binary artifacts, dangerous workflows, unpinned dependencies, unsigned releases, absent fuzzing, broad token permissions, unauthenticated webhooks, and whether the project is maintained, read beside its security policy file and project security insights file, at the cost of proxy measures that reward configuration over care.
- **Cybersecurity supply chain risk management**, also **C-SCRM**: discipline of identifying and controlling supplier-originated risk across a system's life, covering a supply chain risk management plan, supplier tiering, a supply chain map, critical dependency identification, flow-down requirements, a notification of compromise clause, supplier continuous monitoring, right to audit, fourth-party risk, provenance traceability, and an exit plan for a dependency, at the cost of standing program funding.
- **Third-party risk assessment**: evaluation of the security a supplier brings to the systems depending on it, run as a vendor security review with a security questionnaire and supplier attestation, backed by contractual security requirements, a right to audit, and source code escrow against supplier failure, at the cost of evidence the reviewer cannot confirm.
- **Acquirer**: the party that buys or obtains a product or service and holds the risk its supplier creates, facing the supplier itself, the system integrator that assembles components from several suppliers, the external service provider that operates a service rather than delivering software, and the sub-tier supplier reached through no direct relationship.
- **Counterfeit component**: part or package presented as a genuine supplier's product when it is not, distinguished from the tainted component altered somewhere in the delivery path and the grey market component obtained outside the supplier's authorized channel with no assured handling history, each caught only by component authenticity verification and provenance traceability.

## Failing safely

### The vocabulary of fault, error and failure

- **Dependability**: the ability to deliver service that justifiably earns the reliance placed on it, organized by the dependability tree into the attributes wanted, the threats against them, and the means of attaining them.
- **Correct service**: delivered behavior that agrees with the functional specification within its stated tolerance, judged at the service interface where users consume it, incorrect service being any departure whether or not a user notices.
- **System boundary**: the frontier an analyst draws between the system under study and its environment, parts left unexamined inside it being atomic components and the whole condition at an instant being the total state.
- **Fault**: the cause of a deviation, whether a physical defect, a mistake in construction, or a hostile input, dormant while it produces nothing and active once it is producing wrong state.
- **Error**: a part of the state differing from what a correct execution would have produced, latent until a check signals it, effective once ongoing computation uses it, detected once its presence is announced.
- **Service failure**, also **failure**: the event where delivered behavior first departs from the specification, graded by severity from minor and benign, worth about the service lost, to catastrophic and malign, worth incomparably more.
- **Fault-error-failure chain**, also **fault pathology**: the causal sequence in which an input activates a dormant cause, the cause produces wrong state, and that wrong state reaches the boundary as a departure from specification.
- **Error propagation**: spread of wrongness through use of a bad value, internal while it stays between components inside the boundary and external once it crosses the point of delivery to a consumer.
- **Fault hypothesis**: the stated assumption about which faults, and how many at once, a design must survive, including the single-fault and independent-fault assumptions, with assumption coverage the probability that reality stays inside it.
- **Steady-state availability**, also **instantaneous availability**: readiness to deliver correct service as a fraction of time, steady-state or instantaneous, graded in nines, set by mean time between failures against mean time to repair, and complemented by unavailability, uptime and downtime.
- **Reliability**: continuity of correct service, the probability of running failure-free through a stated interval or mission time, described by the reliability function, failure rate, mean time to failure, hazard rate, and the bathtub curve.
- **Safety**: absence of catastrophic consequences for users and the environment whatever the system does or fails to do, standing beside integrity, maintainability, resilience, robustness under unanticipated input, and survivability despite attack or partial destruction.
- **Means of dependability**: the four families of action by which trustworthy service is pursued, fault prevention keeping causes from being introduced, fault tolerance carrying service through causes already present, fault removal eliminating them by verification and repair, and fault forecasting estimating how many remain, removal after a failure being corrective maintenance and removal before one preventive maintenance.
- **Failure mode and effects analysis**, also **FMEA**: enumerating how each part can break and tracing each break to its consequence, where fault tree analysis instead reasons backward from an undesired event to the combinations of causes producing it and event tree analysis reasons forward from an initiating event through each barrier, at the cost of laborious tabulation.
- **Reliability block diagram**: modeling a system as series and parallel paths so overall survival probability can be computed, where a Markov dependability model instead uses states and transition rates, common-cause analysis searches for shared conditions defeating supposedly independent parts, and single point of failure analysis traces dependencies whose loss stops everything, at the cost of assumed independence and state explosion.

### Classes of fault

- **Fault class**: a category of causes distinguished by a single viewpoint, the recognized axes being phase of creation, boundary, phenomenological cause, dimension, objective, intent, capability and persistence, most of whose crossings never occur.
- **Development fault**: a cause introduced while the system was specified, designed, built or deployed, present in every copy when it is a design fault, against an operational fault arising only after service begins.
- **Interaction fault**: a cause arriving at the boundary from input, configuration or operator action rather than from the system's own parts, external in origin where an internal fault sits inside the boundary.
- **Malicious fault**: a cause introduced deliberately to make a system behave wrongly or leak, treated formally as an interaction fault so that security and dependability share one vocabulary, or as malicious logic when placed during development.
- **Permanent fault**: a cause that stays present once it appears, against a transient one that vanishes on its own, an intermittent one tied to a real defect that shows only sometimes, and solid or elusive by reproducibility.
- **Physical fault**: a cause in the material behavior of hardware rather than in any instruction, covering production defects, deterioration, wear-out, signals stuck at one value, and soft errors where a particle strike flips a bit.
- **Fail-slow fault**, also **limping hardware**, **limpware**: a part that keeps returning correct answers far slower than its specification allows, producing limplock when a whole system waits on it, while a babbling idiot node instead floods a shared medium, fail-slow at scale being the observation that large deployments hold degraded parts more often than dead ones.
- **Heisenbug**: a defect that changes or vanishes when the program is observed or instrumented, beside the reproducible Bohrbug, the chaotic-looking Mandelbug, the aging-related bug needing accumulated runtime, and the Schrodinbug nobody noticed until reading the code.

### Modes a system can be built to fail in

- **Failure semantics**: the stated set of ways a component is permitted to fail, which bounds what its users must tolerate, from fail-controlled halting only, through crash-stop and crash-recovery assumptions, to fail-arbitrary components assumed capable of anything.
- **Fail-safe**: designed so a detected failure leaves the system in a state that cannot cause harm, at the cost of surrendering function to reach it.
- **Fail-secure**: designed so a failure leaves protections in force rather than relaxed, at the cost of denying legitimate use for as long as the failure lasts.
- **Fail-closed**: arranged so a check that cannot complete refuses the guarded action, at the cost of an outage whenever the checker itself breaks.
- **Fail-open**: arranged so a check that cannot complete lets the guarded action proceed, buying availability at the cost of a broken checker permitting everything.
- **Fail-stop**: designed so a component that cannot behave correctly halts and stays halted, the fail-stop processor being the abstract unit assumed never to produce a wrong result, at the cost of losing its capacity entirely.
- **Fail-fast**: designed to report trouble at the first detected sign rather than continuing, at the cost of aborting work that might have completed.
- **Fail-silent**: designed so a faulty component emits nothing rather than something wrong, at the cost of internal checking hardware or duplicated computation to know when to go quiet.
- **Fail-operational**: designed to keep delivering full function after a failure, where a fail-soft design instead sheds function and continues at reduced capability, at the cost of spare capacity able to replace any part.
- **Fail-passive**: designed so a failed unit stops driving its outputs and holds them inert while another unit or a human takes control, a fail-active unit instead driving on until a monitor intervenes, at the cost of that second controller.
- **Fail-deadly**: designed so loss of control triggers the guarded action rather than suppressing it, as a deadman switch fires when its holder lets go, accepting harm in order to guarantee the response happens.
- **Consistent failure**: a broken component presenting the same wrong behavior to every user, inconsistent or two-faced when different users see different behavior, signaled when announced as an error and unsignaled or silent when delivered as though correct.
- **Content failure**: delivered information is wrong while its arrival stays inside the time window, against timing failures early or late, omission failures never delivered, commission failures never asked for, and crash failures producing nothing further.

### Detection, checking and liveness

- **Error detection**: recognizing that state or output has gone wrong, concurrent when it runs during service delivery and preemptive when service is suspended for it, at the cost of checking work and of checks that are themselves wrong.
- **Acceptance test**: a check on a computed result deciding whether it is fit to use, by range, limit, sanity, plausibility or consistency with related state, at the cost of a criterion weaker than the specification it stands for.
- **Error-detecting code**: an encoding whose redundant bits make corrupted words illegal, as parity, checksums and cyclic redundancy checks do, their Hamming distance bounding what they catch, at the cost of extra bits and undetected collisions.
- **Error-correcting code**: an encoding with redundancy enough to rebuild a word after limited corruption, as single error correction double error detection and ECC memory do, scrubbed periodically, at the cost of more bits and decode latency.
- **Duplication and comparison**: running the same work twice and comparing outputs through a comparator, in lockstep when both units share clock and inputs, a self-checking pair either agreeing or declaring itself broken, at the cost of doubled work.
- **Self-checking component**: a part built to detect its own misbehavior and announce it rather than emit wrong output, at the cost of internal redundancy that produces no useful work.
- **Watchdog processor**: a small independent unit checking a main processor's behavior against expectations, by control flow checking or signature monitoring of executed instructions, at the cost of extra hardware and a model to check against.
- **Defensive programming**: writing code that validates its assumptions instead of trusting them, through executable assertions, invariants, preconditions and postconditions declared as contracts, at the cost of bulk, duplicated checks and real defects hidden behind them.
- **Watchdog timer**: a countdown that forces a reset unless software kicks it in time, windowed when the kick must be neither early nor late, in hardware or in software, at the cost of resets on slow but healthy work.
- **Health check**: a probe whose answer decides whether a component receives work, shallow when it confirms only that the process answers and deep when it exercises dependencies, at the cost of checks that pass while service is broken.
- **Liveness probe**: a repeated check whose failure restarts a component, where a readiness probe only withdraws it from traffic and a heartbeat or keepalive merely shows it alive, at the cost of restart loops when checks are strict.
- **Failure detector**: a component reporting which participants it believes down, complete when every dead one is eventually suspected and accurate when live ones are not, at the cost of false suspicion in an asynchronous network.
- **Timeout**: a limit on how long a wait may last before the waiter gives up, adaptive when recomputed from recent latency and absolute when stated as a deadline propagated downstream, at the cost of abandoning work about to succeed.

### Containment and blast radius

- **Fault containment region**, also **FCR**: a portion of a system built so a cause inside it cannot affect anything outside, its containment unit the smallest grouping guaranteed to break as a whole rather than partially.
- **Error confinement**: structuring a system so wrong state cannot spread past a chosen frontier, the containment boundary checking what crosses and the confinement area bounding how far wrongness reached, at the cost of checks and copies at every frontier.
- **Independence of containment regions**: the requirement that two regions share no part, power, clock or channel whose failure would take both at once.
- **Fault isolation**: locating a failed part and cutting it out of the working configuration, then reassigning work among those that remain and recording the new arrangement, at the cost of diagnosis time and reduced capacity afterward.
- **Bulkhead pattern**: partitioning resources so exhaustion in one partition cannot starve the others, at the cost of unusable slack sitting in idle partitions.
- **Cell-based architecture**: running many complete independent copies of a service, each with its own capacity and dependencies, so one fault's blast radius covers only one cell's users, at the cost of duplicated operations and routing.
- **Anti-affinity placement**: spreading replicas across separate failure groupings, subsetting having each client talk to a fixed small group of servers rather than all, shuffle sharding making each group a distinct random draw so no two clients share a full fate, at the cost of uneven load and lost cache locality.
- **Failure domain**: a grouping of resources sharing one cause of failure such as a power feed or a switch, an availability zone being one isolated in power, cooling and network, fate sharing binding two components the same way.
- **Single point of failure**, also **SPOF**: a component whose loss alone stops the whole service, a hard dependency with no path around it, where decoupling middleware placing a message layer between caller and callee, or a default answer, lets a caller absorb a soft dependency's absence.
- **Damage assessment**: the fault tolerance phase determining how far an error spread before recovery begins, preceded by damage confinement structuring the system to limit that spread, followed by fault treatment keeping the same fault from producing further errors, at the cost of time spent before repair and of working capacity removed.

### Recovery, rollback and durable state

- **Error masking**, also **fault masking**: absorbing wrong state with redundancy already held in the state so nothing wrong reaches the boundary and nothing is reported, at the cost of hiding accumulated faults until the redundancy runs out.
- **Backward error recovery**: recovering by discarding the corrupted present and rolling back to a saved state, where forward recovery instead builds a new state to continue from, at the cost of continual saving and of work redone.
- **Checkpoint**: a saved copy of state a later run resumes from, incremental when only changes are written and coordinated when participants save at agreed points rather than each on its own schedule, log compaction discarding superseded records so replay from it stays short, at the cost of time and space per save.
- **Domino effect**: the cascade in which rolling one participant back forces its partners back too, avoided by a recovery line of saves forming a state the system could have held, and leaving orphan messages recorded as received but never sent.
- **Message logging**: recording messages in order so a restarted participant is replayed forward, pessimistically written before acting or optimistically written late, idempotent replay letting repeats do no further harm and state reconstruction from a log rebuilding the present from recorded history, at the cost of log volume and deterministic replay.
- **Recovery block**: a construct that runs a primary routine, tests its result and tries an alternate module when the test fails, at the cost of several routines to maintain and nothing to return once the alternates are exhausted.
- **Write-ahead log**: recording an intended change durably before applying it so recovery can finish or discard it, the redo log reapplying committed work and the undo log reversing uncommitted work, journaling doing the same for a file system, held on stable storage assumed to survive the failures tolerated, at the cost of writing data twice.
- **Atomic commit**: making a group of changes take effect all together or not at all, reversed afterward by compensating transactions or a saga pattern of local steps each paired with an undo, at the cost of locks held while a silent coordinator leaves participants blocked.
- **Atomic action**: an activity whose participating components neither observe nor are observed by anything outside it until it completes, extended by a coordinated atomic action that adds cooperative exception handling across participants, and bounded by a recovery region whose entry state is captured so it can be reentered, at the cost of restricted concurrency and capture overhead.
- **Backup**: a separate copy kept so the original can be replaced after loss or corruption, an immutable backup unalterable for a retention period and an offline backup copy unreachable from the running system, point-in-time recovery combining a copy with the log after it, restore testing and backup verification catching silent backup failure, at the cost of storage and upkeep.

### Restart, repair and deliberate failure

- **Crash-only design**: building crash-only components whose only stop is a crash and only start is recovery, so recovery is exercised on every stop, externalized state keeping what they hold in a durable store, recursive restartability making a restart of any subtree a safe action, at the cost of every component tolerating vanished dependencies.
- **Restart as recovery**: treating a fresh start as the standard repair for a component in an unknown state, cold from persistent state alone or warm with some state preserved, at the cost of discarding volatile work or carrying corruption forward.
- **Microreboot**, also **fine-grained restart**: restarting the smallest component that can clear a fault rather than the process or the machine, guided by a restart tree recording which components must go down together and in what order, at the cost of building every component to restart in isolation and detection precise enough to choose one.
- **Process supervision**: running a parent that replaces a child when it dies, arranged as a supervisor tree whose restart strategy and escalation decide how much goes down with it, at the cost of hiding a defect that keeps recurring in a crash loop.
- **Recovery time objective**, also **RTO**: the longest interval between disruption and restored service a system's owners will accept, the recovery point objective bounding how much recent committed work a recovery may lose, both rehearsed by a recovery drill and by a disaster recovery exercise standing in for the loss of a whole site.
- **Software rejuvenation**: planned restart of a running program before software aging, the drift and leakage of long-running state, reaches failure, at the cost of scheduled downtime and lost warm state.
- **Rolling restart**: restarting instances a few at a time so the service keeps answering throughout, ramped back to full load as caches and pools warm, at the cost of two versions coexisting and slower capacity recovery.
- **Fault injection testing**: deliberately causing a fault to observe detection, containment and recovery, at compile time or at runtime, as crash injection, latency injection, network partition injection, clock skew injection, resource exhaustion injection, disk failure injection or Byzantine behavior injection, at the cost of instrumentation that must never ship enabled.
- **Software-implemented fault injection**: corrupting state, returning errors or delaying calls from inside the program itself, judged against a golden run comparison of the same workload without injection, beside error seeding that plants known defects to estimate the unknown ones, at the cost of instrumentation that perturbs the timing it measures.
- **Robustness testing**: driving a component's interfaces with invalid and extreme inputs to find where it fails rather than refuses, using a test harness for failure that stands in for a remote dependency at its worst, at the cost of unrealistic crashes to triage and a harness to maintain.
- **Chaos engineering**: introducing faults into a working system against a written steady-state hypothesis, scoped to a small share of traffic with an abort condition fixed in advance, at the cost of real disruption to real users, a game day exercise rehearsing an injected failure with the team present and a failover drill moving work to a standby on schedule.

### Redundancy, voting and diversity

- **Redundancy**: keeping more resources or information than a fault-free run needs, in hardware, software, information, time or separate locations, at the cost of the extra resources themselves.
- **Static redundancy**, also **masking redundancy**: spare capacity permanently in use so faults are absorbed without reconfiguration, where dynamic redundancy brings a spare in only after detection, at the cost of running every copy always or of a gap.
- **N-modular redundancy**: running N identical units on one input and deciding from their outputs, tripled so a majority outvotes one wrong unit or doubled to reveal disagreement without naming the culprit, at the cost of N times the resources.
- **Majority voting**: taking as the answer whatever more than half the units produced, through a voter whose own failure defeats the arrangement, sift-out and self-purging schemes dropping units that disagree, at the cost of nothing returned when no majority exists.
- **Exact voting**: requiring bit-for-bit agreement before an output is accepted, where inexact voting allows a tolerance, median voting takes the middle numeric value, plurality the commonest and weighted the most trusted, at the cost of tolerances that hide errors.
- **Hybrid redundancy**: a voting core backed by spares that replace outvoted units, pair-and-a-spare backing two compared units with a third, at the cost of paying for the core and the spares together.
- **N-version programming**: independently written implementations of one specification run together and compared, with design, data and functional diversity as the ways alternatives are made different, at the cost of funding every implementation.
- **Diversity assumption failure**: the observed result that independently written programs still make correlated mistakes, because the hard parts of a problem are hard for everyone.

### Standby, failover and agreement

- **Standby sparing**: keeping a spare ready to take over a failed unit's work, hot when running and current, warm when partly current, cold when powered down, at the cost of capacity that does nothing or delay before it serves.
- **Active-active**: two or more units all serving traffic and all covering for each other, where active-passive keeps one waiting, at the cost of reconciling state while both write or of idle capacity and a transfer step.
- **Process pair**: a primary and a backup arranged so the backup continues the primary's work, as a checkpointing process pair sending whole state, with delta checkpointing sending only the change since the last one, or as a persistent process rebuilding from durable records, at the cost of doubled resources and state transfer.
- **Failover**: automatic transfer of work from a failed unit to a standby, switchover being the planned version and failback the return after repair, at the cost of transfer time and the risk of two units both acting.
- **Flapping**: repeated rapid alternation of a component between healthy and failed in its monitors' eyes, causing transfer after transfer and false failovers of units that never failed.
- **Quorum**: the least number of members whose agreement lets a group act, chosen so two disjoint groups cannot both act, with quorum loss halting service and a witness node breaking ties, at the cost of another participant to keep alive.
- **Split brain**: the condition where a partitioned group divides into parts that each believe they alone are in charge and both act, leaving replicas divergent until someone decides which changes lose.
- **Fencing**: forcibly stopping a suspect member from touching shared resources before its replacement starts, at the resource itself, by resetting the node as STONITH does, or with a rising fencing token, at the cost of cutting off members merely slow.
- **Lease**: a permission granted for a bounded time that lapses unless renewed, so a silent holder loses its claim and a stale leader or zombie node cannot keep acting, at the cost of clocks the participants must agree on.
- **Byzantine fault**: a fault whose effect is unrestricted, so the component may send wrong, inconsistent or entirely plausible messages and collude with others, the byzantine generals problem asking how correct participants still agree.
- **Byzantine fault tolerance**, also **BFT**: continuing correct service while some participants behave arbitrarily, needing more than three times as many participants as tolerated faults and quorums intersecting in correct members, at the cost of many more replicas and message rounds.
- **Self-stabilization**: the property that a system started in any state whatsoever returns to a legitimate one within bounded time and stays legitimate while no further fault occurs.

### Breakers, retries and overload

- **Circuit breaker**: a guard that stops calling a failing dependency once errors pass its trip condition, then admits trial calls half-open until the dependency answers again, at the cost of refusing calls that would have worked.
- **Retry**: repeating a failed operation on the chance the failure was temporary, bounded by a retry limit or a shared budget, skipped when the deadline cannot fit it, and held back by client-side throttling, adaptive throttling cutting a client's rate in proportion to observed rejections, at the cost of load on something already struggling.
- **Exponential backoff**: doubling the wait before each successive attempt, jittered so clients do not retry in unison and capped by a ceiling, at the cost of long delays before a late attempt finally succeeds.
- **Idempotency**: the property that repeating an operation leaves the same result as performing it once, recognized by an idempotency key or deduplication token so at-least-once delivery becomes effectively once, at the cost of remembering what was already seen.
- **Backpressure**: propagating the signal that a consumer is full so producers slow down, by flow control, granted credit, handshaking that asks a callee whether it can accept work, or bounded queues that refuse work, at the cost of pushing the problem onto callers who must handle it.
- **Load shedding**: refusing part of the offered work early so the accepted part still completes, dropping by request criticality carried with each call and capping concurrency or rate at intake, at the cost of failing requests that capacity might have served.
- **Resource pool exhaustion**: the overload condition in which every worker, connection or handle is already committed, as thread pool exhaustion, connection pool exhaustion and file descriptor exhaustion, ending in out-of-memory termination when the platform kills a process for exceeding its allowance.
- **Tail latency**: the response time at high percentiles, which decides user experience while the average looks healthy, tail at scale making a request that fans out to many servers almost certain to touch a slow one, and latency amplification turning a small delay deep in a dependency chain into a large one at the edge.
- **Four golden signals**: the measurement set of latency, traffic, errors and saturation, chosen so few dashboards cover most failure modes, saturation reading how full the most constrained resource is and capacity headroom naming the share deliberately left unused so a surge stays inside what remains.
- **Service level indicator**, also **SLI**: a chosen quantitative measure of service quality, a service level objective being an internal target for it over a stated window and a service level agreement an externally promised target with a consequence attached, service level agreement inversion being a promise stronger than the dependencies can hold, the error budget being the unreliability an objective still allows.
- **Steady state pattern**: designing every accumulation of data, logs or cached entries so the system purges it automatically without human intervention, at the cost of purge logic and records deleted sooner than someone wanted.

### Degradation, fallback and safe states

- **Safe state**: a condition a system can be placed in where no further harm results even though function is reduced or absent, chosen against availability since every failure mode trades refusing service against acting on state that may be wrong.
- **Inert output default**: driving an output to a value that causes no action when the controlling logic cannot be trusted, where hold-last-value instead keeps presenting the last good output, at the cost of losing the function or acting on stale data.
- **Graceful degradation**: shedding function in a planned order so the most valuable service survives, through degraded responses, a brownout of quality under load, or a limp-home mode that only reaches repair, at the cost of designing and testing every reduced mode.
- **Fallback**: serving something weaker when the live path fails, whether a fixed prepared answer, the last cached one, or a chain of progressively weaker sources, at the cost of staleness and of callers unable to tell a default from a real result.
- **Fail-static behavior**: continuing to serve on last known configuration and state when the control plane or the writable path is unreachable, offering reads only or a maintenance notice, at the cost of ignoring changes made meanwhile.
- **Graceful shutdown**: stopping a component through a controlled sequence that refuses new work, finishes what it accepted and drains connections until a drain timeout aborts the rest, at the cost of held capacity and code that honors the signal.

### Failure shapes under stress

- **Cascading failure**: a sequence where one component's failure shifts load or corruption onto neighbors until they fail in turn, named a chain reaction when a dead instance's load kills its peers, carried through integration points where a call hangs, and showing as blocked threads waiting on a slow response.
- **Metastable failure state**, also **metastability**: a condition where a system stays broken under load it previously handled because a sustaining feedback loop keeps generating the work that keeps it failing, retry-induced metastability when clients retry, cache-induced metastability when a cold cache never refills, hysteresis in recovery putting the exit threshold below the level that broke it.
- **Gray failure**: a failure that degrades delivered service while still looking healthy to the checks deciding whether the component is used, the observability gap between what a component reports about itself and what its users measure defining it, and silent partial failure losing part of a system's function while its interfaces still report success.
- **Thundering herd**, also **dogpile**: the surge when many waiting clients are released at one instant, as a cache stampede on an expired popular entry, a cold cache after restart, or a fleet restarting together, a self-denial attack being the same surge invited by the organization's own announcement.
- **Congestion collapse**: the state where rising offered load drives completed work toward zero because capacity goes to waiting and repeating, goodput falling as queues grow past the point where anything in them is worth finishing, load amplification making each request cost more internal work and capacity degradation shrinking what remains, queuing latency growth taking over as utilization nears capacity.
- **Unbalanced capacity**: a caller able to generate far more load than the callee was sized to absorb, the scaling effect turning a relation that worked at small instance counts into a bottleneck, an expensive user or unwanted traffic from crawlers consuming the share meant for ordinary users, and a hot spot concentrating load on one shard or key.
- **Force multiplier**: an automation tool carrying a wrong instruction across a whole fleet faster than any human can intervene, answered by the governor pattern that limits how fast an automated action may repeat, at the cost of slowing legitimate bulk operations.
- **Correlated failure**: simultaneous failure of components whose failures were assumed independent, from a shared design, version, input, power feed or configuration change, so redundancy delivers less than it promised.
- **Dormant failure**, also **unrevealed failure**: a failure of a spare or protective element that nothing notices because nothing calls on it, discovered at the moment it is finally needed.
- **Silent data corruption**: alteration of stored or transmitted data that no check reports, so wrong data is used as though correct, bit rot accumulating unnoticed and damage amplified as it is copied and aggregated.

### Defaults and error paths

- **Deny by default**: refusing anything not explicitly permitted, the fail-safe defaults principle resting a decision on explicit permission rather than on absent prohibition, at the cost of enumerating every legitimate case first.
- **Insecure default configuration**: a shipped setting that leaves a system exposed until someone changes it, exploited most easily where nobody knows to look.
- **Definite initialization**: giving every variable, flag and mode a chosen starting value so no behavior depends on whatever was left there, at the cost of initialization code everywhere.
- **Explicit failure**: reporting that an operation did not succeed rather than returning something that resembles success, loud enough that it cannot be overlooked, since a silent success leaves nobody a reason to look, at the cost of callers that must handle the report.
- **Error swallowing**, also **exception swallowing**: discarding a caught error without handling or reporting it, as an empty exception block, an unchecked error condition, an unchecked return value, a check written wrongly so failures pass as successes, or detection without action where code recognizes the condition and does nothing.
- **Uncaught exception**: the weakness in which a raised exception reaches the top of the program and terminates it, beside a reachable assertion that input can drive execution to, a catch of a generic exception treating unrelated faults alike, and improper cleanup on a thrown exception leaving resources held and state half-written.
- **Idealized fault-tolerant component**: a component split into a normal activity part delivering the specified service and an abnormal activity part holding the handlers, signaling an interface exception when its caller broke the contract, a failure exception when it cannot deliver, and handling a local exception invisibly, at the cost of declared exceptional outcomes at every boundary.
- **Exception propagation**: passing an unhandled exception outward to the caller, turning a local problem into an interface obligation, where exception masking handles it so completely no caller learns of it and exception resolution picks the single exception to handle when several arrive at once from cooperating components, at the cost of masking losing the signal that something is degrading.
- **Failure to fail securely**: the weakness in which an error path leaves a system more permissive than its normal path, as when dropped privileges are assumed rather than verified.
